# Changelog

Notable changes, newest first. This project is pre-1.0 and follows no compatibility promise until it has one, per `spec/18-package-layout.md`. The storage format version is stated in every release, because a file outlives the build that wrote it.

The version number says how far through the plan we are. **The minor version is the number of milestones finished**, and from 0.4.0 the milestones it counts are the G series. 0.4.0 is the release where G1 closed, 0.4.y is work inside G2, 0.5.0 is the release where G2 closes, and so on up to G10, which is the ten times claim on TPC-H SF100 and is named 1.0 rather than 0.13.0 because pretending otherwise would be silly. Patch releases happen whenever enough has landed to be worth a tag, which in practice is every few pull requests. The milestones are the issues at https://github.com/tamnd/rudb/issues.

The count does not restart at a handover, because a version number cannot go backwards, and there have now been two of them. 0.0.y through 0.2.y were the M series, where 0.1.0 closed M0 and 0.2.0 closed M1. 0.3.0 closed F0 and 0.3.y was work against the F series. The G series is the graph engine plan and it took the number over at 0.4.0, with G0 and G1 both landing inside 0.3.y, so 0.4.0 is the first release the G series names rather than the fourth milestone the project has closed. Each plan runs beside the ones before it rather than replacing them, per `notes/Spec/2140/engine-v2/00-README.md`, and two plans cannot both own one version number, so one of them has it and the others do not. Work that lands against an M or an F milestone still ships in whatever release it lands in.

## 0.4.15

A patch release of eight pull requests, and the one to take instead of 0.4.14, which was tagged but whose release run stops at the gate, because the gate finds a flatten in #1494 with its reason one line too far from the call. The native directory format number stays at 28 and the storage format version at 9, so this build and 0.4.14 read each other's files.

#1504 fixes a load that 0.4.14 can fail. #1491 made the chunks the Parquet reader hands out windows of one shared page, and `bit_packed` judged whether packing pays by the window's share of that page rather than by its rows. An INTEGER column whose values span nearly the whole `i32` range was then packed at 32 bits and refused, so `CREATE TABLE ... AS SELECT * FROM read_parquet(...)` over ClickBench `hits` could stop with `packed values from ... which a INTEGER cannot hold`, depending on the thread count. The same decision also let the bytes of a file depend on how many readers a page had. #1507 then packs such a column instead of leaving it flat, by moving the base down until both ends of what the width can say fit the type, so a thousand values just under `i32::MAX` pack at ten bits. #1505 holds a distinct count's pair runs in fixed size chunks, so a chunk one instance frees is the one it asks for next, which took ClickBench q9 on the 10M file from about 116 MB to 102 MB resident and q10 from 118 MB to 106 MB.

Three make queries faster or smaller. #1511 gives `substring` with a literal start and length a loop over whole chunks, and runs a scalar subquery with no join condition as a `Broadcast` that puts its one row beside each driving chunk on every thread, which took TPC-H q22 at SF1 from about three times slower than DuckDB to 1.74 times faster, with the same answer and a new error when a subquery gives more than one row and no driving row arrives, as DuckDB does. #1513 decides LIKE for a whole dictionary group even when a chunk is small, so the decoded blocks are dropped after the sweep, and ClickBench q22 on the 10M file went from 288 MB to 111 MB resident and q23 from 338 MB to 159 MB. #1514 lays a hash join's build side end to end with one concat and reads a plain key column from it instead of laying the key out a second time, which took TPC-H q9 at SF1 from about 73 ms to about 60 ms.

Two more change no code. #1508 moves that reason next to the call so the gate passes. #1515 fixes a catalog test that #1491 had quietly turned into a different test: it cloned one column to make a second, and since a clone now shares the page, the two columns really were one allocation, and the budget was right to let the second through. The test now builds each column from two parts, so each is a run the parent owns.

## 0.4.14

A patch release of eleven pull requests. The native directory format number stays at 28 and the storage format version at 9. #1495 writes exact nonzero counts into the file catalog of a native file, and upgrades the metadata of an older file once when it opens it, so that a count can be answered without reading the table directory.

Most of this release is the sorted load of milestone S0 in #1380. #1491 shares pages instead of copying them when a chunk is cut, cloned or laid back into a row group, and #1493 holds dictionary codes the same way, so a clone of a coded column shares them too. #1494 writes a sorted fixed width column to its places straight from its pieces in one pass instead of three. #1497 stops the thread that drains a finished query from reading every dictionary code to check it when the values are already in memory, which was about a fifth of the statement. #1502 hashes a dictionary's entries once for all the chunks that share it in the append. The sorted CTAS of SF1 `lineitem` now takes 0.38 s to 0.52 s against 0.50 s to 0.65 s for DuckDB 1.5.5 on the same machine, and the unsorted one 0.21 s against 0.38 s, which closed #1365.

On ClickBench, #1495 answers a cold Q2 count from the catalog certificate, 2.1 ms against 31.7 ms for DuckDB on the 10M file, and #1496 probes a row the direct map misses as soon as it is met, so the rest of its run hits the map. #1500 adds fourteen aggregates, among them `list`, `first`, `last`, `string_agg`, `bool_and`, `product` and the variances and standard deviations, and the `list_<aggregate>` macros on top of `list_aggr`. #1492, #1499 and #1501 add and fix `cargo xtask linkjoin`, which times each TPC-H query with the link join against the same query with the hash join forced.

## 0.4.13

A patch release of thirty five pull requests. The native directory format number goes from 27 to 28, in #1473, which keeps a small signature of the grams in each dictionary block so a substring LIKE can skip blocks that cannot match. This build reads 22 through 28, so it opens every file the last few releases wrote, but 0.4.12 does not read a file written by this one. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The v0.4.12 tag never became a release, because its release run failed the row loop check at the verify step, so everything in 0.4.12 ships for the first time here too.

The bulk load is the first part of the W series, milestone W1 in #1384. #1452 decodes and sorts a global dictionary on every core in the close, and #1486 encodes the dictionary blocks left over at the end of a load across threads as well. On the ClickBench 1M sample that close was 72% of the profile on one thread, and the load went from 19 to 29 s down to 3.3 to 3.9 s on the 32 core box with byte identical output. #1476 stops the chooser from testing integer kinds it would never keep, #1481 counts FSST training pairs in a flat array instead of a hash map, and #1478 counts numeric frequencies by a value's bits. #1454 lays a sorted string column in ranges of rows on several threads, #1461 reads an integer column into its sieve a block at a time, #1460 syncs the directory after a load renames its file into place, and #1469 lets queries run while a load into a new table does.

On ClickBench, #1473 and #1487 make a selective LIKE cheaper, one by skipping dictionary blocks and one by fetching the second scan column only for the rows left. #1462 caches native frequency summaries and repeated filtered counts, #1468 counts a narrow integer column into an array, #1470 keeps a dictionary block the second time a sweep decodes it, #1479 picks a filter's rows straight from its answers, #1485 reads the lengths of dictionary values from a table built once, and #1482 binds a mirrorable Parquet read from its outline. #1483 makes the rule report print the change over the whole suite rather than only its median query, and #1489 says how wide that column's noise is, from the rules that changed no plan.

On TPC-H and JOB, #1453 removes projections that only pass columns through, #1456 folds a run of rows into one group when the slots come in runs, #1464 adds up a grouped total per chunk, #1458 and #1472 fold full pair and count runs instead of growing them, #1471 reduces a child with no link through the parent's key map as an exact bitmap, #1474 keeps each sort instance's rows in place instead of copying them under the lock, and #1455, #1475 and #1484 reuse plans for exact counts and the Q3 and Q4 summary aggregates. #1466 sums a decimal column into DECIMAL(38,s), #1457 moves timestamps by intervals a vector at a time, and #1477 adds `list_aggr`.

#1459 builds on Rust 1.85 again and #1488 is the fix for the check that stopped 0.4.12: it says why the lambda row loop and its flattens are there, and it reads a flatten through a question mark. #1465 is doc 35, the forward run measured. The release itself teaches `cargo xtask version` to leave an outside crate alone when it happens to share our version number, which `slab` at 0.4.12 did.

## 0.4.12

A patch release of twelve pull requests. The native directory format number stays at 27 and the storage format version at 9. #1434 adds a new section to the directory, and 0.4.11 refuses a directory section it does not know, so a file this build writes with it may not open in 0.4.11. This build still reads every file 0.4.11 wrote.

The bulk load is most of this release. #1426 gathers a run of adjacent small Parquet row groups into one morsel when the sink asks for it, which is what the 1,203 row groups of the ClickBench 10M sample needed, because each one used to become an 8k row stripe written on its own behind the writer's lock, a summed 961 s of waiting by the load metrics #1418 added. #1439 then takes the encode out of the lock, so a stripe is encoded with no lock held, its dictionaries and statistics are merged under the lock, its pages are built outside it and only the write is under it again. #1435 lays a sorted string column's bytes in the order of its rows rather than the order they arrived in, which took the distinct count of `l_comment` on the sorted SF1 `lineitem` load from 550 to 1570 ms of CPU at full width down to 190 to 250 ms. #1436 is a follow up to #1423 in 0.4.11, which took one thing too many away: an arena nobody counted is laid in one copy again, and it is only its address that is no longer written down.

On ClickBench, #1434 keeps certified host aggregate state in the directory, so q29 on the 10M file is 0.834 ms against 254.765 ms before it, and #1438 gathers the inputs of a pushed LIKE only when a quarter of the rows or fewer are left, which takes q23 on the same file from 208.965 ms to 126.374 ms. #1447 stops binding a table from opening the order summaries of all 105 columns when the query has no grouping, since those only help a grouped aggregate whose key arrives sorted. #1448 rewrites a filter on `DATE '1970-01-01' + EventDate` as a filter on `EventDate` itself, so row group statistics and zones can skip on q37 to q43, and it answers q7's minimum and maximum from the footer instead of 10M rows.

#1431 adds `list_reduce` and #1437 adds `invoke`, which is the last function on the pin that takes a lambda. #1449 takes the let chains back out of `assemble`, because the workspace floor is Rust 1.85 and let chains only became stable in 1.88, so the minimum supported version job had been failing on every pull request since #1435. #1432 is the measurement of the Parquet mirror.

## 0.4.11

A patch release of fifteen pull requests. The native directory format number stays at 27 and the storage format version at 9. #1416 and #1425 add new sections to the directory, and 0.4.10 refuses a directory section it does not know, so a file this build writes with them may not open in 0.4.10. This build still reads every file 0.4.10 wrote.

On TPC-H SF1 native the suite total is 46.67 G instructions on server3, down from 55.39 G at 6e6af60e, measured the same way. Most of that is three things. #1412 closes a group as soon as a sorted key moves past it, so `GROUP BY l_orderkey` in q18 skips the hash table for nearly every group, and q18's inner aggregate now takes 1.75 G against DuckDB's 1.81 G. #1422 fixes a regression from #1410, where an integer key read by value built a new map of up to 262,144 places on almost every chunk in every radix partition, which took q18 to 10.2 G and about 1 GB resident. It is 4.1 G and 173 MB now. #1428 fixes a regression from #1414, where data pages were decoded through the new windowed directory cursor and paid its check on every small read. q06 went from 0.94 G to 0.72 G, and 20 of the 22 queries went down.

On ClickBench, #1410 and #1419 read an integer group key by value against a window that lasts across chunks, #1416 answers certified two key TopN queries from pair counts kept in the directory, and #1425 keeps bounded string frequencies with their text so string predicates plan without opening the dictionary. #1427 decodes only the rows it needs when building a pair summary, and #1424 makes the numeric frequency pass at the end of a load cheaper. #1414 lowers the memory floor of opening a table and links the shell at a fixed address on Linux.

#1415 lets the link reduction take a parent key that reaches the join through another join, which is Q3 and Q5. #1421 mirrors a large Parquet file into a native file on first read. #1418 adds `rudb_write_metrics()` for the stages of a bulk load, and #1402 adds `rudb_device_card()`. #1423 fixes q16 at SF1 sometimes returning `p_type` strings no row holds, because a string arena was known by an address that was about to be freed. #1411 is a doc change.

## 0.4.10

A patch release of two pull requests, both on the sorted load of SF1 `lineitem`. No format changes. The native directory format stays at 27 and the storage format version at 9, so this build and 0.4.9 read each other's files.

A dictionary string column written through the inverse of the sort order now writes its codes that way too, in #1407, where #1404 had left it pulling each four byte code through the order. The four dictionary columns of the sorted SF1 CTAS gather in 17 to 47 ms each instead of 57 to 103 ms. A sorted string column reserves its whole arena before laying the pieces, in #1408, rather than growing it by doubling, which had copied the 183MB of comments again at every step. That lay went from 111 to 146 ms to 62 to 120 ms.

Writing long strings straight into an arena in output order was measured and not merged, and the reasons are on #1408.

## 0.4.9

A patch release of six pull requests, five of them on the sorted load of SF1 `lineitem` and one on memory for string statistics. The native directory format goes from 26 to 27, in #1398. This build reads 22 through 27, so it opens every file the last few releases wrote, but 0.4.8 does not read a file written by this one. The storage format version is unchanged at 9.

The sort sink writes its normalized keys a column at a time with a typed loop per layout, in #1396, where it had asked every key column for a `Value` a row. Each thread sorts its own rows as it hands them over and the finish merges the sorted runs by splitters on every thread, in #1400, so the sort overlaps the scan and has no single threaded first cut. When the order is a few long ascending runs of the input, which a sort by ship month over rows in order key order is, the columns are written through the inverse of the order rather than read through it, in #1404, which reads each column once front to back instead of spending a cache line on every value. The zone ends of a string column held as views are found from the four byte prefixes in the views, in #1403, so the append reads the bytes only when a prefix ties. On the sorted SF1 `lineitem` CTAS at full width, #1400 took a busy machine from 1.46 to 1.61 s down to 1.25 to 1.28 s, #1403 took 30 to 45 ms off, and #1404 took the best of nine runs from 0.71 s to 0.63 s.

The load writes each dictionary block into the file as soon as it is encoded, in #1398, so it no longer holds every encoded block until the table closes. Peak memory for the five dictionary columns of `hits` at eight million rows went down about a third. Reading a string frequency synopsis visits the dictionary blocks it needs once and keeps none of them, in #1397, which takes ClickBench q13 at ten million rows from 28 MiB to 18 MiB.

Two approaches on the same load were measured and not merged, and the reasons are on #1380 and #1401: packing the payload into rows in the sink, and copying a string column a chunk at a time or pointing its views into many arenas.

## 0.4.8

A patch release of twenty one pull requests. Most of it is speed: the TPC-H hash kernels and join probe, and the sort and append at the end of a load. Beside that are the first two pieces of the G5 semi join reduction and more of the D4 list surface. No format changes. The native directory format stays at 26 and the storage format version at 9, so this build and 0.4.7 read each other's files.

The hash kernel reads the rows a filter kept through the filter's codes, in #1366. A filter hands its columns on as a dictionary whose codes are the kept rows, so the flat and packed fast paths never ran behind one, and most TPC-H scans are behind one. `Packed::code` is inlined into the kernels in other crates, in #1370, because thin LTO was leaving it as a call there. The join probe reads the first matching row for a whole driving chunk in one pass and skips the chain when every key has one row, in #1392, which is every primary key join. Measured on server3 on TPC-H SF1 native, each against the build before it, #1366 takes the suite from 51.161 to 50.690 G instructions and #1370 from 50.341 to 49.601. #1392 saves waiting rather than work, so its instructions are flat and it takes 1.7 to 2.7 percent off wall time on an idle server2. `spec/perf/18-the-rows-a-filter-kept.md` in #1367 writes down what the presize fix in 0.4.7 and #1366 taught.

A sort that fitted in memory finishes on every thread of the lease, in #1364, where the laying of the columns in order had been on one. Sorted columns are gathered through one shared order rather than a scatter per column, in #1374. A dictionary string column stays a dictionary through the sort, in #1375, and a page of strings shared by many cuts is copied once rather than once per cut, in #1379. The statistics of a bulk append are taken a column a thread in #1369 and then over runs of chunks in #1381, so one long string column no longer holds up the rest.

A string length is handed over a vector at a time out of offsets unpacked once, in #1357, which was ClickBench 28's worst cost. The native writer counts each integer column's distinct values exactly on the pass it already makes, in #1373, so `COUNT(DISTINCT x)` over an integer column is answered from the directory. ClickBench q5 at ten million rows goes from 1.20 s to 0.01 s.

`rudb_graph::Rids` is new in #1371, a set of row ids in a full, sparse or dense form that can be pushed through a link. #1378 uses it: when a hash join's build side is a filtered scan of a parent table joined over a stored link, the driving scan is given the exact rows that can match rather than a Bloom filter, and skips every part that holds none of them. A key that cannot be read falls back to the Bloom filter. #1393 stops a push that has removed no row by the time it has passed a third of the child table and keeps every row instead, skipping the Bloom filter too, so a reduction that was never going to remove anything costs a third of a push. It also shows the reductions in `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`.

`list_append`, `list_prepend` and their four aliases are expanded into `list_concat` the way the pin defines them as macros, in #1368. Lambdas are in with `list_transform` and `list_filter` and their aliases, in #1377.

Every G4 statistics rule has a setting that turns it off, in #1363. `cargo xtask ablate` produces the per rule table on ClickBench in #1372, and settles a tie at a limit by all four steps of the tie rule in #1382, so a query that limits inside a tie is no longer reported as changed. #1376 records a measured negative result on per thread compaction of grouped distinct pairs.

## 0.4.7

A patch release of eighteen pull requests. Most of it is G4 work on the optimizer and memory work on the load, with the first four pieces of the D4 list surface alongside. The native directory format goes from 25 to 26, in #1345. This build reads 22 through 26, so it opens every file the last few releases wrote, but 0.4.6 does not read a file written by this one. The storage format version is unchanged at 9.

A list literal is a call to `list_value` now, and a list casts by casting its elements, in #1346. `[a, 2]` over a column computes where before only a list of constants did. `CAST(['1', '2'] AS INTEGER[])` is a list of integers, and `TRY_CAST(['x', '2'] AS INTEGER[])` is `[NULL, 2]` and not null, because the element that did not go is the one that becomes null. `[]` is a list of the untyped null, so `read_parquet([])` reaches the reader and gets the pin's sentence rather than an overload message.

Two lists compare in #1348, element by element and then by length, which is the order two words are in a dictionary. A null element is the largest value there is and equals itself, which is the sort's rule and the pin's, so `[1, NULL] = [1, NULL]` is true while `NULL = NULL` is null. `=`, `<`, `ORDER BY`, `DISTINCT`, `GROUP BY`, `IN`, `min` and `max` over a list column all answer now, because they all reach that one order.

`||` joins two lists in #1354, and `list_concat` is new with `list_cat`, `array_concat` and `array_cat` as aliases of it. Before this `[1, 2] || [3]` answered the string `[1, 2][3]`, which was a wrong answer and not a missing one. A list against something that is not a list is refused with upstream's sentence. The two spellings differ on nulls the way they do on the pin: `[1] || NULL` is null and `list_concat([1], NULL)` is `[1]`. `list_concat()` with no arguments is refused here, where the pin hands back an empty VARCHAR from a function declared to return a list. That is tamnd/duckdb#11.

`length` counts a list's elements in #1359, and `array_length` is new with its optional dimension. `len`, `char_length` and `character_length` come along as aliases. A dimension other than 1 gives the pin's not implemented sentence.

The totality certificate reaches the optimizer in #1342. A relationship said whether its link was built, which is uniqueness, and now also says whether every child found a parent, which is read off the link's own counts so an older file still answers. Nothing read it in that change on its own.

Joins that a verified relationship says do nothing are deleted, in #1343. With both certificates a relationship is a foreign key that was measured rather than declared, which licenses three rewrites: an inner join nothing reads a parent column of goes, a left join over it becomes an inner join, and an `EXISTS` over it is deleted. The pass is `join_elimination`, which was already a name rudb accepted and did nothing with, and `stats.join_elimination` turns it off.

The conjuncts of a filter are put in the order the catalog's numbers say is cheapest, in #1350. They are ranked by the fraction a conjunct rejects over what it costs, which is the rank and the cost weights the executor's runtime ordering already uses. The two agree on the question and the runtime still wins wherever it has measured something, and what this buys is the first chunks of every scan, which run before the runtime has anything to say.

The null questions a file has already answered are taken out of the plan, in #1358. Over a column the store counted zero nulls in, exactly and not by sampling, `x IS NULL` is false, a filter on `x IS NOT NULL` goes away, and `count(x)` is `count(*)`. The rewrite walks from the operator down to the scan first, because an outer join in between can put a null into a column the store says has none. `stats_validity_free` turns it off.

A regression from #1293 on TPC-H q18 is fixed, in #1353. Two things were wrong. The executor took the presized room in every kind of hash table an aggregate builds, including tables an instance hands over almost at once, so q18 asked the kernel for pages it never wrote. The pass also took the row count as a group count when the product of distinct counts was larger, which is a ceiling that says nothing about how many groups there are. Now only the tables that hold the groups take the room, and the pass declines in that case.

A sort's spilled run is written a column at a time, in #1355, which closes #1347. A run used to be a sequence of chunks, so nothing went to disk until every column of the sorted output had been built in memory, and that output is bigger than what the sort was holding. The spill itself was running out of memory, which is why every trigger above three quarters of the limit had failed. Each column is written and dropped as it is laid now.

A global dictionary's payload blocks say where they are, in #1345, and that is the format change. A block of a dictionary is final the moment the next thousand codes are handed out, but the index described the payload as one run and recorded only where each block ended, so nothing could be written until close. Each block carries its own start and length now, which is eight more bytes of index a block. The file this build writes is laid out the same as before otherwise, and a file from before is turned into the new shape at open.

A global dictionary stops holding the raw bytes of every value for the whole load, in #1356, which is the change the format change was for. On `hits` those bytes are about twelve of the seventeen gigabytes the load peaks at. A block is sealed and encoded when it fills and the raw bytes go then. Whether a value was seen before is a hash lookup and a second hash under a different seed rather than a byte comparison, and the sort at close decodes one column at a time rather than all five at once.

A run of text offsets is unpacked straight into the answer, in #1351, rather than into a buffer of its own and then copied, and three fixes to the gate came with it because main was red on them. #1360 is the fourth such fix, teaching the flatten lint that `iter_mut().flatten()` is the option flatten and not a column being copied.

`cargo xtask sections` runs TPC-H with the graph sections off and on and compares the answers byte for byte, in #1349, which is the measurement section 9.2 of the graph spec asks for before G3 can be claimed. It found two bugs on its first pass and both are fixed in the same change. It reports how many link joins each query read, because a query where the answers agree and nothing was read has measured nothing.

Three measurement documents land in `spec/`. #1344 takes the inventory of the ClickBench suite and finds four more grouped queries that were already served by the reader, each using between 182 and 575 times less processor time than DuckDB. #1352 intersects the four blocking causes named in document 29, finds that the aggregate restriction on its own unblocks no query, and prices a bound that certifies q14 after reading 0.31 percent of the table. #1361 measures all forty three ClickBench queries unmodified on both native formats at ten million rows, where three clear ten times on both axes and six lose, four of those through `COUNT(DISTINCT UserID)`.

## 0.4.6

A patch release of six pull requests, most of it the G3 graph work reaching the point where the layer can be measured and turned off. The native directory format is unchanged at 25 and the storage format version is unchanged at 9. There is one new section kind, the degree statistics of a relationship, and by section 3.1 a reader that does not know it ignores it, so a file written by this reads in 0.4.5 and a file written by 0.4.5 reads here.

A relationship's measured shape is stored beside its forward link and reported, in #1334. The degree distribution, the certificates and the gather locality that #1329 measured had no caller, and this is the caller: the link build attaches them to the child table as their own section kind and `rudb_links()` grows six columns for them, the mean, the maximum and the ninety ninth percentile of the fan out, the gather locality, and whether the parent key is unique and the child side total. Their own section rather than the link's payload, because the link's reader checks its length exactly and rejects an unknown layout, so growing it makes an older build drop the whole link, where an unknown section kind is ignored. They are measured in their own pass over the parent row ids rather than fused into the loop that chooses a form, because what a degree histogram costs is the scattered increment into a counter per parent and not the sequential read the build makes twice already, so fusing would save the cheap half and put a histogram inside a function whose job is to choose a form.

What a graph structure the build turned away would have cost is written down, in #1337. A key map or a forward link that was measured and not kept left nothing in the file, so `rudb_links()` reported a null where a size belongs and somebody deciding whether to raise the budget had to rebuild to find out what they were deciding about. It leaves a section table entry with no extents now, the form it would have taken in `flags` and what its payload would have been in `header_bytes`, which that field can carry because an entry with nothing behind it has no header for it to describe. Fifty six bytes a refusal, paid once per column the builder looked at. The form column stays null for a structure that is not there, because a size is a fact about a structure that does not exist and a form is a way of reading one, and filling both would mix what is believed with what is there, which is the one thing that table does not do.

`graph_sections` turns the layer off, in #1335. The setting had been in the rules module since the switches landed and nothing read it, so `SET graph_sections = 'off'` parsed, reported itself as set and changed nothing. That is worse than a dead switch usually is, because section 9.2 of `spec/graph/09-measurement.md` is the exit criterion for the whole layer and is written entirely in terms of that setting: run the suite with the sections off and again with them on, and the answers have to be identical on every query of every suite. A switch that switches nothing makes that comparison two runs of the same thing. The rule is read where a stored section reaches a plan and nowhere else, so with it off the optimizer is handed no relationship, no rewrite has anything to fire on and the executor never opens a link. The sections stay in the file and the checkpoint still writes them, which is what section 3.1 says they are for.

Every plan node has a test saying whether its output carries a row id, in #1340. Twenty two of the twenty three had one and the one that did not was the link join, which is the node the analysis exists for. The compiler already forces the answer, because the match over the node enum is exhaustive and a new operator does not build until somebody answers for it, and nothing forced the answer to be tested. `cargo xtask rids` is the other half: it reads the variants off the enum and looks for each of them in the test module beside the analysis, so an operator added without a test is a failed check naming the operator.

A key column with no nulls is hashed as two runs, and the spread moves into the last column's pass, in #1336. The hash is the largest single symbol across TPC-H at nine to ten percent of nearly every query. The first change is worth 1.4 percent on a group by on an unfiltered key and nothing anywhere else, and why is the more useful half: a filter hands on a dictionary whose codes are the rows that got through, so behind a filter a column is never flat, and every fast path that begins by asking whether a column is a flat run is dead on the columns most of a query works on. The second change is the one that paid. The hash walks the run again at the end to move entropy from the high bits into the low ones, because every table here buckets on the low bits, and that walk only has to happen once, so it belongs in the last column's pass rather than in a pass of its own. Sixteen of twenty two TPC-H queries came down and the suite came down 0.70 percent, nearly all of it the second change.

A sort spills when the database is short of memory rather than when the sort is big, in #1339. The trigger in #1330 was a share of the memory limit, and a run is a file on a disk that has to have room for it, so the clustered SF10 load started writing runs at nine and a half gigabytes on a load that had memory to spare and filled the disk. It spills when the instance is within a quarter of the limit now, and then only if it is holding enough for a file to be worth opening, which stops a query that is short of memory for another reason from turning every chunk into a run of one chunk. Spilling later is also less memory, because fewer and larger runs means the memory that is there gets used rather than written out: the smallest limit the clustered SF1 lineitem load runs under goes from 250 MB to 200, against 1400 before any of this, and the stored table is byte identical at 200 MB, at 4000 MB and against the build before the spill existed.

## 0.4.5

A patch release of eight pull requests, most of it the statistics work of G4 and the memory work that came out of it. The native directory format is unchanged at 25 and the storage format version is unchanged at 9. Statistics are written by a different code path than before but the sections themselves are the sections that were already there, so a file written by 0.4.4 reads the same and a file written by this reads in 0.4.4.

A sort that does not fit spills and merges the runs as they are read, in #1330. A sort that could not hold its input died at the allocator, and it now writes what it is holding out as a sorted run when it passes its share of the memory limit, starts again empty, and merges the runs at the end. The merge is a source rather than something `finalize` builds, which is the point of the change rather than a detail of it: a sink's peak is inside its own `finalize`, where it holds what is left of its input and the output it is assembling from it at the same time, so an operator that spills carefully on the way in and then builds the whole answer on the way out has bounded nothing, and it has bounded nothing at a moment no downstream behaviour can reach. A run carries one extra column beside the rows, a fixed forty bytes holding the normalized sort key and then where the row arrived, so comparing two of those as bytes is comparing the rows the way the sort compares them and the merge needs no key expressions and no types. The arrival being in there is what keeps the answer independent of where the spills happened to fall. SF1 `lineitem` clustered on `quarter(l_shipdate), l_orderkey, l_linenumber` goes from needing a 1400 MB limit to needing 250, and the clustered SF10 load of 59,986,052 rows completes under 12 GB where before it stopped 206 seconds in with the allocator refusing 1.5 MiB and left the table empty. The stored SF1 table is byte identical across a spilling load, a load that does not spill and the build before this one.

A group's slot is read out of an array where the key is an integer inside a known range, in #1297. A hash aggregate answers one question per row, which is which group this row's key belongs to, and the hash table answers it by hashing, masking to a bucket, loading the bucket, comparing a salt and then loading the stored key to be sure, which is two trips to memory per row and on a table larger than cache both are misses. A key that is one integer column with a known smallest and largest value does not need any of it, because the value is the address. The array is not a replacement for the table and that is the whole of the design: the operator keeps the hash table, fills it beside the array, and a value the range has no cell for or a key column in a form the batched read cannot take falls back to the buckets, which hold every group either way. That matters because a range narrower than the column is the one failure here that would be silent, and it means no reading of the new pass and no bound a store ever writes can turn into a wrong answer. `SET disabled_optimizers = 'aggregate_dense'` turns it off, and the end to end tests use that setting to run the same query twice and compare row for row rather than against a set of rows somebody typed out.

Room is taken for the groups before the first row arrives, where the distinct count says how many, in #1293. A hash aggregate started at sixty four buckets and doubled, so a group by that ends with a million groups had written about two million buckets on the way to holding one million and the last three doublings moved nearly all of it, none of which produces a row. The number that removes it is the distinct count of the grouping key, and the pass takes it as a ceiling rather than as an estimate: one key column cannot make more groups than that column has distinct values, two cannot make more than the product, and a filter underneath does not affect it because removing rows never adds a group. What it deliberately does not take is the modelled group count, which is an estimate wherever it comes from. A ceiling is the safe end because the two failures are not symmetric, since a table sized under the truth grows the way it grew before and a table sized over it holds bucket memory it never fills, which is charged against the query's budget.

A table's statistics are built as it is written rather than by reading it back, in #1328. The build had to move because the first thing it did was open the file it had just been handed and read every column of it, and a second full read with decode included is not the ten percent of the write time the plan gives it, it is most of it: on TPC-H SF1 `lineitem` the standalone build was 11.4 seconds of processor time against a write of 20.0. The writer already has the vectors, buffered as chunks and handed one column at a time to each encode worker, so every value is in memory in rid order on a thread that is about to walk it anyway, and what is left once the read is taken out is the hashing and the comparisons, which do fit. Moving it was not enough on its own, because the pass asked for every row through the generic vector accessor and then compared through a bound, which was about 355 instructions for a value whose whole job is three comparisons, so the pass reads a vector at a time now. The old build stays and is not dead, since it is the path for a file written before any of this existed and for a checkpoint that promotes a column to per stripe sketches.

A relationship's degree distribution, certificates and gather locality are measured, in #1329. This is the type on its own with no caller, and it is three of the open boxes on the graph statistics work in one loop, because they all come out of one pass over the parent row id of each child in child order, which the link build is already walking. The histogram is thirty two log buckets, which is there rather than just the mean because a mean of four with a maximum of four is a uniform fan out that parallelises by parent while a mean of four with a ninety ninth percentile of nine hundred is a workload where one worker gets the whole tail, and the mean does not tell those apart. Totality is derived, since every child found a parent when the linked count equals the child count, and uniqueness is a parameter, because it is a fact about the parent's key column and this only sees the children. Gather locality is the mean absolute distance between the parent row ids of adjacent linked children, summed into a `u128` because six hundred million children landing as far apart as they can is past what sixty four bits holds and a sum that wrapped would read as excellent locality, which is the one wrong answer here a planner would act on. It goes in its own section rather than in the link's header, because an unknown section kind is ignored where a payload of the wrong length makes an older reader drop the whole link, so a build that runs out of budget drops the statistics and keeps the link and not the other way round.

A sieve's block is read as a line rather than as four indexes into a run, in #1324. Once the group key gap closed, the top symbol on the two largest TPC-H queries is the runtime join filter, at 11.98 percent of q21 and 11.05 of q09. The filter is 512 bits a block, which is one cache line, and a value sets four of them, so a row is one trip to memory and that part was right. What it was not is cheap in instructions, because the words were a vector and each of the four lanes indexed it separately with its own compare and branch against a length nothing in the loop knew. Reading the block out once as an eight word array puts the length in the type and the four checks become one. Counted out of the disassembly rather than sampled, the loop body goes from 62 instructions a row to 56, and the filter's share of q21 goes from 12.26 percent to 11.40, which is 8.6 percent off it and agrees with what the disassembly counts. Over the suite eighteen of twenty two queries came down and the total came down by 0.79 percent, which is the claim, rather than any individual row. Which bits a hash names is part of the on disk format, so the bits are now pinned by number in a test.

The commonest dictionary values are picked rather than all of them sorted, in #1323. Counting a dictionary column builds one frequency entry per distinct value and keeps the five hundred and twelve commonest, and it was getting there by sorting all of them, which on `URL` at eight million rows is a sort of 2,266,417 entries to keep 512. A partition answers both of the questions the sort was being used for, and only the survivors are then sorted. The entries and their order are the same as before because the tie break makes the comparison total, so there is no pair the partition could order differently. Over two loads of eight million rows of the five dictionary columns it is 13.94 seconds against 8.10 and 14.35 against 9.05, faster on all ten column runs, and the same run checked that the kept entries and the largest omitted count came out identical between the two implementations rather than assuming it.

The caret under an error message is as wide as the span it points at, in #1331. It was one character wide whatever it pointed at and the pinned duckdb underlines the whole token, and the range was already in hand because the source span work at the start of D3 put a real range on every plan node and expression. A column that does not resolve and a function that does not exist now match the pin byte for byte. A span that covers the whole statement still gets one caret, because the binder attaches the span of the query it is working on to any error raised underneath it that carried none of its own, so a span the width of everything is the placeholder for not knowing where rather than a location.

## 0.4.4

A patch release of fourteen pull requests. Most of it is the G2 and G3 graph work, which is the first point in that plan where a query gets faster rather than better described. The native directory format is unchanged at 25 and the storage format version is unchanged at 9. The forward link and the two G3 bodies are sections and vector shapes rather than changes to how a column is stored, and section 3.1 still holds, which is that deleting every graph section from a rudb file changes no answer, only the time.

G3, the link join, reads the link instead of building a hash table, in #1258. A hash join over a foreign key reads the parent into a table, hashes every row of it, and probes that table once per child row, and the file already knows the answer. Where a forward link exists the join is a gather: read the row id beside the data columns and hand the parent's projected columns on as pointers into the parent, with no build side, no probe and no materialization of the parent at all. Inner, left, semi and anti, and a semi or an anti never reads the parent, so it is a sentinel test per child row. The rule that picks between the two is about the memory hierarchy rather than about algorithms, so a parent that fits in cache keeps its hash join and a parent that does not takes the link when its projection is narrow. Every join now prints which of the two it is and why the other was not chosen, because this layer's failure mode is silence and a link that was not used because a projection dropped the row id two nodes up looks exactly like a link that does not exist.

A correlated subquery over a grouped query binds against the group, in #1319. A subquery in the select list, the `HAVING` or the `ORDER BY` of a grouped query was refused by name when it correlated to the query it sits in, and that was the last construct the binder turned down for being the construct it is rather than for being wrong. The correlation that has an answer is the one on a column the block groups by, because above the aggregate that column holds the value every row of the group agreed on, so the query joins against the groups rather than against the rows going into them and answers once per output row. A correlation on a column the block neither groups by nor aggregates now gets the ordinary complaint about a missing `GROUP BY` naming the column the subquery reads, which is what the pinned duckdb says about the same query.

The whole of one parent column is read once for a gather to point into, in #1236. The row ids a child chunk carries are row ids of the parent table, so two thousand of them out of an unclustered child straddle the whole table, and a gather whose source was one part would be several gathers with the rows interleaved back together afterwards. Every part of the column is read once and laid end to end, cached per column, under a budget shared across the columns of one parent. A refusal is `None` and not an error, because the caller's answer to `None` is to run the hash join and the query answers the same either way, and an error here would make the graph path load bearing, which it must never be.

Each packed value is written once on the way out of a decode, in #1315. The frame of reference decode unpacked a unit of 1024 values into a scratch buffer and then walked that buffer adding the base back on, so every value was written twice, and the buffer is 8 KB against a chunk that is about one unit, which puts the second pass in the same order of work as the unpacking it follows. The mapping is applied at the store now, where the value is already in a register. The double write is worth about two percent, which is what the arithmetic says, and the rest of the win came from somewhere else: writing a tail through `iter_mut` instead of pushing onto a `Vec` lets the loop vectorize, and that loop is the one that decodes the offsets of a text block. Over all forty three ClickBench queries at one million rows it is 65.4 G instructions down to 56.7, with ClickBench 22 and 23 both halving.

A parent's columns are held as row ids into the parent rather than as copies, in #1234. This is the `Gathered` vector body, one row id per row into a source that is far longer, and it is the shape the link join's claim about not materializing the parent rests on. Physically it is a dictionary and logically it is the opposite of one, because a dictionary's values are few and its codes are many while a gather's source is a whole parent table, so every kernel that folds over the values to answer the codes would read fifteen million values to answer two thousand rows. A separate variant makes the compiler ask every kernel author which of the two they are holding, and nine exhaustive matches needed an arm, which is the cost and also the point.

G2, the forward link, is stored, built at checkpoint and measured, in #1206. A forward link is one parent row id per child row in row id order, in a packed form and in a monotone form which is available when the child is clustered by parent and every child has a parent and which carries the backward direction for free. It is a section rather than a stored column, because the checkpoint's second pass can append a section to a committed file and cannot add a column to stripes that are already written. A link's payload carries the parent's generation, column and name in front of it and a payload whose binding does not match is refused, which is the one thing in this layer that could be a wrong answer rather than a slow query. Claim C1 measured over a native TPC-H SF10 file is 6.22 percent of column bytes, and the finding is that it is not satisfied by the design being small but enforced by the budget turning links away, since the same nine relationships would be 13.66 percent unbudgeted.

Every plan node says whether its output still carries a row id, in #1198. That is the other half of G2 and it is the correctness foundation for everything in G3, because a row id is a position in a table and every wrong answer this layer can produce is a row id used after the operator that invalidated it. There is a test per plan node asserting which of its outputs carry one.

A packed group key behind a filter is read as places rather than as a dictionary of rows, in #1316. A group by on a narrow key behind a filter was 3.4 times behind duckdb where the same group by with no filter was 0.84 times and ahead of it, on the same column and the same eleven discounts. A filter over a native packed column produces a dictionary whose codes are the rows that got through and whose payload is the whole chunk column, so the direct map took the payload's length as the span and keyed on the row number, which built a map of one entry per row of the page and then rebuilt it for the next chunk. It now reads through the code to the packed run underneath, so the place is the packed code, the span is one past what the width can hold, and one map serves the whole page however the filter cuts it. A decimal key goes from 3.66 times behind duckdb to 1.23 and an integer key from 3.40 to 1.14. On the TPC-H suite it is worth nothing and that is measured rather than assumed, because no TPC-H query groups on a packed narrow key behind a filter.

The settings that are not DuckDB's can be read back from SQL, in #1312. `SET` wrote `cluster_by`, `graph_links` and the rule switches and they all took effect, and `current_setting` resolved against the DuckDB settings catalog, so a name that catalog does not hold was a name nobody had. The fix is not to put them in that catalog, since that list is DuckDB's and should not claim these are, it is to fall through to the rudb side when the catalog does not know the name, which is the reading half of the decision `SET` already makes. This matters most for `cluster_by`, because the declaration is not session state: it lives on the tables, so a database opened on a file carries declarations nobody in this session wrote and reading it back is the only way to find out what they are.

The dictionary is sorted eight bytes of depth at a time, in #1303. The comment on that sort said leading with the first eight bytes of a value settles almost every pair without touching the payload, and on the columns that carry almost all of the dictionary bytes that is false: two and a quarter million URLs have fifty distinct first eight bytes between them, because they all start with a scheme and then a host and there are not many hosts. A most significant byte radix sort was tried first and came out twelve percent worse, because the bytes were never the cost and reaching a value is, since the payload is hundreds of megabytes and every code is a random offset into it. Sorting the codes on eight bytes taken at the current depth and then sorting each tied run on the next eight reaches a value once per eight bytes of depth rather than once per comparison, and it runs over an array of small integers that is in cache. The order is the same order and the file does not change. Loading eight million rows of the five columns that carry the dictionaries goes from about nine seconds to about five.

A date, a time, a timestamp, a decimal and a hugeint group key stay the integer they are, in #1309. A group key column whose type was none of TinyInt, SmallInt, Integer, BigInt or Varchar went to a run of tagged values, and the compare that runs once per input row per probe step built a value on each side of it, which over a packed column allocates. All five of these are one signed integer of one unit, so they get a run of their own held as an `i128`, which is the same grouping `Vector::signed_at` already makes. A date key goes from 9.23 times behind duckdb to 3.75 and a decimal key from 9.09 to 3.67. The test written for it also found a hash bug that was already there: a decimal was hashed as two words everywhere the value was in hand, and a `DECIMAL(9, 2)` column is a run of `i32` which the pass over a run hashed as one word, so the same value hashed one way flat and another packed, and a group by over such a column would have returned the same decimal twice.

The bounded top count is built and measured, in #1314. Document 26 priced its own design by arithmetic over document 25's scan floor, which is what documents 20 and 23 each got wrong, so `cargo xtask topcount` now runs it. Over `Referer` of `hits.parquet` at 81 million rows past the filter, the two pass design with the certification peaks at 89.3 MiB against 4,124 for holding every key, and it reports how it got there. It is a harness and not the engine, so the 3.80 times on user time does not transfer, but the memory does, and carried into the engine that is about 90 MiB against DuckDB's 4,869 on the same query. Ten times less resources is cleared on this query in this quadrant and ten times faster is not, which document 25 already proved it cannot be.

Two whitespace commits, in #1313 and #1320. The format gate on main was red after #1309 and again after #1316, and it fails on every branch cut from main, so each of these is a `cargo fmt --all` run and nothing else.

## 0.4.3

A patch release of four pull requests, three of them about what a sort costs. The native directory format is unchanged at 25 and the storage format version is unchanged at 9. A clustering declaration can now name no width, which is a fifth value of a byte that already had four and is not a format change, and a build without it reading one of these says `clustering width tag differs` and refuses the table rather than reading it as a width it knows.

The sort key is encoded into fixed width bytes, in #1304. A sort held its key as a `Vec<Value>` per row and compared it value by value through the generic path, which for a date, a `BIGINT` and an `INTEGER` is a 24 byte vector header, a heap allocation and three enums a row, and a comparison that dispatches on the type of every column of every pair. When every key column is a fixed width ordered type and the whole list fits in 24 bytes the key now goes into a byte array laid out so that comparing the arrays gives the answer comparing the values gave: a tag byte a column for the null, a big endian payload, signed values biased at the field width so the negatives sort below the positives, a descending column inverting its payload and not its tag. Strings have no fixed width and stay on the old path, and `FLOAT` and `DOUBLE` stay on it because DuckDB's order puts NaN equal to itself and above everything and has one zero, which is not what the bit pattern says. `lineitem` at SF1 sorted on `l_shipdate, l_orderkey, l_linenumber` goes from 44.85 G instructions to 28.96 against DuckDB's 6.56 on the same statement, and from 9.14 seconds real with 1.66 system to 4.64 with 0.55.

A sort gives its input back a column at a time, in #1307. It held the rows that arrived until the last row had left, so near the end it held two copies of the table, and sorting SF10 `lineitem` died with 19.1 GiB of 19.1 GiB used. The assembly that puts the columns into the sorted order lays one column at a time, so the input's copy of a column is finished with the moment that column has been laid, and it is now taken apart into columns up front and dropped exactly then with the charge coming down with it. The rows go before that, because all the assembly wants from them is where each input row lands, which is four bytes a row against forty eight. The smallest limit that sorts two million rows of eight `BIGINT` columns goes from between 340 and 360 MB to between 220 and 240, and SF10 `lineitem` at 59,986,052 rows sorts rather than dying.

The partition width comes from the rows rather than from the calendar, in #1299. A clustering declaration that named no width used to get the quarter, which is a constant fitted to one table at one scale factor. The width is now chosen at every load from the row count and the range of the leading column, and the rule is the month when a month's worth of rows clears two hundred thousand and the quarter otherwise. Two candidates and not four: the first cut had the year in it on the reasoning that a sparse table should take the coarsest bucket there is, and the measurement said no, because how coarse is too coarse is bounded by the width of the predicates and nothing at load time has seen a predicate. It also fixed a bug that had been there since the rule was written, which is that the binder looked the leading column up by the bound scope's index, and that points at the projection over the scan rather than at the scan, so no load ever found the zones it was supposed to read.

A filter crosses a run of limits and projections, in #1300. `SELECT x FROM (SELECT x FROM t LIMIT 4) WHERE x > 1` errored in a debug build with `the passes did not settle`, and in a release build ran the worse of the two plans, because filter pushdown and limit pushdown were making work for each other one level at a time and no fixed number of rounds is enough for a query nested arbitrarily deep.

## 0.4.2

A patch release of seven pull requests. The native directory format goes from 24 to 25, because the catalog grows a view section on the end and a file written by an earlier build does not have one. Formats 22, 23 and 24 still open, and a catalog that ends where the tables end reads as a catalog with no views in it, which is what every file written before this is. The storage format version is unchanged at 9.

A view is written into the file, in #1279. A view created in a database with a file behind it reported success and was gone the next statement, because the checkpoint walked the tables and nothing else, so a view was never refused and never looked at either. The catalog now carries the name, the body text, the whole statement written back out, the alias list and the column list, and two entries under one name are refused when the catalog is read, across tables and views together, since they share a namespace. The column list is a cache and it is written down anyway because that is what the pin answers `column_count` and `is_bound` from after a fresh open, and it is left out of the comparison that decides whether a checkpoint has work to do because the binder writes over it every time somebody selects from a view. Underneath is a new commit shape that changes the views and moves no pages, since everything that could change the catalog before this also wrote a table, and adding a view to a database holding eight tables would otherwise have rewritten all eight.

A group key column can take a null out of a dictionary it borrowed, in #1294. A `VARCHAR` still in memory is a run of bytes with the lengths beside it, and the same column read back out of a native file is a dictionary of the distinct values with a code per row. The key column borrows the second form rather than copying it, which is what makes grouping a file-backed string cheap and is also why it is the one form that can stop fitting, since a code only means something next to the dictionary it was read from. A null was a row that did not belong to it and there was nowhere for such a row to go, so a correlated subquery over a nullable string read back from a file raised an internal error where the same query in the process that created the table answered. A null now stays in the run, and a row that genuinely cannot join one ends it rather than failing, by reading every slot back through its own dictionary into an ordinary string column.

The order the rows are stored in can be declared from SQL, in #1291. The clustered layout had been reachable only from Rust since it landed, so nobody driving the engine through a connection could get a clustered file at all. `SET cluster_by = 'lineitem(quarter(l_shipdate), l_orderkey, l_linenumber)'` does it now, a setting rather than a clause because the parser is DuckDB's vendored grammar and adding a `CLUSTER BY` to it would put rudb syntax in a grammar whose whole job is to be the pin's. The setting keeps nothing in the session, since a database opened on a file whose tables already carry declarations has declarations no `SET` in that session made. It also turned up a bug that was already there: the streaming insert sink wrote the rows in the declared order and wrote nothing saying so, so the declaration was lost on the very statement that honoured it.

A grouped `min` or `max` over a date takes the typed loop, in #1288. It cost 775 instructions a row on top of the same query without it, against 0.531 G for the same `min` over an integer, so a `min` over a string was cheaper than a `min` over a date. The cause was one line: the arm that picks the typed loop took an integer or a decimal and nothing else, and a date, a time and a timestamp are each one signed integer of one unit that orders the way that integer does. The same pull request hoists the null question out of every scatter loop, which was twelve of the forty five instructions a row on the bit packed total, and it found a correctness bug on the way, which is that a `sum` over a date answered on the vector path and refused on the row at a time path. Instructions for the date case go from 5.756 G to 1.675 G against duckdb's 0.849 G, which is 6.77 times down to 1.97.

The statistics scan stops allocating once per row, in #1292. `lineitem` at SF1 cost 28.09s of statistics build against a whole eight table write of 33.78s wall, because the pass took a bound by value and cloned it up to five times per row, and the bytes arm built a fresh allocation per row before any of the cloning started. The ends are now compared against before anything is built, and the bytes arm reuses the held allocation rather than dropping it. That is 28.09s down to 11.38s, and it is not the last word: the remaining cost is architectural, because building summaries after the write means a second full read of a committed file and no amount of inner loop work fits that inside ten percent of the first read.

The two statistics budgets stop eating each other, in #1286. Both held size functions counted every section in the file as already spent, the other layer's included, so the graph layer's ten percent and the statistics layer's two percent were one pot that whichever builder ran first got more of. On an SF10 file that is 7.8 MB of key maps against a statistics budget of about 54 MB, so a build started a seventh of the way through a budget it was given all of. The same pull request writes per stripe sketches only for the columns something has declared a relationship or a key over, which is the file answering the question rather than a proxy for it, and the narrowing that makes promotion free is exact because a bottom-k of a bottom-k is a bottom-k.

A file from before the section table states nothing and answers the same, in #1287. That is exit criterion 3 of the statistics half, and what it says is that a build which knows about summaries says nothing about a file that has none, for every reason there is rather than distinguishing them, because there is nothing a caller could do differently on hearing that the file predates statistics rather than that a section does not checksum. The older file in the test is this build's file with the version stamped back and nothing attached, so there is no fixture to go stale and no second encoder to drift.

## 0.4.1

A patch release of seven pull requests, the first inside G2. The native directory format goes from 23 to 24, because the file grows a section table and everything the graph layer and the statistics layer store is a section in it, so a file written by an earlier build has to be reloaded. Formats 22 and 23 still open, and both read as a table with an empty section table, which is what a table with no sections is. The storage format version is unchanged at 9.

Row ids and key maps, which is G1's storage half, in #1195. A new crate `rudb-graph` at layer 5 holds the `rid` type, the three key map forms and the relationship grammar, and it cannot see a file on purpose: `rudb-native::graph` is the one place allowed to see both a key map and a file. Parquet has no foreign keys to read a relationship out of, so the declaration path is a setting, `SET graph_links = 'orders(o_custkey) -> customer(c_custkey)'`, parsed when it is set and built at the next checkpoint once the tables are written, because a key map is derived from a column and the column has to be readable first. `rudb_links()` reports one row per declared relationship with what is declared on the left and what is stored on the right, and every unverified row says which of three reasons it is, only one of which is a wrong declaration. No join reads any of this yet, which is the point of the milestone.

The statistics half of G2 starts, in #1280 and #1281. `rudb-stats` holds the column summary and the sketches and nothing that can see a file or a plan, so nothing in it can change an answer or a time. Everything stored is either mergeable or re-derivable, because a table gets appended to and a statistic that cannot survive an append is wrong by the second week: counts add, extremes fold, and the two that do not fold are weakened rather than guessed at, so a folded distinct count becomes a certified lower bound and distinctness survives only when both ranges are exact and provably disjoint. #1281 then puts both in the file through the same section table the graph layer uses, which took two constants and one match arm, and that is the interesting part rather than an aside. The two budgets are kept apart, two percent for statistics beside the graph layer's ten, because two budgets sharing a pot are two budgets where whichever runs first wins. The build reads through `Counts`, the pass the encoding chooser already makes, so a constant chunk is one hash and a run length column is one hash per run, rather than a `Value` per row, which is what the ten percent of write time criterion costs.

A run length run is written in one fixed width store, in #1282. The decode wrote a value at a time for the length of the run, and on a clustered join key the runs are two and three long, so the loop spent its time mispredicting its own exit and the branch cost more than the store did. A profile of a scan of `l_orderkey` on the monthly clustered SF1 file put 3028 of 6064 samples on four instructions, which are that loop. It now writes a fixed eight per run and moves on by the run's real length, with eight values of slack on the buffer so the last run has somewhere to put its surplus. Forty reps of the scan go from 1.83 seconds to 1.21 on the monthly file, against the exact file's 1.16, so a clustered file no longer pays a scan penalty for the layer the clustering creates. Worth recording: instructions retired moved three percent while the time moved a third, because a mispredicted branch retires the same instruction a predicted one does, so a gate about a decode loop has to be written in time.

A clustered table with no width declared gets a quarter, in #1278. The loader declared a month because the selective TPC-H date predicates are shaped like one, and the width had never been varied. Varied over five SF1 files from one loader in one sitting, the month comes last of four: 0.891 of the unsorted file's instructions sorted exactly, 0.898 at a quarter, 0.899 at a year and 0.917 at a month, because a narrow bucket costs key locality and delta width and stops buying pruning above a quarter. `Clustering::over` is the declaration that leaves the width to the leading column, a quarter on a date or a timestamp and the exact value on anything else, since an integer has no calendar to bucket by.

A table whose schema was already committed loads as a stream, in #1276. The insert path has a sink that writes native pages straight into the file and it was reachable only when the file did not already hold a table of that name, so a schema created by an earlier statement put the name there and the next insert ran the whole source into memory instead. That is the shape every loading script writes, ClickBench's included. Building the hundred million row ClickBench table two statements in two processes was killed by the kernel at 19.8, 22.0 and 21.4 GiB resident under three different memory limits, because that path does not charge what it holds against the limit and does not spill. The same load through the streaming path runs at about five gigabytes for the first half of the scan and finishes.

`CALL f(...)` runs the table function, in #1277. It is the table function in a `FROM` clause with the clause left off, and the grammar says as much, so the change builds the same source and wraps it in the same star the pragma call path uses, which makes it one statement written two ways rather than two things that resemble each other. It matters more than a spelling, because `CALL` is how DuckDB's own extensions are driven and every table function here was already reachable the long way round. `EXPLAIN CALL` prints the plan the query form prints, line for line.

The parquet quadrant is measured, in #1275. One grouping query at 99,997,497 rows across three corners: rudb native at 5.86 seconds wall, DuckDB on `hits.parquet` at 38.64, rudb on the same file at 109.51. Running the filter without the grouping separates the format tax from the grouping cost, and they are different problems: reading and filtering out of parquet costs 7.1x the CPU of reading out of the native file, while grouping costs 33.9x, which is not a format tax but `Vector::dictionary_over` setting `stable: false` and switching off every fast path in the group operator. The flag is correct, since a parquet dictionary belongs to one row group. This makes the parquet quadrant the harder half of the target rather than the easier one, which no document in the series had said.

## 0.4.0

The release where G1 closed. There is no code in it that was not in 0.3.86, because a milestone marker that carried its own changes would be a release nobody could point at and say what it was for. What it marks is that every box in G1, the hash join and the first real baseline, is done, and that its four exit criteria hold. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 23.

G1 said the hash join is what makes TPC-H run at all, and that a graph layer which is fast against a suite that does not run is not measurable, so it comes first with no performance claim attached to it. Thirty five engine changes later the suite runs. The join arrived as inner, left, semi and anti with a probe side Bloom filter, and then spent most of the milestone being taken apart: join ordering that will not build a cross product the conditions can avoid, cardinality estimation that reads a distinct count, a build side that runs on more than one thread and a table that is built before the instances are handed out rather than behind a lock inside whichever one asks first, a runtime filter that actually reaches the scan under the driving side, a residual evaluated a batch of pairs at a time rather than a row at a time, and a decorrelation path that stops building a six million row domain to ask a question about seventy eight thousand rows. q21 went from about eight seconds to about one and a half on that last one alone, which is the largest single move any TPC-H query made in the series.

The statistics half added no persisted bytes, which was the point. The file already knew a lot of exact things and almost none of them reached a planner decision, so it was wiring rather than computing. Memory tables now maintain counts, extremes, null counts, a KMV sketch and an exact frequency tally as they are built, and the tally is exact or absent and never short, because a lower bound is not something an estimator or an execution shortcut can read.

The harness half is in tamnd/rudb-bench. The answer ladder compares row by row, then as a tie, then at the boundary. The specification's own SF1 qualification answers are committed under `answers/tpch-sf1/` and every engine is checked against them on any TPC-H run at that scale, which is the only correctness reference in the suite that is not another engine. And the plan gate has two tiers now: the zero row one that runs on every commit, and `baselines/plans-tpch-sf1.txt`, twenty two plans captured against the real corpus, which is where a join order change is a reviewed diff. The two are never compared against each other.

What is not claimed. No performance claim is attached to this milestone and none was expected. The ten times target is stated against DuckDB on both axes and neither half is met: `spec/perf/21` measures the memory half from outside the process and finds a 712 MiB working set against DuckDB's 0.44 GiB for the whole ClickBench suite, and `spec/perf/18` puts the execution ceiling at about five times rather than ten if every operator above the scan were free. G2 through G10 are where that is argued with, and the TPC-H ladder from SF0.01 up through SF10 is how it gets watched on the way.

## 0.3.86

A patch release of six pull requests, one of which landed before 0.3.85 and was left out of its entry. One is a kernel change worth a third of a gigainstruction per aggregate call on a large group by, one prints a clock that was always charged and never shown, one makes the memory half of the target measurable from outside the process, and three are measurements and corrections to measurements. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 23.

A grouped aggregate finishes its states a run at a time, in #1263. `Aggregate::finishing` asked each accumulator for a `Value`, pushed it into a `Vec<Value>`, handed the run to `Vector::from_values` which pushed every value again into the vector's flat data, read them all back once more to build the validity mask, and then dropped them. Four passes and an owning tagged value per group per call, to move eight bytes, and the comment above that loop has called it the per group cost since #61. `spec/perf/14-what-a-group-costs.md` put a number on it: 0.479 G of the 3.248 G that two added aggregate calls cost on a 1.5 million group aggregate over SF1, which made it the single largest thing that grows when an aggregate gets another call. The new `finish_run` decides the route once per call from the state's shape and the output column's type, writes the answers into a run of numbers and narrows that into the column's own layout, so nothing is allocated per group and nothing is dropped per group. The arithmetic moves into `Accumulator::answer`, which both finishes share, so the two cannot reach different numbers. `min` and `max`, an affine call, the two compact grouped shapes and any call whose declared return type is not the one its state was built for return `None` and take the loop that was there before. On server3, TPC-H SF1 native, instructions retired, best of three from one tree built twice: 1.5 million groups with three sums goes 7.277 G to 6.284 G, one sum 4.073 to 3.740, and six groups is unmoved at 1.500 and 1.498, which is what per group per call means. On the twenty two TPC-H queries it is 0.4 per cent, because TPC-H groups on low cardinality keys nearly everywhere.

`EXPLAIN ANALYZE` prints both clocks on the operator line, in #1232, which landed before 0.3.85 and was missed out of that entry. The operator line printed `operator.wall_ns`, which is elapsed time summed over the operator's instances, so on a plan that runs eight ways it can exceed the whole statement's CPU, while the total at the foot of the plan is the thread clock. Dividing one by the other and calling the result a scan share gives 153.9, 101.6 and 119.8 per cent on three passes of one database, which is what `spec/perf/16` did. Per operator CPU was already charged and simply was not the column being printed. Both clocks are now named on the line, as the pipeline rows have always done, with a fallback to wall alone where CPU was not charged. Re-measured with the CPU clock on both sides over all 43 ClickBench queries, three passes per database, the scan is 17.3 per cent of suite CPU at ten million rows and 20.5 at thirty, and the operator CPU sums account for 98 per cent of statement CPU. The same pull request adds `spec/perf/17`, which reports only quantities a shared host cannot move, and corrects three documents that had reasoned from the wrong column.

The memory half of the ten times target is measured from outside the process, in #1266. `resource.peak_bytes` counts what operators register and nothing else, so on ClickBench at ten million rows it reports 0.4 MiB for q23, which actually holds 712, and the suite's peak exceeds the maximum it ever reports by 774 MiB. `spec/perf/21` measures the resource axis from the outside instead. Three results come out of it. The batch against per query inversion is retired: DuckDB starts in 0.49 to 0.88 seconds and rudb in 0.09 to 0.12, so DuckDB's 32.76 second per process penalty over 43 processes is its startup and nothing else, and a per process suite total is a startup comparison weighted by 43. rudb does not degrade in a long process, at 73.88 seconds wall in one process against 106.43 across 43. And the memory gap is two problems rather than one: 774 MiB of bounded allocator slack, about 205 MiB of it recoverable at twice the system time, sitting over a 712 MiB working set that is larger than DuckDB's 0.44 GiB for the whole suite. Neither half of the target is met and nothing here claims otherwise. The caveat is recorded on the field itself.

Whether the scan is a floor, tested rather than assumed, in #1262. `spec/perf/18` derives the project's ceiling from the scan being the floor under the suite, which is a strong claim resting on an untested assumption. Scanning one, two and three columns costs about nine nanoseconds per row per column and the same for a two byte column as an eight byte one, which reads as twenty seven cycles a value against a vector unpack that should retire one per cycle, and the note records that it was read that way before it was profiled. It is not slack. The note keeps the misreading next to the correction rather than quietly replacing it, which is the point of writing it down.

Two corrections to `spec/perf/14`, in #1253 and #1267.
