The first is that the note's profile was measured with the wrong event: `perf record` with no `-e` samples cycles, and the note read the percentages as shares of instructions, which is the unit every target in this project is stated in. Re-profiled with `perf record -e instructions`, the addition the query asks for is twelve per cent of an added call rather than two, the state vector's memory traffic is about five rather than fifteen to twenty, and the largest single item is finishing a state into an output value at fifteen, which the first version did not mention at all. The second correction is that the note's chunk walk and merge rows were the wrong way round, because `fold_slots` is named like the per chunk fold and is in fact the merge of one partial table into another. Corrected, the merge is 0.277 G and nine per cent of an added call and the chunk walk is 0.136 and four, which matters because #1173's fourth bullet is about the merge and had the smallest number attached to it.

## 0.3.85

A patch release of four pull requests. Half the types the engine can hold could not be written to a file at all and now can, which takes the native directory format from 22 to 23, so a file written by an earlier build has to be reloaded. The other three are a kernel that was reading a packed column a value at a time, a new way to ask a file what form its columns are stored in, and two counting bugs in the metrics document. The storage format version is unchanged at 9.

Native storage gains a tag for the other fourteen types, in #1255. A table with a float, a double, a hugeint, a time, an interval, a uuid, a blob or a bit string in it used to fail at the `CREATE TABLE` with a storage word in the message, and the same statement worked in a session with no file. Most of the new tags ride a lane something already used: the three timestamp precisions, a time, a zoned time and a zoned timestamp are all the 64 bit lane, and a hugeint and a uuid are the 128 bit lane a wide decimal used. Floats and doubles go down plainly in their IEEE bytes, because no float codec is worth having before somebody has measured a corpus of floats, and an interval keeps its three counts apart on disk because a month is not a fixed number of days. A blob turned up a real bug on the way past: the string column validates UTF-8 when a page is read in place, which is right for a varchar and wrong for a blob, so a blob holding a byte that is not valid UTF-8 was refused by the reader for holding exactly what it was told to hold.

A packed column's bits are read for an `IN` list instead of a value a row, in #1250. The membership kernel dispatches on the column's physical form and had no arm for a bit packed one, which is the form every narrow integer column of ClickBench is stored in, so `TraficSourceID IN (-1, 6)` fell through to the walk that allocates twice per row. ClickBench query 40 goes from 1.152 ms to 0.832 ms and it is the only one of the 43 with an `IN` list in it. The reason nobody noticed is the second half of the change: every other kernel reports when it takes the row at a time path, and this one was not in that table, so it paid for this for as long as the file has existed.

A native file can be asked what form a column is stored in, as `pragma_storage_info`, in #1254. The directory says a column took 41 megabytes and says nothing about what shape they are in, and the shape is what a scan pays for. The reader now walks the codec byte at the front of every part and names what it finds, both the format's own choice and the choice the encoder made per chunk under a cascade, so a part reads `RLE(DELTA(FOR+BITPACK[6]), FOR+BITPACK[3])`. One read per column per stripe rather than one per part, and nothing goes in the page cache. The columns are the pin's sixteen, in the pin's order and types. It immediately answered what it was built for: `l_orderkey` on the clustered SF1 file is the same cascade as on the unsorted one with the delta width gone from 6 bits to 11 or 12, for 4.1 times the bytes.

The rows an operator was handed are counted once, on the side it was handed them, in #1248. The `parent` edge from #1240 made it possible to check an operator's input against what the operators under it produced, and over twenty query shapes three did not add up. `Shape` was walking the right input into the gather every time rather than reading the build side flag the builder reads, so on a join the optimizer had turned around it put both pipelines and both edges on the wrong sides. And a cross product answers `Progress::Again` and gets called back with the chunk it just produced, so the shim was counting a query's own answer as its input.

## 0.3.84

A patch release of two pull requests. The one worth reading is a comparison kernel that was turning itself off on exactly the data the clustered layout produces, which is the last thing standing between stage 1 of the ten times plan and its gate. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

A bit packed column against a flat one is compared where the two sit, in #1241. Sorting lineitem by month of `l_shipdate` leaves `l_commitdate` with about a hundred distinct values inside a partition, so it bit packs, while `l_receiptdate` arrives flat. `compare` had a loop for a packed column against a literal, for two packed columns and for two flat ones, and none for a packed column against a flat one, so `l_commitdate < l_receiptdate` over six million rows built a `Value` for both sides of every row. The better encoding the clustering buys was what made the query slower, which is not a trade anybody chose. The new loop reads the packed side as its base plus its code and the flat side as the number it already holds, and it takes all four ways the pair can be indexed, because a stored date column comes back as a dictionary over a packed run about as often as it comes back as a packed run on its own.

On TPC-H SF1, clustered against unsorted, instructions retired at one thread: q4 goes from 1.44 times the unsorted file to 0.96, q21 from 1.33 to 1.06 and q12 from 0.42 to 0.33. That is the whole of #1209, which had four measurements on its checklist and was answered by none of them. It was answered by the `--fallbacks` flag, which prints the kernel calls that took the row at a time path and named the pair in one run.

A metrics document says which operator each one's rows go into, in #1240. Every operator row already carried a pipeline id and a pipeline holds several operators in a line, so a reader could add up the rows a query moved and could not check that any of them added up. There is now a `parent` on each one, absent on the operator the answer is read from, and it is the operator the rows actually went to rather than the parent in the plan, which differs for a join's build side and for a materialised `WITH`.

## 0.3.83

A patch release of one pull request, and it is the third of the three that make a database on a file behave like one. A table that is already in the file can be added to, so the second run of a session that loads a table and inserts into it is no longer an error. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

A committed table takes an append, in #1235. An `INSERT` into a table that had been written down used to refuse with "appending to a committed native table", and every table is a committed table the next time its file is opened, so a database on a file was writable exactly once in its life. A table is now allowed to be a committed file and a buffer of rows at once, with the file's parts numbered first, so everything that reads a part by number subtracts the file's part count and reads whichever half the number lands in. Nothing above the catalog had to learn about it, because a scan asks for parts and gets all of them.

The statistics are where it gives something up, on purpose. A file answers most of them exactly out of what its writer stored and the rows that arrived since are not in there, so the ones that cannot be combined without reading the column now say nothing at all rather than reporting the file's answer as though it were the table's. The null count and the integer sum do add up and are added up. The planner sees a table in this state as a sketch and leaves its columns out of the summary. A checkpoint rewrites the file rather than carrying the table forward, because the file and the catalog disagree about a table that grew, so the cost of committing one is the size of the table rather than the size of what changed. Appending a stripe to an existing table's directory is the follow up.

## 0.3.82

A patch release of two pull requests, both about a database being a file rather than a session. A table created in one run is now there in the next one without anybody saying `CHECKPOINT`, which is what DuckDB does. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

A native file can hold no table at all, in #1224. The writer took its first table when the file was created and committed the one it was on at the end, so a catalog with nothing in it could be read and never written, and a checkpoint over a database somebody had dropped the last table out of raised and left the dropped table in the file. That is a statement that succeeded being undone by the write that was meant to publish it. The format already allowed it, because a catalog is a count and then that many entries and a count of nought encodes the same way every other count does, which is why nothing about this is a version change.

A database opened on a file is written when the last handle on it goes away, in #1226. The write is a `Drop` on the shared state, so it happens once however many handles and connections there were and it is the last one going that does it, and `Database::close` is the same write with the error handed back, for a caller that wants to know whether its last session reached the disk. A `Drop` has nowhere to put an error, which is the whole reason `close` exists beside it. The shell calls it at the end of a session and turns a failed write into a message and a failing exit code, and `.open` goes through the same door.

`Config::read_only` is new in the same pull request, and it means the file is never written. The `-readonly` flag was parsed and used nowhere before this, so a write on close would have started writing files a user had asked not to be written. It is enforced in the three places that write the file, which are `CHECKPOINT`, the create and insert paths that write rows into the file as they run, and the write on the way out. A `CHECKPOINT` under it succeeds and writes nothing, which is what the pinned binary does. What it does not do is refuse the statements by name or refuse to open a database that is not there, both of which DuckDB does and both of which are #1225, so a write under a read only database still changes the tables that process can see and is still gone when the process ends.

## 0.3.81

A patch release of one pull request. It is the last construct the binder refused by name, which was a limit written as a percentage whose share or offset is a subquery. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

`LIMIT (SELECT 30)% OFFSET (SELECT 2)` works, in #1220. Neither end of a percentage limit has to be a number the query wrote out. A subquery has to run before there is a value, so the value is joined in under the limit as a column of every row the limit sees, read off the first chunk that reaches it and kept for the rest of the query, which is exactly how a plain limit already read a row count it could not work out in advance. The column is one the query never asked for, so the projection over the limit drops it again. Only the `%` sign can hold a subquery, because the grammar will not put the word `PERCENT` after a closing bracket, and that is true of the pinned binary too.

The share goes through the cast to DOUBLE rather than the cast to BIGINT a row count goes through, so `(SELECT true)%` is one percent and not one row, and the cast now lives in `rudb-kernels` next to the row count one so both paths that can settle a share answer the same way. A null share or a subquery that answers no row is no limit at all. The range check moves to where the value turns up, and the two ways of being outside the range have two different sentences on the pin, so they have two here: above a hundred is the out of range sentence a written share gets, and below nought names the value and says a percentage cannot be negative. An input with no rows in it reads neither end, because there is nothing to read the value off and no rows to take a share of, so a share of minus one over an empty input answers nothing rather than raising.

Thirty records covering all of that are pinned against DuckDB in tamnd/rudb-compat#202, which takes that corpus from 52 files to 53.

## 0.3.80

A patch release of one pull request and one spec. The one pull request is `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE` and `CREATE TEMPORARY VIEW`, which were two of the three statements the binder refused outright. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

`CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE` and `CREATE TEMPORARY VIEW` work, in #1216. A temporary entry goes into the `temp` database and its `main` schema, and every accepted way of writing the name lands in the same place, whether that is a bare name, `temp.t`, `main.t` or `temp.main.t`. Naming a database that is actually attached is refused rather than redirected. Reads put `temp.main` at the front of the search path so a temporary table hides a stored one of the same name, and creates leave it out, so `CREATE TABLE t` after `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE t` makes a second table rather than an error and both exist at once under the one name. A drop takes the one a bare name finds. The `temp` database is internal and the entries somebody put in it are not, which is the only place in the catalog those two answers come apart, so `duckdb_databases()` and `duckdb_schemas()` say true for the same object that `duckdb_tables()`, `duckdb_views()` and `duckdb_columns()` say false for. `information_schema.tables` calls a temporary table a `LOCAL TEMPORARY` and a temporary view a plain `VIEW`. A checkpoint writes the stored tables only, because a temporary table in the file would come back as an ordinary table nobody created the next time the file was opened.

A temporary entry here lives for the life of the database rather than the life of the connection, which is a divergence from DuckDB and is deliberate. `Connection` carries no state of its own yet, so making `temp` per connection means a catalog view threaded through everything that takes a catalog today. That is #1215.

The spec is the native quadrant replication, three passes per engine, in #1214.

## 0.3.79

A patch release of six pull requests and one spec. Most of it is the physical order a table is stored in, which now gets sorted into on load rather than only recorded, plus what a table in memory knows about its own columns. One is a window function gap against DuckDB. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

A bulk load is sorted into the order the table declared (#1211). The declaration landed in 0.3.78 and did nothing, and this makes it do something. The sort goes in the plan under the projection, one key per declared column with the leading one wrapped in `date_trunc` when the declared width is a calendar bucket rather than the value itself, which reuses the parallel spilling sort the engine already has and leaves the sink streaming. The width being honoured is the point rather than a detail: bucketed by month, `l_orderkey` runs in order across the whole of a month and the dates inside it are in no order at all, which is the key locality the joins want, and sorting on the date exactly gives the other one. On TPC-H SF1 loaded through it, q6 costs 0.17 of what it did, q14 and q15 cost 0.25, q12 costs 0.42 and the suite total is 0.949. Three queries come out worse, q4 at 1.46, q21 at 1.34 and q18 at 1.16, which the gate on #1187 asked not to happen and which is not hidden.

Every filter over a stored table moves into the scan (#1199). Whether a filter can move and whether its predicate reads as tests a zone map can answer were being decided as one question, and only the second is dangerous. A filter over a stored table does the same work in either place, so moving it costs nothing and saves an operator boundary. Nineteen ClickBench queries push their filter into the scan where about six did before. Two things had to come with it: the pipeline was losing the filter's weight when it moved, which cut the instance count on exactly the queries that had just got cheaper, and the scan kept its filter's working space behind one mutex taken twice per chunk, which is nothing uncontended and is the whole query once nineteen more paths run through it. The free list is now sixty four slots with a thread going to its own. Mean of the ten queries that moved is 0.954 of what it was.

A group by over a stored integer key reads the key as places rather than hashing it (#1194). The direct map path only fired when the key arrived as a dictionary, which is what a Parquet scan hands over for a low cardinality string. The native file bit packs a small integer column, which already holds each value as the value minus a base in as many bits as the widest value needs, so `l_linenumber` takes seven values, is stored in three bits, and a group by on it was hashing six million rows to find one of seven answers. The places are worked out one key column at a time into a buffer the chunk reuses, because matching on the form once per row cost more than the probe it saved, and that column at a time pass turned out to beat the old loop on the dictionary case too. On TPC-H SF1 in the native format the two packed key shapes go from 3.35x and 3.46x DuckDB's instructions to 0.98x and 0.92x, the dictionary case is a quarter cheaper, and q01 is eleven percent better.

A table in memory counts how many rows hold each value of a column (#1202). It kept a distinct count and nothing about how the rows sat across the values, so every estimate over one was rows divided by distinct values, which on a skewed column is wrong by the skew and wrong in the direction that picks the wrong join side. `rudb_storage::tally` is a bounded exact per value row count, complete up to 512 values and absent past that, never short, because a list of the first 512 values of a wider column would say the rest hold no rows. On a 100,000 row table where `flag = 1` holds 90,000, the estimate goes from 10,000 to 90,000 and `flag = 5` from 10,000 to 1,111. Two shortcuts that only fired on a native file fire on a memory table now: a grouped `count_star()` reads the list and the scan under it reads nothing, and a `count(*)` behind an equality reads the one count. It costs nothing measurable, which took three designs to reach, because the tally and the sketch hand over rather than both running.

The ends of a string column in memory are answered from that tally (#1207). `MIN(s)` and `MAX(s)` over a string column in memory read every row. The zone maps were not the problem, since a memory zone keeps whole bytes; the gap is that a string column arriving as a dictionary narrower than its chunk has zone ends that are a superset of what the rows point at, and refusing them correctly left a full scan. A column still counting holds every value its rows hold and nothing they do not, so the two ends of that list are the two ends of the column. On a 4,096 row fixture the aggregate goes from 309.709us to 583ns.

An `ORDER BY` written inside a window function's brackets is taken (#1208). The clause looks like the one in the `OVER` and asks a different question: the one in the `OVER` lays the partition out and decides which rows are in the frame at all, and this one decides what order the call reads those rows in once they are, which is why `first_value(v ORDER BY v DESC)` is the largest `v` in the frame rather than the first row of it. `first_value`, `last_value`, `nth_value` and the aggregates used as windows take it. The sort happens within the frame and not within the partition, and `EXCLUDE` drops its row before the sort runs rather than after it, both settled by probing the pinned binary. Eight traversals matched aggregates and windows in one arm and four of those feed column pruning, so each was split or a column the call still reads would be pruned away. It takes out the whole of `test/sql/window/test_value_exclude.test` upstream, all six of its records.

The native quadrant measured, and three refutations retracted (#1200). Spec only, no code. Document 13 measured rudb against DuckDB with both engines reading Parquet and then used that to declare three native format mechanisms refuted, which it could not have tested, because rudb reading Parquet has no dictionary of its own and no stripe of its own. Document 15 is the same 43 queries at 100,000,000 rows with each engine over its own format: Q35 goes from 45.84s to under 0.5s, Q34 from 37.71s to under 0.6s, Q6 from 16.65s to under 0.2s. rudb's native file is 1.82x smaller than DuckDB's and the suite peaks at 4.2 GiB against 8.62. One obligation is still not met and it is the only one: every query rudb loses in the native quadrant is a `COUNT(DISTINCT UserID)` or a group by on a key close to unique per row. Two problems the run found are written down rather than smoothed over, that the load peaks at 17.58 GiB at 100m rows against 1.5 GiB at 1m, and that the host cannot resolve a per query timing to better than about 3x.

## 0.3.78

A patch release of four pull requests and one spec. Two of them are the first work on the physical order a table is stored in, one is a `COUNT(DISTINCT)` that was allocating for the values that arrived rather than the ones it kept, and one is a row loop deciding the null case once per vector. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

A table can declare the order its rows are stored in (#1196). The per fragment ranges are built from whatever order the rows arrive in and prune on it without being told anything, which is why sorting TPC-H before loading it makes q6, q14, q15 and q12 cost 6.13, 3.85, 3.50 and 2.38 times less with no engine change at all. What was missing was any record of the order: a checkpoint rewrites the file in catalog order, an insert appends to the end, and the order goes with nothing having said so. `Clustering` is a column list, outermost first, plus how coarsely the leading column is bucketed before the rest break the tie, which is the trade the layout is making, since bucketing `l_shipdate` by month prunes the same as sorting on the date for any predicate a month wide and leaves `l_orderkey` in order inside each bucket for the joins. It goes into the table directory as a new trailing section with its own magic, so every file already on disk still opens and every table that declares nothing writes the same bytes it did. The checkpoint's test for having nothing to do now asks whether the file agrees about the order as well as about which tables exist, because a declaration made after a table was written was being decided as nothing to do and lost. Nothing sorts anything yet and nothing claims the rows arrived in the declared order.

A scan says how many parts its statistics ruled out (#1193). The scan has counted the parts its ranges skipped since the walk was written and the number was private to it, so a predicate that prunes and a predicate that matches nothing produced the same scan line and nothing in any output said which had happened. `parts_read` and `parts_pruned` are on the operator row, in the metrics JSON and on the `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` line, and a scan that pruned nothing leaves the clause off rather than printing a zero. The same query over the same TPC-H rows in two orders reads `[75983 rows after the filter above, 52.986ms]` unsorted against `[75983 rows after the filter above, 1.184ms, 1392 of 1402 parts skipped]` sorted. Pruning happens in two places and both are counted: the walk steps over ruled out parts inside a morsel, and the runs handed to workers leave whole stripes uncovered, which on a sorted file is almost all of it.

The distinct table is sized by what it keeps rather than by what arrives (#1186). `bigint_distinct_partition` sized its open addressed table at twice the number of values scattered into the partition, which on ClickBench Q4 is 1.56 million values arriving at a partition that keeps about 270 thousand, so it allocated 32 MB for 2.2 MB of contents across 64 partitions at once. It now starts at 1024 slots and doubles at a load factor of one half with the old size as the ceiling, which is cheap to rebuild here because only the value is stored and the new slot derives from the value the same way the first one did. On the hundred million row table at 32 threads the median went from 294 ms to 254 ms and peak resident set from about 2.3 GiB to about 1.6 GiB.

The grouped aggregate decides the null case once per vector (#1192). `Validity` is three cases and the module promises the cost of knowing which one you are in is a branch per vector, and in the grouped fold loops it was a branch per row, because the case sits behind a shared reference and the loop calls into code the compiler cannot prove is read only, so it reloads the discriminant every row. `Live` is the same three cases as a `Copy` value, which leaves nothing for an opaque call to have written through. Worth saying plainly: the estimate before it was measured was 18 percent of q1 and the answer is 0.54 percent, 4,224,834,623 instructions against 4,201,841,269 at nine reps with a 0.02 percent control, and q10 and q18 come out inside their own control spread. Only q1 moves because q1 reads its decimals as a dictionary over a packed run, and everywhere else the compiler had already worked it out.

Order and density as committed facts, and the operators that disappear when the reader knows them (#1178). Spec only, no code. Four bits and two integers per column per snapshot saying whether a column is non-decreasing, whether its page ranges are disjoint, whether its values are distinct, and what dense domain it covers. Every TPC-H primary key is already stored with zero inversions and customer, part and supplier are dense sorted unique over 1 to N, so the facts are there to be recorded rather than produced. It is explicit that this observes the order a table already has and does not ask the loader to reorder anything.

## 0.3.77

A patch release of twelve pull requests. Four of them are the optimizer reading statistics the files already held, two are loads that held a table in memory that did not need to be there, and the rest are speed. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

A load streams into a database that already has a table (#1161). `NativeSink` writes rows from the source straight into the file so the table is never held in memory, and it was reachable only for the first table into a file that did not exist yet. Every load after that built the whole table in RAM and reached the file at the next checkpoint, so the cost of loading a table was the size of the table. Loading TPC-H at scale factor 100 one table at a time on a 31 GiB machine peaked at 23.6 GiB on orders and took the machine down on lineitem, which is four times bigger. The append path the previous release added can be streamed the same way: the sink opens the database itself rather than a temporary beside it, the pages go past the catalog the committed generation points at, and the header slot is the last write, so until it lands the file still reads as the generation before. The condition is the one the checkpoint's append already uses, asked of the table about to be written.

`CREATE TABLE AS SELECT` streams into the file (#1176). It ran the whole query into a result, moved the result into an in-memory table and waited for a checkpoint, where an `INSERT` into a table just created reaches the sink and goes from the source to the file as the rows are produced. The two statements write the same thing and only one of them streamed. Ten million rows and fourteen columns of ClickBench hits into a fresh database went from a peak of 6.20 GiB to 3.65 GiB with no memory limit set, and the rows under a limit are the ones to sit with: at 4 GiB the process held 6.17 GiB and the limit never fired, because the result handed its reservation back at the moment the table took the rows, and at 2 GiB and below the statement failed outright where it now finishes in about ten seconds.

The optimizer reads a frequency count for `<>` and for an `IN` list (#1170). Both were getting the flat fifth every condition nobody can read gets, `<>` because only `=` was wired up and an `IN` because the estimator splits an `AND` into conjuncts and an `IN` binds to a single `OR`. Neither is an assumption anybody has to make on a column whose synopsis lists its values. `c <> k` is every row except the ones holding `k` and except the nulls, since `<>` against a null is null and a null row does not pass, so it needs a count out of the synopsis and a count out of the bounds and gives up without both. `c IN (j, k)` is the counts summed, and only where every branch is an equality on one column against a constant, because a disjunction across two columns is somewhere between the larger count and the sum and no per column synopsis says where. On a checkpointed table of 100,000 rows with a tenth null and a tenth holding one value, `a <> 8` went from 20,000 to 80,000 against a truth of 80,000 and `a IN (1, 8)` from 20,000 to 90,000 against 90,000.

The frequency synopsis is read where it lists only the leading values (#1175). It was only being read where the list covers every value of the column, which is the easy half. The writer keeps a bounded candidate set and then recounts the candidates that survived the pass, so what an incomplete synopsis lost is values rather than counts: a listed value carries the number of rows that really hold it either way, and the values that survive a heavy hitter pass are the leading values of the column, which are exactly the ones an equality guesses worst about. A column with one value in half its rows and a long tail behind it has no complete synopsis and never will. `Reader::frequency_prefix` hands over the list and the bound on what was dropped, a value in the list is an exact count, and a value outside it is a proven zero only where nothing was left out. On a 100,000 row table over 601 values with half the rows on one of them, `a = 1` went from 45 to 50,000 against a truth of 50,000.

A value the synopsis left out is estimated from what it left out (#1183). The prefix above left a value outside the list answering nothing, which fell back to dividing the rows by the distinct count, the guess the synopsis exists to replace, and at its worst on exactly the column that gets an incomplete synopsis. The listed values take the most rows and their counts are exact, so subtracting them leaves the rows of the tail, and subtracting the number of values the list holds from the column's distinct count leaves how many values those rows are shared between. `Frequencies::remainder` is those two numbers and the bound the writer recorded, and the estimate is the division capped by the bound and floored at one row. Only an exact distinct count divides: a native integer column gets a ceiling out of the span between its two ends, and dividing by a ceiling divides by more values than the column has and lands under the truth by that factor, so without a count the bound answers on its own. On a 100,000 row table with one value in ninety thousand rows and a thousand tail values holding ten rows each, `a = 1600` went from 50 to 10 against a truth of 10, `a <> 1600` from 20,000 to 99,990, and the same equality on the string column from 99 to 10.

A group by is estimated off the distinct counts of its keys (#1177). It kept a tenth of its input whatever it grouped by, so `GROUP BY flag` over three values and `GROUP BY key` over twenty five thousand got the same number, while the catalog and the native store have carried a distinct count per column all along. Several keys multiply, the rows going in cap the product, and a filter underneath lands between those two ends rather than at either: a hundred values over a thousand rows is ten rows each, and a filter down to two hundred rows drops a value only when all ten of its rows went, which is about a ninth of them. `DISTINCT` is a group by on the columns it names and goes through the same arithmetic, including the plain form, where the key is the whole row and the columns are read off the projection underneath. On a 100,000 row table, `GROUP BY key` over twenty five thousand values went from 10,000 to 25,000 and `GROUP BY flag, status` over six pairs from 10,000 to 6.

A top N trims by partitioning rather than sorting (#1165). A top N above a bound of 64 collects rows and trims, and the trim ran a full sort over the candidates to keep the first `bound` of them. ClickBench 39 is `LIMIT 10 OFFSET 1000`, so the bound is 1010 and the aggregate under it has already cut itself to exactly 1010 groups: nothing is ever thrown away and the operator still sorted a thousand rows through a comparator walking five `Value` key columns, which was 0.302 ms of a 1.486 ms query. Ties are settled by where the row arrived rather than by the order the rows were handed over, so no two candidates compare equal and the ordering is total, and a total order does not need a sort to be trimmed by. A partition around the `bound`th best row answers which rows to keep and nothing else.

The scan starts instances from the work behind a row rather than the row count (#1167). Three of the five worst ClickBench queries ran the whole query on one thread of thirty two, because each prunes to about twenty four thousand live rows and the first divisor in `instances_for` is twenty five thousand. Twenty four thousand rows is small, and twenty four thousand rows of 39's aggregate, which groups five columns with two wide strings among them at a hundred and eleven nanoseconds a row, is a millisecond of work sitting on one core. The rows were standing in for the work behind them, which holds only while every query spends the same on a row. An operator now says how much more than an ordinary one it spends, zero being ordinary and the scale being what the source spends reading a row, the pipeline sums them and adds one for the source, and only the first divisor is multiplied by the result.

A grouped aggregate reads its argument slice once per vector rather than once per row (#1168). The grouped fold loops indexed the argument column as a `Buffer`, which holds its values either in a `Vec` it owns or in a window into a shared page, so every row went through a match on that enum, a load of the page pointer, a load of the offset and the length and the bounds work behind it. None of that changes inside a vector and the compiler was not hoisting it, about fifteen instructions a row re-deriving the same pointer. The ungrouped path has bound `as_slice()` before its loop all along, which is why an ungrouped sum of six million decimals costs 34 instructions a row where the same sum grouped into six groups cost 88.

A grouped mean is read without asking what it is folding into (#1166). `feed_of` picks the read shape off the state of the first accumulator the call owns, and every accumulator a call owns was made by that call, so `Feed::Whole` is only ever answered for a mean. The loop serving it still asked per row through a function that matched three states and did the one the caller already knew, and it did not inline because two loops shared it. The loop destructures the mean itself and does the add in place, the overflow path where the exact total has left an `i128` moves to a cold function off the side, and the packed arm gets the same treatment, which is the arm q01 takes because it reads its decimal columns as a dictionary over a packed run.

A split's group record is read once a probe rather than three times (#1169). The comparison compared an `i32` group, a `u32` hash and a `bool` one field at a time out of a `Vec`, and the compiler cannot fold those indexes into one because between any two of them the vector is a pointer and a length it has to reload. The record derives `PartialEq` and the probe is one index, one load of twelve bytes and the same three comparisons in the same short circuiting order, the hash first because that is what rules a bucket out cheaply. The count beside it binds `&mut counts[slot]` instead of indexing once to read and once to write.

The native checksum walks its input as chunks rather than as offsets (#1174). A slice taken at an offset is a bounds check and the `try_into` behind it is a length check, and the main loop did four of each per thirty two bytes because the hash has four lanes, none of which can be folded away while nothing tells the compiler where the offset stops. A chunk carries its own length, so both checks go. This is the same function it was, which matters more than usual: nothing requires the checksum to be any particular hash, but a file already written carries the answers the version that wrote it gave, so a change here would not break the format loudly, it would make every stored file quietly fail to verify. There is now a test pinning what it answers at every length its branches split on, one of which is the published xxHash64 vector for an empty input.

Two notes went into the specification rather than the engine. `spec/storage-v3` was measured at 100,000 and 1,000,000 rows where ClickBench has 100,000,000, and #1171 reruns the suite at full size: rudb is 1.11x ahead at a million rows and 2.30x behind at a hundred million, monotone across two decades rather than a step, and 34 of the 43 queries moved against rudb. It reverses three of the document's conclusions and leaves one standing, the zone maps, which is the only mechanism in the series whose benefit is work not done rather than a smaller constant on work still done. #1172 is the first TPC-H note and the first measured in instructions retired rather than wall clock, on the grounds that the shared boxes have a wall clock noise floor larger than most of the effects being chased and that wall clock can be bought with threads where the goal asks for less resource as well as less time. It puts rudb at 53.18 G instructions on the 22 queries against DuckDB's 27.63 G, and the two rankings of the worst queries differ enough that a work list taken from the wall clock table alone sends effort to the wrong ones.

## 0.3.76

A patch release of seven pull requests. One of them is a load that did not finish, four are speed, one is a page layout and one is an allocator. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

A checkpoint appends a generation instead of rewriting the file (#1159). `persist` wrote every table in the catalog into a fresh file and renamed it over the database, so a table already backed by the file was read out of it, decoded and encoded again, and the global `is_native` check above the loop meant one dirty table sent every clean one through that round trip. The cost was the size of the database rather than the size of what changed: adding one row to a file already holding forty million rows took 5.73 seconds, and loading the eight TPC-H tables one statement at a time was quadratic and did not finish at scale factor 100. `Writer::open` now opens a committed file and starts a table in the generation after the one it holds, carrying the tables already there forward by name and by directory pointer without reading their pages. Nothing is overwritten. The new pages and the new catalog go on the end, past the catalog the committed generation points at, and the last write is the slot in the header, so until those bytes land the old generation is the file. The generation is taken from the slot whose bytes checksummed rather than from the higher number, because a torn slot can hold any number and picking which slot to overwrite from an unverified value is how a half written commit destroys the good copy beside it. The rewrite stays for the case an append cannot answer, which is a file and a catalog that disagree about which tables exist, and that is also what makes a `DROP TABLE` stick: the old "nothing has changed" check found nothing to do and the dropped table was still there at the next open. Adding one row to a file of forty million rows now takes 0.03 seconds.

A gather off a fixed width column does not ask who owns the run (#1158). A gather writes one value per wanted position into a run it allocated one line earlier, and it wrote them with `Buffer::push`, which is the copy on write point: it asks whether the buffer owns its values and copies the page out if it does not. The answer was known before the loop started, so the question was asked once per value and had the same answer every time, and it was a call, because the inline hint on `push` did not survive the size of the copy out path behind it. The loop now fills a `Vec` and hands the finished run over, and the map over the positions is exact sized, so the extend reserves once. This is the pass every filtered scan, every join gather and every hash table build runs over every fixed width column, so it turns up across the suite. Instructions retired at one thread on the native file at SF1: q5 1.105x, q9 1.085x, q2 1.081x, q13 1.077x, q18 1.049x, q10 1.049x, q11 1.029x, q7 1.026x, q21 1.024x, q16 1.019x, q14 1.018x, and nothing regressed.

A lopsided grouped distinct split is cut across pair partitions (#1162). The counting pass of a grouped `COUNT(DISTINCT)` is partitioned on the group hash, so a split is all the pairs of some set of groups and it is exactly as lopsided as the grouping column is. On the million row ClickBench file `COUNT(DISTINCT UserID) GROUP BY RegionID` puts eighteen percent of the pairs in one region, which is one group, so no number of splits divides it, and the pass had 2.42 ms of work and finished in 0.403 ms with one thread holding 0.376 ms of that. A split whose share is more than one thread's worth is now read in several pieces, each taking a range of the pair partitions. Those pieces cannot pick top rows, because none of them holds all of any group's pairs, so they hand their counts back to be added up, and that is cheap exactly when it happens, since a split is only oversized because a few groups in it are huge and the adding up walks groups rather than pairs. A split that fits is left as it was. ClickBench 10 0.87x, 08 0.92x, 11 0.94x.

A grouped distinct split's table is sized by its groups (#1157). The table was made once at twice the pairs the split was handed, on the argument that a group cannot appear more often than that, which is true and is not the number that matters. Fourteen thousand pairs and ten groups gave a hundred and thirty kilobyte table holding ten things, far enough apart that nearly every probe landed on a cache line nothing else had touched. It now starts at sixty four slots and doubles while it is less than half empty, up to the size it used to start at. Growing rehashes the groups found so far and nothing else, because the counts are indexed by the number the buckets hold rather than moved with them.

A sink says how wide it finishes and the rows decide how wide that is (#1156). The plain aggregate, the grouped distinct and the mixed one all ramped at one thread per sixteen thousand rows, which never bit because the sink's answer was dropped before it reached the lease. With that fixed the ask arrives, and on a thirty two thread box it asked for sixty two threads on a million rows, which is worse than sixteen: the pass probes a table with a slot per distinct pair, so it waits on memory, and past the machine's memory level parallelism another thread adds a wake and a join and takes nothing off the critical path. The three now share the rule the scan already uses to cut morsels, one thread per sixteen thousand rows up to eight and then one more per sixty five thousand, whichever is larger.

A column with no repeats is written without a dictionary (#1152). The string cascade was measured on every page and rejected, because it is a search and on the TPC-H orders table it took the load from 6.9 to 48.3 seconds while reading back no faster than the dictionary it replaced, 1.807 against 1.810 billion instructions, since unpicking a nest of layers a value at a time costs what the payload decode cost. Raw pages of the same column read in 0.686 billion, so the difference is what the page has to be put back together from. A dictionary-less varchar page is now one FSST layer, which keeps most of the ratio and decodes in one pass into one buffer with the values laid end to end, the layout the raw form already has, so the reader hands out views over it.

The allocator is asked for alignment only when it is not free (#1163). The `mimalloc` crate's `GlobalAlloc` answers every allocation with `mi_malloc_aligned(size, align)` whatever the alignment is, and mimalloc's fast path for that only fires for a block small enough to come off a thread's free list of small blocks, which is a kilobyte. A column of a chunk is two thousand values, so every buffer a query took went down a path marked `noinline` that re-derived what the caller already knew and concluded that a plain allocation would have done. Growing was worse, because `mi_realloc_aligned` takes the slow path at an alignment of sixteen and the slow path allocates and copies rather than growing a block where it is, so a `Vec` of decimals or of aggregate accumulators copied itself every time. The shell now writes the four call `GlobalAlloc` itself over `libmimalloc-sys` and decides the alignment at the call, where the `Layout` is a constant. Sixteen of the twenty two TPC-H queries at or above even against a per query control, mean 1.003x, best q22 1.018x and q16 1.014x.

## 0.3.75

A patch release of four pull requests. Three of them are speed and one is an error message where a wrong answer used to be. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

The three speed changes are all TPC-H and all found the same way, by counting instructions retired at one thread with each engine reading its own native file. Two of them are q13, which was tied with q1 as the worst ratio in the suite at 3.07x and is 1.37x now. The third is q21, which was the second largest absolute gap and is 2.00x.

A multi piece LIKE is answered without backtracking (#1151). A `LIKE` pattern compiled to one of four shapes, exact, prefix, suffix and contains, and anything else fell through to a character at a time walk with a backtracking point. `%special%requests%` is anything else, so q13 ran that walk over 1.5 million order comments. A pattern of literal text with `%` between the pieces and no `_` anywhere is the general case those four are the short cases of, and it does not need backtracking: the pieces are separated by runs of any length, so taking the leftmost occurrence of each piece in turn is not a guess that can be wrong. A `_` would break that argument because then the run between two pieces has a length to satisfy, which is why the shape is only built for a spelling without one. q13 went from 4,461,910,588 instructions to 2,196,022,140, which is 2.032x.

A dictionary over a plain run is compared without a Value per row (#1153). The hash table's batched key compare had four passes for the forms a key column can arrive in and none for the one a filter actually produces, which is a dictionary whose codes point into a flat run. That is the shape the driving side of every join after a filter is in, and before this a probe of one fell back to building a `Value` per row per column. One pass now serves both the flat case and the dictionary case by reading the run through a mapping, with the nulls answered separately because a dictionary keeps them in two places. q13 1.056x, q5 1.045x, q21 1.024x.

Only the columns a residual reads are gathered (#1154). A join condition the lookup cannot answer is run over a chunk built at the pair list, and building that chunk gathered every column of both sides so that the numbering the conjuncts count against would line up. q21's residual is one comparison of two `l_suppkey` columns and the two sides carry eight columns between them. The conjuncts are walked once when the probe is built, and a column none of them names is stood in for by a constant, which lines the numbering up for the price of one value rather than one pass over the pairs. The same function also read its answer one `Value` at a time when a conjunction of comparisons over a chunk is always a flat boolean. q21 went from 4,860,966,544 instructions to 3,975,013,135, which is 1.223x.

A correlated subquery no unnesting rule covers is refused by name (#1131). One whose inner side has a shape the rules do not recognise used to reach execution with the dependent join still in the plan, and the error named that node, which is one the binder invented and nobody wrote. The refusal moves to where the rules give up, which is the last point at which the plan still looks like the query someone typed, and it names the construct instead. The construct that brought it up is a positional join inside a correlated subquery, which the pinned DuckDB refuses for the same reason, so the two engines already agreed on the answer and differed only in what they said about it. Execution keeps a guard, and it is an internal error now rather than a not implemented one, because reaching it means a pass has a bug rather than a query having a gap.

## 0.3.74

A patch release of eleven pull requests. Nine of them are speed, one is a plan the pass that writes it could not reach, and one is a set of comments that had gone out of date in a way that mattered. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

Three of the nine come out of the same measurement and are worth reading together. Instructions retired per query at one thread, each engine reading its own native file, is a comparison that does not move with what else the machine is doing, and against DuckDB v1.5.5 at SF1 it puts the twenty two queries at a geometric mean of 2.11x rather than the twenty odd times that wall clock on a loaded laptop had been saying. q1 was the largest gap in the suite in both senses, 3.07x and 4.15 billion instructions behind, and it is 2.48x now. The three changes that did it are the same mistake in three places: a question asked per row whose answer the loop knew before it started.

A gather off a dictionary column answers its nulls a word at a time (#1146). A filtered scan copies the rows it kept out of the chunk, and for a dictionary column that copy asked each row whether it was null, which for a dictionary reads through the codes to the value the code stands for. A column with no nulls in it can say so once for the whole vector, by counting the words of its mask rather than reading its bits, and a gather whose indices are all in range off a column like that is all valid. q1 1.089x, q10 1.083x, q13 1.030x.

A packed column with no scale to move is cast in one pass (#1147). A decimal column of our own storage is bit packed, and the binder widens it on the way into arithmetic, so q1 casts DECIMAL(15,2) to DECIMAL(18,2) six million times. The scale is the same at both ends and only the width grows, but the cast still gathered the codes into a run of i128, walked that run asking whether each value fits the target, and walked it again narrowing it. Every value a packed column holds lies between its base and its base plus the widest code its width can hold, so the two ends settle the fit for all of them, and the cast becomes a read of a code, an add and a store. q1 1.062x.

A grouped sum adds into its total without asking what it is adding into (#1148). Every accumulator a call owns was made by that call, so they are all the same state, and the pass that picks the loop has already read that state off the first of them. The loop then called one shared function per row which matched on the state again, forty seven million times in q1 for eight aggregates over six million rows. Splitting the sum feed from the mean feed, since only a mean needs the scale a sum was carrying, lets the sum loop be a load, an add and a store. q1 1.100x, q18 1.036x.

A result is flattened on the threads that made it (#1135). A caller outside the engine reads a value at a time, so every column of a result set has to be flat before it leaves, and that flattening was done by the loop that drains the finished queue, which is the one part of a parallel query that runs on a single thread with every worker already joined. A four column scan of lineitem was 103.7ms of run and then 225.3ms of drain, and a single string column was 67.8ms of run and then 2452.2ms of drain. The root sink does it now, as it queues each chunk, on the worker that produced it, and since the sink was cloning the chunk into the queue anyway that copy is spent on the flatten rather than on a copy that is thrown away. Making the move free needed a by value flatten, because flattening through a borrow has to clone, and a clone of a flat vector copies every value to produce a vector identical to the one it was handed. The drain loop keeps a flatten as a safety net and every chunk reaching it is flat already. The reason `Vector::into_flat` copies at all is written next to it in #1138, which is what the flattens gate asks of every call that copies a column out flat.

A string column is cut and gathered without copying its payload (#1140). A flat string column is a run of views and an arena, which is the pair the string view form already is, and a view says where its bytes are, so keeping only some views or reordering them leaves each one pointing at the bytes it pointed at before. A cut, a gather and a flatten of one are the views and nothing else when the arena is a page somebody else is holding too, which is sixteen bytes a row moved and the payload left where it is. A column whose arena is its own still copies, because sharing an owned arena means cloning every byte including the values nobody asked for.

A column read out of a native file is held as a page (#1145). A reader that keeps a column past the chunk it arrived in used to have no choice but to clone it, which for a flat run is the whole run copied, and a group by does that for every key column of every chunk. The buffer already had the answer: its shared form is a window into an `Arc` page and copying or cutting one of those is a refcount. The producer says so once with `into_page`, a stored column is exactly the producer that should, and the native reader now says it for the part it decodes and for both string payloads. This is what makes #1140 pay on our own files.

An aggregate under a pushed down bound splits like any other (#1141). ClickBench 40 goes from 42.2ms to 26.1ms on a thirty two thread box against a ten million row native table, which is 1.62x, and the interesting part is that neither of the two changes in it is worth anything without the other.

A distinct pair is written into its split where it is found (#1139). The pass behind a grouped `COUNT(DISTINCT)` wrote every surviving pair into a vector and then read that vector back to work out which split of the group hash each pair belonged in, which learns nothing the first pass did not already have in registers. On ClickBench 8 the pass that went away read nine hundred thousand sixteen byte records out of memory a second time to turn them into twelve. It was a second pass because each split is asked for its share up front rather than left to double its way there, and the share needed a pair count; it is worked out from the rows the partition holds now, which is an upper bound, with a split that ends up holding less than half of what it asked for giving the difference back.

Group key pushdown reaches the join that restricts the keys (#1142). The pass copies the relation an outer query reads its keys out of and semi joins the aggregate's input against it, so the aggregate builds only the groups that will be read, and it looked for that relation by descending to the smallest node producing exactly the key tables. TPC-H q20's keys are `partsupp`'s and `partsupp` carries no filter, so the smallest such node held all eight million rows and the pass refused. What makes those keys few is the join to the parts named `forest%` just above, and the descent now takes the lowest node that both can be copied and would actually remove something, which is that join.

The aggregate says sixty four where it means sixty four (#1136). `RADIX_PARTITIONS` has been sixty four since #1000 and a dozen comments in `group.rs` still read sixteen. One of them was load bearing to a reader: `partition_from` multiplies a pushed down TopN bound by the partition count, so the threshold for every aggregate under such a bound quadrupled when #1000 landed and nothing in that change mentions it. For a bound of a thousand the split threshold went from sixteen thousand groups to sixty four thousand.

## 0.3.73

A patch release of six pull requests. Four of them are speed, one is a plan estimate and one is what the command line prints. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

A grouped aggregate probes its hash table once per run of a key rather than once per row (#1130). Rows reach the table in the order the file holds them, and a group by over a column the rows happen to arrive sorted on asks the table the same question over and over: 4,501,215 of the 6,001,215 rows of TPC-H's lineitem carry the order key of the row before them. Whether two rows next to each other hold the same key is answerable where the rows are, in one sequential pass per key column, and that is arithmetic against a probe's trip to memory. So the table marks every row whose whole key repeats the row before it, probes only the rows that begin a run, and copies the slot into the rest. It refuses under a spill file, under a group limit, when the coded direct map has already answered the chunk, and when fewer than half the chunk's rows are marked, and that last case is the one that has to stay cheap because it is what an unsorted group by hits. It costs one pass over one key column to find out. On the native file at SF1 with ten threads the q18 inner aggregate goes from 0.6650 to 0.6000 CPU seconds, q18 whole from 0.9550 to 0.8950, and the 22 query suite from 6.2400 to 6.1825. A sample at one thread puts the probe at 306 of 7238 samples where it was 1335. The same treatment written against the join probe measured slower on five of the six queries it touched and is not here, and the reason is written down on #761.

A join asks its sieve about a whole chunk at once (#1133). A fact table probing a filter built over a dimension table's keys reads a filter far too large to sit in the cache, and a row of it is one trip to memory and a branch. Asking about a run of rows and answering into a run of flags lets the core keep several of those trips outstanding instead of waiting out each one in turn, because nothing in between branches on what it finds. The four bits of one hash are all inside one block, so a row costs one cache line and the three loads after the first are already there. On the native file at SF1 with ten threads, q8 goes from 0.1650 to 0.1375 CPU seconds, q21 from 0.5975 to 0.5450, q5 from 0.3525 to 0.3375 and the suite from 6.9550 to 6.5850.

A `CASE` whose branches all name values of one dictionary is answered as codes rather than as strings (#1132). ClickBench 39 groups by a `CASE` over `Referer` beside `URL`, and writing that one column out was a quarter of the query, because a text column out of a native file arrives as codes over a dictionary and `Prepared::case` was building a plain string vector out of its branches. Every row read its value out of the dictionary, the dictionary was dropped, and the aggregate above had to hash strings. The answer to that `CASE` is one code per row copied from the branch that claimed it, with a literal contributing one code for all of its rows once the dictionary has been searched for it. The shape is narrow on purpose: a branch that computes anything is out, a `CASE` with no `ELSE` is out because the rows nothing claims are null and a null is not a code, and a literal the dictionary does not hold is out, which is decided per dictionary at run time rather than when the expression is prepared. q39 is 1.29x on instruction counts at both one thread and eight, and the rest of ClickBench does not move.

A wide decimal is totalled and compared without building a value per row (#1107). This is the rest of the aggregate half of #1088, and what was left after the packed run landed was three shapes that the vector paths declined on width or on type. A grouped total stopped at sixty four bits for no reason, since the loop checks per row and raises on the same row the row at a time path raises on, so it carries whatever an `i128` holds. An ungrouped total stopped there for a good reason, since it adds a whole vector into a local with no check at all, so the narrow loops are untouched and a hugeint column gets its own checked loop that hands the vector back rather than reporting anything itself. And a grouped extreme read integers only, where a decimal column carries one scale for every row in it, so the unscaled integers of two values out of the same column order the way the numbers do.

`IS NULL` and `IS NOT NULL` are costed off the null counts a store already keeps beside its bounds (#1129). Both got the same fifth that any condition nobody can read gets, while a native file has carried the null count of every column of every stripe since the format existed and a Parquet footer carries one per column chunk. On a hundred thousand row table with every tenth row null, `a IS NULL` was estimated at 20,000 and is now 10,000, and `a IS NOT NULL` was 20,000 and is now 90,000. The Parquet side gives up the whole sum if any row group states none, because summing only the groups that answered reports a number about part of a file under the name of all of it. A column the store says has no nulls makes `IS NOT NULL` the whole table, which stops a filter that throws nothing away from being costed as though it threw four fifths away. Part of #1106.

The command line builds a string only for the rows a box actually shows (#1120). It used to build one for every value of every row and then print the first twenty and the last twenty, which on a two hundred thousand row result is four hundred thousand strings built and dropped, and it cost 189ms against the duckdb binary's nothing for output that is byte identical. The mode decides what it will leave out, the cell builder builds only what is left, and the row writer is told where the gap goes rather than guessing it from a length. Separately, `value_text` was asking the session for its zone offset once per value whatever the type was, which is a walk through the zone's transition table for an answer only a timestamptz or a timetz uses. Two differences from duckdb turned up on the way and are fixed here: a box is now as wide as the rows it prints rather than as wide as a long value hidden in the gap, and only duckbox leaves rows out, where box, table and markdown print every row. Closes #1119.

## 0.3.72

A patch release of two pull requests, one of which finishes a change the last one but two started. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

A top N keeps a candidate as codes rather than as values since 0.3.69, but its sort key was still a value, and the key is the part that mattered. A row is kept once and rejected many times, so reading the key ran once per row of the input while reading the row ran once per row the operator kept, and reading a string out of a native text column means decoding a compressed dictionary block. A code on its own cannot be compared, because first appearance order says nothing about sort order, but a rank can: the file stores the sorted order of every text column's dictionary, so the rank of a code is where its value sits among all of them and two ranks in one dictionary compare exactly as the two strings do. A candidate's key carries its rank, a row is rejected by looking its own rank up through the codes, and nothing is decoded. #1123.

The measurement is worth writing down because a profile at one thread would have said not to bother. At one thread ClickBench 25 goes from 212.5 million instructions to 196.3 and ClickBench 26 from 86.4 to 80.4, eight and seven percent. At eight threads the same pair is twenty to thirty four percent, because one instance fills its candidates from the first chunk and the vectorized pass then rejects nearly every row before the row loop runs, while sixteen instances each see a sixteenth of the input, each set of candidates is far weaker, and the rows the pass cannot settle are the ones that reach the reject. At thirty two threads, which is the shape the benchmark runs in, ClickBench 25 goes 0.815 ms to 0.698 and ClickBench 26 0.520 to 0.463, with the forty three query suite at 70.3 ms against 71.0 and nothing else moving.

Answers are unchanged across the suite apart from the queries that have no deterministic answer to begin with, which is the same set as always.

The other one is a `WITH` definition that is named more than once. It was put into every place it was named, so a query that names it twice ran it twice, and it is held and read where it is named now. A definition named once is still inlined, a definition written inside a subquery is still inlined however many times it is read because it is allowed to name a column of the query around it, and a name that a later definition writes again is left alone because which of the two a read means is a question about scopes that a count of spellings cannot ask. TPC-H q15 names its definition twice, both copies read six million rows and grouped a million and a half of them, and the second copy threw away everything but one number. q15 goes from 0.1850 to 0.1000 CPU seconds on the native file at SF1 and from 3.11x behind DuckDB on its own format to 1.52x, and the 22 query suite goes 5.5875 to 5.2550. Nothing else in the suite names a definition twice. This is also what DuckDB does, which I checked in v1.5.5 rather than assumed. #1125.

## 0.3.71

A patch release of one pull request, which is a speed change that turned out to be a correctness change as well. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

`AVG` over a decimal column adds the integers the column stores and divides once at the end, instead of turning every row into a double and dividing that by a hundred before adding it (#1121). A decimal is an integer with the type saying where the point goes, so the exact treatment `AVG` over an integer column already had was always the right one for both. I found it by sampling TPC-H q1 at one thread and seeing a quarter of the query inside the routine that widens a 128 bit integer to a double, which was surprising because nothing in q1 is 128 bits wide. The call is on the running total, on the one row that ends the exact phase, and it ran on all six million rows because a pure call can be run before the compiler knows which side of the branch it wants. q1 goes from 0.4650 to 0.3600 CPU seconds on the native file at SF1, and the 22 query suite from 4.3250 to 4.2050.

Two of q1's answers change, and both changes are improvements. All twelve cells of its three average columns now agree with duckdb to the last digit, where before they drifted, because a per row division rounds a hundred and fifty thousand times per group and those roundings do not cancel. And q1 is repeatable for the first time: a parallel average used to add its partial sums in thread completion order, so every run of every build answered it slightly differently, and an exact integer total does not depend on that order.

## 0.3.70

A patch release of two pull requests, both of them found by sampling one TPC-H query and both of them the same kind of mistake. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

q20 was the query furthest behind DuckDB on our own format, and almost all of it is one decorrelated subquery that filters a year of lineitem and then groups nine hundred thousand rows by two keys. Splitting that in half at one thread gave two separate gaps, one in the scan and one in the aggregate, and this release is one pull request against each.

The scan was calling a function pointer per row. Both bit packed comparison kernels decided the operator ahead of the loop and handed it to the loop as a `fn`, so a range predicate over a packed column was a bit extract, an indirect call and a return per row, and a loop with a call in it is a loop the compiler will not widen. The operator is a match outside the loop now with its own body under each arm. That bare q20 filter goes 1.50x, q10 1.35x, and q6, q14 and q15 gain a few per cent each, which is the right shape, because every query that moved is one with a range predicate on a packed column.

The aggregate was building a value per row to hash it. A column of few distinct numbers over a wide range goes to the native file as a dictionary whose codes point into a packed run, and the hash had an arm for a dictionary over plain data and an arm for a straight packed run but not one for the two together, so every row of that form fell to the loop at the bottom that asks the vector for a `Value`. The batched key comparison fell through the same way. Both now read the run directly. That aggregate on its own goes 1.14x, q7 1.43x, q15 1.11x and q20 1.08x.

All 22 answers are unchanged on both the native file and Parquet, apart from q1, whose two double averages differ in their last digits between any two runs of any binary because a parallel average adds its partial sums in whatever order the threads finish.

## 0.3.69

A patch release of five pull requests, all of them about the native format carrying its weight. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

The work here came out of a four way comparison that had not been possible before, because the native format could not hold a DECIMAL column until 0.3.68 and seven of the eight TPC-H tables have one. Running the suite four ways, this engine and DuckDB each over the same Parquet files and each over its own native file, says that Parquet against Parquet we are 1.60x behind and native against native 2.42x, which is the finding: DuckDB gains 1.92x from its own format and we gain 1.27x from ours, so our native path was giving back most of what the format is for.

The largest single case of that was an `IN` over a text column. The binder has no `IN` node, so `x IN ('a', 'b')` arrives as an `OR` of equalities and the pipeline folds it back into a set, and the set lookup had a coded path only for a dictionary whose values live in a vector. A Parquet dictionary is a vector. A native one is held by the storage reader, so the lookup got nothing back and fell to the row at a time loop, which read six million strings to answer a question with seven possible answers. The list is now searched against the dictionary the native writer sorted, once for the whole query at one binary search per literal, and what comes out is a handful of codes, after which every row is a code against those codes and no value is read at all. Dictionaries with no order to search take the memo the single literal comparison already uses. `count(*) WHERE l_shipmode IN ('MAIL', 'SHIP')` over SF1 native goes 0.4500 to 0.0425 CPU seconds, which is 2.5x faster than DuckDB on its own format rather than 4.3x slower, and q12, which was the worst query in the suite at 7.4x behind DuckDB native, goes 0.6450 to 0.1675 and is now 1.2x behind. #1112.

The top N keeps a candidate as codes. An instance keeps far more rows than it hands back, about sixty seven to hand ten back on ClickBench 24, because the worst candidate improves every time something better arrives and each improvement reads a whole row out of the chunk. A column read out of a native file arrives as codes over a dictionary the whole query shares, and a code is four bytes that name the value without reading it, so a candidate holds the code where the column gave it one and the value is read in finalize for the rows that actually won. #1111. The same operator also had no cut in its batched path, which is the one any bound above the sorted threshold takes: the key it would have compared against was only worked out after the instance held twice its bound, so an instance that never sees twice its bound never rejects anything and pays a vector per key and a vector per row for everything it sees. #1108.

Two changes in what the planner knows about a filter. A native file has counted how many rows hold each value, per column, since the format existed, and nothing outside the scan had ever looked at those counts. Where the synopsis accounts for every row of a column, `c = k` is not an estimate at all but the count the synopsis holds, or zero when the list does not hold `k`, because a complete list proves no row does. #1110. And `EXPLAIN (STATISTICS)` now says which of the three places a distinct count came from, rather than printing every one of them as the dictionary. A count can come from a native table's dictionary, from what a Parquet writer counted per row group, or from the bottom-k sketch #1101 added, and two of the three were lying about which. #1105.

## 0.3.68

A patch release of sixteen pull requests, in the vector size, the native format, the forms a stored column actually arrives in, the statistics a table in memory keeps, the top N, and the gate's own clock. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 22.

The vector is 8192 values instead of 1024, closing #480. It was 1024 because that is the FastLanes unit, and the spec said so in five places and called it the single decision that most constrains the rest of the system. The argument was about the wrong level. That constant was also deciding how much of a table one zone map covered and how much work one call into the pipeline did, and both of those wanted a much larger number than the encoding alignment did. #984 gave a table in memory row groups of its own, which took the storage question away from the vector, and this is the measurement of what was left: five release binaries from one commit, one per size, over the scans and over ClickBench. Every scan number improves monotonically up to 8192 and the suite picks the same size. #1084.

Decimal columns in the native format, closing #1061. Seven of the eight TPC-H tables have at least one DECIMAL column, so until now the native format could not hold the benchmark it exists to run. A decimal is an integer of unscaled units and the engine already stores one in whichever integer its declared width calls for, so the writer writes that integer and the directory carries the width and the scale beside the column name. The type tag grows two parameter bytes rather than taking a tag per width, because width and scale are what say how many bytes a row costs, and a reader that guessed would read the wrong number of bytes rather than the wrong number of digits. The cascade and the bit packing pick a decimal up for free, since both work on the physical integer and neither asks what the column means. All eight TPC-H tables at SF1 now load into one file, 322 MB against DuckDB's 265 MB for the same eight, and all twenty two queries run over it, which is the first time the native column of the four engine contract can be filled in at all. #1079.

That immediately exposed what had never been on the hot path before, which is that a column of numbers written to a native file does not come back flat. It comes back bit packed when its range is narrow and as a dictionary over a packed run when it has few distinct values, and three of the places that read a column had a path for neither and built a `Value` per row. The compare kernel knew a packed column against a literal, which is the pair the form was added for, and had nothing for two packed columns against each other, which is `l_commitdate < l_receiptdate` in q4, q12 and q21: `count(*)` under that predicate was 395.2 ms on our own file against 46.4 ms on Parquet. A packed value is its base plus its code, so each side's base is added to each side's code and the two sums compared, and where the two ranges do not overlap at all the answer is four numbers and no bit of either column. #1090. The cast sweep had the same hole for the same reason, since a packed column's values are not held at the width of any layout, and that is the form a stored decimal arrives in. #1094. And the aggregate had the hole twice over, because a grouped aggregate does not go through the code an ungrouped one does, so the gather and the scatter each needed the packed loop and the dictionary over a packed run. It fixed a wrong answer nothing was testing on the way through. #1096.

The planner sees what a native table wrote down about itself. A native file has carried the two ends and the null count of every column of every stripe since the format existed, and a global dictionary count for every string column, and the scan has been reading them the whole time while the planner was ordering the query from the same constants a table nobody had measured would get. `Stripes` answers the zone question off the stripe bounds and the distinct count off the dictionary for a string column and off the span between the two ends for every other, which is a ceiling rather than a count and a tight one on the columns that decide a join order, since nationkey runs 0 to 24 and holds 25 values. Neither opens a page. #1074.

A table in memory counts its own distinct values, closing #1089. It kept zone maps and null counts as its rows arrived and nothing else, so the optimizer had no distinct count for any of its columns and every join order, every aggregate sizing and every filter selectivity over a memory table was built on a constant. It now keeps one bottom-k sketch per column over the whole table, built in the pass that already builds the zone maps. A sketch that has not filled up is holding every hash it was given, so a column with fewer distinct values than the sketch has room for is counted exactly rather than estimated, and the two halves are read in different places: the exact one answers a `COUNT(DISTINCT c)` out of the statistics without reading a row, and the estimate goes to the binder and the optimizer carrying the class that says what it is. The first version of this was too expensive to ship. Adding a hash was a binary search over a sorted vector plus an insert that memmoved the tail, once per row of every column, which cost 23 nanoseconds a value for a column of a thousand distinct values against two to hash it, and the ClickBench hits load spent 3.3 seconds of its 7 in there.
