The hashes now live in an open addressed table four times k long that fills to half its slots and then keeps the k smallest in one pass, so an add is a threshold compare and a probe at 6.8 nanoseconds, and holding a superset of the bottom k between two of those passes costs 128 KB a column instead of 32 KB. On server2 over three loads each, hits went from 3.3 seconds of distinct counting to 1.8 and TPC-H lineitem at SF1 from 3.8 to 1.6, with hits' whole load going from about 7 seconds to about 4.5. What a memory table still has nothing for is the frequency synopsis and the string extremes, which is #1103. #1101.

Two changes in the grouped aggregate. A grouping over two dictionary encoded string columns hashed the strings and byte compared them six million times to reach six distinct answers, because every dictionary fast path was gated on the key being a stable dictionary, which is the form the native reader produces and the Parquet reader never does. A chunk whose key columns are all dictionaries with few enough values between them is read as codes now, with each column contributing its code times a stride and the combined index picking a slot out of a small direct map kept across chunks, so a row group that hands the same dictionary to every chunk pays for the probe once. Two string keys over SF1 lineitem go 27.4 ms to 8.4 against DuckDB at 12.0. #1086. The other one notices that an aggregate reading nothing but its own group key is a projection over a count: the smallest referer in a group whose key is the referer is the key, so the pass keeps the grouping, drops every call, counts rows, and works the original answers out above. On ClickBench, `GROUP BY Referer` with `COUNT(*)` is 0.55 s and the same query with `MIN(Referer)` added was 3.34 s, which is six times the whole of the first query for one more question about a group the engine already had. #1091.

ClickBench 25 is `SELECT SearchPhrase ... ORDER BY SearchPhrase LIMIT 10` and it was the worst query in the suite against DuckDB, with half its instructions in the top N. Once a chunk the operator asks whether any row left can beat its tenth best candidate, and it asked by building a constant vector of that candidate's value and handing it to the comparison kernel, which answers out of the dictionary's sorted order but has to find where the value sits first, and that is a binary search of a hundred thousand values where a probe the stored eight byte head cannot settle decodes a payload block. #1093 remembered the last sixty four answers to that search, shared across the instances of a scan. #1095 stopped asking: the value came out of that same dictionary carried by a row that arrived with its code, so its rank was known when the row was kept, and two new kernels record the rank and select against it.

A LIMIT or an OFFSET holding a subquery is the last of the three forms the clause takes and the one nobody has a value for until the query is running. The binder folds it where it can, as before, and where it cannot the subquery is joined in under the limit as a single row, so its one value is in a column of every row the limit sees, and the operator evaluates the bound once off the first chunk and keeps it, which is the only reading that makes sense of a volatile bound. #1092.

The decimal arithmetic kernel computes an add, a subtract and a product at the run's own width. It widened every row to `i128`, rescaled, asked whether the answer fitted and pulled both operands out as `Value`s the moment it did not, all inside a closure returning a `Result`, on the reading that decimal arithmetic is rare on a scan. It is not rare here: `l_extendedprice * (1 - l_discount)` is the argument of an aggregate in eight of the twenty two TPC-H queries. The three operators that are one native instruction now take an optimistic pass that accumulates whether any row wrapped or overflowed the answer's width and hands the vector back to the careful loop the moment one did. Three of the casts the binder writes into that expression change only the decimal width, and since a decimal is kept as its unscaled integer that is a relabelling, so a flat run comes out of it sharing the buffer it went in with. `sum(l_extendedprice * (1 - l_discount))` over SF1 lineitem goes 0.2325 to 0.1287 CPU seconds and q1 goes 0.8875 to 0.7225. #1099.

Queueing a pipeline's instances did one `notify_one` per instance, which is a wake syscall, and they are paid one after another by the thread every instance is waiting on, so a sixteen way pipeline paid fifteen syscalls before it read a row. One wake covers all of them when there is a job for every parked worker, and below that they are still counted out, because a worker woken to find nothing has cost a wake and a lock for nothing. Measured with `strace -c -f -e trace=futex` over sixty reps of ClickBench 8, which is load independent: 9,431 futex calls before and 9,107 after. #1085.

The metrics learn to tell stagger from imbalance. A pipeline reports the wall of its slowest instance and enough to work out the mean, and the gap reads as imbalance, and much of it is not: the instances start as the dispatching thread gets round to waking them. The two want opposite fixes and acting on the wrong one is not free, which is how a native scan's whole stripe per worker got cut into stealable pieces and made the suite slower, 88.3 ms against 86.4, because the page ownership a whole stripe buys is worth more than the stealing. The number is measured now, at one clock read and one atomic maximum per instance. #1087.

And the gate says what machine it ran on. It was reported at 18.7 minutes and the ask was to make it faster, and the first thing was to find out where the time went: that run was an eight core box at load 13 to 21 with two other sessions each running a full workspace test, and a second run on the same tree came out at 22.6 minutes. On an idle thirty two thread machine the same gate is 1.1 minutes cold and 44 seconds warm. Neither of the first two numbers says anything about the gate, so the last line names the machine now, and the one real find underneath was the corpus step rebuilding the crate graph because Cargo hashes `RUSTFLAGS` into the fingerprint. #1097.

The version is bumped in the workspace manifest and the lock is refreshed.

## 0.3.67

A patch release of fifteen pull requests, in the native format, the table in memory, the correlated subquery, the join estimate, the distinct count and the allocator. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format goes from 21 to 22, because a database file holds every table of the database now instead of one.

A native database file holds many tables. `CHECKPOINT` with two tables in the catalog used to answer "Not implemented Error: more than one table in a native database file", which meant the native column of the four engine contract could not be filled on TPC-H at all, since TPC-H has eight tables and ClickBench has one. The directory gains a level: a catalog directory names every table with its schema, its row count and the span of that table's own directory, and a table directory is the v21 body unchanged from the fields onward, so the page grammar is the same and the diff is one level of wrapping. One footer slot write publishes every table, so a reader sees the generation before a checkpoint or the generation after it and never half of one, and a crash between two tables leaves the previous generation intact. #1057 is the specification and #1062 is the implementation.

A table in memory is held as row groups. One page per column per row group, with a chunk a window into a page rather than an allocation of its own, so twenty million rows over two columns go from 19,532 chunks and about 39,000 buffers to 163 groups and 326 pages. Nothing above the storage seam sees the change, because the chunk numbering is what it was and a directory says where each chunk lives rather than the layout implying it. #1063. The group then earns its keep on the read side, by carrying the zones of its chunks folded together and letting a scan rule the whole group out before any chunk in it is looked at. `count(*) WHERE k = 7` over twenty million rows used to rule out almost every chunk and pay 748 nanoseconds each to say so, because an empty chunk still had to travel the pipeline. #1072. One consequence is that the pruning tests stopped being a test: they check a native scan against the same rows in memory, on the grounds that memory reads every row, and memory prunes now too, so the memory side runs with every optimizer pass turned off. #1075.

Two changes in the optimizer, both about TPC-H and both worth a third or more of the query they fire on. A join is estimated by containment, which says the join of two whole tables is as tall as the larger of them, and containment is a floor no filter can get under, so no equijoin in any plan was ever estimated as producing fewer rows than the side it joins. q9 pays for that twice over: its join to supplier matches every lineitem row and its join to the parts a colour filter keeps matches about one in twenty, both estimate at 6,001,215, and the greedy pass then breaks the tie on the sum of the inputs and runs the join that removes nothing first. Every side now carries the rows it produces and the rows it would produce with the filters under it taken out, and a join is the containment reading over the two unfiltered sides scaled by what each side's filters kept. q9 goes 0.2352s to 0.1518s, q5 and q21 eleven per cent each, and the suite six per cent. #1070. The other one tells the aggregate that answers a correlated scalar subquery which keys are going to be asked about. q17 groups the whole of lineitem by part key, so it reads six million rows and builds two hundred thousand groups, and the single join above it keeps the two hundred whose part has brand 23 in a medium box. The keys are sitting on the other side of that join, so the pass copies the relation they are read out of and semi joins the aggregate's input against the copy. It refuses a key source bigger than a tenth of what the aggregate reads, which is q20, and a key source with no filter under it, which is q15, where every supplier key in lineitem is a supplier and the semi join would remove nothing. q17 goes 130.1ms to 43.4ms and q2 33.5ms to 23.5ms. #1080.

Three changes in the grouped `COUNT(DISTINCT)` path, which is what ClickBench 8 and 9 are. The open addressed table that throws duplicate pairs away held only where each pair was and not what it was, so a probe landing on somebody else's pair had to read that pair to find out, which is a second random load into a megabyte of records for nine rows in ten. The spare bits of the bucket carry a tag of the hash now and the record is only read when the tag says it might be the pair. #1054. The fan out of that table was a fixed sixteen answering two questions with different answers, how large a partition's table should be to stay in the cache of the thread building it and how many pieces of work there should be for threads to take. The scatter now spreads into sixty four and the finishing pass picks the real number once it knows the row count, merging the partitions it does not want into the ones it does, which costs nothing because a partition is a list of the runs the instances handed it. #1071. And the numeric half of a mixed aggregate probed its table through a call the compiler kept declining to inline, thirty instructions of frame around seventeen of body, and asked for the group's hash twice where the pair hash is the group hash with the counted value mixed in. ClickBench 9 goes from 1036 million instructions to 896. #1078.

The finishing half of the same aggregate collects the pairs that survived deduplication into a vector that started empty, and a vector that doubles its way from nothing to n copies about n records on the way there, which on ClickBench 8 is nearly a second pass over the data the loop had just written. A partition cannot hold more distinct pairs than it holds rows, so the count is asked for once, and that costs no more memory than the doubling reached anyway since a vector that has just grown holds up to twice what is in it. The measurement that pointed at this half is a thread sweep: the sink halves as the threads double, 3.158 ms at eight to 1.414 at thirty two, and the finishing half went 2.562 ms at eight to 2.985 at thirty two, so what it is bound by is traffic rather than serialisation. #1082.

A narrow integer group key gets a run of its own. Half of ClickBench's schema is `SMALLINT` and a key of that width went into the general run of tagged values, so `Column::holds` built a tagged value on each side of every comparison and the batched comparison that exists to match on the type once per probe rather than once per row had no arm for it at all. `TINYINT` and `SMALLINT` now keep runs of `i8` and `i16` beside the ones `INTEGER` and `BIGINT` had. #1064.

The shell sets mimalloc as its global allocator, behind a default on feature and nowhere else, because a bulk load is an allocator benchmark and half of one was going to the system allocator. #1060. That turned the determinism job red, since mimalloc writes `__DATE__` and `__TIME__` into a banner nothing we ship ever prints, so two builds a minute apart differed in eleven bytes of string and in the twenty byte UUID derived from the content. Both gcc and clang freeze those macros when `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` is set, which is the switch the reproducible builds effort defined for this case, and setting it in `.cargo/config.toml` rather than in the workflow makes every build of the workspace comparable rather than only the two CI runs. #1066.

Memory accounting stops counting a shared part once per holder. `Vector::footprint` charged a part behind an `Arc` in full to every vector holding it, on the argument that over counting refuses a query that would have fit while under counting lets through one that does not. That argument does not survive the same `Arc` being handed out once per chunk of output, which is exactly what a stable dictionary key does: a `GROUP BY URL` over 18,342,019 groups charged its 47.6 MiB dictionary 17,912 times and refused itself every limit from 2 GB to 25 GB while the process held 1.14 GB. #1059, closing #1058.

The version is bumped in the workspace manifest and the lock is refreshed. The gate is green locally, including the rudb-compat corpus against this build.

## 0.3.66

A patch release of five pull requests, in the statistics the planner publishes, the grouped aggregate, the distinct count exchanges and the packed tail reader. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 21.

Two of the five publish what the optimizer knows and change no plan at all. `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, STATISTICS)` now ends with the q-error of every operator that had a row count in front of it, split into exact, certified and estimated, which is P0's third exit criterion asked of the engine rather than worked out in a spreadsheet afterwards. Splitting by class is the point of it, because an estimate an order of magnitude out is an ordinary bad guess and an exact count an order of magnitude out is a bug, and one histogram averages the two into a number that is true of neither of them. The rule that goes with it reports an operator that produced more rows than its exact count said exist as a wrong answer bug however small the disagreement is, and in that direction only, because this engine has three ways to produce fewer rows than the plan says and none at all to produce more: a limit stopping a pipeline part way, a filter the scan applies itself, and a hash join handing the scan under its driving side the range and the key filter of the side that finished first. A measurement bug fell out on the way, which is that a scan with a folded filter under it was recording the table's exact count as its estimate and so was comparing two different questions, and under the new rule every query with a `WHERE` over a stored table reported the catalog as having counted wrong. Over the 381 cardinalities in TPC-H SF1 there is now not one contradiction, out of 146 exact counts and 235 estimates, with a third of the estimates within a factor of two and a seventh out by more than a thousand. #1041.

The other one works out what tells a node's rows apart, which is the evidence the four P1 rewrites are licensed by. A group by whose key already tells the rows apart is a projection, a `DISTINCT` over rows that are already distinct is a copy, and a join to a table that contributes no column and matches at most one row is nothing at all, and none of those is a decision about cost, so a rewrite licensed by a guess produces a wrong answer rather than a slow query. Every case that is not obviously sound says nothing rather than guessing, and that is why a scan claims no key: `PRIMARY KEY` parses and the catalog does not keep it, so a scan that claimed one would be claiming it from the spelling of the DDL. It lives in rudb-plan rather than in rudb-opt because the `match` over `Node` does not compile when a variant appears, so whoever adds an operator says what it does to uniqueness the day they add it, and that earned itself once already when the rebase onto `LimitPercent` would not build. What it holds is column sets and fixed columns, where the empty set is a legal key meaning at most one row, and it prints on the plan line beside the cardinality under `EXPLAIN (STATISTICS)` and prints nothing where nothing is known. Across the same 381 nodes, 75 carry a key, 14 are provably at most one row, and every join in TPC-H is onto a primary key that this cannot see yet. #1048.

A partitioned grouped aggregate stops keeping a table per thread once the copies are too large for the cache. Giving every instance its own table per partition is the right trade while the tables are small, because no lock is taken on the fold, and it stops being the right trade once the copies no longer fit in the cache, because they are not sharing it, they are competing for it, and a hash aggregate is nothing but random touches. `GROUP BY l_partkey` over SF1 lineitem folds at 23 nanoseconds a row on one thread with a 19 MiB table and at 182 on ten threads with ten copies totalling 160 MiB, so ten threads buy 1.28 times on the operator. The machinery to fold every instance into one shared set of tables was already there and only running out of memory reached it, because nothing asked about the cache. Three questions now do. One asks whether one set of tables per instance still fits in sixteen megabytes. One asks whether the tables are really copies of each other, which an instance reads off its own table by dividing the rows it folded by the groups they opened, since a hundred thousand groups of fifty rows is ten copies of everything and a hundred thousand groups of one row is none. And one asks whether the input has divided the keys between the instances already, counted as runs of equal keys on the pass that was hashing them anyway, which is what saves q18 and every other query grouped by order key, because lineitem is written in order key order. q17 goes from 0.1525 to 0.1354 seconds and its peak memory from 160.3 MiB to 84.3 MiB, with the 81 ms merge stage gone, and every other query is inside the noise. #1050.

Two are about speed and both are the same kind of change, which is reading a column in bulk where the code read it a row at a time. The two distinct count exchanges now read their columns as blocks, using the reader built for the fixed width exchange in 0.3.65, which callgrind had put at 13 per cent of ClickBench 9 and 11 per cent of ClickBench 8. A flat `BIGINT` run is a copy, a narrower one is a sign extension of a couple of instructions a lane, and the forms the vector will not hand over are filled a row at a time exactly as before, so no caller has to know which kind of column it was given. Asking whether a column has any nulls is now `Vector::none_null`, which looks inside a dictionary's values as well as at the top level validity, so a dictionary holding a null that no code pointed at no longer takes the fast path and hits an `expect`. #1047.

Reading one value out of a packed tail is now a single eight byte load. A tail value begins at most seven bits into a byte, so anything up to fifty seven bits wide ends inside the eight bytes it starts in, and that is every width that turns up, since the offsets a block of text carries are seventeen bits and a dictionary code or a run length is fewer. The old code built a sixteen byte window for all of them and filled it with a copy whose length the compiler could not see, so it was a call rather than a load. A string's length is where it ends less where it starts and where it starts is where the string before it ended, so the text column now asks for both offsets together and gets them out of one load wherever the pair fits in one. ClickBench 27 goes from 2.48 billion instructions to 1.94 billion, and the suite against DuckDB moves from 4.11 to 4.42 times overall. #1049.

The version is bumped in the workspace manifest and the lock is refreshed. The gate is green locally, including the rudb-compat corpus against this build.

## 0.3.65

A patch release of nine pull requests, in the binder, the grouping, the exchange, the aggregate, the scan and the Parquet estimator. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 21.

Three of the nine take a construct the binder refused by name and make it work, which is the first exit criterion of D3. `UNION BY NAME` lines the two sides of a union up by column name rather than by position, so the result takes the left side's columns in the order the left side wrote them, then the right side's columns the left side did not write, and a column only one side wrote is filled with a null on the other. That is why the two sides do not have to be the same width here and do have to be everywhere else. Names match without regard to case and the spelling that comes out is the left side's. A side that wrote one name twice is refused, because matching by name needs the name to say which column and that side has no answer to give, and the ordinary union does not care because position says which column there. The set operation binder now asks for a list of merged columns before anything else, built either by position or by name, and the projection that puts a side into the agreed shape reads that list rather than a list of types. Twenty seven hand written shapes are byte for byte identical to duckdb v2.0.0-dev84237, and the four upstream files that use it went from three answered queries to twenty one. #1037.

A `LIMIT` or an `OFFSET` can be written as an expression rather than as a literal row count. Anything whose value is settled before the first row is read will do, so `LIMIT 1 + 1` is two rows and `OFFSET abs(-2)` skips two, and the value is then cast to `BIGINT` whatever it was written as, which is the whole of the type rule. A string converts, a decimal rounds so `LIMIT 2.5` is three rows and `LIMIT 2.4` is two, a boolean is one row or none, and a date is the cast refusing a date rather than a rule of its own. Working the value out can raise, and then the error is the answer and the query fails while it is being planned, which is where the pin fails it too, because there is no row count for the plan node to hold. A `CASE` is worked out one arm at a time, so an arm the condition excludes is not evaluated. The evaluation itself is a new module in rudb-bind and the optimizer's folding pass calls it rather than keeping its own copy, which is where it belongs because the binder is below the optimizer in the layer rule. One thing fell out of the move: rudb-opt no longer depends on rudb-kernels or on rudb-vector at all, because the folding pass was the only thing in it that called a kernel. #1040.

A `LIMIT` can be a share of the input rather than a row count. The count is worked out from how many rows arrived and it rounds down, so thirty and thirty five percent of ten rows are both three rows and five percent is none. The share is taken from the whole input and the offset is applied to the rows it left, which is why `LIMIT 30 PERCENT OFFSET 2` over ten rows runs past where a share of the eight remaining rows would have stopped. The value is cast to `DOUBLE` rather than to the `BIGINT` a row count takes, and a share outside nought to a hundred is refused while the query is planned, the way the pin refuses it. It gets a plan node of its own rather than a wider `Limit`, for three reasons: a row count streams and a share does not, so the operator holds its rows and the pipeline closes under it; the rewrites that fire on a plain limit are wrong on this one, in particular the filter pushdown and the turn of a limit over a sort into a top N, and a separate variant stops them by construction; and the pin builds a separate physical operator for it too. Holding the share as an `f64` cost `Node` its `Eq`, which is now `PartialEq` only, following the precedent of `Value` in rudb-common. Upstream's `test_limit_percent.test` goes from nothing answered to eleven of twenty one records and `limit_percent_view.test` to two of two. #1045.

A subquery written in a clause of a grouped query is joined in over the grouping rather than under it. It used to be refused with a sentence about the `GROUP BY` that was wrong twice, because the grouping rule is about columns of the query's own `FROM` and a subquery in the select list is not one of those, and because the column it produces belongs to no table anybody wrote so the name slot printed a placeholder. The real problem was where the join went. Underneath the grouping makes the subquery's column a column of every row going into the aggregate, and the aggregate carries nothing but its groups and its aggregates upward, so the projection could not have read it even if the rule had let it through. Both halves go away when the join goes on top. There was already a list for queries that belong on top, because `HAVING` has the same problem and TPC-H q11 is the case, and the select list and the `ORDER BY` join it. Two things stay underneath, a correlated one because what it correlates to is a column of the rows going in, and one written inside an aggregate's own argument because that is read once per row going in. Closes #1027, and the correlated case it leaves is filed as #1032. #1033.

The fixed width exchange reads its four columns as blocks. `GROUP BY WatchID, ClientIP` with a count, a sum and an average over it builds one fixed width record per row out of four integer columns, and it read them with four `is_null_at` calls and four `signed_at` calls a row, each of which matches on the vector's body, calls into the data under it and matches again on the layout, to fetch a number already sitting in a flat slice. About sixty five instructions for a load, and callgrind put the two together at a third of ClickBench 32. The vector gets a bulk form of each now, one that copies a flat run and sign extends a narrower one and walks a constant, a sequence or a bit packed body dispatching once per chunk, and one that asks the nulls question once for the whole vector. Neither has to answer: a dictionary and a run are a gather rather than a copy, `HUGEINT` does not fit, and a caller that gets a refusal reads the vector the way it read it before, so the record the exchange builds is the same record for every shape of input. #1043.

The compact overflow map is asked its length before it is asked for a slot. `CompactNumeric` holds the count, the sum and the average for one group of `SMALLINT` arguments and keeps the exact total in a side map for the groups whose sum leaves sixty four bits. It asked that map for the group's slot once per row, and the map is keyed on a `usize`, so every row paid a SipHash of a number to be told the map was empty. It is empty in every query that does not overflow, and a `SMALLINT` sum needs on the order of ten to the fourteen rows in one group to get there, so on anything anyone runs the hash bought nothing. Callgrind put it at 7.96 per cent of ClickBench 32. The length is a load, the map is only touched when the length says there is something in it, and the slow path is the code that was already there. #1039.

A part's own bounds wave it through as well as ruling it out. The page of per part ranges landed in 0.3.64 and the skip path reads it, but the path that lets a scan drop the comparison on a chunk where the statistics already prove every row passes was still asking the stripe and nothing else. A stripe covers sixty four parts at once, so on any predicate that keeps a slice of a clustered column the stripe straddles the comparison and settles nothing, which is every ClickBench query from 27 onwards. It asks the stripe first for the probes it can settle and reads the part's own ends for the rest, per probe rather than once, so a filter of four comparisons where the stripe settles one still gets the other three from the part. Both steps are in the safe direction, because a stretch where everything passes contains no narrower stretch where something fails, and a string end written into the file is cut down to its first bytes which only makes a part look wider than its rows really are. #1038.

A Parquet dictionary size says how well it is known instead of being handed over as a count. A writer that built a dictionary for a column of a row group can state how many entries went in it, and that number is exact for the row group, but the estimator wants it for the column and the footer brackets that rather than being it: the column holds at least as many distinct values as its largest row group and no more than all of them added up, capped at the file's rows. So the supplier hands over both ends, and where they meet, which is a file of one row group, the count is exact and says so. A file where any row group stated nothing gives up the column entirely, because the group that said nothing could hold anything. The lower end is the value because it is the safe end for both readers today, since an equality divides by it and dividing by too small a number keeps too many rows, which costs a scan rather than an answer. No plan changes and all twenty two TPC-H plans are byte for byte identical, but a distinct count now says it came from a dictionary rather than from a sketch, and there is no sketch in this engine and never was. #1024.

The version is bumped in the workspace manifest and the lock is refreshed. The gate is green on gamingpc, including the rudb-compat corpus against this build.

## 0.3.64

A patch release of six pull requests, in the native directory, the string kernels, the optimizer and the zone maps. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format goes from 20 to 21, because the directory now keeps a range per part as well as per stripe, and a format 20 file still reads.

A native file keeps the two ends of every part rather than only of every stripe. A stripe is sixty four parts, and on any column the rows are not already ordered by, a stripe covers nearly everything the column holds and rules out nothing while the parts inside it are narrow and most of them could have been ruled out. ClickBench q24 orders by `EventTime` and takes ten rows, and the cutoff its answer settles at leaves 36 rows of 999,975 alive: at stripe grain that keeps 8 stripes of 16, which is half the file, and at part grain it keeps 30 parts of 974, which is three per cent. Counting the rows under that cutoff goes from 100.4M instructions to 26.5M under callgrind, and with the floor taken off both sides the scan work goes from 84.6M to 10.4M, which is 8.1 times and is the number the granularity predicted. The ends were already being computed, since the writer built a range per part to hand to the sieve and then merged them into the stripe and threw the detail away, so the cost is the bytes and not the walk: the file grows 1.32 per cent and the load 1.4 per cent. It is stored a page per column per stripe next to the sieve pages and read the same lazy way, holding the two ends and the null count but not the exact flag or the total, because those two answer a `MIN` or a `SUM` out of the directory where the same number costs sixty four times less to keep, and what a part range is for is skipping the part. A stripe of a single part writes no page. q39, which is the worst query in the suite against DuckDB, is 1.14 times faster for free. #1029.

A one argument string function reads its argument out of a file rather than out of a buffer. It had two ways to read, a slice of `Vector::data` indexed directly or a dictionary's codes indexing the values' `data`, and a column read out of a native table is neither, so `data` answered `None` and every string function fell through to the row at a time path, which builds a boxed `String` per row out of the page the reader just decoded, hands it to the general dispatch and copies the answer back. `LENGTH` was the exception because somebody hit this before and wrote a twenty five line special case for it, which fixed the one function rather than the shape. There is now a `Text` enum saying where a string kernel reads from, either a column and an index mapping or the vector answering for a row itself, and the special case is gone because it is what the general path does. On 99,997,497 rows `COUNT(LOWER(Referer))` goes from 1.91 s to 1.44 and `COUNT(UPPER(Referer))` from 1.95 to 1.58, with no pass of the slower binary beating any pass of the faster one over five interleaved reps, while `LENGTH` and `STRLEN`, which already had the special case, do not move. This is half of #1019 and the cheap half. The three gigabytes that query holds is the point reads rather than the `Value` path, and we are still behind DuckDB on `lower`, but the reason is no longer the fallback. #1030.

A decorrelated existence test that never got a domain collapses to a semi join. Where the correlated column is compared for equality against a column of the subquery's own relation, the binder groups that relation rather than inventing a domain, and the Deliminator did not recognise the shape that comes out, so the grouping stayed. TPC-H q4 is the case, with an aggregate taking 3,793,296 rows in and 1,375,365 out for no reason anybody reads. It collapses now and the grouping goes away, which is how DuckDB answers q4. #1026.

A stored minimum and maximum say what domain they are in. A timestamp column is an `INT64` of microseconds and a decimal column is an `INT64` with a power of ten under it, and the walk that finds the two ends of a column saw the integer and nothing else, so the ends came out as a bare `Bound::Int` while the constant a query compares them against arrives as a `Bound::Scaled` carrying its own power. `Bound::order` has no arm pairing those two, so it answered `None`, and `None` is the answer that keeps every row, which means every zone map on a `TIMESTAMP`, a `TIME`, a zoned one of either, a `DECIMAL` or one of the three sized timestamps was built, stored, read and then thrown away. This is a missed skip and never a wrong answer, which is why nothing caught it. The scale table moves from `rudb-parquet`, which already got this right, into `rudb-common` next to the bounds it is about, and both stores call the one copy. A format 20 file holds a bare integer there and that integer is the column's own, so putting the power back on read means an old file prunes as well as a new one and no file needs a rewrite. On the million row `hits` sample the two timestamp range queries go from 1.011 ms to 0.589 and from 0.750 to 0.504, and the three queries on integer columns do not move. #1022.

An unnesting rule stands aside when the subquery's own relation reads the outer row. Each of these rules reads the correlation out of exactly one place, a filter's predicate or a projection's expressions, and builds the domain over the outer columns it finds there, so a column of the outer row read anywhere else is invisible to it. The commonest way to write one is a second subquery nested inside this one whose name resolved past this query to the one above it, which the binder is right to hand up to the query that owns it, leaving the query in between uncorrelated by that name and planting its own relation with the reference still sitting in it. The rule then puts that relation under a join with the domain, where the outer side is not in scope, and the column is asked of an operator that was never given it. Six rules stand aside now and let the general domain rule take the query, since that one collects every outer column the whole subtree reads and pushes a domain down to wherever each is read. The cost is honest: the general rule's plan is heavier, and the one shape that used to answer did so only because a later pass happened to delete the column it read. Eight hand written shapes are byte for byte identical to duckdb v2.0.0-dev84237, six of which were internal errors before, and over the 86 upstream subquery files answered goes from 401 to 405 with internal errors from 9 to 5 and nothing else moving. Closes #999. #1025.

A scalar subquery whose body is a grouped count answers NULL for an outer row that matches no inner row, where it used to answer zero. A `GROUP BY` over an empty input produces no groups at all, so the subquery returns no row, and a scalar subquery that returns no row is NULL. The zero came from the domain left join, which keeps one padded row for a key nothing matched, and that row is there because an ungrouped aggregate over an empty input is still one row and a count over that row is zero. With a group there is nothing to pad, and worse, the group expressions are rewritten to read the domain, so a group written on an outer column takes the outer value even on a padded row and the invented group looks exactly like one the inner rows produced. Two rules had it, and both now recognise the grouped case, one by standing aside and one by joining the domain inner rather than left. An ungrouped count is untouched and still answers zero, with a test holding that line, because the two cases look alike and only one of them wants the padded row. Closes #1013. #1023.

## 0.3.63

A patch release of five pull requests, in the join, the scan, the Parquet reader, the optimizer and the binder. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 20.

An outer join runs from the gathered end. TPC-H q13 is `customer LEFT JOIN orders`, where the kept side is 150,000 rows and the other side is 1.5 million, and the pass that chooses which side to gather refused to gather the side an outer join keeps. That made customer the driving side, which made the pipeline's degree what 150,000 rows are worth, which on a ten thread machine is two, and the 1.5 million probes ran on those two while the other eight sat idle. The reason for the refusal is real but only half of it is true. A join that keeps one side's rows whatever they match cannot gather that side and stay a stream, because a gathered row nothing has matched yet may still be matched later. That argument covers the padded row owed to a gathered row that matched nothing. It does not cover the pairs, which are known the moment a driving row arrives exactly as they are for any probe, and on q13 the pairs are 1.5 million rows against about fifty thousand padded ones, so ninety seven per cent of the answer could always have streamed. So the operator is a stream with a tail. An operator says whether it owes anything once every instance has finished reading, and a driver told no by every operator does no work and allocates nothing, which is every pipeline today but this one. #1015.

`MIN` and `MAX` are answered out of the Parquet footer when the bounds are exact values. A writer records the smallest and the largest value of every column of every row group, and the footer has already been read by the time anything plans the query, so `SELECT MIN(EventDate), MAX(EventDate) FROM hits` is a fold over a few thousand values the planner is holding rather than a hundred million off disk. What makes it safe is that a writer is allowed to shorten a long string bound as long as it moves it outward, which keeps every skip the pruner makes correct and is exactly what stops the bound from being an answer, so `MIN(URL)` off a shortened minimum is a string the column does not hold. Parquet states this in `is_min_value_exact` and `is_max_value_exact`, most writers state neither, and where nothing is stated the physical type decides it, because shortening is defined for byte strings and for nothing else. Floats are refused even where the writer called the bounds exact, since the format keeps NaN out of the bounds and this engine sorts NaN above every number. The pass is named `statistics_propagation` after the one DuckDB has, so `SET disabled_optimizers` means the same thing on both. ClickBench q7 over the full 14.8 GB file on server3, five runs, is 0.11 s on the median against 0.61 with the pass off and 0.37 for DuckDB, which does have this pass and does not use it here because that file states neither flag. `MIN` and `MAX` of all 105 ClickBench columns and all 61 TPC-H SF1 columns are identical to DuckDB. #1007.

A scan applies the filter above it. A zone map that can prove a chunk holds nothing the filter wants can also prove a chunk holds nothing the filter would throw away, and pruning used the first half of that while nothing used the second, because acting on it needs the zone and the predicate in front of the same operator and a filter above the scan has only the predicate. A predicate whose every conjunct reads as a comparison of one of the scan's own columns against a constant is handed to the scan now and no filter operator is built above it, which is what DuckDB calls the table filters of a scan, and the scan makes a three way decision per chunk: skip the chunk, skip the comparison, or run it. It is all of the filter or none of it, because a scan applying some of one with nothing above it applying the rest is rows that should have gone and did not. Twenty million rows on one thread, `count(*) WHERE v >= 10` goes from 1.58 ns a row to 0.25 against DuckDB's 0.40, `sum(v)` with the same filter from 1.86 to 0.99 against 1.00, and the no filter control is unchanged at 1.58. A filter the scan applied has no operator, so `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` printed `[not measured]` on its line, which reads like a measurement that went missing, and it now says `[applied by the scan below]`. #1014.

A subquery that aggregates and also reads a column of the query above it has an answer, where it used to come back with `column a column must appear in the GROUP BY clause`. That one sentence was wrong twice. The grouping rule is about columns of the subquery's own `FROM`, and a name that resolved past it to an enclosing query is not one of those, because the subquery runs once per outer row and such a name is one value for the whole of it. The column name was missing from the message because the lookup that fills that slot only searched the subquery's own scope, where an outer column is not. The other half is four unnesting rules that move a projection, or a whole subtree, onto the right side of an ordinary join, where a column of the left side is not in scope. Two of them stand aside now and let the general domain rule take the query, one carries the outer columns as domain keys and points the projection at them, and the fourth is the rule for a correlated filter, which is the one that hid, because a `HAVING` binds as a filter above the subquery's aggregate and the correlated filter the query really has sits two nodes further down where the guard was not looking. Fourteen shapes are byte for byte identical to duckdb v2.0.0-dev84237, and over the 86 upstream subquery files answered goes from 398 to 401 with no query that answered before now erroring. Closes #995. #1012.

A dictionary sweep reads its offsets a run at a time. Asking where every value starts and ends one value at a time shifts a sixteen byte window to the bit the offset starts at, and the copy that fills that window is a length the compiler does not know, so it is a call to `memcpy` rather than a load, and the loop asked for the same offset twice because the start of a value is the end of the one before it. The offsets of a whole run are unpacked in one call now, which makes the window a fixed sixteen bytes and therefore a real unaligned load, so a block of 1,024 values costs two calls here and nothing per value. Everything that walks a string dictionary goes through this, and on 99,997,497 rows `URL LIKE '%google%'` goes from 0.59 s to 0.53 while equality, which binary searches the sorted order and never sweeps, is unchanged at 0.08. That is about a tenth of the sweep, which says the offsets were a tenth of it and the payload decompression is the other nine tenths, so the next win on this path is not a faster loop, it is not running one. #1010.

## 0.3.62

A patch release of fifteen pull requests, spread across the optimizer, the binder, the join, the aggregate, the scan and the storage. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 20.

A semi join can now be turned around when its subject is the smaller of the two sides. A semi join produces its subject side's rows, so the operator answering it has to gather the other side, and on TPC-H q21 that means gathering the whole six million row lineitem in order to answer about seventy five thousand questions. There is no mirrored kind to swap the inputs into, because a semi join's left input is the subject rather than a side, so this adds the operator that runs the join from the other end instead. It gathers the subject, builds the table on it, lets the other side stream past setting a bit against every gathered row it matched, and when the driving side is finished the marked rows are the semi join's answer and the unmarked ones are the anti join's. It holds a probe and calls into it, so the table, the lookup and the residual are written once. It has to be a sink rather than a stream, because a bit that is clear now may still be set by a driving row that has not arrived, which is the argument the streamed list already gives for RIGHT and FULL, and sinks gained the prepare hook streams got in 0.3.5x for the same reason it exists there. The other half is that the pass choosing which side to gather asked whether every condition of the join is an equality, when the executor's hash join needs only one and evaluates whatever is left as a residual on the candidates the equality found. q21's joins are written `ON l_orderkey = o_orderkey AND l_suppkey <> o_suppkey`, so both were read as nested loops and handed the side a nested loop wants, which is the larger one. That was not a missed rewrite, it was the wrong one applied on purpose. No new join kind, since which side is gathered is an execution decision and not part of what the plan means. Measured at SF1 on Parquet with each query in its own process and the order of the two binaries alternated, q21 goes from 0.4211 s to 0.1272 s at 3.31 times, confirmed at 3.26 on the minimum and 3.07 on the median over fourteen further reps of that query alone, and the suite goes from 2.4227 s to 2.1831. q20's semi join turns around too and is two thousand rows, so it moves neither way, and no other plan changes. #1005.

A join hashes a dictionary column through its codes rather than through a `Value` per row. Every arm of the hashing pass was written against a flat run of values, so a dictionary column, which is what the Parquet reader hands back for any column its writer found worth coding, fell through to the row at a time path at the bottom. The type arms read through an index now, with a closure saying which index of the data a row reads, so a flat column picks the row itself and a dictionary or a run length column picks the code, and the eleven arms are written once. Reading through the codes rather than hashing the dictionary once and gathering is deliberate, since a Parquet dictionary covers a whole column chunk and can hold far more values than the thousand rows being hashed. The other half is a copy: composing a dictionary built over another dictionary took the leaf out of its `Arc`, which copies the whole leaf whenever anybody else holds it, and somebody always does, because the page holds the dictionary and every chunk cut from it composed through it. The TPC-H suite goes from 2.0998 s to 1.9501 at 1.077 times with twenty one of twenty two faster, q11 at 1.29, q7 at 1.23, q5 at 1.20 and q21 at 1.16. #990.

A sink can say how many threads it finishes on. A pipeline borrowed as many threads as its source had morsels, and the sink's finish runs after every one of those instances has joined, on those same threads, so on a thirty two thread machine a million row scan cutting sixteen morsels left a hash aggregate merging a million groups on sixteen threads with half the machine parked. The pipeline now borrows the larger of what the source wants and what the sink says it can finish on, and the instance count stays the smaller of the two. The grouped aggregate is the one sink that answers with more than one, since its finish is a merge of radix partitions and nothing a partition holds depends on any other, so the partition count went from sixteen to sixty four. The instance count deliberately does not move, because a probe that doubled it made one query faster and two others burn nearly twice the CPU for no wall gain. #1000.

A whole table aggregate over a table in memory is answered from its zone maps. A table in memory builds a zone map per chunk as it arrives, holding an exact null count for every column, the two ends, and the total when the column is integers, and a native file has answered `COUNT`, `MIN`, `MAX`, `SUM` and `AVG` out of its directory since the format was written. The in memory table answered nothing, and the builder turned away anything that was not a file, so the whole apparatus was there and switched off for the tables most queries actually run against. The decision is still one decision in one place: the question goes to the rows, a table that cannot answer says so, and the query reads them. What only a file answers is the two that need a persisted synopsis, the distinct count and the frequencies. #989.

The optimizer plans from one generation of the catalog rather than rebuilding its counts per statement. It used to be handed a fresh map of row counts and distinct counts for every statement, built by walking every table in the catalog and asking every column of every table for a distinct count, whether or not the query named any of them, so the cost was the size of the catalog rather than the size of the query and `SELECT 1` paid it in full. The catalog counts its own versions now, moving the count on for anything that could have written rather than only for what really did, which is the safe direction to be wrong in, and a statement that finds its version still current plans from the set the last statement built. #982.

A subquery in an inner join's `ON` that reads rows from both sides of that join has a plan. A query written in an `ON` goes into whichever input produces the rows it reads, and the one with no answer there is the query that reads both, since it has to be evaluated once per pair and no input produces a pair. What produces a pair is the join, so for an inner join the join becomes a product, the query is joined into the product's rows, and the condition becomes a filter above that. The product is not the plan that runs, because filter pushdown turns a filter over an inner join back into a join condition, so the part of the `ON` that reads one column from each side goes back down. It is the same query for an inner join and for no other kind, since every other kind does something with the pairs it dropped and a filter above a product has already thrown away which left row a dropped pair came from. Twelve shapes checked against duckdb v2.0.0-dev84237, byte for byte identical including row order. #1001.

The rule that turns a correlated filter under a scalar projection into an ordinary join stands aside when the projection reads the outer row. It seeds its output map from the projection's column expressions, so an outer column the projection reads was recorded as a projected output, the correlated condition's reference to it was rewritten against that map, and the join condition came out comparing the right side with itself. The other half is that the projection is about to become the right side of an ordinary join, where a column of the left side is not in scope, and it still reads one. Nothing that used to get a plan loses one, because the guard fires exactly where the plan it produced could not run. Closes #993. #998.

A `LIKE` over the ClickBench `URL` column used to leave 4.2 GB resident and now leaves 159 MB, and it got faster doing it. The native text payload is read and decoded a block at a time and was kept after that, which is right for a point read that will be asked again and wrong for a walk, and the stable dictionary memo is a walk: it decides all 1,024 values of a block in one go and never reads any of them twice. #997. That first cut kept none of it, which moved the cost from once per session to once per statement and took four ClickBench queries from beating DuckDB to losing to it, a `LIKE` going from 2.75 ms to 16.2 at a million rows. What the sweep keeps is bounded now rather than nothing, and those four queries come back to where they were before either change. #1002.

An ungrouped min over a sorted dictionary holds a rank rather than the string. The grouped one already held a dictionary code and its rank and read the payload once per group at the end, and the ungrouped one reduced each vector to the winning row and then read the string there so it had something to hold, which on a dictionary that lives in a file decodes the whole block the value sits in. Ninety seven thousand vectors is ninety seven thousand of those reads. It only shows under a filter, which is why it sat there: with no filter there is a whole dictionary fast path, and with a filter every row passes so every vector wins on the same value and one block stays resident. Selectivity turned out not to be the variable at all. A filter keeping 81,696,926 rows and one keeping 81,032,736 measured 0.26 s against 0.78, and what separates them is whether the empty string survives, because it is the smallest value in that dictionary and a min that keeps winning on it only ever reads one block. #1004.

A packed unit is unpacked straight into row order in one pass. Every scan of a packed integer column went through a transposed unpack and then an untranspose, which is a 1,024 value buffer to hold the middle, a second read of all of it and a scatter. A row has the same bit schedule in every lane, which is the point of the FastLanes layout, so which word to read and how far to shift it are decided once for the row and what is left for the lanes is a load, a shift, an or, a mask and a store with no branch. Where a row lands is the permutation the untranspose was applying, and since the lane index is the low part of it, it comes out as a base address for the row and costs nothing. It is the largest line in the ClickBench `LIKE` profile because the text payload block is LZ over FSST and the LZ layer keeps three token arrays of about 8,700 entries per 1,024 value block, so a block holding 1,024 URLs unpacks about 26,000 packed integers before it decompresses anything. #991.

A matched string chunk's literals are decompressed where they belong. Such a chunk keeps the bytes no copy covered as a chunk of its own, and on the ClickBench `URL` column the chooser encodes that nested chunk as FSST, so a block of a thousand values comes out as about eight thousand seven hundred literal runs. Decoding the block meant decompressing the whole nested chunk into a buffer, building an eight thousand seven hundred entry length array to cut the buffer up, and copying every run out of it, so every literal byte was written twice and both the buffer and its length array were allocated and freed per block. The replay asks for a literal run through a callback that appends to the output now rather than handing back a slice, so when the nested chunk is compressed the callback is a decompress of a subslice straight into the output, and when it is anything else the literals are decoded first the way they always were. #1006.

A cascaded integer page's range is checked once instead of once a value. The page decodes to `i64` and is read back into whatever width its column is declared at, and that step went through a fallible conversion a value at a time collected into a result, which is a compare, a branch and a short circuit per value, and the branch decides whether the loop keeps going so it is a loop no compiler widens. It is two loops now: the first compares the page's two ends against the range, which is two instructions a lane, and the second narrows with no branch and is only correct because the first has run. Between 1.4 and 3.8 per cent of the instructions of four ClickBench queries. #1003.

`EXPLAIN (STATISTICS)` prints which use every number in a plan was read for. Answer means the statistic is the result, enable means it licenses a rewrite that would be wrong if the number were wrong, and decide means it chooses between two plans that produce the same rows. The consumer declares its use and the class rule follows from the use, and the first two halves of that were already here while the third was not, so nothing this engine printed said what any number had been read for. Every line of the tree gains the use on the end of the bracket that already carries the class and the provenance, and a statistics section under the plan counts the reads by use and by class, naming the uses that did not happen rather than leaving them out, because the line saying nothing was read to enable is the reassuring half of the section. #987.

Incremental compilation is off for the gate's remote build. Every run rsyncs a tree in, and the files it changed are the ones the incremental cache would have to throw away, so what the cache does instead is grow. It cost two gate runs, one to a bus error and one to a full disk, neither of them anything to do with the code being gated, and of the 23G under one gate tree there 5.6G was incremental state. #996.

## 0.3.61

A patch release of four pull requests, one in the binder and the optimizer together, one in the top N operator and two in the storage. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 20.

A correlated column read more than one level below the query it belongs to never reached the query that had to carry it. The binder records a correlation into the frame of the query being bound, and the call that takes that frame back handed the whole of it to the caller as what the query read, so a column belonging two frames out was written into the innermost frame, came back as a read of the inner query, and was dropped there. The query that had to feed it down then looked uncorrelated and was planned as one. It is handed up to the frame it belongs to now, the way a lateral entry has always handed its own leftovers up. The query in between stays uncorrelated, which is right, since it reads nothing of the outer row itself and the dependent join the outer query gets pushes the domain down through it to wherever the column is read.

That alone turned the failure into a refusal rather than an answer, because the domain then had nowhere to go. The rule that pushes a domain through a join left out the single join, and the reason it gave was the malformed plan a subquery in a join condition used to produce, which 0.3.60 removed. A single join preserves its left side the way a left join does, and the insistence that the right side hand back at most one row is about the rows rather than about which of them survive, so it carries the domain the same way. Nine shapes were checked against the pin and agree exactly, from an `EXISTS` inside an `EXISTS` to a correlation three levels deep. Over the four upstream files that exercise this, 132 queries, the internal error is now 0, down from 64 before 0.3.60 and 5 after it. #980.

The top N operator rejects a chunk with one comparison of the first key column against the worst candidate it holds, and that comparison went through the kernel that reads its operands through a selection, with an identity selection built on the spot. Building the identity allocates and fills a position per row to say all of them, the preparation walks it again to range check it, the comparison reads each row through the indirection, and the narrowing pass reads the array once more. None of it is needed when the answer is every row, and the kernel that reads operands where they lie already exists. `SELECT EventTime FROM hits ORDER BY EventTime LIMIT 10` over ClickBench at 1M rows on one thread falls from 143,508,554 to 112,512,177 instructions, 21.6 per cent fewer, and the comparison machinery goes from 54.2M to 24.1M. All 43 ClickBench queries were run on both builds and the 38 deterministic ones come back byte for byte identical. #983.

A read from an in memory table cloned the columns of the chunk it was asked for, which was a malloc and a memcpy of every value. The reason was honest when it was written, since a vector owned its buffer and there was nothing to borrow from, and the shared buffer that became a window into a page removed it. An append stores every chunk as pages and a read hands out the stored vectors, so a read costs one atomic increment per column. Nothing above the storage seam changes and nothing downstream can tell, because a vector has no mutating method and the only way at the values copies the page out first. Only a flat body changes, since every other form already shares what is expensive: a dictionary shares its values, a packed body its words, a string body its arena, an FSST body its codes and its table. On one thread over twenty million rows, `count(v)` falls 42 per cent, `sum(v)` 11 per cent, a filtered count 10 per cent and `count(*)` 9 per cent. Closes #972. #985.

Four of the five most expensive ClickBench queries are string queries, and the worst of them was spending 2.74 billion instructions to find 149 rows out of a million. The substring search is 2 per cent of that and the rest is getting the column into a shape the search can read. The single worst line was the call that builds a `Vec<u8>` for every symbol the FSST decompressor decodes, which is a heap allocation, a shift loop, a copy and a free to move at most eight bytes that were already sitting in a `u64`. A symbol goes out as all eight of the bytes its word holds now, with the cursor stepping back over the ones that were not part of it, because eight is a length the compiler knows and is therefore one store, while copying the length a symbol really has is a call for one to eight bytes. The second change is in the native reader, where a page of dictionary codes was decoded as `i64` and narrowed to `u32` through a fallible conversion per code, which is the short circuit that stops the loop vectorizing and about twelve instructions a row to narrow a number that already fits. The codes are converted in one pass and checked once at the end by or-ing them together. At 1M rows on one thread the `LIKE '%google%'` count falls 23.4 per cent, the one that also sorts and limits 22.5 per cent, the `REGEXP_REPLACE` one 18.2 per cent and the two sided `LIKE` 14.4 per cent, which is 2.42 billion instructions off four queries and about 9 per cent of what the whole 43 query suite executes. All 43 were run on both builds and the 33 deterministic ones come back byte for byte identical. #986.

## 0.3.60

A patch release of seven pull requests, across the optimizer, the binder, the scan, the join and the aggregate. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format goes from 19 to 20.

A Parquet footer states the smallest and the largest value of every column of every row group, and 0.3.58 taught the estimator to read those two ends and say where in that stretch the constant falls. It could only do it for a column whose two ends are a plain integer or a float. A decimal is a scaled integer, a time or a timestamp is a count of seconds at whichever of 0, 3, 6 or 9 its unit is, and the two sides of one comparison can disagree about that power of ten, so none of those columns produced a test at all, nothing reached the footer, and the estimate fell back to the fifth that every unreadable condition gets. That was the whole of the gap 0.3.58 left: of the sixteen range predicates in TPC-H SF1, the five it could not improve were the five decimals.

A bound now holds the integer and the power of ten together, one shape serving DECIMAL, TIME and TIMESTAMP rather than three, since a temporal constant is a microsecond count and a temporal column is a count at a known unit, which is the same question with the scale already fixed. Both sides of a comparison are restated at the finer of their two scales, which is upwards for at least one of them and keeps every digit either of them had. Upwards is a multiplication that either fits or does not. Downwards is only exact when the digits being dropped are zeroes, and a bound that has to round is no bound, because rounding a minimum up or a maximum down would rule out a stretch holding rows the query wants, so an inexact restatement is refused and the stretch is kept. Reading the footer is the other half, and a wide decimal arrives there as two's complement big endian bytes of whatever length the writer needed, which is the one place in the format the bytes run the other way round.

Over the sixteen range predicates the geometric mean q-error goes from 1.58 to 1.01, five better and none worse, and the five are every decimal in the set. `l_discount BETWEEN 0.05 AND 0.07` goes from 6.82 to 1.00, `c_acctbal > 0.00` from 4.54 to 1.00, `ps_supplycost > 100` from 4.51 to 1.00, `s_acctbal > 1000` from 4.10 to 1.00 and `l_quantity < 24` from 2.30 to 1.02. All sixteen now read from the footer rather than from the constant, and taken with 0.3.58 the same set goes from 3.10 to 1.01. End to end the suite is flat at 2,899 ms against 2,894 over eleven repetitions with nothing moving further than the repetitions themselves span, which is the expected shape: these numbers feed join order, TPC-H already had its join orders right, and what this buys is the estimate being right for the plans where it is not already. #962.

The native directory format goes to 20 because a saved bound has a fourth tag in it now and an older reader would take the new tag for something else. A mismatch is a hard read error, which is what that version is for.

One thing fell out of the test fixture this needed. A Parquet column written as TIMESTAMP(MILLIS) or TIMESTAMP(NANOS) is read here as if the integer under it were microseconds, so every value is a thousand times off and a filter over one answers rows it should not. It predates this work and is filed as #959.

A join condition written as a subquery is attached to the side that produces its rows rather than above the join. `bind_select` binds the `FROM` and then joins each scalar subquery it collected into the rows the whole `FROM` produced, which is right for a query written in a `WHERE` or a `SELECT` and wrong for one written in an `ON`. A join condition is evaluated by the join itself over the rows its two inputs handed it, so a column produced above the join is a column the join was never given, and every shape of this failed with an internal error about a column not being in the schema. Which input a subquery goes to is decided by what its body reads, and for a mark join the comparison counts as a read too, because the comparison is carried by the join rather than by the condition. A query that reads both sides has no answer here and is now a refusal that names what was written instead of an error out of the executor, with #913 still open for the dependent join that would answer it. Checked against duckdb v2.0.0-dev84237 over a thirteen query battery covering both outer joins, semi, anti, `IN` over either side, `NOT IN` and `= ANY`, agreeing on all nineteen statements. #968.

String inequalities are answered out of the dictionary's sorted order. The equality path has resolved a literal to a code and compared integers since the native format started writing that order, and the four orderings never got it, so `WHERE url < 'x'` and `ORDER BY phrase LIMIT 10` fell through to a loop that fetched each row's bytes and called `cmp`, which on a dictionary column is a walk to wherever the value lives followed by a byte compare, one row at a time. One lower bound search puts the literal at a rank boundary and the comparison becomes one `usize` against another, with no value read and no bytes compared. The search answers how many values sort before the literal and whether one of them equals it, and those two numbers answer all four comparisons between them. The loop bails to the old path unless the column is a shared dictionary that both knows its own order and will hand it back inverted, so every in memory column is unchanged. #966.

A `LIKE` against a table wide dictionary remembers its answers in two bits a value rather than a byte. It has been answered once per distinct value since #288, and the memo holding those answers was a byte array of 18.3 million entries on ClickBench `URL`, probed a hundred million times, which at eighteen megabytes across a dozen threads does not stay in the last level cache. It is now the decided bit and the matched bit packed thirty two values to a word, which is 4.6 MB rather than eighteen and one load rather than two at two random positions. A chunk big enough to be a scan decides a whole group of 1,024 values at once, which is what the native format puts in a payload block, so deciding a group walks consecutive dictionary values rather than scattering over a gigabyte of them. Two threads deciding the same group is benign, since they walk the same values and reach the same answer and neither can clear a bit the other set, and a decision is one `fetch_or` carrying both bits so a reader that sees the decided bit is looking at the answer written with it. #970.

A join's residual is evaluated a batch of pairs at a time rather than once per driving row. The condition is split into the equalities a hash table answers and whatever is left, and the left over part used to take one driving row, gather its candidates, repeat the row across them as constants, walk the expression tree and read the answer back, all of it a fixed cost paid per driving row over vectors as long as one key's candidate list. On TPC-H q21 that was a whole expression tree walked over a four row chunk 75,871 times for the semi join and 73,089 for the anti join, and a sample profile was dominated by malloc, free and madvise. The pairs of many driving rows are now collected first and the residual is asked about a full batch, capped at sixteen vectors so that a key a million rows share does not become a burst of memory, and never splitting one driving row across two batches. The two residual joins on q21 go from 335 ms and 365 ms of summed CPU to 203 and 233, the query from 1.264 s of CPU to 1.047, and the suite from 2.3237 s to 2.2565, with q3 at 1.18x and nothing outside noise the other way. #974.

An aggregate's instances merge their tables in a tree rather than a queue. An instance that finishes folding gives its table up into partition zero, whole if nothing is there and merged into what is there if something is, and that merge ran holding both the `built` lock and partition zero, so sixteen instances merged one at a time and the last waited for the fifteen before it. The merge now runs with both locks dropped: an instance that finds partition zero empty leaves its table and goes, and one that finds a table takes it out, merges the pair on its own thread and starts again. The race the held lock was deciding is still decided, because the flag is read under `built` and the table is taken under it, which is the lock order `begin_partitioning` already kept, and an instance that was merging while somebody turned partitioning on sees the flag next time round and hands the merged table over. A tree copies more than a queue, and what pays for it is depth being a logarithm instead of a count, with the cost of one merge bounded by the group count rather than the level. ClickBench 11 groups down to 143 groups so it never partitions and all sixteen instances arrive holding a table, and it goes from 3.510 ms to 2.224. #975.

A note on what a chunk costs and why a scan copies one, measured on server2 against duckdb v2.0.0-dev84237 on the same machine and data, one twenty million row table of two `BIGINT` columns, one thread, best of four. rudb's inner loops are competitive, what it loses on is everything that happens once per chunk, it pays that twice as often because its chunk is half the size, and its chunk is also its unit of storage so every chunk of every scan costs an allocation and a copy that DuckDB does not pay. A chunk costs about seven hundred nanoseconds to push through the pipeline before anything has looked at a value, which at 1,024 rows is the whole of what `count(*)` over twenty million rows costs. Four binaries differing only in `VECTOR_SIZE` put `sum(v)` at 3.43, 2.49, 1.76 and 1.84 ns per row for 1024, 2048, 8192 and 32768 against DuckDB's 1.75, so one constant takes rudb from 1.96 times behind to level, and the curve turns back up at 32768 exactly where a copied vector stops fitting in a 512 KiB L2. Callgrind says where a chunk's instructions go: about 3,800 on `count(*)` with 40 percent of them the metrics shim and its clocks, and about 13,700 on `sum(v)` with 3,672 of those malloc, free and memcpy that exist because of the data model. #969.

## 0.3.59

A patch release of two pull requests, one in the instrumentation and one in the optimizer. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

The shim around every operator read two clocks per chunk, and one of them was `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID`, which has no vDSO entry on any Linux this runs on, so every reading of it was a real system call. A source and a sink came to four per chunk of 1,024 rows, which is more than a thousand rows of counting costs, and a `count(*)` over twenty million rows in memory spent most of its time finding out how long it was taking. The thread clock now goes on only when somebody asked to read a CPU number per operator, which is `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` and `PRAGMA enable_profiling`. The wall clock stays on for every query, because `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` does come out of the vDSO and a reading is about twenty nanoseconds. On eight cores `count(*)` goes from 0.0587 s to 0.0116 and a filtered count from 0.0780 to 0.0145, which moves that one from 2.8 times behind DuckDB to 1.9 times ahead. The spans around the whole execution and around each pipeline are taken once per pipeline rather than once per chunk, so those are unaffected and `resource.cpu_ns` is still exact.

Filter pushdown now derives, from a disjunction, what it says about each table on its own. It already pulled out the conjuncts every branch of an `OR` states identically, and real branches rarely agree word for word, so the rule found nothing in most queries. The weaker reading is the useful one: take the atoms each branch states about one table, conjoin them within the branch, disjoin that across the branches, and keep only the tables every branch restricts. A branch that never mentions a table lets every value of it through, and then the disjunction implies nothing about it. This is the derivation DuckDB does in `GenerateORFilters`, and it happens inside the pass already named `filter_pushdown`.

TPC-H q7 is the query that needed it. Two copies of nation under `(n1.n_name = 'FRANCE' AND n2.n_name = 'GERMANY') OR (n1.n_name = 'GERMANY' AND n2.n_name = 'FRANCE')` share nothing between the branches, so the whole predicate waited above a join of lineitem to orders and the plan carried 1,828,450 rows up to a filter that kept 5,924. Each copy of nation now goes from 25 rows to 2 before anything joins to it, and supplier and customer shrink behind them. At SF1 on ten threads q7 is 2.41 times faster and goes from 7.85 times behind DuckDB to 2.43, and q19 comes along for free at 1.76 times and is now close to parity, because its three branches each restrict part and lineitem and none of them agree. The rule applies only when the disjunction reads more than one table, since over a single table the derived predicate is a weaker copy of one that was already going to the same place.

## 0.3.58

A patch release of two pull requests, one in the optimizer and one in the join. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

A range filter used to be worth a fifth of the rows, which is the constant every condition nobody can read gets. The Parquet footer already says the smallest and the largest value of the column in each row group, so where the constant falls inside that stretch is a much better guess than a fifth, and `Zones` now answers that as a second question beside the one that proves a group holds nothing. The first is a ceiling and is never wrong, the second is a guess that can be wrong in either direction, so a caller caps with one and multiplies with the other.

A mark join is answered by a lookup now rather than by the row major operator. It had been on the list of kinds a lookup cannot answer, because its answer is about the whole gathered side rather than about one driving row, and the one fact it needs about that side is whether the side holds a null key. That turns a miss from false into null, and the gathered side is finished before the first driving row arrives, so it is settled once while the table is built and read as a flag after that. TPC-H q16 is the only query in the suite with a mark join in it and it was both the worst query against DuckDB and the least parallel one. Its join goes from 92.7ms to 2.7ms and the query from 144.3ms to 33.5ms.

## 0.3.57

A patch release of one pull request in the join, which takes the last serial part of a hash join and spreads it over every thread the query already has. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

- The hash table a join finds its gathered rows in is built in partitions, one per thread, split by the top bits of the key's hash. #954. The build was the last part of a hash join running on one core, and after #952 handed it the threads up front it was the thing the other nine were waiting for: on q21 it was 78 percent of the query's serial time with the workers asleep for most of it. A key lands in exactly one partition, so no two threads ever touch one bucket, one stored key or one chain, and the chain array all of them write is safe because an entry belongs to a gathered row and a row belongs to one partition. The split is on the top bits because the bottom ones are the bucket number inside a partition's own table. Below 64k rows nothing is split, because on a join against `nation` the partitioning costs more than the build. The order a chain comes out in is unchanged, so a join's output is the same rows in the same order.
