At SF1 on ten threads q8 is 1.64x, q4 1.54x, q21 1.53x, q9 1.39x and the suite is 1.20x, with the queries that have no join in them flat as the control.

## 0.3.56

A patch release of two pull requests, one in the optimizer and one in the driver, both of them replacing something that was written when nothing better was available. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

- An equality filter's selectivity comes from the column's distinct count rather than from a constant. An equality against a constant kept a fifth of its input whatever column it was on, which is the Selinger constant from 1979 and is what you use when you know nothing. 0.3.50 taught the Parquet reader to read the distinct count out of the footer for the join arithmetic and the same number answers this question directly, so a column with two hundred values in it keeps one in two hundred. Over twenty equality filters on the SF1 files the geometric mean q-error goes from 13.35 to 1.95, thirteen better, two worse and five unchanged. `ps_suppkey = 100` goes from two thousand times too many to exact and `o_clerk` from two hundred times too many to two percent. The two that got worse are line numbers and return flags, where the values are skewed and the uniformity assumption is simply wrong, and the constant happened to sit closer. Fixing those needs a frequency synopsis, which is separate work.
- A hash join builds its table before the pipeline hands its instances out rather than inside whichever instance asks for it first. The table is shared by every instance and it sat behind a lock the others blocked on, so one thread built it and the rest of the lease slept. On q9 at ten threads that was 5169 of 11254 worker samples in `semaphore_wait_trap`, 46 percent of all worker thread time, and q9's top pipeline has five probes in it so each worker paid five separate rounds of sleeping and waking. A stream can now say what it needs doing once before any instance runs and it is handed the threads the pipeline has already leased, so the join builds there and the gathered side is laid out a column per thread. The suite goes from 3.35 s to 3.08 and q7 is 1.31x, q2 1.15x, q18 1.12x and q3 1.12x. The profile is the evidence that it is sleeping going away rather than work: twelve call tree sites in `semaphore_wait_trap` summing 27,036 samples become none, and `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` puts q9's two large joins at 996 ms and 769 ms before against 145 ms and 164 ms after. The table itself is still built on one thread, because two rows of one key have to reach their chain in the order they arrived.

## 0.3.55

A patch release of four pull requests, two of them execution work found by profiling one query at a time and two of them housekeeping. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

- Two dictionary columns are compared with the loop that was already there. `l_commitdate < l_receiptdate` and `l_shipdate < l_commitdate` are two dictionary encoded date columns of the same table over six million rows, and every one of those rows was going through the row at a time path on a pair of freshly allocated values, 29,305 kernel calls over the suite. The loop existed and could not be reached: the dispatcher takes an index closure per side precisely so that one body serves flat against flat, a column against a constant and a dictionary against a constant, and the branch calling it asked both sides for their run of data, which a dictionary does not have. A dictionary is the flat loop with `codes[n]` where the flat loop has `n`. The oracle test covered a coded side against a flat one and against a constant and did not cover a coded side against another coded side, which is why no test found this. It does now, across eleven types and three null densities. q4, q12 and q21 are the queries with the shape.
- A join's build side stops copying its strings three times. An assembly lays its pieces end to end into one run of data and then gathers that run into output order, and laying the chunks of a hash join's gathered side end to end hands it the identity permutation, so the gather walked it and copied every string into a second arena the same size as the first to produce the run it already had. Two changes. An assembly whose rows read straight through keeps the run it built instead of gathering it, and a string column finishes as views over the arena the pieces were appended into rather than as a flat column, which is the trade the string view form already is for a page: sixteen bytes a row move and the payload stays put. The second one pays twice, because the gather keeps the views form and shares the arena when it copies one, so the byte copy also leaves `Build::gather`, which runs once per output row rather than once per build row. A single threaded profile of q7 had the platform memmove at 19 percent of the whole query, split between those two places, with 286 samples in the string push alone. The suite goes from 3.56 s to 3.26, and q7 is 1.17x, q18 1.15x, q9 1.14x, q8 1.13x, q16 1.12x and q21 1.11x. All twenty two answers are unchanged except q1, whose floating point averages differ in the last two digits run to run anyway from parallel summation order.
- A metrics test no longer depends on a clock coincidence. It asserted that two durations read at different points came out in an order the clock does not promise on a loaded machine.
- The three pull requests that had no note anywhere have one each, which is the rule this changelog is under.

## 0.3.54

A patch release of three pull requests, two of them wiring faults in the execution side of the hash join and one an estimate that reads what the file already knows. Between them the first two are the largest move the execution side has made in this series, and both were found by taking one query apart rather than by reading the code. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

- The build side of a hash join runs on more than one thread. The sink that gathers that side said it could not run twice, and a sink that says that pins the whole pipeline it ends, so every hash join in TPC-H was reading its build side on one core of ten while the other nine waited. The refusal was real for the other operator that keeps a side the same way and not for this one: a cross product pairs each left row with these chunks one after another and a positional join pairs row n with row n, so for those the order the chunks arrive in is part of the answer and one thread is the only way to make it the order they were produced in. A hash join builds a table over them and reads them by position, so the order decides which of two equal rows comes out first and nothing else, which SQL does not promise and which the driving side of the same join has been deciding on several threads all along. The keep is now told which of the two it is. On a filtered `orders JOIN lineitem` at SF1 the analyzed plan puts the build side pipeline at 60.4 ms of wall and 60.4 ms of cpu before, which is one thread, and 20.6 ms of wall and 102.9 ms of cpu after. No plan changes and all twenty two TPC-H answers are unchanged. The suite goes from 4.93 s to 3.60, q12 is 4.35x, q13 1.97x, q3 1.79x, q7 1.62x and q21 1.36x.
- A join's runtime filter reaches the scan under its driving side. The probe side Bloom filter has been in the engine since the join landed and it had never been read by a single TPC-H query, for two reasons that are both wiring. The builder drops the filter at anything that is not a `Get`, a `Filter` or a `Project`, and a `read_parquet` call is a `TableFunction`, so every query in the benchmark suite built the filter and threw it away. `FileScan` now takes the handoff the way `Scan` does, puts each chunk through it at one hash of one column and one cache line touched per row, and gives up on it the same way when it stops turning enough rows away to pay for itself. The second reason would have bitten native tables too: the join knows its driving column as the projection above the scan binds it, `#1.0`, and the scan knows its own, `#0.0`, and a scan asked about another table's binding answers nothing, so with a projection over almost every scan this was usually a filter built, handed over and read by nobody. `sideways::beneath` is the walk between the two, down the same two nodes the builder already allows and through nothing else, and a projection that computes its column rather than passing one through ends the walk with nothing. The key expression is armed only when the walk finds a scan, so a build side that would have filled a filter nobody reads no longer makes one. On the q12 join counted at SF1 the driving side goes from 1,500,000 rows to 19,266. Five percent over the suite with nothing worse than a percent the wrong way, no plan changes and the answers unchanged. Only the filter tier reaches a file scan and not the range tier, because the range would have to reach the row group bounds and those are asked before the build side has run.
- A filter's row estimate is held under what the file's zone maps allow. A Parquet writer records the smallest and the largest value of every column of every row group and the scan already reads them to walk past the groups a filter rules out, so the rows held by the groups that nothing rules out is a number no answer to the filter can exceed. The estimator takes it as a ceiling and the rule is the minimum of the ceiling and the constant guess, which is the whole argument for it: a ceiling is never below the truth, so a minimum of it and the guess is never further from the truth than the guess was, and reading these bounds cannot make an estimate worse. Two other rules were tried and measured first, taking the surviving count as the estimate and taking a fifth of it the way the conjunct constant does, and both scored better on a fourteen predicate set and neither is safe. The one answer allowed below the one row floor is zero, because when every group is ruled out that is a fact about the file rather than a fraction of it, so it is recorded as exact with zone map provenance and `EXPLAIN` says so. On a ten million row file `id < 200000` goes from a q-error of 10.00 to 1.23, `id >= 9000000` from 2.00 to 1.03, and `id > 999999999` from infinite to exact.

## 0.3.53

A patch release of two pull requests, both of them found the same way. The twenty two TPC-H queries were taken apart into the operators under them and each shape was timed against DuckDB on the same parquet files, which is what the suite has always compared and is worth saying plainly: both engines read the same eight files through `read_parquet`, both default to ten threads on the machine this was measured on, and both are timed by their own `.timer on` inside one process. What that decomposition found is that the parquet reader is not behind, since summing four lineitem columns is 0.020 s against DuckDB's 0.029, and neither is the evaluator, since q1's expression in doubles is 0.020 s against 0.022. The gaps were in two specific places and these are they. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

- The side an outer join keeps is never the side that gets gathered, when the join is one a lookup could have answered. `customer LEFT JOIN orders` counted took 0.392 s where the same two tables as an inner join took 0.019 and `orders LEFT JOIN customer` took 0.019. It was not the rows and it was not the key, it was which side the build side pass picked: it picks from the estimates alone, customer is the smaller side, and customer is also the side the join keeps. Gathering the kept side is the same thing as running the mirror of the join, a right join, and no lookup answers one, because the lookup decides about a driving row from that row's own matches and a gathered row nothing has matched yet may still be matched later. So the join fell out of the streaming operator into the nested loop and built a boxed row per pair, one and a half million of them. An outer join now gathers the side it does not keep, whatever the estimates say, because the alternative is not a smaller hash table but no hash table. A full join keeps both sides and no lookup answers it either way, so it stays with the size rule, and a join with no cross side equality is a nested loop whichever side it holds, so nothing is forced there. One plan changes at SF1, q13, which goes from 0.710 s to 0.476, and all twenty two answers are identical.
- A decimal answer is checked against its type's width by comparing rather than by counting its digits. The count was a loop dividing the value by ten until nothing was left, which is a 128 bit division per digit, run on every row of every decimal operator, to answer a question that does not depend on the row: the width is the same for the whole run. A value fits a width exactly when its magnitude is below ten to that width, so ten to the width is worked out once and the per row check is a comparison. Over the whole of lineitem, three runs, minimum, base against new against DuckDB: `sum(l_extendedprice + l_discount)` 0.0366, 0.0184, 0.0100, `sum(l_extendedprice * l_discount)` 0.0416, 0.0204, 0.0110, `sum(1 - l_discount)` 0.0125, 0.0101, 0.0040, and `sum(l_extendedprice * (1 - l_discount))` 0.0538, 0.0241, 0.0110. On the suite it is q1 and nothing else, 0.155 s to 0.104, because q1 is the only query doing decimal arithmetic on every row of lineitem and the rest filter first. No plan changes and all twenty two answers are identical. The row at a time paths still count the digits, which is right, because they have one value and no run to amortize anything over.

## 0.3.52

A patch release of one pull request, a planner rewrite that takes the largest single step any TPC-H query has taken in this series. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

- The domain comes back out of a decorrelated existence test once filter pushdown has run. A correlated subquery is lowered by inventing the relation of distinct values its correlated columns take, running the inner side once against that relation rather than once per outer row, and joining the answers back. That relation is the domain, and it is built at a point where the `FROM` list is still a cross product and the `WHERE` is still one filter above all of it, so it is taken from whichever branch holds the tables the keys come from. That makes it a superset, which is all it has to be, since the join back matches each answer to the rows that asked for it and a value nobody asked about matches nothing. On q21 the superset is the whole of lineitem: both existence tests were building a six million row hash table, grouping six million rows, and joining the result back, to answer a question about the seventy eight thousand rows the outer query has after its filters. By the time pushdown has run the outer side of that join back is no longer a cross product, it is the relation the domain was standing in for, so the six nodes decorrelation left behind are written as one semi join against it, or one anti join where the test was negated. This is what DuckDB calls the deliminator and the name was already in the list of upstream optimizer names, so `SET disabled_optimizers = 'deliminator'` now turns off a pass that exists. It refuses a marker that is not a non-null constant, a domain grouped on anything but bare columns of the outer side, a join back that is anything but one null safe equality per domain column, a subquery relation that still reads the outer side, and a plan where anything above reads a column of the marker projection. One plan changes at SF1, q21, and it is the plan DuckDB picks for it. All twenty two answers are identical. q21 goes from 11.05 8.29 7.39 to 1.51 1.45 1.37, which takes it off the top of the list of the suite's worst queries for the first time.

## 0.3.51

Never tagged. Four pull requests, a planner bug that only a debug build could see, one rewrite, one change to where an aggregate finishes and one to how a pipeline divides its work. The notes for two of the four were written after the release pull request had already merged, and by the time they landed #932 had merged too and taken 0.3.52 for itself, so this number has a section and no tag, and 0.3.52 is the release that published its work. The rewrite is the first of two about q21, which was the slowest query in TPC-H by a factor of ten, and the second of them is in 0.3.52 above. It also carries the notes 0.3.50 was tagged without, which are below. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

- A join no longer grows a duplicate condition when the pushdown pass runs a second time. TPC-H q19 would not plan at all in a debug build, because the optimizer checks that running its pass sequence again gives the same plan back and q19 did not: its join came out holding the same equality twice. The rule that pulls out the conjuncts every branch of an `OR` has is what q19 needs, since `p_partkey = l_partkey` sits inside all three branches of a single `OR` and pulling it out is what turns a cross product of six million rows against two hundred thousand into a hash join. That rule runs on every visit rather than once, because the disjunction has to stay where it is, so the second visit extracts the same equality again, and the step that decides which predicates become conditions of an inner join was not looking at what the join already held. It looks now, by the same comparison the extraction rule already uses on its own list. No plan moves: a release build never ran the passes twice so it never produced the duplicate, and both plan baselines in tamnd/rudb-bench match a debug build of this.
- An aggregate finishes on the driver's pool instead of on threads it starts itself. Six places in rudb-exec opened a `std::thread::scope`, handed a partition to each thread they spawned and joined them all, while the driver's own pool threads sat parked a few microseconds away with nothing to do. The finalize is not a rounding error: on the million row native fixture at thirty two threads, ClickBench 16's main pipeline is 3.411 ms of wall with 1.953 of it in the finalize, and ClickBench 9's is 4.490 with 1.825. A thread creation costs about sixteen microseconds here, so sixteen of them is a quarter of a millisecond before any partition is touched, on every rep of every grouped query. `Sink::finalize` takes the lease the driver is already holding now, which widened the trait, all fourteen implementors and the twenty eight call sites in tests, and `Lease::alone` is new for the serial driver and the hand built pipelines that have no lease to pass on. `Lease::scatter_at_most` is the other new piece, because a lease is sized for the pipeline and the finalize is not always that wide: there are sixteen radix partitions, so a seventeenth thread handed one of sixteen is a lock and a notify spent to find out there is no work. All six sites go through one helper now, since they were the same function with a different body, and the two things easy to get wrong are in that one place: a borrowed thread's stage clock has to come back as a difference rather than a whole reading, because a pool worker carries what the queries before this one spent, and a thread that panicked has to fail the query rather than leave a partition looking like it was never reached. Two bugs fell out of the move. Every close degree is bounded by the thread count now, which it was not, so a session that asked for one thread still finished its aggregate on sixteen. And the dense path reported no stage readings at all, because it was the one site that never collected what its threads measured, so its emit and merge time was simply missing. What a caller gives up is a finish wider than the lease, since a pipeline's lease is sized by the morsels its source has, and the tradeoff is written down in the helper's doc comment.
- An inner join under a duplicate eliminator becomes a semi join. An aggregate that groups and aggregates nothing is asking which of its input's rows are distinct, and when its input is an inner join and every group is a bare column of the left side, the question is which left rows had a match, which is what a semi join answers directly. The inner join is the expensive way to ask it because it makes a row per matching pair and the aggregate then throws all but one of each group away. The shape comes from decorrelation: `EXISTS (SELECT * FROM l2 WHERE l2.k = l1.k AND ...)` unnests into the correlated keys joined against the subquery's relation and made distinct again, and on q21 that relation is lineitem joined to itself on the order key, so an order key with four lines under it makes sixteen pairs. Six million rows a side become about twenty four million pairs that the aggregate immediately cuts back to six hundred thousand. The pass refuses an aggregate that actually aggregates, a group reading the gathered side, a group over an expression, a join a second node also points at, and a plan where anything above still reads a column of the right side. One plan changes, q21, and it is the only query in the suite with the shape. All twenty two answers are identical. q21 goes from 5.62 5.77 6.07 to 5.25 5.35 5.63, about eight percent, and the profile says why it is not more: only a tenth of q21's time is in the join, and the distinct feeding each semi join is still taken over the whole of lineitem rather than over the keys the outer query has.
- A pipeline cuts its morsels finer when the statistics have piled the work into few stripes. ClickBench 39 at thirty two threads on the million row native fixture was 3.588 ms and its slowest instance was working the entire time, 2.427 against a mean of 0.996 with no waiting, which is the number #925 added and it says the work was divided unevenly rather than contended for. The division is by the rows each stripe holds, which is right for a scan and wrong for the pipeline above one: all five instances read about the same rows, the filter then keeps 7,225 of 116,736, and because the file is in key order the survivors sit together, so whichever instance drew them does all the hashing while the other four stop. Cutting finer costs page ownership, sixty four parts to a page, so it happens only where that has already been given up: when there are fewer stripes with work in them than there are workers the stripes are being shared anyway, and a quarter of a share is the piece. The instance count does not move, only the run count the handout walks. Fifteen reps at thirty two threads with the order balanced both ways is 88.8 ms to 88.1 over the suite, with the movement on the pruned scans, q39 1.17x, q13 1.118x, q38 1.099x, q34 1.063x, q08 1.061x and q37 1.037x. q39's skew halves, 1.431 to 0.707.

## 0.3.50

Tagged and never released. The tag is at 2cc112b and the release workflow refused it, correctly, because these notes were not written at the time and it checks for them. Nothing was published under that number, and neither was 0.3.51, so 0.3.52 is the release that carries this work. Six pull requests, three about the planner, two about what the metrics document records and one about what a function refuses. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

- A join on a low cardinality key is estimated at what it produces rather than at its larger side. The containment assumption says an equijoin answers as many rows as its bigger input, which is right when one side of the condition is a key and wrong by orders of magnitude when it is not. Two sides spreading their rows over the same key values give `left * right / keys` pairs, which agrees with containment exactly on a key and disagrees with it entirely on a column of twenty five values. The distinct counts come from two places: the catalog, and the Parquet footer's `distinct_count`, which the reader had been skipping. DuckDB states that field for the columns it already knew the number for, which at SF1 is `c_nationkey` 25, `s_nationkey` 25, `n_regionkey` 5, `l_suppkey` 10000 and a dozen more, and not the high cardinality primary keys, where containment was already right. A column counts only when every row group of every file stated one, and the number is the largest any group stated, capped at the rows. Four joins over the SF1 files against the real answers: customer to supplier on nation is 60,000,000 where it was 150,000, and nation to customer, customer to orders and lineitem to supplier are unchanged at 150,000, 1,500,000 and 6,001,215. All four are the exact row count. The first is the estimate that made q5 seventy times slower. No plan moved on this pull request, on purpose.
- Join ordering acts on more than the cross product count. 0.3.48 built the pass and held it to one claim, that an order replaces the one written down only when it builds fewer cross products, because everything else its cost function said was the containment assumption talking. With distinct counts behind the estimate the narrowing comes off: the scoring and the estimate now go through one function rather than two that happen to agree, and an order building the same number of cross products goes to whichever has the smaller sum of rows. Six plans change, q2, q5, q7, q10, q11 and q21, and all twenty two answers are identical. On q21 it builds supplier joined to nation first, ten thousand rows against twenty five on a key, rather than joining lineitem to supplier. The times are inside the noise and this is not sold as a speedup. The honest finding under it is that the join cardinalities are reasonable now and the filter selectivities are not: supplier joined to nation where the nation is Saudi Arabia produces about four hundred rows and the estimate says ten thousand, and the whole of that error is the one fifth constant guessed for an equality on a column nothing has a frequency synopsis for.
- A scan cuts its morsels by the rows a stripe holds rather than by the stripe. A worker owning a whole stripe owns the page under it, which is why the old rule cut a stripe further only when there were fewer stripes with work in them than workers. What it missed is that statistics prune parts and not stripes, so a stripe with three live parts and a stripe with three hundred both counted as a stripe with work in it, and a morsel apiece handed one worker a hundred times what it handed another. A stripe holding no more than a worker's share now stays one run and keeps its page, which is every stripe of an unpruned table, and a stripe holding more is cut into half shares. On the million row ClickBench file at thirty two threads, minimum of twenty five runs in both orders, the suite is 1.0530x on the sum of the minimums, query 24 is 1.251x, 17 is 1.174x, 25 is 1.155x and 10 is 1.124x, which is the set that filters hard enough to leave the live parts piled up. Query 24's pipeline goes from 1.367 to 1.057 milliseconds and its imbalance between slowest instance and mean from 0.454 to 0.151.
- The metrics document measures the slowest instance and the finalize instead of subtracting for them. A pipeline reports one wall clock and the work inside it happened on several threads, so the difference has to be explained by something, and the only way to ask was to subtract the operators from the pipeline and call what was left the close. On ClickBench 39 what was left was more than half the query, and it could have been the sink's finalize, one instance finishing long after the others, or the cost of starting and joining the threads at all. Those three have nothing in common and three different fixes. `run_parallel` returns two more measured numbers now: `slowest_ns` is a `fetch_max` over every instance's own wall including the caller's, which is the one thing the pipeline actually waited for, and `finalize_ns` is a span around the sink's finalize, which runs once on the caller after every instance has combined. The one ambiguous subtraction becomes three readings that add up: wall minus slowest minus finalize is what dispatch and join cost, slowest minus CPU over the instance count is the imbalance, and the finalize stands alone and is divided by nothing because there was only ever one of it. On the million row file at thirty two threads, query 16's close is 1.932 of a 3.668 ms pipeline and it is the finalize, with dispatch at 0.177 and imbalance at 0.364, while query 39's finalize is 0.392, its dispatch is 0.045 and its imbalance is 1.260 of a 2.654 ms pipeline. Two queries that looked like the same problem under the subtraction are two different problems and only one of them is the aggregate close. No behaviour changes and the cost is one span per instance and one per drain.
- The metrics document records the CPU of the slowest instance beside its wall. An instance that took twice as long as the average either was handed twice the work, which wants the work divided more finely, or spent half its time waiting on a lock, which wants the sharing removed, and dividing more finely makes the second worse. The slowest wall alone cannot tell those apart. Its own CPU can: near its wall means it was working, far below means it was parked. The two are kept as a pair from one instance rather than as two independent maxima, which would report the longest wall of one beside the largest CPU of another and describe neither. It comes out as `slowest_cpu_ns`. The question it exists to answer is ClickBench 39, which stops getting faster at four threads however many more it is given, and whose two string group keys make the aggregate share sixteen radix partitions rather than keep a table per instance.
- A `regexp_extract` group index outside zero to nine is refused. DuckDB takes zero to nine there and errors on everything else, and rudb answered the empty string for a ten and for a minus one. Upstream refuses it while binding and this file cannot, because preparing a query here is not allowed to raise what running it would raise, which is already written on `hoist`: a pattern that does not compile and an option letter that is not one both come back as nothing to hoist and the sentence comes out of the chunk that reached the call. The group index is read in the same function those two are, so it is refused in the same place. That leaves three shapes still divergent, all of them the same missing piece, which is the binder knowing anything about this argument. A query that reaches no rows answers here where upstream errors, a `BIGINT` outside the range errors with this sentence rather than the binder's, and a group index that is not constant is a binder error upstream and an answer here. This is the first half of #496 and does not close it. A group index inside zero to nine that the pattern does not have stays the empty string, which is what upstream answers, so nothing here counts the pattern's groups, and a null group index keeps the null path it had.

## 0.3.49

A patch release of one pull request, about where the planner's row count for a Parquet file comes from. It is tagged on its own rather than held for company because the plan gate in tamnd/rudb-bench pins a tag, and the plan diff this produces is the thing that gate exists to show. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 19.

- The row count for a Parquet read is taken from the footer the binder already read. 0.3.48 got that number by reopening the file between binding and optimizing and reading the footer a second time, keyed by the path string the call wrote. A Parquet file states its columns and its row count in the same few kilobytes at the end of it, so the binder had already read it a moment earlier to settle the schema, and every statement over a file was parsing the same footer twice. One read now gives back both, the binder records the count on the plan against the table index of the call, and the pass in between goes away. Forty binds of `SELECT * FROM read_parquet('hits-10m-snappy-rg8k.parquet') LIMIT 0`, four rounds alternating the two binaries, is 97.91 / 90.59 / 93.64 / 111.82 ms before against 48.11 / 49.73 / 48.89 / 52.71 ms after, on a file of a hundred and five columns in eight thousand row groups. TPC-H SF1 over the nineteen queries that finish is 11.60 s against 10.45, and the per query wins line up with how many tables a query touches, q02 at -29.5%, q07 at -18.7% and q04 at -16.2%, since that is how many footers were being parsed twice. It also counts the calls the path keyed route could not: `read_parquet('/tmp/p*.parquet')` and `read_parquet(['/tmp/p1.parquet','/tmp/p2.parquet'])` both said rows unknown before and both say seven rows exact from row count now.

## 0.3.48

A patch release of three pull requests, one about the allocator, one about join order and one about what the storage format knows. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format goes from 18 to 19, because the directory now carries a distinct count per column.

- The pages a query frees are kept rather than handed back to the kernel. Callgrind said one run of ClickBench 25 at one thread is a hundred and fifty five million instructions against seven and a half milliseconds of CPU, which would be twenty one billion instructions a second on one core, and no core does that. The time was going on page faults: query 16 over twenty runs takes 203,618 minor faults, forty megabytes of pages the kernel had to find and zero before the query could write into them. That is the default glibc policy, which is right for a program that allocates a few large things and keeps them and wrong for one that allocates a scatter buffer and a group table per partition, frees them all at the end of the query and asks for the same sizes again on the next one. Three `mallopt` parameters set once per database, glibc only, compiling to nothing elsewhere. Query 16 is 1.392x, 18 is 1.376x, 28 is 1.218x and the suite is 1.0850x on the sum of the minimums. The cost is resident memory and it is real: query 16 goes from 99 MB to 170 and query 28 from 251 to 488, which on query 28 gives up a lead over DuckDB rather than spending part of one.
- A run of inner joins is ordered so a cross product the conditions can avoid is not built. Until now the order was the order of the `FROM` list. On q9 no condition reads part and supplier and nothing else, because the two are joined through lineitem, and they are the first two entries of that list, so the plan held a genuine cross product of ten thousand suppliers against every part whose name has green in it. A run of inner joins and cross products is now one region, every condition in it is a condition of the region since an inner join is associative and commutative, and the order is chosen greedily over the leaves. Nothing rewrites an expression, because a column is bound to the operator that produces it rather than to a position in a row. The order is taken only when it builds fewer cross products than the region already had, which is the one comparison the row counts can carry without a distinct count: an earlier version that took any order estimating cheaper made q5 seventy times slower, because the containment assumption puts customer joined to supplier on `nationkey` at a hundred and fifty thousand rows when the real answer is twelve million. Three of the twenty two TPC-H queries have a region this applies to and the other nineteen come out of the pass the plan they went in as. Paired against the same binary without it, q9 is 0.486 s against 4.984, q8 is 0.340 against 2.201 and q2 is 0.073 against 0.721.
- The distinct values of a nullable string column are answered instead of given up on. A string column is written against one dictionary covering the whole table and the size of it is the distinct count exactly, which made `COUNT(DISTINCT column)` a directory read for every column without a null in it. A null row is written as the code for the empty string, so a nullable column's dictionary can hold an empty string no row of it has, and the reader could not tell that from a column holding a real one. The writer can, since it counts the non-null rows using each code on its way to the frequency summary and a code nothing counted is a code no row holds, so it now records how many codes a row holds and the directory carries it. Counting the groups of a grouping moved with it: that is the distinct count plus the null group, and the path answering it was relying on the file refusing nullable columns to keep the two the same.

## 0.3.47

A patch release of four pull requests, two about the planner and two about the global dictionary on disk. The planner half is why every one of the twenty two TPC-H queries now finishes, which had never been true before: q21 had timed out at every timeout it was ever given. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format goes from 16 to 18, because the global dictionary writes both its sorted order and its offsets differently.

- A filtered mark join becomes a semi join. `WHERE x IN (SELECT ...)` binds to a mark join, which answers a marker per driving row and is therefore a sink: it gathers its whole driving side into rows before it produces anything. A filter over that marker keeps a row where the marker is true and drops it where the marker is false or null, and the marker is true exactly where the driving row matched, so the rows that filter keeps are the rows a semi join produces, and a semi join is a stream. On q18 the driving side is the three way join of customer, orders and lineitem, so six million wide rows were being held as boxed values to answer a question about one column of them. The rewrite refuses a predicate that is anything but a bare read of the marker, because `WHERE NOT (x IN (...))` is an anti join and `WHERE x IN (...) OR y > 3` genuinely needs the marker as a value, and it refuses a plan where anything above still reads a column of the gathered side. At SF1 against a binary from the same commit without it, alternating: q18 is 0.398 s against 7.015, q20 is 0.134 against 0.384, and q2 and q1 are the controls and do not move.
- A correlated subquery's domain is built from the branch its keys come from. The domain is the distinct values the correlated columns take, and every rule that built one grouped over the outer side, which at the point these rules run is the whole `FROM` list as a cross product because nothing has turned the `WHERE` into joins yet. q21 correlates on two columns of `lineitem` and its `FROM` list is supplier, lineitem, orders and nation, so finding the distinct values of two columns of a six million row table meant grouping a product `EXPLAIN` reported as 2,250,455,625,000,000,000 rows, exact from row count rather than estimated. The domain is now built from the branch that still produces every table the keys read, which holds every value the product does and usually more. More is allowed, because every rule joins the domain's answer back to the real outer rows on the correlated columns and groups it by them, so extra values are extra groups rather than extra rows in a group. A branch is descended into only where the node above leaves that branch's columns alone. q21 goes from never finishing to 8.10 s and its answer is byte identical to DuckDB v1.5.5. Under it was an older bug the change exposed: column pruning walked the plan breadth first, which is not a parents before children order on a graph, and narrowing the domain is what first made two paths of different lengths reach the same table with different column needs. A node's children are behind it in the arena, so the walk sorts and the order is real.
- A global dictionary packs its sorted order. An entry was twelve bytes flat, an eight byte head and a four byte code, and on the five ClickBench string columns that is 62.0 million entries and 744 MB, six percent of the file. The order is sorted, so a block of 512 consecutive heads spans a sliver of the column and often holds one distinct head, and a code is a position in a dictionary of eighteen million, which is twenty five bits and not thirty two. A block now writes its smallest head, the bits the largest is above that, then the heads and the codes at the width each needs, heads first because a probe reads a head and only the block it settles in reads a code. On the full 99,997,497 row `hits` the file goes from 12.22 GB to 11.68, and an entry from twelve bytes to about 3.2.
- A global dictionary writes its offsets relative to the block. The offsets said where each value starts in the decoded payload, four bytes a value, 62.0 million values and 248 MB on the same five columns. They were absolute into a payload of a gigabyte, so all thirty two bits were needed, but no reader wants an absolute offset: a reader decodes the one 1,024 value block the value lands in and slices it, so the first thing it does is subtract that block's base. They are now written relative and as ends rather than starts, which makes a block 1,024 numbers rather than 1,025 since a value starts where the one before it ended. One width for the whole column. The file goes from 11.68 GB to 11.58 and an offset from four bytes to 2.44.

## 0.3.46

A small patch release, three pull requests, one of them the last join kind that had never been given a hash table. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format is unchanged at 16.

- A mark join is answered by lookup where one equality answers it. It was the one kind whose equalities were set to `None` on purpose, because its answer is three valued and a lookup only answers two of the three, so every `IN (SELECT ...)` and every `NOT IN (SELECT ...)` in TPC-H was evaluated over every pair of both sides. The third value can be settled outside the lookup. Read the condition as `d = g` over a gathered side with rows in it: a hit is true, and a miss is false unless some pair came out null rather than false, which happens exactly when `d` is null or when the gathered side holds a null anywhere. Neither of those is a question about which row missed, so one is asked per driving row and the other once for the whole side. A gathered side with no rows in it has no pairs at all, so every marker is false. That is exact for one equality and is not exact for two, since `d1 = g1 AND d2 = g2` is false whenever either half is, so more than one equality and any residual go back to the loop unchanged. At SF1 against a binary built from the same commit without it, alternating: q20 is 0.507 s against 101.6, q16 is 0.183 against 0.510, and q18 is 47.9 against three baseline runs that all timed out at five minutes. q2 has no mark join and does not move, which is the control.
- A membership filter is sized from the values a part holds rather than from its row count. A flat vector knows its length and nothing else, so every part asked for a filter over 1,024 values and got 1,285 bytes whether it held a thousand distinct numbers or forty. #889 dropped the filters that came out larger than the page they protect, which stopped the waste and left a dozen columns of `hits` with no filter at all where a correctly sized one would have paid for itself many times over. The hashes are taken in one pass and the distinct ones counted on the way past by linear counting over 2048 bits on the stack, which is a shift and an or a row on top of a walk the fill was making anyway and allocates nothing. It does not have to be exact, because a filter is sized in whole blocks of 51 values and a count a percent out gives the same filter. On the full 99,997,497 row `hits` the file goes from 12.55 GB to 12.22, the filter pages from 1.105 GB to 0.775, and filters from 8.8 percent of the file to 6.3.
- A radix exchange hands its run over instead of copying it into a shared one. The two exchanges under a multi key `GROUP BY` with a count descending limit appended their records onto the run already in the shared partition, which is a copy of every record but the first instance's, made while holding that partition's lock. On the million row ClickBench file that is twenty two of the twenty four megabytes the scatter had just written, copied again with sixteen instances queueing to do it. The partition now holds one run per instance, handing it over is a move, and the fold takes the widest run as its table so the most records stay where they are and folds the others into it. Query 16 is 1.601x, 18 is 1.481x, 32 is 1.321x, 14 is 1.242x, and the suite is 1.1018x on the sum of the minimums. The cost is peak memory, which on query 16 goes from 50.2 MB to 73.2, because the table is reserved for the whole partition while the unfolded runs are still alive.

## 0.3.45

A patch release about the join deciding what not to read. Two of the ten pull requests in it are the runtime filter a hash join hands to the scan under its other side, which is the first thing in this engine where one operator changes how much work another one does while the query is running. Three more are the join's own inner loop, one is the row count the optimizer never had for a Parquet file, and one is a wrong answer in a radix partition that a test for something else found. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format goes from 15 to 16, because the integer cascade has a new kind in it.

- A hash join tells the scan under its driving side what its build side's keys covered. A join finishes gathering one side before it reads a row of the other, and at that moment it knows the smallest and the largest key it will ever match, which is something no statistic could have told the planner. A driving row outside that range matches nothing and a whole stored chunk outside it is a chunk the scan never reads, never decompresses and never decodes. The range crosses the edge between the two pipelines in the same direction the rows do, on one shared object: the join arms it while the query is built, the sink at the end of the build side widens it a chunk at a time, and the scan reads it when it is asked to divide its work. Nothing locks, because each step happens strictly after the one before it. A scan may only drop a row the join was going to drop anyway, so three things are refused. The kind has to be inner or semi, because a left, an anti and a single join all answer with a driving row that matched nothing. The equality has to be plain `=`, because under `IS NOT DISTINCT FROM` two nulls match and a range has nothing to say about a null. And the only nodes allowed between the join and the scan are a filter and a projection, because a `LIMIT` over a `SORT` decides which rows survive by counting them. On a star schema shape, twenty million fact rows clustered by key against a thousand dimension keys, this is 0.005 s wall and 0.062 s cpu against 0.027 and 0.419 with the filter disarmed.
- The second tier of that filter is a blocked Bloom filter over the build side's keys, and it is the one that answers the shape the range cannot. A key spread over its domain puts the lowest and the highest far apart and every chunk falls between them, so the range rules out nothing. The filter rules out single rows instead, and the scan drops the ones whose key the build side has never held. It reuses the filter zone maps already have, because a filter built for a join is never written down and so is free to hash its values the way the hash table does, which is what lets the scan hash its raw column and get the same word back. It is filled when the build side finishes, since the exact row count is what it is sized from and a side only knows that once it is done. The part that needed care is that a filter turning nothing away is pure loss, so the scan counts what it kept and stops applying it once sixty four thousand rows have gone by without a quarter of them being dropped. Twenty million fact rows against a million dimension keys: at five percent matching, 0.102 s wall and 0.520 cpu against 0.128 and 1.083. At every row matching, which is the worst case, 0.118 and 0.759 against 0.114 and 0.771, where without the give up it was twenty percent of cpu worse.
- The join's output columns are gathered by position. The probe loop built a `Vec<Value>` for the driving row, another for every match, pushed them into a list and transposed the list back into columns at the end of the chunk, none of which is the join. The answer is the driving side gathered by one list of positions and the gathered side by another, so the probe writes two lists and gathers once per column. `PAD` is `u32::MAX` and a gather past the end already reads as null, so an unmatched row on a left join is a position like any other and the gather has no branch in it. A driving row is only boxed into values when a residual conjunct needs one, so a plain equi join allocates no row at all. Ten million probe rows against a one million row build: 0.110 s wall and 0.449 cpu, 45 ns a probe row, against 0.347 s and 232 ns.
- The join looks its rows up in a columnar hash table. It was a `HashMap<Vec<Value>, Vec<usize>>`, so every driving row built a vector for its key and hashed it a tagged value at a time, and every distinct build key cost two allocations. The group by next door has had an open addressed columnar table for a while, with the hash taken one pass per key column and the probe walked a batch at a time so the cache misses overlap, and this puts the join on it.
- A Parquet footer states its row count exactly and nothing carried that number to the planner, so every scan of a file was an unknown and every pass that needs an estimate skipped every join in a query over files. That is all of TPC-H as the harness runs it. The count goes in through `Statistics`, gathered between binding and optimizing because the plan is what says which files the query reads, and the optimizer opens nothing. At SF1, against the same binary with the pass disabled, q5 is 2.6 s against 43.3, q3 is 1.8 against 12.8, q7 is 5.2 against 15.1 and q10 is 2.2 against 8.0. q2 is 3.0 against 2.8, which is a real regression and is the join cardinality model rather than this.
- An integer column divides out its common step before it is packed. A timestamp column loaded from a source that recorded whole seconds is microseconds with twenty zero bits under every value, and the frame of reference pays for those bits on every row: the three ClickBench timestamp columns need 36 bits a value for a part spanning a working day, of which 17 are the range of seconds and 19 are zeroes written down a hundred million times. `STRIDE` writes the smallest value, the greatest common divisor of every value's distance from it, and the step counts as a chunk in their own right, so everything already in the cascade applies to them. Finding the divisor is one pass that stops the moment it reaches one, which on a column of arbitrary numbers is the first pair of values.
- A scatter reads its key columns a chunk at a time. The encoded count scatter is the two leading integer keys and one dictionary string key ClickBench 16 and 18 group on, and it asked each vector for a value a row at a time, which costs a match on the form, a match on the width, a widening to a hundred and twenty eight bits and a checked narrowing back, none of which depends on the row. Callgrind put the three accessors at twenty eight percent of query 16. There is now a reader that settles the layout once per chunk, and a chunk whose keys are flat with no nulls goes down a loop that does no form matching and no null checking at all. Beside it is a wrong answer the test for that found: a radix partition keeps no validity vector until it sees its first null and fills in what it skipped when it does, and filling in behind an empty vector fills in nothing, so a partition whose very first record was the null one kept no validity at all and read every record back as valid.
- A sieve larger than the part it indexes is not written. A reader reads a part's membership sieve to decide whether to read the part, and when the sieve is the larger of the two it has already spent more than the read it was meant to avoid, which holds even when it rejects every time. On ClickBench twelve of the 105 columns are in that state and they carry about 840 MB of the 1.97 GB the sieves take, with DNSTiming spending 265 bytes a part to protect 101 bytes of data. The full rule is that a sieve pays when its bytes are under the rejection rate times the part's, and the writer cannot know the rejection rate, so this enforces the necessary half from two numbers already in hand. UserID, which is what query 19 probes, is at 0.76 and keeps its filter.
- Every statistic carries a provenance and says what a caller may do with it. Provenance hung off `Estimated` alone, on the reasoning that a bad plan is diagnosed by asking which guess was wrong, and that is half the job: an exact row count out of the catalog and an exact join cardinality out of a link header are different kinds of exact and a reader of `EXPLAIN` has to tell them apart. There was also no word for what a class permits. `Answer` takes exact, or a certificate the caller discharges. `Enable` takes exact and nothing else, because a bound is not an equality and those rewrites need one. `Decide` takes any class including none. Three methods rather than three comments, because the difference between them is the difference between a slow query and a wrong answer. No plan changes.
- The binder's module documentation said the one thing it does not do is a table function whose arguments read a `LATERAL` column, which #865 made work, so the sentence had been wrong since it merged and it is the sentence D3 quotes as the entry state for the whole milestone. It now names what is actually refused, which is `UNION BY NAME`, a `LIMIT` written as a percentage, a `LIMIT` or `OFFSET` that is not a constant, `CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE`, `CREATE TEMPORARY VIEW`, and the `~` and `!` operators.

## 0.3.44

A patch release about the join deciding it does not have to be a nested loop. Three of the nine pull requests in it are a condition the lookup could not read, and between them `optimizer/table_filters.test` goes from stopping after thirteen statements to finishing the file in four seconds. One is the last shape of `LATERAL` the reference binary answered and rudb did not, and four are the native reader and writer. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format goes from 14 to 15, because the global dictionary payload is compressed now.

- A table function whose arguments read a `LATERAL` column is called once per distinct value of that column. `SELECT t.id, r.i FROM t, range(t.n) r(i)` was a not implemented error. Every other correlated shape is unnested by pushing a relation of the distinct outer values down through the correlated side until the correlation stops, and a table function is the one place that push has nowhere to go, because its arguments are what make its rows rather than something read over rows that already exist. So the rule makes the domain the input instead. `LateralFunction` is a table function with an input and it appends its column the way a window does, so the domain columns come out where the join back expects them. The operator is `crates/rudb-exec/src/lateral.rs` and it is not a loop over the outer rows: an outer side of 200,000 rows over 200 distinct buckets makes 200 calls. A call that makes more values than fit in a chunk keeps its place and carries on, so `range(1000000)` on one input row is one call and five hundred chunks rather than anything held in memory. The binder's old refusal is gone because nothing was left for it to catch, a reader settling its columns by opening the file having already turned down a name that is not a constant.
- A join key is an expression rather than a bare column. The lookup only recognised an equality whose two operands were both a column of identical type, and the binder turns `ON p.k::INTEGER = b.k` into a cast around one operand, so real conditions fell to the nested loop. What decides a key now is where its columns come from rather than the shape of the operand, and an equality whose two operands resolve against opposite sides is a key whichever way round it was written. Both sides' keys go through the same vectorized evaluator the nested loop's conditions use, deliberately, because a second row at a time interpreter is a thing that has to agree with the first about every cast and every overflow. On the sizes `optimizer/table_filters.test` uses, one statement goes from 6516 ms to 399 and five that did not stop now answer in between 276 ms and 1831.
- The side a join gathers is the smaller one when the join is going to be a hash lookup. `sides` was written when there was one join operator, and a nested loop walks the gathered side once per driving row so it wants the larger. The lookup builds its table out of the gathered side and reads it once per driving row, which is the opposite, so since 0.3.41 every hash join had been building its table out of the larger of its two inputs. The pass now asks which operator the join is going to get before it asks which side is bigger. At TPC-H SF1 over tables, q5 goes from 3.833 s to 0.743 and q9 from 61.1 to 16.5. Over the parquet files as the harness runs them it does nothing yet, because a view over `read_parquet` has no row estimate and the pass skips the join.
- A join with an equality and something else beside it looks the equality up and evaluates the rest on what that found. `ON p.k = b.k AND p.g >= b.g` used to fall to the nested loop whole, which over two million driving rows and six hundred thousand gathered ones is more than a million million pairs to answer what one lookup and a handful of comparisons answers. One equality is enough now, and the rest becomes a residual evaluated on the candidates rather than on every pair. It is sound because an equality filters pairs and so does everything beside it, so narrowing first and filtering after reaches the same set by a cheaper route, and a join with no equality in it has nothing to narrow to and stays on the loop. A gathered row counts as matched only once the residual has had it, or a `FULL` join would swallow a row whose only candidate the residual threw away. Three statements of `optimizer/table_filters.test` go from about 460 ms to about 41, two that did not stop answer in 652 and 643 ms, and the whole file runs in four seconds where its parent was still working on the fourteenth statement forty five minutes in.
- A packed tail is read with one load per value instead of one byte at a time. The transposed layout is all or nothing, so every short array in a cascade goes down the sequential tail path, and a cascade puts a short array in every chunk. ClickBench 9 is where it showed: UserID is nearly unique, so its dictionary lands one value short of a full unit and sends the whole column that way, about a million values fed through a `u128` accumulator eight trips at a time. The writer needs the dependency chain because it has to know what is left over from the value before, and the reader does not, since value `index` starts at bit `index * width` and always lies inside sixteen bytes read from the byte it starts in. A scan of UserID goes from 380.9M instructions to 206.8M and `unpack_tail` inside it from 211.0M to 36.9M. A second commit fixes what the faster scan uncovered: asking a `OnceLock` whether it holds a global dictionary answers the question a worker that already has one is asking, not the question a worker without one is asking, so every worker of a scan read and decoded the same dictionary and all but one dropped it. Total of the minimums over ClickBench is 1.0532x with the best queries between 1.12x and 1.43x.
- The global dictionary payload is compressed. It was the last thing in a native file still stored raw and on ClickBench it is 11.05 GB of a 21.37 GB file, which comes out at 14.11 GB. A payload block was 64 KiB of bytes and is now 1,024 values, so a value cannot straddle a block and the cache that existed to hold the ones that did is gone. The encoding is settled once per column rather than searched per block, because the exhaustive search runs at 2.1 to 6.3 MB/s and over 11 GB that is an hour of processor time. Five shapes are offered and the smallest over a sample of eight blocks wins, which lands at 98 percent of what the search would have found for a tenth of the time. `chooser::Settled` narrows to the chosen kind per level and falls back to the search wherever the shape does not reach, so it cannot produce something that will not decode.
- A string chunk decodes into one buffer and copies a block at a time. Compressing the dictionary payload put 7 percent on the query suite, because reading a value became decoding a block rather than slicing one, and this takes that back and 8 percent more. The decoder handed every level of the cascade back as a `Vec<Vec<u8>>`, so a three level nest over a block of 1,024 URLs allocated about three thousand times and copied every byte three times, to produce a thousand strings the caller then concatenated into one buffer anyway. `string::Flat` is one buffer with the values end to end and an end per value, `decode` is a copy out of it and keeps its signature, and the dictionary reader takes the flat form directly because a global dictionary already has the offsets and only ever wanted the bytes.
- A mixed aggregate folds locally before it is exchanged. It had one phase where DuckDB's has two: an instance wrote a sixteen byte record for every row it saw into one of sixteen partitions and the owners read every one of them back, which is a materialization of the whole input before any of it is added up. On the million row ClickBench file that is sixteen megabytes written and sixteen read again for a grouping column with three thousand eight hundred distinct values. Each instance now keeps a table of its own and only gives up on it past four thousand groups, which is where a local table stops fitting in the cache of the core probing it, and the owners fold the local states in at the end. That is the same answer because every part of a state is a sum. The probe got cheaper on the way past: what identifies a group is now twelve bytes in an array beside the states rather than the first two fields of a sixty four byte state, which is five slots to a cache line instead of one. ClickBench 9 goes from 6.248 ms to 5.300 at thirty two threads. Instructions only move three percent and wall clock moves eighteen, which is the point: what went away was thirty two megabytes of record traffic rather than work.
- `spec/planner-v2/` is sixteen documents about why three hundred and fifty two of the last four hundred commits touched `crates/rudb-exec`. That is not where the value is, it is where the decisions have nowhere else to go, because there is no physical plan and no cost based choice between two ways of running a node, so every optimization that arrives becomes an unnamed branch inside an operator. Six artifacts, two of which do not exist in rudb, and the rule that a decision belongs in the highest one that has the information to make it.

## 0.3.43

A patch release about where the work goes when there is more than one thread to put it on. Three of the nine pull requests in it are a phase that divided its work by counting rather than by looking, one is a phase that refused a second thread at all, and two are a predicate the optimizer could not see. ClickBench 39 was the last query in the suite still slower than DuckDB and it is not any more. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format is unchanged at 14.

- A native scan is cut by where the work is rather than by where the stripes are. A morsel was a whole stripe, which is the right unit when every stripe has work in it, because sixty four parts sit under one page and a worker that owns the stripe owns the page. ClickBench 39 filters on a column the file is written in order of, so the zone maps rule out all but about ninety of nine hundred and seventy four parts and those ninety sit inside two of the sixteen stripes. Fourteen workers took a stripe that was already ruled out, found nothing and stopped, and the query was faster on one thread than on the whole machine. The scan now asks the statistics where the work is before it starts anybody, in two passes because the two kinds of statistic cost very different amounts: the stripe bounds are in the directory already and read nothing, and the per part sieves are a page off the disk each and are only read here when the bounds have left the work piled into fewer stripes than there are workers. From the answer it takes the instance count, which now comes from the rows the statistics leave rather than from the size of the table, and the cut, which stays one morsel per stripe whenever enough stripes have work. q39 goes from 5.363 ms to 4.313 at thirty two threads, q36 through q42 are all between 1.14x and 1.49x, and q08, q16, q17, q18 and q22 are between 1.11x and 1.30x.
- An aggregate with a count descending bound pushed into it waits longer before splitting into radix partitions. The bound is applied when a table is finished, so sixteen partitions apply it sixteen times and let through sixteen times as many rows. That is invisible while a partition still holds more groups than the bound and total once it does not: q39 groups five columns down to 5445 groups under a bound of 1010, so one table hands the pipeline above 1010 rows and sixteen partitions of about three hundred and forty hand it all 5445, each carrying a URL and a Referer through a project and a top n that run on one thread. The threshold is now the bound times the number of partitions rather than a flat four thousand and ninety six, which is where splitting stops costing rows. A crowded memory budget still forces the split whatever the group count says. q39 goes from 4.05 ms to 3.06 and its top n from 0.835 ms to 0.396, and with the scan change above the query is 5.27 ms to 3.06, which is 1.72x.
- An `INSERT` that writes the native format reads on more than one thread. `Pipeline::degree` drops to one whenever any part of a pipeline says it cannot be instanced, and the native sink said so, which pinned the Parquet scan feeding it as well. Reading all 105 columns of `hits.parquet` takes 49.9 s at one thread and 6.9 at thirty two, and the insert loop is 168 s of a 211 s load, so about fifty seconds of it was a read running alone. The writer still holds one lock and the file comes out the same shape, and what several instances buy is the read overlapping the encode. The part that had to move is the stripe grouping: it used to be decided by watching the orders go past, which ends a stripe every time two instances cross, so a caller now hands over a run it already knows is contiguous and a stripe never spans two morsels.
- The frequency pass over the numeric columns hands them out through a queue instead of in equal runs. A `BIGINT` carries eight times the bytes of a `TINYINT` through the decode and the wide columns sit together in a schema the way they do in `hits`, so the worker given the wide end finished long after the one given the narrow end. They are sorted by the same weight the encode queue uses and taken from the expensive end, and the worker cap goes from 16 to 32 because ninety numeric columns against a cap of sixteen made the cap the thing deciding how long it took. A full ClickBench load goes from 94.2 s to 82.9 and the phase itself from 23.4 s to 12.2.
- A join condition that reads one side only is pushed into that side. TPC-H q13 is the one of the twenty two queries written with the `JOIN` keyword, and it carries a `NOT LIKE` on orders inside the `ON` clause. A join takes its hash lookup only when every condition is an equality across the sides, so that one condition dropped the whole join to reading the gathered side once per driving row, over 150,000 customers and 1,500,000 orders. It ran for over five minutes and now answers in 3.9 seconds with the same 42 rows DuckDB gives. The rule is that a condition read while the pairs are made removes a row that fails it, so where the join drops such a row the condition may as well have removed it from the side, and the eight kinds split on that question differently enough to want a table of their own.
- The conjuncts every branch of a disjunction has are stated beside it. TPC-H q19 is a cross product under one predicate of three branches, each beginning with the same equality, and filter pushdown splits on `AND` and stops at `OR`, so it saw one predicate reading both sides and asked for six million rows against two hundred thousand. A row satisfying `(A AND X) OR (A AND Y)` satisfies `A` whichever branch it took, so `A` may be stated beside the disjunction, which stays exactly where it was. It costs one evaluation per row and buys a predicate the rest of the pass can work with: the shared equality becomes the join condition and two other shared conjuncts go into the scan. q19 goes from not finishing to 3.0 seconds, and 3083843.0578 is what the specification's validation output says.
- A star over a semi or an anti join expands to the left side alone. `SELECT * FROM a SEMI JOIN b ON a.k = b.k` died with an internal error about a column not in the schema, because those two kinds produce the left side's rows and nothing else and the star expanded over both. The scope is trimmed after the condition is bound, since the condition is still about both sides, and at the boundary recorded before `USING` drops the right side's copy of a joined-on column. Naming the right side after the join is now a binder error, which is the same rule seen from the other end and what the reference binary says. tamnd/rudb#847.
- The mixed aggregate, which asks for a `SUM` and a `COUNT` and an `AVG` next to a `COUNT(DISTINCT)` over the same groups, uses the pair partitioned pass instead of keeping a set of every distinct pair inside each group owner. ClickBench q09 goes from 8.541 ms to 6.434 at thirty two threads and from 45.5 to 30.4 at one, while the instruction count does not move at all, 737.4 million against 734.9. The set held a slot per distinct pair, about nine hundred thousand of them, so it was tens of megabytes and every probe missed out to memory, and a sixteenth of it fits in L2. It also fixed a null group coming out as zero when it was the first row of a partition's run.
- `encode_only(Kind::Delta, &[])` answers that the kind does not apply instead of panicking. The delta encoder writes the first value and then the differences, so it read `values[0]` before it knew there was one. The search never hands it an empty chunk, but `encode_only` is the door round that filter, and a new test runs all six kinds over eight shapes rather than over the one shape the search would have offered.

## 0.3.42

A patch release about joins. Five of the seven pull requests in it are a join that stopped being a nested loop or a plan that can now be a join at all, and the other two are a grouped count distinct and the integer decoder. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format is unchanged at 14.

- A join no longer holds the side that drives it. The lookup added in 0.3.41 was the right answer in the wrong shape: the whole operator was a sink, so it collected the driving side into rows, answered all of them at the end, and held the entire answer until something read it. A join therefore cost two sides plus its output, and a sink that refuses to run twice pinned the driving pipeline to one thread. Five of the eight kinds decide about a driving row from that row's own matches, which are inner, left, semi, anti and single, and those are now a stream that holds the table and nothing else and hands each output chunk downstream before it looks at the next input one. Right and full need the end of pass scan for gathered rows nothing matched, mark asks a question of the whole gathered side per driving row, and positional is not a lookup, so those three stay on the sink. On a MacBook Air M4, 200,000 rows joined against 1,000,000 goes from 0.329s to 0.160s, and two 200,000 row tables on a thousand keys, which is forty million output rows, goes from an out of memory error under a 256 MB limit to 40,000,000 rows in 1.20 seconds real against 7.16 user. DuckDB v1.5.5 takes 0.005s and 0.12s on the two.
- A cross product under an equality is an inner join. This is the rewrite every textbook has. It was written for the first filter pushdown pull request and taken back out before that merged, because the join it produced was slower than the cross product it replaced, and the two changes above are what make it true again. Ten thousand rows against fifty thousand, both plans out of one binary with `SET disabled_optimizers` picking which, is 1.38 seconds as a cross product with a filter above it and 0.010 seconds as a join. It matters for TPC-H rather than for a microbenchmark: seventeen of the twenty two queries write at least one join as a comma separated `FROM` list with the equalities in the `WHERE`, which is how the specification writes them, and q13 is the only one of the twenty two that uses the `JOIN` keyword at all. Only the equalities move, because a join no lookup answers is still a sink that gathers both sides, and on the same two tables a cross product under `a.x < b.x` answers in 0.71 seconds where the join it would have become runs out of 19 GiB.
- The same question is now asked before any predicate is moved onto a join that already has conditions. Filter pushdown used to move every leftover onto an inner join, on the grounds that a condition and a filter above the join mean the same thing and the condition runs earlier, and that was a wash while every join was a nested loop. It is not a wash now, because adding one predicate a lookup cannot answer costs the join its table and drops the whole thing back to reading the gathered side once per driving row.
- A join condition written as `ON a = b AND c = d` is split into its parts. The lookup is taken when every entry in the condition list is an equality across the sides, and a conjunction is not an equality, so the whole join fell to the loop. The same query with the second equality in a `WHERE` was already fast, because pushdown had split that one. On gpc, 200,000 rows against 2,000 goes from 3.06 seconds to 0.08, which is what the `WHERE` spelling always took. A correlated subquery over a right join went from timing out at two minutes to 1.71 seconds.
- `IS NOT DISTINCT FROM` takes the lookup as well. It was left out with a comment saying no plan writes one, which stopped being true when the unnesting rules started equating the domain key null safely, so that the outer row whose key is NULL gets the subquery's answer about NULL rather than no row at all. Every correlated subquery in rudb was joining its answer back to the outer row with a comparison the lookup refused. The two comparisons are the same table with opposite answers about a null, which is one flag per column and not a second kind of table. Five correlated shapes over 200,000 row tables improve between 1.5 and 5.4 times, and the full join shape, which carries two null safe comparisons in one condition, goes from 8.11 seconds to 0.11 on a tenth of the rows. The optimizer was then taught the same thing, so a cross product under a null safe equality is rewritten too.
- A grouped `COUNT(DISTINCT)` partitions on the pair rather than on the group. Four ClickBench queries count distinct users inside a group and they are the biggest cluster of losses against DuckDB in the suite, three of which reach this path. A grouping column is allowed to be as lopsided as it likes, and on the million row file `RegionID` 229 holds eighteen percent of the distinct pairs, so fifteen threads finished and waited for the sixteenth. Partitioning on the pair makes an even share the floor rather than the good case, and a second pass partitioned on the group counts the groups afterwards. On the minimum at 32 threads q08 goes from 4.627 to 3.828, q10 from 1.991 to 1.923 and q13 from 2.928 to 2.238.
- Integer decoding reuses three buffers the thread keeps instead of allocating and zeroing them per unit of 1024 values. The zeroing was the largest single exclusive item in a ClickBench profile, ahead of the unpacking it was making room for. The obvious version of this, holding the buffers in the decode call, is worse than what it replaces and the measurement that says so is recorded in the pull request. Callgrind at one thread is 2.1% fewer instructions on q09 and 2.2% on q39. Wall clock is flat, which is stated rather than hidden: the work is gone rather than moved, and what it buys is a profile with the real cost at the top of it.

## 0.3.41

A patch release that finishes the general rule for correlated subqueries and makes an equality join a lookup rather than a loop. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format is unchanged at 14.

- The domain rule has a rule for every operator it used to refuse. A limit and a top N become a `row_number` per outer row and a bound on it, which gives up the top N's own bound because the alternative is refusing the query. A set operation pushes the domain into both sides and writes a projection over each that puts the side's own columns first, since a set operation matches its sides by position and the domain column lands in a different place depending on what the side is made of. Nothing is added to the operation itself, so a `UNION` deduplicates inside one outer row. A right join and a full join came from saying out loud that a side needs the domain for two reasons and not one: because it reads the outer row, and because the join preserves it, since a preserved row that matched nothing is told nothing by the side it did not match. That fixes a left join whose correlated side is the right one as well, which had its own refusal before. A full join preserves both sides, so which outer row a row belongs to is whichever copy of the domain is not NULL, which is a column neither side has and takes a projection. A full join is also the one rule that needs two copies of the domain rather than the one every other rule shares, because which copy is filled in is exactly the question it asks. Forty one shapes across the five operators were compared against the pinned build and all of them match, and twenty one corpus records went into rudb-compat. The one shape still refused is a semi, anti or mark join, which produces one side's rows rather than both sides put end to end.
- A join with an equality in its condition answers it with a hash table instead of a nested loop. Every join was a loop over one driving row against a chunk of the gathered side at a time, so ten thousand rows against fifty thousand evaluated the condition five hundred million times to find fifty thousand pairs. The gathered side goes into a hash table keyed on the columns the equalities name and each driving row reads one entry, so the work is the two sides added rather than multiplied. That query goes from 14.25s to 0.03s. It does not make this engine fast at joins, and at 200,000 rows against 1,000,000 it is 0.76s against DuckDB v1.5.5's 0.01s on the same machine, but the shape of the curve is fixed and what is left is the row layout. All the equalities take the lookup or none of them do, because a mixed condition needs a chunk of candidate rows built before the rest can be evaluated over them, and that is a second copy of part of a side.
- A scan walks past the parts its zone maps ruled out instead of handing each one up as an empty chunk.
