An empty chunk is still a call into every operator in the pipeline, and a selective query is mostly ruled out parts, so a filter that could match nothing at all cost more than half of a full scan to return no rows. A point lookup on the million row ClickBench file goes from 974 chunks reaching the filter to 16, and from 1.307 ms to 0.257 at one thread. A single threaded query needed a second fix, because the pipeline was answering a worker count of one without asking and so kept the morsel per part shape. All 43 ClickBench queries at 32 threads go from 136.5 ms to 128.5 on the minimum.
- A `CASE` is assembled out of its arms rather than built a value at a time. The old path made a `Vec<Value>` the length of the chunk, wrote each arm's results into it one row at a time, and rebuilt a vector from that, so every VARCHAR row was a heap `String` allocated and dropped. A new `Assembly` in rudb-vector lays the arms end to end and does the interleave once as a single typed copy, which is the inverse of a gather and is the scatter side a join payload wants as well. Nested types keep the old fallback, because a `LIST`, a `STRUCT` and a `MAP` are a child vector and a run of entries rather than a run of values. An arm that is getting every row no longer cuts the chunk either, which a one armed `CASE` never needed. q39 is the one ClickBench query with a `CASE` in it and the one rudb was losing to the pinned build on.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85.

## 0.3.40

A patch release about two places a subquery was going wrong, one of which is TPC-H q11. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format is unchanged at 14.

- A `HAVING` can compare a group against a query of its own. An uncorrelated scalar subquery written anywhere in a select block was joined below the grouping, which is right for a `SELECT` and for a `WHERE` and wrong here: the single row becomes a column of every row going into the aggregate, and the grouping rule then demands that column in the `GROUP BY`, so the query was refused with a message about a column that does not exist. It was refused with an empty column name, in fact, which is how the doubled word in "column a column must appear in the GROUP BY clause" got there. A `HAVING` subquery is held back and joined above the aggregate instead, since what it produces is one row per query and not one row per group. A correlated one still goes underneath, because what it correlates to is a column of the rows going into the grouping and there is nothing above the grouping for it to read. A mark join carries its comparison rather than the predicate carrying it, and that comparison is written over the outer rows, so it takes the same rewrite. TPC-H q11 binds, and rudb and DuckDB v1.5.5 both answer 1048 rows for it at SF1 with the files byte identical.
- The domain is pushed through a window. The domain columns partition first, which is one evaluation of the window per outer row, and the ordering, the window expressions and the frame bounds are rewritten to read the domain. Five shapes match the pinned build exactly and all five were refused by 0.3.39.

## 0.3.39

A patch release about correlated subqueries, which no longer have to match a shape somebody wrote down to be answered, and about five ClickBench queries. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format is unchanged at 14.

- A correlated subquery of any shape is unnested through a domain. There were eight rules in the optimizer, each recognising one shape, and a query that did not match one of them was refused. The general rule is Neumann and Kemper, "Unnesting Arbitrary Queries", BTW 2015, which is what the reference binary implements as well: the dependent join is written as a join against the distinct values the correlated columns take, and that relation is pushed down through the correlated side one operator at a time until the correlation stops. The eight shape rules stay, because knowing the shape is what lets them keep the inner input to one scan and one grouping, and the general rule is what everything else falls through to. The inner side is still evaluated once per distinct value of the correlated columns and set at a time, which is the difference between this and the per outer row loop the executor deliberately does not have. Eighteen correlated queries were compared against the pinned build and the sixteen rudb can answer match it exactly, including the count bug case, where a subquery that matches no rows answers 0 for a count and null for a sum.
- A FROM entry can read the entries written to its left, which is `LATERAL`. The word is read and dropped in the transformer, because a comma separated FROM here already looks left and the pinned build resolves the same query with and without it. An entry that does read its left neighbour binds to a dependent join and the general rule above lowers it, since a lateral entry is a correlated subquery in the FROM clause. An aggregate over a column the entry read from its left is refused, because there is one left row per evaluation and a sum over it would be a sum of one value, and a right or full join with a lateral right side is refused because a right row exists only for the left row it was evaluated against. Both messages are the pinned build's own. A correlated `VALUES` gains a rule too, where the domain becomes the input rather than something crossed with one. The one shape still refused is a table function whose arguments read a lateral column, which now says so by name at bind time.
- `WITH name AS MATERIALIZED (...)` runs its definition once and holds the rows for every place the name is used, through two new plan nodes. The binder projects the definition onto exactly the columns a read sees, so what is held is what a read gets. Whether the rows are held or the query is put into each place it is named is settled in the parser from the word the person wrote, because that is where the pinned build settles it too. A materialisation the body never names is dropped, which the pinned build does as well.
- The executor chooses which side of a join it gathers instead of taking whichever side the tables were written in. A join runs one side to the end and holds it, then matches the other side's rows against what it held, so the work is the driving side's row count times the gathered side's chunk count. Rows of the driving side are paid for one at a time and rows of the gathered side two thousand at a time, which on server3 is worth a factor of five between four rows joined against four hundred thousand and the same two tables the other way round. `Node::Join` carries the choice, a new optimizer pass writes it from the row estimate, and the executor runs the inputs the other way round under the mirror of the join kind when it says so.
- A grouped minimum or maximum over a string column ranks dictionary codes instead of reading strings. The file already keeps the sorted order of every string column's dictionary and has done since the format learned to binary search one, so turning that order round gives a code to rank map and the comparison becomes an integer one. A group holds the dictionary along with the position, which is what makes the identity check between two dictionaries sound rather than merely likely, and a mix of sources reaching one aggregate settles each position into its value and compares bytes as before. The state is no wider, because the value was already boxed to keep the accumulator at 32 bytes and the position rides in the same allocation. ClickBench q28 goes from 25.724 ms to 20.166 on the minimum at 32 threads, and under callgrind the string fetch comes out at what it reads when the query has no minimum in it at all.
- The ungrouped form of that aggregate takes the same path. An extreme over a whole stored column has been a directory read with no scan since 0.3.34, but a filter takes that answer away, because the directory knows the ends of the column and not the ends of what the filter left. The fold that runs instead was finding the winner on the bytes, skipping only a row whose code was the code already winning, which on a column like `URL` with 515,958 distinct values almost never fires. `MIN(URL), MAX(URL)` under a filter goes from 26.052 ms to 7.701 at one thread and 4.256 to 1.383 at eight. No ClickBench query has that shape, so none of it is in the suite total.
- `COUNT(*)` with one comparison on it is answered out of the frequency synopsis. The count over a whole stored table has been answered from the directory for a while and one filter gave all of it up, so q01 read 999,975 rows to keep 6,284. The synopsis is a heavy hitter sketch that records a bound on everything it dropped, and a bound of zero says the list is every distinct value with an exact count, which is when one equality or one inequality against a constant can be decided by walking fourteen entries instead of a million rows. The grouped form of the same question follows, so q07 answers thirteen groups out of the directory: the recogniser no longer needs a top N above the aggregate to ask it, because a complete synopsis is the whole grouping whether or not anything above wants only part of it, and a filter between the aggregate and the scan is unwrapped and handed to the same recogniser rather than making it give up.
- A text sort key is compared against the top N's bound without building a value per row. The operator asks every chunk one question before it reads a row, which is whether anything in there can still beat the worst candidate it holds, and with an integer key that went to a specialized loop while with a text key it allocated a `String` on both sides of every comparison and dropped it. The reader that compares bytes where they lie was there already and was gated to equality, so it takes the four ordering comparisons now. There is no memo across chunks and that is not an oversight: the top N improves its bound as it goes, so the constant is different on almost every chunk. q25 goes from 3.084 ms to 1.397 on the minimum at 32 threads, and from 18.287 ms to 7.613 at one.
- The top N's batched path rejects a chunk too. Below a bound of 64 the operator holds its candidates in sorted order and asks that same question of every arriving chunk. Above 64 it collects and trims periodically instead, and that path asked nothing at all, so every row that reached it got a vector of values for its key and another for the whole output row. The worst candidate at the last trim is carried and the same pass runs against it. It is a step behind the true worst and still a true bound, because the running only improves and everything that ties the bound arrived first. q39 goes from 6.599 ms to 5.081 on the minimum, and it was the query the pinned build was furthest ahead on.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85.

## 0.3.38

A patch release about three ClickBench queries that were falling through to the general hash table because of a type check that was stricter than the code behind it, about what a native file spends its bytes on, about the load running on one core of the thirty two the machine has, and about `FILTER (WHERE ...)`. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format moves from 13 to 14, because a part of plain integers can go through the cascade now and an older reader would not know what codec 5 is.

- A part of plain integers goes through the cascade when the cascade beats what the page was costing. Fixed width columns were going out at exactly their declared width, because the bit packer only takes a part when it halves it and nothing else was ever asked, so three timestamp columns sat at 8.00 bytes a row, six bigint columns at 7.99 and a date column at 3.81. The encoded form is taken only when it comes out smaller than both the bit packed form and the plain width, since both of those are cheaper to decode. Delta is the one that matters here, because a timestamp column climbs and nothing in the file was able to say so. On the full 99,997,497 row ClickBench file the file goes from 34.9 GB to 21.4 GB and the column pages from 20.6 GB to 7.1 GB, so the file is 1.044 times what DuckDB v2.0.0-dev84237 stores the same rows in where two releases ago it was 2.20 times.
- The grouped distinct exchange groups a string key by its dictionary code rather than by its bytes. The code is already there in the column and it is a dense integer, which is a cheaper thing to hash and a cheaper thing to compare than a string is. ClickBench q13 goes from 8.751 ms to 2.799 on the minimum over the whole suite, which takes it from behind DuckDB to 1.94 times ahead of it, and q08, which was already on this path, does not move.
- The encoded count exchange takes a signed key of any width. It used to demand a `BIGINT`, so q14 grouping by a `SMALLINT` search engine id fell through to the general hash table even though its shape matched the exchange exactly. That was a wasted exclusion, because the record the exchange builds already holds every signed key as eight bytes: one width is what lets a radix partition hold one record type. The exchange carries the key types now and the emit puts each value back into the type the query asked for, with the narrowing checked rather than assumed. q14 goes from 7.818 ms to 3.063 at 32 threads.
- The fixed width radix exchange takes narrower keys, which is the same exclusion in a different exchange. It asked for exactly `[BIGINT, INTEGER]` and q30 groups by `[SMALLINT, INTEGER]`. q30 goes from 8.149 ms to 3.537, and the merge stage goes to zero, which is the whole story: the general path builds sixteen hash tables and then merges them, and an exchange has no merge because each group lands in exactly one partition on the way in. A `BIGINT` is still turned away in the second position rather than truncated, because widening that field would take the record from sixteen bytes to twenty four and charge every exchanged row for it.
- A stripe is encoded one column to a thread instead of all of it on the caller's. The ClickBench load takes 586 seconds on gamingpc against DuckDB's 40.8 on the same machine and the same file, and none of that was the write path: the sink said it could not run in parallel, so the Parquet read, the page decompression, the casts and the whole native encode shared one core, which a `/usr/bin/time -v` of the load in flight confirms at 99.5 percent of one CPU. The split is by column because a column is the only thing here that shares nothing, its dictionary is its own and its page bytes go in its own vector. The columns go through a queue sorted by a rough per type weight rather than being dealt in equal piles, because a pile that happened to hold the four large string columns would be the whole stripe with everything else waiting on it.
- The planner says how much it knew, one class per operator. The estimator used to return a bare number, so a reader of `EXPLAIN` could not tell the catalog's count of a table from a fifth taken off it by a constant. A scan is exact because the catalog counted those rows, an ungrouped aggregate is exact at one row whatever the statistics say, a limit over an input nobody measured is certified to a bound, and everything above the first filter, group by or equijoin is estimated from a constant. One guess anywhere under a node makes the node a guess, which is the part worth having, because two counted tables joined on a column nobody has a distinct count for is the commonest way a plan goes wrong. `EXPLAIN` prints the class beside the number and the metrics document grows an `estimates` block, both of which a reader of the old shape still parses.
- Opening a file says what it cost to open, and the plan is pinned to the file. The specification says opening a table reads the header and the directory and nothing else, and that a plan is a function of the data, the generation and the settings and never of what happened to be cached. Both are true today and neither was checked anywhere, so the day one of them stops being true is the day somebody notices a benchmark's second run is faster than its first and works backwards from there. Two files with the same stripe count and a thousand times the rows in one of them now cost the same to open, asking for five plans reads no page and no index, and a second database over the same file reaches the same plans as the one that opened it cold.
- `FILTER (WHERE ...)` is answered on an aggregate call and on a window call. The predicate is a condition over the input rows rather than over the answer, so it is bound in the scope the arguments are bound in and it is cast to `BOOLEAN` the way a `WHERE` is. It is part of the generated column name for the same reason `DISTINCT` is, since a sum and the same sum under a predicate are two different answers. An aggregate binds its filter before its arguments and a window binds its after its frame, which is what decides the four messages you get for nesting one call inside another filter, and all four were measured against the reference binary rather than guessed. A scalar function, a ranking window and an aggregate inside another aggregate's filter are refused in the reference binary's own words.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85.

## 0.3.37

A patch release about where the parallel aggregate was losing its threads, and about what a native file spends its bytes on. Three of the four most expensive ClickBench queries got faster, two of them by more than half, and in each case the cause was the same: an instance that finished copied everything it held onto a shared structure while holding that structure's lock. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format moves from 12 to 13, because the dictionary code stream is encoded now and an older reader would read the codes as plain words.

- A limit pushed into a grouping runs on more than one thread. The operator refused a second instance because an instance that stops at its own tenth group drops rows of a group another instance kept, so the counts come back short. The instances now agree on which groups to keep before any of them starts dropping rows, which is one shared key set filled in a fixed order against the fold. ClickBench q17 goes from 12.086 ms on one instance to 2.233 on sixteen, and it was the second most expensive query in the suite.
- `COUNT(DISTINCT BIGINT)` stops copying the column to combine it. Each instance scattered into sixteen radix partitions and then appended each one onto the shared partition, which is fifteen sixteenths of the whole column copied under sixteen locks. An instance hands its run over instead. q04 goes from 12.554 ms on one thread and 6.157 on thirty two to 6.829 and 1.449, so the curve is flat from eight threads on where before it went backwards past eight. It is now at the floor the scan sets.
- The grouped form of the same aggregate had the same two problems and got the same two fixes, and the reader that picks a column's layout once rather than once a row moved into a module both aggregates share. q08 goes from 20.077 ms on one thread and 7.511 on thirty two to 12.062 and 3.661.
- A native file says where its bytes went, per column, from the directory alone. `cargo xtask native` reads the committed directory, which `open` reads anyway, so asking costs one directory read whether the file is empty or 45 GB. ClickBench `hits` is 2.2 times the size DuckDB stores it in and nothing in the tree could say which column that was in.
- What it said first is that every one of the 28 varchar columns had exactly 4.002 bytes a row of code stream, the same for `URL` with sixty million distinct values as for `SocialNetwork` which is the empty string nearly everywhere, because dictionary codes were written as a bare `u32` a row. The code stream goes through the integer encoder now. Codes are dense integers counted from zero and a part holds 1024 of them, which is the shape frame of reference is best at, and the encoded form is taken only when it comes out smaller.
- A native file is written at an offset the writer owns rather than at the file's cursor. The Windows job had been failing on a checksum error on a file nothing had damaged: `finish` reads the pages back through `read_at`, and Windows has no `pread`, so the positional read moved the cursor and the directory landed on top of the stripe index.
- Opening a native file from another format says which format the file is and which one the build reads. One sentence used to cover a path that was never ours, a file of ours from an older build and a file of ours from a newer one, and the number that tells them apart was already four bytes after the magic.
- `parse_ns`, `bind_ns` and `optimize_ns` are real numbers. All three phases happen above the function that writes the metrics document and none of them was timed, so planning was the one cost of a query that nothing could see. `total_ns` is the five phases added up. A statement prepared once and run a thousand times charges zero for the parse on purpose, because charging it every time would report one parse a thousand times.
- RANGE window frames written with a distance are answered, which was the last frame rudb refused. The end of the frame is wherever the order key reaches the key plus or minus the offset, found by binary search over the partition rather than by walking it, and a row whose key is null gets its peer group because there is no distance from a null to anything.
- `regexp_extract` with a group index that does not fit a `usize` no longer overflows on the way to its slot. A debug build panicked inside constant folding and a release build read whichever slot the wrapped index landed on, which is quieter and worse.
- The focused gate refuses a handed over diff that arrived on one line. The contract is one path per line, and a caller that joined the list with spaces got a green in four seconds without compiling anything.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85.

## 0.3.36

A patch release that runs the pragmas that are a statement, answers the three `show` pragmas and the `SHOW` statements that spell them, puts a membership filter beside every zone map in the native format, and hands a scan a whole stripe at a time. The storage format version is unchanged at 9 and the native directory format version is unchanged at 11, because a file written before this release still reads: a part with no sieve in it is a part that gets read.

- The nineteen pragmas that are a statement rather than a query are done, which is every name beginning with `enable_` or `disable_` plus `force_checkpoint` and `verify_parallelism`. Each is a `SET` with the name and the value folded into one word and each answers a BOOLEAN column called `Success` with no rows. The table saying which setting each one writes was measured against the reference binary rather than read off the names, because four of the names do not do what they say. That is 271 corpus records and it got 143 files past the first wall they hit.
- `PRAGMA show_tables`, `PRAGMA show_databases` and `PRAGMA show_tables_expanded`, and with them `SHOW TABLES`, `SHOW DATABASES`, `SHOW ALL` and `SHOW ALL TABLES`, plus the `DESCRIBE` spelling of each. The reference binary reads those words before the name reaches the catalog, so a table named `tables` does not get itself back from `SHOW TABLES` and has to be qualified to be described, which is now true here too.
- Pragmas live in a namespace of their own. Of the nineteen that return rows, only nine also answer to `pragma_name()` in a `FROM` clause, so `SELECT * FROM pragma_show_tables()` is a catalog error while `PRAGMA show_tables` returns rows. That was measured rather than assumed and the table function catalog now records which side of the fence each name is on.
- A zone map answers a range question and cannot answer an equality question on a column of scattered identifiers, because the value asked about sits inside the low and high of nearly every part. Every part now carries a sieve beside its zone: an exact bitmap when the values fit a small integer range, and a register blocked Bloom filter otherwise. A bitmap that comes out more than half set is thrown away, since no sieve gives the caller the same answer for nothing.
- A scan is handed a whole stripe at a time rather than a part at a time. Of the 114,236 preads a cold ClickBench q12 made, about 107,000 were single parts and a quarter of the bytes it moved were moved twice. The reader's page cache is now a slot per stripe rather than a list to walk, and it sizes itself to twice the workers a scan says it will use. Measured on the 43 GB file over all 43 queries, cold goes from 84.15s to 26.52s, which is 3.17 times, and hot is unchanged inside its own run to run spread.
- A string column's dictionary is read half a megabyte at a time rather than in sixty four kilobyte blocks, and a string extreme is compared as bytes rather than by checking every row is valid UTF-8 first.
- The statistics layer says `Unknown` out loud rather than inventing a number when it has none, and every rule sits behind a switch of its own. A plan built on a made up number is confidently wrong and nothing in it used to record which number was made up.
- The graph, statistics and TPC-H specifications are in the tree.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85.

## 0.3.35

A patch release that takes the settings the reference binary has and rudb does not read, keeps the query threads parked rather than starting them per pipeline, stops a scan reading one stripe page per worker, and answers `MIN`, `MAX`, `SUM` and `AVG` over a whole stored table out of the directory. The storage format version is unchanged at 9. The native directory format moves from 10 to 11, and there is no reader for the older one, which is developer facing and has no file on disk that had to survive it.

- `duckdb_settings()` is the pin's 192 rows rather than 22, in the pin's order and with the pin's descriptions, input types, scopes, alias lists and defaults. rudb reads 23 of those names as it did before. 133 are knobs, meaning nothing they can be set to changes what a query returns, so rudb takes one, keeps it and hands it back through `SET`, `RESET`, `current_setting()` and the table. The other 36 could change an answer, so rudb takes those at the value it already behaves as and refuses every other, since a setting it accepted and did not act on would be a wrong answer one statement later. Measured against DuckDB's own corpus that is 1091 of the 1475 records that failed on an unrecognized setting name, and those records were the first failure in 584 files.
- There are seven alias pairs now rather than two, and either spelling of a pair writes what the other one reads. `null_order` is one of them, which the pin lists as a row of its own and rudb did not have at all.
- A name that is not a setting gets the five nearest by edit distance rather than the whole list. `SET memory_limitt = 1` says word for word what the reference binary says to the same mistake.
- The pool keeps its threads parked rather than starting them per pipeline. A scoped thread is about sixteen microseconds to start and join and it is paid on the thread that starts it, so a sixteen way scan spent a quarter of a millisecond before it read a row, which is why a scan over a stored table was held to a handful of instances whatever the machine had. Every query that takes about a millisecond improves: q14 goes from 11.80ms to 7.44ms, q37 from 1.82 to 1.03, q36 from 1.82 to 1.33.
- A worker that finds a stripe page already being read neither waits for it nor reads it again. The scan handed parts out in order, so every worker crossed into a new stripe at the same moment and every one of them read the whole page, which was 8.36 copies of every page for the whole scan. A cold `SELECT MIN(EventDate), MAX(EventDate)` over the full ClickBench file goes from 629.85s to 88.15s.
- A stripe says whether each column's two ends were walked or inherited from somebody else's summary, and carries the column total when the column is integers. With that written down, `MIN`, `MAX`, `SUM` and `AVG` over a whole stored table are a directory read. `SELECT AVG(UserID) FROM hits` goes from 2.120ms to 0.004ms on the million row sample. Floats, decimals and the widest integers are deliberately left out, each for a reason that is in the code.
- A bit packed column is read through its packing, a dictionary column through its codes and a run encoded column through its runs, so the three forms that used to write only a bound now write the column's own two ends. Parquet hands out a dictionary vector for most of the low cardinality columns in `hits`, so this is what lets `SUM(AdvEngineID), COUNT(*), AVG(ResolutionWidth)` go from 1.238ms to 0.006ms and `MIN(EventDate), MAX(EventDate)` from 1.281ms to 0.004ms. An exact end is also a tighter zone map, so fifteen other queries skip more chunks than they used to and the million row suite goes from 200.0ms to 162.6ms by mean. String columns stay on the cheaper of their two walks, because being exact buys them nothing and walking them all cost 0.8s of a 3.0s load.
- `count()` written with nothing in it is the third spelling of `count(*)`, in a window and out of one, and it is named after the function it really is.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85.

## 0.3.34

A patch release that finishes DuckDB's 13 window function names, answers three ClickBench queries out of the file's directory, and cuts the native directory by a factor of sixty four. The storage format version is unchanged at 9, and there is no version 9 reader for the old native directory layout, which is developer facing and has no file on disk that had to survive it.

- All five value window names answer: `first_value`, `last_value`, `nth_value`, `lag` and `lead`. The first three read the frame and obey its exclusion, the last two read the partition and ignore whatever frame was written around them, and `IGNORE NULLS` makes all five count values rather than rows.
- `fill` answers, which is the thirteenth name and the only one that reads neither a position nor a row. It reads the straight line through the nearest value on either side of each gap, measured along the sort key rather than by counting rows, so both ends carry the line on rather than flattening out. Its five binder refusals are its own and all of them match the reference binary word for word.
- Two DuckDB bugs were found in `fill` and both are answered correctly here rather than reproduced. The reference binary extrapolates by putting the smaller of the two values first and negating the distance with it, which turns the line around for any column that falls as the sort key rises and for every descending `ORDER BY`, and it checks the container a DECIMAL is stored in rather than the declared width, so it can put 1997 in a `DECIMAL(3,0)` column. Both are filed against the fork with reproductions.
- `COUNT(*)`, `COUNT(column)`, `COUNT(DISTINCT column)` on a string, and `MIN` and `MAX` on a string are answered from the native file's directory rather than by reading rows, when the column has no nulls in it. The check runs before the input is built, because a pipeline that exists is a pipeline that runs.
- The native format splits a stripe into sixty four parts. A part is one appended chunk and stays the scan unit, and a stripe becomes the directory and IO unit, which takes a hundred million rows of the hundred and five column ClickBench table from over seven hundred megabytes of directory to the low tens of megabytes. A sparse fetch reads one index section and then the part it needs rather than a whole page.
- A native scan asks for eight instances below six hundred thousand rows instead of four, and has no ceiling above that beyond the threads the database was given. Over the million row ClickBench suite that is 188.1ms at sixteen instances against 261.3ms at four.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85.

## 0.3.33

A patch release that gives a global dictionary its sorted order, so a filter on a string column resolves the literal to a code by binary search instead of comparing values. **The storage format version moves from 8 to 9.** A version 9 reader still opens a version 7 or version 8 file, and a version 8 reader cannot open a version 9 one.

- A global dictionary is written with its values in sorted order, one entry per distinct value giving the code and the first eight bytes of that value as an integer that sorts the way the bytes sort. Codes themselves stay in first appearance order, because the writer streams stripes and because a stripe's codes being consecutive is what frame of reference compression wants.
- A text comparison against a literal searches that order rather than deciding per value. After the search the filter is one integer against one integer, and a literal the dictionary does not hold is decided for a whole chunk without reading the codes.
- The order sits outside the part of a dictionary page a reader checksums on first touch, in blocks of five hundred and twelve entries with a checksum each, read only when something searches. A query that never compares a string column against a literal never reads any of it.
- A probe compares the stored first eight bytes and only reads a value when two entries agree on them, so a search of a half million value dictionary reads one value rather than nineteen scattered blocks of the file.
- `TextSource` gains `ranks`, `compare_rank` and `code_at_rank` in place of the flat order it briefly had, so a format decides what a probe costs and the search itself stays in the kernels crate.
- All 43 ClickBench queries at one million rows total 397.9ms against 406.9ms, with fifteen queries between 0.65x and 0.92x and none slower. A full load costs 3.0 percent more time and the file is 3.61 percent larger.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.32

A patch release that runs window functions, answers the seven ranking names, and reads dictionary columns as dictionaries in two more places. The storage format version is unchanged.

- Window queries run. The operator sorts by the partition keys and then the order keys, walks each partition once, and evaluates each call against the frame the plan wrote, so `ROWS`, `GROUPS`, the default `RANGE` frame and all four `EXCLUDE` forms answer. A `RANGE` frame with an offset is still refused by name, because the distance there is measured on the order key rather than on the position.
- The seven ranking names answer: `row_number`, `rank`, `dense_rank` with its `rank_dense` alias, `percent_rank`, `cume_dist` and `ntile`. They read the peer groups rather than the frame, so a frame written around one of them is ignored, which is what the reference binary does.
- `duckdb_functions()` reports a fourth kind, `window`, for the names that can only ever be written inside an `OVER`. Writing one anywhere else is refused, and so is `DISTINCT` inside one, both in the reference binary's words.
- A frame distance that is negative is accepted and answers null instead of being refused, and one that is NULL is refused as an invalid input rather than as a binder error, which is how the reference binary reports both.
- A minimum or a maximum over a dictionary column is decided on the dictionary bytes rather than on a value per row.
- A text comparison against a dictionary column is decided once per distinct value rather than once per row.
- The compatibility corpus gained a window file with 37 records, all of them measured against the pinned binary.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.31

A patch release that binds window functions, carries `OVER` through the parser, and makes native integer storage and page buffer costs explicit. The storage format version is unchanged.

- `OVER` parses in full, with partitions, orders, every frame form, `EXCLUDE`, `IGNORE NULLS` and a `WINDOW` clause whose names are resolved and inlined where they are used.
- Window calls bind to the window operator, grouped into runs that share a partition, an order and a frame, with two identical calls in one run becoming one column and a call that disagrees getting its own operator. The operator lands after the grouping and after `HAVING`, which is where the reference binary puts it.
- The grouping rule applies inside the `OVER` as well as to the arguments, including partition keys and order keys, and every clause refusal matches the reference binary word for word, including a window in a join condition naming the `WHERE` clause rather than one of its own.
- Every integer width is stored in the native format rather than only the signed ones.
- The page buffer memset cost is recorded along with why the two obvious ways around it are worse.
- A column totals through a slice or a padded dictionary instead of one bounds checked index at a time.
- Window execution is still explicit follow-up work, so every window query in this release binds to a plan and none of them run.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.30

A patch release that broadens D3 correlated subqueries, adds non-recursive CTE inlining, establishes the window plan contract, and improves native grouped TopN metadata. The storage format version is unchanged.

- Correlated scalar aggregates and existence tests use distinct outer domains for arbitrary predicates, preserving zero counts, null results, null-safe comparisons, empty inputs, and strict scalar cardinality without evaluating the inner query once per outer row.
- Projection-only scalar correlations work over dummy, values, table, and table-function inputs, and aggregate arguments or grouping expressions can read outer values through the same set-based domain rule.
- Plain and `NOT MATERIALIZED` non-recursive CTEs inline through the existing subquery path, including chained definitions, declared column names, repeated references, nested `WITH` clauses, and CTE name precedence over catalog tables.
- The logical plan now represents windows with partition expressions, ordered keys, `ROWS`, `RANGE`, and `GROUPS` frames, all five bound forms, all four exclusions, filters, distinctness, and null treatment. Parsing, binding, and execution remain explicit follow-up work.
- Native frequency metadata can answer grouped TopN from exact page membership, builds numeric synopses in parallel, uses encoded radix counts for two-key groups, and stores bounded candidate ordinals without widening the hot grouped records.
- The committed compatibility corpus has 35 files and passes through the library, shell, isolated runner, four shards, and optimizer-disabled path.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.29

A patch release that advances D3 correlated subqueries and improves native scans and grouped aggregation. The storage format version is unchanged.

- Plan nodes and expressions carry source spans through binding and every optimizer pass, so binder and runtime expression errors can point at the relevant SQL.
- Correlated scalar, `EXISTS`, `NOT EXISTS`, `IN`, `NOT IN`, `ANY` and `ALL` equality filters become set-based joins instead of evaluating the inner query once per outer row.
- Correlated mark joins carry hidden correlation keys without moving their visible result marker. Null candidates, missing groups and filter-null behavior match DuckDB.
- Wide native row fetches reuse parallel workers across stripes, while sparse reads avoid extent prefetch that cannot pay for itself.
- Fixed-width and mixed grouped aggregates use smaller records and radix owners to reduce memory traffic during finishing.
- The committed compatibility corpus has 34 files and passes through the library, shell, isolated runner, four shards and optimizer-disabled path.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.28

A patch release that begins D3 with set-based uncorrelated subqueries and finishes the D2 session setting list. The storage format version is unchanged.

- Uncorrelated scalar subqueries become `SINGLE` joins and produce DuckDB's empty, nested, multi-column and multi-row behavior. `scalar_subquery_error_on_multiple_rows` selects the strict error or one arbitrary row while binding the plan.
- `EXISTS` and `NOT EXISTS` become one joined marker pipeline with an early limit, so the inner query runs once and stops after proving that a row exists.
- `IN`, `NOT IN`, `ANY` and `ALL` use a three-valued mark join. Matches stop early, NULL comparisons remain unknown, empty inputs keep the SQL identities, and no cross product is materialized.
- Generated result names and `DESCRIBE` output for every new subquery form match the pinned DuckDB binary.
- `allow_parser_override_extension` completes the pinned D2 session setting surface without installing a parser extension rudb does not ship.
- The committed compatibility corpus grew from 29 to 33 files and passes through the library, shell, isolated runner, four shards and optimizer-disabled path.
- The full gate passes on Rust 1.85. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.27

A patch release about SQL session compatibility, structured errors, and faster grouped distinct aggregation. The storage format version is unchanged.

- `dialect_compatibility_mode` accepts the pinned NONE and SPARK modes, preserves the accepted spelling, resets to NONE, and returns DuckDB's enum error and candidate list for any other value.
- `preserve_identifier_case` folds unquoted identifiers once during parsing under preserve, lowercase, or uppercase policy. Quoted identifiers, strings and single-quoted file paths keep their written case, and prepared statements capture the policy when they are prepared.
- `warnings_as_errors` matches DuckDB's no-logger configuration. False and reset succeed, while true returns the exact Settings Error and leaves the setting unchanged. Settings Error is now part of the public error taxonomy.
- `errors_as_json` applies to query, execute, plan, prepare and prepared execution. Structured messages preserve the typed Rust error code and carry DuckDB-style exception fields, source positions where spans exist, and core error subtypes.
- The build gate proves that language policy types stop at the binder and do not leak into the plan, optimizer or executor.
- Grouped distinct aggregation exchanges encoded count keys through radix owners and counts grouped distinct pairs in their owning partitions instead of rebuilding a shared set on one finishing thread.
- All session choices are resolved before execution, and JSON conversion runs only on an error path, so these compatibility changes add no row-loop work.
- The pinned DuckDB oracle agrees on the new committed compatibility records. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.26

A replacement patch release for 0.3.25, whose release gate stopped before publishing, with faster distinct aggregation and a stricter hot-loop check. The storage format version is unchanged.

- This release includes the SHOW resolution, SQL dialect registry, metadata tables, native scan improvements and aggregation improvements described under 0.3.25. The v0.3.25 tag exists, but no GitHub release or package was published from it.
- Distinct BIGINT aggregation assigns radix partitions to finishing workers and merges each value into one owner instead of rebuilding one set from every worker on one thread.
- The fixed aggregate exchange reads BIGINT, INTEGER and SMALLINT columns through their native signed layouts. It no longer materialises owned values in its row loop.
- The release gate recognises `Option::ok` as a standard adapter while still requiring every vector flatten to be reviewed. This is the gate correction that lets the valid random fetch flatten pass without hiding a new vector flatten.
- The full release gate and the committed compatibility corpus pass. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.25

A patch release about SHOW resolution, the SQL dialect registry, and lower aggregation and native scan costs. The storage format version is unchanged.

- `show_behavior` selects how `SHOW name` resolves a bare name. AUTO prefers an existing table and otherwise reads a setting, SETTING reads only settings, and TABLE describes only tables. Resolution happens while binding and becomes an ordinary constant plan, so execution adds no setting read or runtime branch.
- SHOW setting lookup ignores case while preserving the spelling the query wrote as the result column name. Table and setting collisions, qualified names, missing names, reset, readback and catalog metadata match the pinned DuckDB.
- A parser dialect registry owns the installed DuckDB grammar. `current_dialect` resolves through that registry, preserves the accepted spelling, resets to `duckdb`, and rejects an uninstalled dialect with DuckDB's error.
- `duckdb_dialects()` reads the registry and returns the installed `duckdb` row. `duckdb_grammar_extensions()` reports the empty registry with DuckDB's two-column schema, which is the default pinned answer. Both functions appear in `duckdb_functions()`.
- Native ClickBench scans keep scaling beyond one million rows instead of letting fixed scheduling and materialisation costs take over as the input grows.
- Singleton BIGINT distinct values stay inline instead of paying for a general set, reducing state and allocation for the common one-value group.
- A count predicate in HAVING is applied before aggregate rows are materialised, so rejected groups do not allocate and fill result values that are immediately discarded.
- The pinned DuckDB oracle agrees on every new compatibility record in this release. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.24

A patch release about zero-division policy, IEEE floating point policy, guarded timestamp casts, and faster grouped aggregation. The storage format version is unchanged.

- `null_on_division_by_zero` returns null for integer division, remainder, and interval division by zero after the session opt-in, while ordinary slash keeps DuckDB's IEEE infinity and NaN behavior. FLOAT divided by FLOAT now remains FLOAT.
- `ieee_floating_point_ops` selects IEEE results or DuckDB's bind-time zero-divisor errors for floating slash and remainder. `null_on_division_by_zero` takes precedence when both settings are changed, and floating overflow remains infinity in both modes.
- `disable_timestamptz_casts` rejects local timestamp and date conversions to TIMESTAMPTZ while binding. The check covers explicit and implicit conversions, `TRY_CAST`, comparisons, CASE, VALUES, set operations, function arguments, and INSERT, while text casts remain valid.
- Session choices are resolved into the bound plan, so these semantics add no setting reads or branches to execution hot paths.
- Grouped string keys remain encoded through aggregation instead of being materialised before the hash table. Fixed-width aggregate rows are exchanged by radix owner, so each finishing worker merges only the partitions it owns instead of every worker scanning every local table.
- Performance reports now live in rudb-bench rather than the engine repository, keeping benchmark evidence beside the harness and datasets that produced it.
- The pinned DuckDB oracle agrees on every new compatibility record in this release. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.23

A patch release about session-controlled integer division, constant sort keys, and regex operator matching. The storage format version is unchanged.

- `integer_division` changes slash between integer operands from floating division to truncating integer division, including the generated column name. Decimal and floating operands keep floating division. The binder resolves the setting once, so execution does not read session state.
- `order_by_non_integer_literal` keeps DuckDB's default error for constant non-integer sort keys and accepts them after the session opt-in. The binder removes accepted constant keys, so sorting adds no setting read or runtime work.
- `regex_match_operator_semantics` selects partial or full matching for `~`, `!~`, `~*`, and `!~*`. `SIMILAR TO` remains a full match in both modes, and the parser now keeps `!~` distinct from `NOT SIMILAR TO`. DuckDB emits a deprecation warning when this setting changes, while rudb does not have a warning channel yet.
- The pinned DuckDB oracle agrees on every integer division, order literal, and regex semantics record added with this release. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.22

A patch release about session time zones, zoned timestamp rendering, sort defaults, and smaller hot paths in the optimizer and grouped string aggregates. The storage format version is unchanged.

- A session owns its time zone and starts with the host zone, while `SET TimeZone`, `RESET TimeZone`, and `current_setting('TimeZone')` use DuckDB's names and errors. The one argument `age(timestamp)` overload reads that zone once while binding the statement and turns the session instant into local civil time before doing calendar arithmetic.
- Casting `TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE` to `VARCHAR` renders the instant in the session zone, including the offset that applies at that instant across daylight saving transitions. Prepared expressions carry a parsed zone, so neither the zone database nor a setting lock is touched per row or per chunk. The optimizer leaves that cast unfolded because a constant folder without a session cannot choose the right text.
- `default_order` and `default_null_order` control an omitted sort direction and null placement with DuckDB's accepted aliases, readback values, reset behavior, and errors. The binder resolves both settings into every sort key once, and an explicit direction or `NULLS FIRST` or `NULLS LAST` still wins. The ordinary default is now `NULLS LAST` in both directions.
- Optimizer pass selection keeps its normalised setting name local instead of allocating and retaining a second copy. Grouped string minimum and maximum share the output arena that already belongs to the result vector instead of allocating one buffer per group.
- The pinned DuckDB oracle agrees on all session time zone and sort semantics records added with this release. No DuckDB bug was found in this work.

## 0.3.21

A patch release about the native columnar format, cheaper grouped aggregates, narrower late materialisation and the `PRAGMA` statement. The storage format version is unchanged.

- The native columnar format writes inserts as stripes instead of keeping a row store behind a columnar name. Initial inserts stream into bounded pages, the scan prunes stripes from their zone maps, and a late read fetches only the selected values from the pages it needs.
- Native TopN keeps row references until the final result asks for values, so rows that do not survive the limit are never materialised. Page checksums use the processor's CRC instruction where it exists and keep the portable fallback for every other target.
- Numeric grouped `count`, `sum` and `avg` keep their common state compact and allocate the wide total only after an overflow. String group keys share radix tables, and grouped string minimum and maximum compare borrowed bytes without taking ownership for every row.
- Flat vector slicing copies contiguous values and validity words directly. Bit-packed values are unpacked eight at a time at a width known to the compiler, which removes a branch from the inner loop.
- Regex host extraction now agrees with DuckDB when the path contains a newline.
- `PRAGMA name` and `PRAGMA name(args)` lower to the existing `pragma_*` table function during transform, so the executor has no pragma operator and does no extra per-row work. Errors keep the spelling the user wrote, and `PRAGMA name = value` reaches the existing setting path.
- Fifteen pragma corpus records agree with DuckDB `cc7e7bac7f`. The shell comparison on an idle machine passed every record, but they were all below the harness timing floor, so no resource ratio is claimed for them.

## 0.3.20

A patch release about the system catalog, the standard views, the two pragmas that take a name, and a parquet scan that hands out smaller pieces of work. The storage format version is unchanged.

- A session starts with the three databases the pin starts with rather than one. `memory` is the one a person creates in, `system` holds what the engine ships with in `main`, `information_schema` and `pg_catalog`, and `temp` is there and empty. That is five schemas, which is what `duckdb_schemas()` returns on the pin from a session that has attached nothing. Resolving a name grew a search path, which is what makes a written `information_schema.tables` and a bare `duckdb_views` find anything at all.
- Twelve views go in `system`, and every one of them is upstream's own SQL rather than a rule reimplemented here. DuckDB defines all 47 of its internal views as ordinary queries over the `duckdb_*` table functions and reports the whole statement back through `information_schema.views.view_definition`, so the body of each one is readable off the pin and the rows agree by construction. Nothing in it is a rudb invention: a view is either upstream's text or it is absent, so a client that finds one can trust it. The other six standard views wait on `duckdb_constraints()`, `unnest`, `generate_series` and lambdas, the four `sqlite_*` ones wait on `duckdb_indexes()`, and the 22 `pg_catalog` ones are their own piece of work.
- The wrapper and the table function are different questions, which looks like a mistake until you see the definitions. `duckdb_tables` is a view over `duckdb_tables()` with the engine's own rows taken out, so the bare name is what somebody made and the parentheses are what is really there. That is why `information_schema.tables` lists two things in a fresh session and `information_schema.views` lists all of them.
- `pragma_table_info()` and `pragma_show()`, the two members of that family that take a name. Both are answered while the query is bound rather than by an operator, because the columns of a table are settled by the time the name has resolved, so the rows are a `VALUES` node and nothing was added to the executor. That means an alias, a column list on the alias, a qualified column reference and a filter all work, because none of them know they are looking at a pragma. Describing a view binds it, which is how a view comes to have columns at all, so `pragma_table_info('duckdb_views')` fills that view's column count the way a select over it would.
- A name written inside a string is split under the identifier rule now, which is what `rudb_parse::identifier_parts` does, so `main.t` and `MEMORY.MAIN.T` both find the same table and a quoted part keeps the dots that are inside it.
- A parquet scan cuts a row group into morsels where the file lets it. A row group is far too large a unit of work: DuckDB writes a hundred and twenty two thousand rows into one, so the million row ClickBench file has nine, and nine pieces of work is nine busy threads on a machine with thirty two and one straggler deciding when everybody is finished. `Reader::split_rows` reads a row range inside a group by stepping over the pages before its first row, reading their headers and none of their bodies.
- It cuts only when there are more threads than row groups and only when the cut is worth what it wastes, which is asked in bytes rather than in rows. A column with four distinct values packs a whole row group into one page of two bit dictionary indices, so the longest page of a file measured in rows is always the cheapest column in it, and asking in rows made the whole thing a no op on every file it was given.
- Every morsel of a row group points into the same dictionary page, so the readers split off one file share the pages they decode through a slot per column chunk, held exactly as long as a morsel of that group is alive. Without that the suite at one thread went from 2.9 seconds to 6.6, and the cutting decoded 35 MB of dictionary where reading it uncut decodes 10.
- What that buys, median of five over a million rows on the 32 thread machine: nothing at all on the file ClickBench ships, because DuckDB writes one page per column chunk and a chunk of one page cannot be cut below a row group by stepping over pages, and 21.9 percent on the same data written with 64 KB pages, 900.1 ms down to 702.6. Sharing decoded data pages between the morsels of a group is the only way into the first file and it is a separate change.
- Snappy reads a copy's tag out of a table instead of branching on it. Decompression is the largest single thing a ClickBench scan does, 55.4 ms of a 104 ms scan on `hits-1m-snappy.parquet` at one thread, and a callgrind run says `decompress_into` is 40 percent of the instructions in the whole query and 54 percent of its mispredicted branches, six million of them on a file of a million rows. The branch that goes is the one asking which of the three copy forms a tag byte is, which a real page gets wrong often enough to be worth paying for and not often enough to be free at 87 percent one byte offsets. Two kilobytes of table, built by a `const fn` so the three forms are still written out once each. Worth 7.0 percent on a real 10 MB `URL` page with an IQR of 1.2 percent.
- The UTF-8 validator is checked against the standard library for three and four byte sequences, which nothing covered before. A validator that accepts an overlong form or half a surrogate pair is not a slow validator, it is two decoders disagreeing about what a string says, and `rudb_common::utf8::valid` is what decides whether bytes off a file are text. Three tests: every leading byte against every second byte and a third from each side of every range boundary, the same for four byte sequences, and the three byte cross product again at every offset from zero to eight ASCII bytes in, which is where the word at a time loop and the sequence reader meet in the middle of a character. Narrowing what `0xF0` allows its second byte to be throws away every character from U+30000 up, leaves all fifteen of the old tests passing and fails the new four byte one on its own. No change to what the module does.

## 0.3.19

A patch release about the two engine tables, the session context and the four phases of a grouped aggregate. The storage format version is unchanged.

- The fourteen names that answer about the connection rather than about the query, which is `now()`, `current_date`, `current_timestamp`, `current_schema`, `current_user`, `current_database` and the eight other spellings of those eight answers. Ten of them take a bare keyword and fourteen take a call, and the five that only take one of the two are the pin's own split: `current_time`, `current_timestamp`, `localtime` and `localtimestamp` have no function behind them, and `now`, `today`, `current_database`, `current_localtime`, `current_localtimestamp`, `get_current_time`, `get_current_timestamp` and `transaction_timestamp` have no keyword.
- The binder folds every one of them into a constant, so one statement sees one instant however many rows read it. That is what the pin reports as `CONSISTENT_WITHIN_QUERY` and what it answers for a count of the distinct values of `now()` over three rows. A column of the same name beats the keyword and a name two tables both carry is still the ambiguity error, both measured rather than assumed, which is why the scope is asked about the word before anything folds rather than after a lookup fails.
- `TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE` and `TIME WITH TIME ZONE` became values in their own right rather than a plain timestamp wearing a type that says the zone. The plan checks that a constant's type matches the value it holds, so there was no way round it, and it was the right shape anyway, since a zoned value prints its offset and an unzoned one does not. That runs through the vector, the comparison, the casts, the spill file, the group key and the arithmetic, and the arithmetic keeps the zone the way upstream does: `now()` plus an interval is zoned, `now()` minus a timestamp or a date is an interval, `date_trunc` of a zoned moment is zoned, and `age` of two of them is an interval.
- `duckdb_optimizers()` and `duckdb_extensions()`, which are the two tables a tool reads on connect to find out what it is talking to, and the two places rudb has to answer about itself rather than copy what the pin says. The optimizer table returns all forty four names because that is the set `SET disabled_optimizers` takes, which it already took, and the eight passes rudb has written are a subset of it. The extension table carries the pin's names, descriptions and aliases word for word and rudb's own two booleans, so `parquet` and `core_functions` are loaded and `icu`, `json`, `shell` and `autocomplete` are not, each for a stated reason. A row for an extension rudb has not got is worth returning, because false is an answer and an empty table makes the client guess.
- A grouped aggregate reports four phases instead of one. It folds rows into a hash table, scatters a table across the radix partitions, merges one worker's table into another and emits the finished table, and those are four pieces of code with four different fixes. Two of the four were not merely unlabelled, they were invisible: the merge and the emit run on threads the aggregate scopes for itself, and a thread like that was counted nowhere. `stage::gained` is how such a thread hands its reading back.
- The first thing that measured said, over the ClickBench suite at a million rows: the aggregate's CPU doubles when it is given eight threads, from 943.1 ms to 1924.2 ms, and the whole of the increase is the merge of worker local tables, which does not exist at one thread. That is what the partitioned build cost to get rid of the lock contention, and nothing measured it until now.
- An ambiguous column error quotes its candidates the way the pin does, which is double quotes around the column name and single ones around the two suggestions under it.

## 0.3.18

A patch release about the fifth catalog table, a deparser under it, and the aggregate getting its worker local tables back. The storage format version is unchanged.

- `duckdb_views()` answers, which finishes the five catalog tables. The column that made it a job of its own is `sql`, because the pin does not report the text somebody typed there. It reports the whole `CREATE VIEW` written back out, so a view created with odd spacing and a comment in the middle comes back normalised and without the comment, and that was measured both ways round. `column_count` is the length of the column cache on the entry and is as stale as that cache is, which is what the pin reports too. `is_bound` is true on every row, because rudb binds a view's body at creation and cannot get an entry into the catalog without one, and the pin reaches false by restoring a database file, which rudb has no way to do yet. So the column is answered rather than faked.
- A view says what its own columns are, which is what `duckdb_columns()` needed to list a view alongside a table. The decision came off the pin rather than out of an argument: upstream keeps a cache written when the body is bound, both tables read it back, and it is stale in between, so a view over `SELECT * FROM t` reports two columns after a third is added to `t` and three after anything reads the view. Being compatible means going stale the same way. It costs nothing to keep, because the binder already worked those columns out twice and threw the answer away both times.
- `crates/rudb-parse/src/deparse.rs` writes a parsed statement back out the way the pin writes it. Two hundred and sixty two view bodies were measured against the pin and it agrees on two hundred and fifty eight. The four left over are three divergences and every one of them is in the transform rather than in the printer: `LIMIT ALL` is dropped where the pin writes `LIMIT NULL`, `^` and `**` are collapsed into one operator where the pin keeps whichever was written all the way down to the function name in the error message, and a subscript is rewritten into the call it stands for before the printer sees it. Those are #623, #624 and #625.
- Type names turned out to be two rules and not one. A name the SQL standard spells is resolved and written back under the one name its type has, so `int` is `INTEGER`, `numeric(5)` is `DECIMAL(5)` and `character varying` is `VARCHAR`. Every other name is written back exactly as it was typed, case and all and without quotes, so `text` stays `text` and `int4` stays `int4`. The split is the grammar showing through, since the standard names are the ones the grammar has rules for and everything else is a name the parser hands to the catalog to look up later. `json` is the single exception in the whole list and comes back quoted, because it is the only name that is both a keyword and one the parser resolves itself. All of it was measured a name at a time.
- The aggregate has its worker local radix tables back, which is DuckDB's design and what #350 asks for. They were turned off in #614 because their merge lost counts, and the merge was not losing counts: a whole worker table was being dropped on the floor. `Aggregate::combine` deposits the tables an instance kept per partition and then hands over the single table it was still holding, and on the local path that hand scattered into a local variable the deposit had already read. One line puts the deposit after the hand. `crates/rudb/tests/grouped.rs` is new and says what a parallel group by has to produce whatever it did with the threads, seven tests of which five fail without that line, the first counting 38,912 rows of 49,152.
- Over the suite that took 841 ms of lock waiting in the aggregate down to 220 ms, and the CPU with it, because a fold into a table nobody else can touch does not take a lock at all. The clock only moved 14 ms, and the reason is worth saying plainly rather than dressing up: FileScan is now the critical path at 1923.6 ms against the aggregate's 1438.3, so making the aggregate faster has stopped shortening the query. The next work is in the scan.
- A bucket in the group by hash table carries a salt, so a probe step is one load rather than two. It used to hold a slot number, with the hash of each group in a separate vector, and the second address was only known once the first load had landed, which on a table larger than the cache is two misses in a row for a step that usually rejects the bucket anyway. A bucket is eight bytes now, the slot in the low half and the top thirty two bits of the hash in the high half. The top bits and not the bottom ones, because the bottom ones are the bucket number and two keys only meet in a bucket by already agreeing on those. What gets past the salt goes straight to the key, since the comparison is exact and a thirty two bit salt lets through about one step in four billion, where reading the stored hash first would cost a load from memory on all the others.
- A string group key goes into the answer as bytes rather than through a `String`. The output side of a group by was doing a `to_vec`, a `String::from_utf8` and then a second copy into the vector it was going to all along, so an allocation, two copies and a validation for every group. The validation was the part that was not merely slow, because those bytes were validated on the way into the string column the scan built and nothing between there and here does anything but copy them. callgrind put `str::from_utf8` at 4.68 percent of a `GROUP BY URL` over 515,958 calls, one per group, all answering a question that had already been answered.
- A string is validated as UTF-8 in one pass instead of two. `push_in_place` ran `is_ascii` and then `str::from_utf8` over whatever the first did not settle, and on the ClickBench URL column that is nearly every string: the ASCII walk stops at the first byte of the Cyrillic in the query string and the real validator starts again from the front. `rudb_common::utf8::valid` steps eight bytes at a time while the bytes are ASCII and byte at a time through the sequences that are not, and it accepts the set Rust accepts, which is the set Unicode defines: no overlong form, no surrogate, nothing above U+10FFFF. The tests check it against `str::from_utf8` over every sequence length, every byte on its own, and exhaustively over all 65,536 pairs of bytes.
- The default thread ceiling is physical cores rather than logical ones on Linux. On a host with sixteen physical cores and thirty two logical CPUs the extra sixteen workers bought nothing and cost memory and contention. The topology is read among the CPUs the process is allowed on, other platforms and hosts without topology data keep the logical count, and an explicit `SET threads` still overrides it. On that host the suite went from 6.854 s of child CPU and 214.3 MiB of peak RSS to 5.723 s and 175.1 MiB with the query time unchanged.
- Over the 41 ClickBench queries that run, at a million rows and eight threads on gamingpc, the release goes from 1064.7 ms to 909.6 ms of summed statement time and from 1.090 s to 0.970 s of process wall. That is still 1.85 times the pin on its own storage format, so this is a set of regressions fixed and a set of passes over memory removed rather than the ten times target being met.

## 0.3.17

A patch release about the four catalog tables, the late materialised read and settings read as values. The storage format version is unchanged.

- The catalog hands out its own oids, and `duckdb_databases()`, `duckdb_schemas()`, `duckdb_tables()` and `duckdb_columns()` answer out of it rather than out of a list the binary was compiled with. A client reads one of these tables and joins it to another on the number, so a null oid is not a small divergence, it is the table failing at the one job it has. `Catalog::stamp()` is a counter, every database, schema, table and view carries a number from it, an entry that is not in a catalog carries `DETACHED`, and a test says one entry carries one number in every table that names it and no number is handed out twice. The numbers are not upstream's and are not meant to be, since upstream's are an accident of one process's startup ordering.
- All four are byte for byte the pin on the statements they were checked against, including the parts nobody would guess. `column_index` counts from one. `numeric_precision` on an integer counts bits rather than digits, so an `INTEGER` reports 32 with a radix of two and a `DOUBLE` reports 53, and `DECIMAL` is the one type where the number means digits. Every unsigned integer reports no precision at all, which looks like an oversight upstream and is reproduced anyway, because a client reading these reads them from DuckDB's side of the comparison. `character_maximum_length` is null even on a `VARCHAR` the DDL gave a length, because DuckDB parses the modifier and then drops it. The `sql` column is a deparse of the entry rather than the text somebody typed, which was measured both ways round, so the quoting rule the binder used for a generated column name moved into `rudb-parse` where the keyword table it asks lives.
- `duckdb_views()` is the one table in that box still missing, because a view in rudb's catalog is a name and the text of its body, so neither its column count nor the deparsed `sql` can be worked out without binding it. That is a decision about what the catalog stores rather than a row builder, so it is #608.
- `current_setting()` answers, which is settings readable as values rather than only settable. The one overload the pin declares returns `ANY`, so the name decides the type and the name is only known once somebody writes it, which is why the binder folds the call before there is a plan. Upstream folds it too, which an `EXPLAIN` of a query calling it shows. `current_setting('threads')` is a `BIGINT`, `current_setting('memory_limit')` is a `VARCHAR`, aliases read the setting they point at, the name is matched ignoring case, and all three error paths are the pin's sentences. The settings used to be read only for a plan that mentioned `duckdb_settings()`, which is no longer something the engine can know before binding, so they are read once per statement and `Settings::session` takes two locks for it instead of six.
- A size truncates the digit it drops, where it used to round. 9,751,000 bytes is 9.2993 mebibytes and the pin prints `9.2 MiB` for it, so `SET memory_limit = '1GB'` read back as `953.7 MiB` here against `953.6 MiB` there. The same formatter is behind both a read of `memory_limit` and an out of memory message on both engines, and the truncation was confirmed on the pin in three sizes and in a real out of memory message, so the shared formatter changed rather than a settings only renderer being added. The arithmetic is in whole tenths rather than in floating point, because a division by 1024 puts a number that is exactly 9.3 a hair under it often enough to matter at one decimal place.
- A late materialised read fetches only the values it asked for. `SELECT * FROM hits WHERE URL LIKE '%google%' ORDER BY EventTime LIMIT 10` was the slowest query in the suite at 156.1 ms with 126.7 ms of it in the Fetch operator, which was reading 141.5 MB and spending 425 ms of worker CPU to produce ten rows. A page holds around a hundred and ten thousand values and the fetch keeps ten, so `Page::decode_at` walks the definition levels to the wanted positions and builds only those, which on its own took the fetch to 62.1 ms. The shortcut is for plain non boolean pages read at strictly increasing positions and everything else falls back to decoding and gathering, so it is never wrong, only sometimes no faster, and plain is where the time is because a hundred and seventy eight of that file's two hundred and twenty megabytes are plain.
- The same fetch puts every column of every touched row group into one list, widest first, and lets threads pull from it rather than dealing them a share of one row group at a time. Five row groups meant five hundred and twenty five column chunks with only a hundred and five ever in flight, so threads waited on the widest column of the row group in hand while four row groups of work went untouched. That took the fetch to 35.3 ms. The list is bounded by bytes rather than by jobs and that part is not an optimisation: unbounded, sixteen workers all starting on the widest columns in the file cost a hundred megabytes of peak RSS for the twenty seven milliseconds it bought, which is a bad trade in a project whose goal is ten times less resource. A fetch may hold thirty two megabytes of chunk at once, and a chunk wider than the whole budget still runs, alone, because refusing it would mean never finishing.
- Over the 41 ClickBench queries that run, at a million rows and eight threads on gamingpc, the suite goes from 1064.7 ms to 959.5 ms of summed statement time, from 1.090 s to 0.990 s of process wall and from 445 MB to 397 MB of peak RSS. That query on its own goes from 0.290 s and 336 MB to 0.190 s and 301 MB.
- One thing found while measuring and not fixed: one run in sixteen of the suite returned 106527 for `COUNT(DISTINCT SearchPhrase)` where every other run of either binary returned 107703. It is a pre-existing intermittent wrong answer rather than anything this release introduced.

## 0.3.16

A patch release about the four introspection tables and the parallel aggregate. The storage format version is unchanged.

- All four of `duckdb_functions()`, `duckdb_types()`, `duckdb_settings()` and `duckdb_keywords()` answer, which is the first box of D2 done and means a tool that introspects on connect gets real rows rather than an error. `duckdb_types()` is 93 rows and every one of them matches the pin exactly across the name, the oid, the logical type, the category, the modifier parameters and their types, including row order, which sorts case insensitively with the underscore ranked after the letters. The eleven the pin has and this does not are ten names for types rudb has no vector for and `geometry` a second time for its `crs` modifier, and they come back when the types do.
- `duckdb_functions()` is 104 rows, 7 aggregate, 83 scalar and 14 table, and it is the one of the four that cannot be row for row compatible. rudb's signature table resolves by shape, so `+` is one entry saying both arguments promote, where the pin carries an implementation per pair of numeric types and reports 44 rows for it. So this reports one row per name per argument count and declares a promoted argument with the pin's own generic spellings, `T` for a type variable and `ANY` for an unconstrained one. Writing 44 concrete rows before there is a kernel behind any of them would be writing the wrong 44 rows. The table reports `system.main` even though rudb has no catalog called `system`, because that is where the pin puts every builtin and where every client query looks for them.
- `duckdb_settings()` is five rows for three settings, because the pin gives an alias a row of its own, and it is the first of these tables whose rows are not all decided at compile time. What each setting is now comes from a `Session` at rank 0 in `rudb-common`, threaded into the executor, for the same reason `Cancel` is down there: it is filled in at rank 13 and read at rank 12 and no two crates in between can see each other. `max_memory` and `worker_threads` now work as second spellings of `memory_limit` and `threads`, since a table listing a name the engine refuses would be a table that lies. `typed_value` is a `VARCHAR` here and a `VARIANT` in the pin, which is the one deliberate divergence and is written down where the table is defined.
- The parallel grouped aggregate gives every instance its own table per partition instead of folding into sixteen shared ones behind a mutex each, which is DuckDB's design. On ClickBench at eight threads the aggregate's wall time over the suite goes from 2415.8 ms to 1515.2 and its CPU from 1539.2 to 1272.7, and on `GROUP BY WatchID, ClientIP` the ratio of wall to CPU falls from 2.11 to 1.14, so the contention is gone. End to end the suite does not move, because FileScan is now the critical path at 2002.9 ms of wall against 1982.9 of CPU and the blocked threads had been blocked while other threads read Parquet. The old shape got worse with every thread added and this one does not, which is the reason to have it before the scan gets faster. Local tables are given up when the aggregate cannot afford them, and the decision to use them is made once and pessimistically, because an aggregate that backs out later wants the most memory it will ever want at the moment it is already short.
- `length` over a list is still refused, which turned up while dumping the function table and is filed as #602.

## 0.3.15

A patch release about the three nested vectors, zone maps and two wrong answers. The storage format version is unchanged.

- The engine has a `LIST`, a `STRUCT` and a `MAP` vector, which is the shared blocker under most of D2 cleared. A list is a child vector of every element of every row plus a start and a length per row, rather than Arrow's n+1 offsets, because offsets require the rows to be contiguous and in order and a start with a length survives a gather and a filter. A struct is one child per field, each as long as the parent, so a cut cuts every child, a gather gathers every child and reading one field is free. A map is a list whose child is a two field struct, which is what DuckDB, Arrow and Parquet all store, so the bytes of a map are a list's and the logical type is the only thing that remembers the difference, which makes the cut and the gather inherited rather than written.
