What a query can see so far is one line each, a cast of a null, because the parser builds no nested literal yet. `ARRAY` and `UNION` are the two forms still with no vector.
- `Value::Map` holds its key and value types boxed, with a test asserting the enum is still 64 bytes. A `LogicalType` is 32 and two inline would have needed 96, so every `BOOLEAN` read out as a value anywhere in the system would have got 32 bytes wider to pay for one arm. Unboxing them now fails at that test rather than turning up later as a memory number nobody can account for.
- Tables in memory keep per chunk zone maps, and a scan skips a chunk a filter has already ruled out. Six of the forty three ClickBench queries have a filter this can answer, all six skip 75 percent of the chunks, and query 37 goes from 11.05 ms to 4.36 ms reading 247,265 rows instead of 999,975. The whole suite is the number rather than the query: the six save 37 ms and building the maps costs 60 to 90, so one pass is a wash and it pays from the second on, which the real ClickBench protocol does run. The reason it is not more is that `hits` is not clustered on `CounterID`, so 7381 rows are spread over 121 chunks of 489 where a sort key would put them in four, and that is the open F2 item this is the other half of. Same file and same statement at a million rows, rudb loads in 1.02 s against DuckDB's 1.18 while building a full set of maps, and the queries are 1.01 s against 1.59.
- The bounds test that decides a skip moved to rank 0, and writing its tests turned up a wrong answer it had all along. A Parquet footer, a table in memory and a block of the storage format all keep the same two numbers and all want the same answer from them, and rudb-storage and rudb-parquet are both rank 5 so neither can see the other. The old form turned an undecidable comparison into a skip, so a `NaN` bound or a constant from another domain dropped rows from an answer. Every arm requires a known ordering in a stated direction now, which fixes the Parquet path too.
- `SELECT NULL::UINT8` was a `UBIGINT` and is a `UTINYINT` in the pin, which is off by a factor of 2^56 in the range the type accepts. The alias table had been written by extending a pattern rather than by reading the pin: `int1` through `int8` count bytes, `int16` through `int128` count bits, DuckDB has both series on the signed side so `int8` and `int64` are one type, and on the unsigned side it has only the bit counting one so `uint8` is a byte wide and `uint1` is not a type at all. Checking the other 86 names the same way added ten and removed seven, and the seven removed are names this engine invented, which are queries that work here and fail there.
- `duckdb_keywords()` is generated from the vendored grammar, so it is right by construction and gains a word the day the pin adds one. All 505 rows match the pinned binary and the four categories come out at 75 reserved, 339 unreserved, 55 column name and 36 type function. Six words are in two categories, which is why 499 words are 505 rows. The operator underneath it is now shared, because D2 has about a dozen more tables of the same kind and the column resolution, the chunking and the morsel handout should be written once.
- A string page becomes the column's arena instead of being copied, which keeps one run per thread. On `hits` that block is ten and a half megabytes taken from the allocator and given back nine times for one scan of one column, and glibc's handling of a block that size is most of what it costs. A count with a predicate on `URL` goes from 110.53 ms to 107.08 with decompression from 57.12 to 54.23, and peak resident size from 38.5 MB to 34.6.
- The Parquet reader knows where the page index is, and on every file this engine is measured against the answer is nowhere. Per page bounds live in a column index and per page byte offsets in an offset index, and the footer holds four numbers saying where they are rather than the structures themselves, which is the format doing the right thing because on `hits` they are larger than the rest of the footer put together and a query wants them for two columns out of 105. The `hits.parquet` ClickBench distributes carries no page index and DuckDB v1.4.1 writes none, so page level skipping cannot be built against this benchmark, and that is a test rather than a note so the day a writer starts emitting one the test changes.

## 0.3.14

A patch release about the string cascade, the Snappy decoder and the compatibility harness. The storage format version is unchanged.

- The string cascade has a match finder in it. Every encoding in that crate was a local transform, so none of them could say that a forty character path segment appeared six hundred values ago, and on a chunk of 122,880 sorted ClickBench URLs the cascade got 3.0 times where ZSTD got 6.53 over the identical bytes. LZ77 with a hash chain, handing its three streams to the encoders that already existed rather than to a Huffman coder nobody has written, takes that to 5.4. The split was measured before it was built: byte aligned tokens get 3.57 and the same tokens through the integer cascade get 4.40, so what makes matching pay here is the bitpacking and frame of reference the crate already had. Adding it to the shared table path as well inverted where sharing wins, because the table now sees only the literal runs the matcher did not cover.
- A run of Snappy elements decides once whether it fits instead of once per element. A callgrind run over a count with a predicate on the `URL` column puts half the query's billion instructions in the decompressor, at 47 instructions and 12 branches for every nine bytes produced, and most of those branches are range tests that were always going to pass. A copy writes at most 64 bytes and a short literal at most 60, so an element with 64 bytes left on both sides cannot run off either.
- `cargo xtask compress` can read a page off a real file with `--page PATH`. It had been reporting 2859 MiB/s on a fifteen kilobyte committed fixture while the same decoder on the same machine read 786 MB/s inside a query, and bench rule ten says a micro number never appears without the end to end number it explains. The fixture is small enough that every copy reaches back into L1, and on a real ten and a half megabyte page 78 percent of the copies reach back between one kilobyte and sixty four. Pointed at that page the table reads 1739 MiB/s against the query's 1769, which is agreement rather than a factor of 3.6.
- Aggregate string keys use a `u32` end offset rather than a `usize`. Radix partition payloads are bounded well below 4 GiB, so the index width was twice what it needed to be, and a partition that exceeds the bound now reports a memory error rather than wrapping. Over the 43 ClickBench queries at a million rows the peak resident size is 282 MiB against DuckDB's 312 native and 653 through Parquet.
- Statement coverage is computed rather than printed as not measured yet. It is one of the three level two denominators in section 1.1, and nothing could answer either half of it before: what the thirty six statement kinds are, and which one a given record is. `rudb_parse::alternatives` reads the kinds off the generated rule table so the denominator comes out of the vendored grammar rather than out of a constant somebody has to remember to edit, and `rudb::statement_kind` asks the parser rather than looking at the first word, which is the only thing that gets `WITH x AS (...) INSERT INTO t SELECT * FROM x` right. Its first value is zero, because thirty five of the thirty six kinds have at least one record that failed and one has no record at all, and the page says so in a sentence beside the per kind table.
- The three resource ratios are on the number page, as medians with the interquartile range at three granularities. They come off the benchmark corpus and are carried, because a sqllogictest record only means anything under a session that replayed every record before it.
- The hash modes are decided, with the count that decides them. At the pin 231 of the 34329 query records store a digest and 212 of those are in files an ordinary run leaves out, so a run sees 19 hashed records in 15 files, and there is no hash-threshold line anywhere in the corpus. Nineteen records cannot move a percentage printed to one decimal place, so requiring a live DuckDB for them would cost the property that makes a corpus run something CI can do on every commit. What the rule protects against instead is the harness growing a hash mode of its own.
- Sharding the corpus run is written down, and it buys nothing today. 364 processor seconds over 4106 files, one file is 125 of them and seven are 310 of the 364, and a run cannot finish before its slowest file, so two machines sharded took 3 minutes 9 against 2 minutes 14 for the faster of them alone. Two findings came out of it on their own: a shard is an index into a sorted list of files and the first run had the two machines at different commits of upstream, and the page is machine dependent because the statement clock is a wall clock.
- Coverage as feedback for the generators has its first answer. It reaches 32.4 percent of the 20171 lines of the query path, which is not a number this project publishes, and it names 22 files that nothing entered at all. The largest are the group, aggregate, join, sort, top n and set operation paths, which says the generated queries never aggregate, never join, never sort and never take a union.

## 0.3.13

A patch release about Parquet reading and about where generated statements come from. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a block.

- `cargo xtask parquet` measures what a Parquet read costs in rudb next to DuckDB on the one path both engines already share. Both are run as a subprocess over a script of SQL rather than one linked and one shelled out to, so there is no argument about which side a difference came from, and process start is subtracted by running an empty script first. The shapes are a ladder, so the difference between two rows says what the stage between them cost: the footer alone, then one integer column, then ten, then one string, then four, then a predicate under the aggregate, then every column into a table. Two of the entries below came off the list it produced.
- A page of definition levels with nothing null in it is answered from its run header instead of decoded. An optional column writes those levels as an RLE and bit packing hybrid stream, and a page with no nulls is one repeated run, a header and a value, two bytes for twenty thousand rows. We were decoding it into a vector with one entry per row and then walking that vector twice more, so a file of a hundred columns that some writer declared nullable because its writer declares everything nullable cost four bytes a row a column and three passes to find out there was nothing to find. `Hybrid::whole_run_of` asks the question first and leaves the reader standing on the run it looked at when the answer is no, which is the same shape DuckDB's `RleBpDecoder` uses and for the same reason.
- A query whose rows are going into a table is not flattened first. Draining a query happens on one thread while everything above it is parallel, and part of draining was flattening every column, which exists to protect a caller outside the engine who reads one value at a time. A table has heard of a dictionary vector, so `CREATE TABLE AS SELECT` and `INSERT INTO SELECT` were paying for the largest copy there is on the one thread that is not shared. Over ClickBench `hits` at a million rows that copy was the whole of the gap: single threaded the two engines are within ten percent of each other, and the pool bought us 1.8 times against DuckDB's 5.4.
- Statements come out of the grammar table as well as going into it. `rudb::generate` walks the same 1088 rule table the matcher walks, in the other direction, weighting each choice by the cheapest text its alternatives can be written down in, which is one fixpoint computed once and does three jobs: the cheap alternatives come up more often, a run that is over budget terminates by construction, and the cost table is checkable against the walk it describes. What bounds the recursion is how many times one rule is on the path rather than the depth, because the expression rules are a chain of twenty precedence levels and a depth bound tight enough to stop nesting is spent before it reaches a leaf. `cargo xtask generate` is the instrument: 192,460 of 200,000 statements from the `Statement` rule parse, at 155,564 written and 56,921 parsed a second.
- Pointed at both engines in `tamnd/rudb-compat`, that generator found nothing in the direction it was built for and a lot in the other one, and both facts are written down in the spec. It writes from the same table the matcher reads, so it cannot write a statement rudb refuses, which means the gap column is a check on the construction rather than a compatibility number. The other direction found 300 statements from the whole grammar and 1264 from the query rule that rudb parses and DuckDB does not, and they say one thing repeatedly: DuckDB checks things while it parses that its own grammar allows.
- Every generated run in the harness records what it was measured against. A seed reproduces a run against the rudb, the DuckDB and the generator that happened to be on the machine, and all three of those move, so the four generated modes now print the six provenance fields and the command that reproduces the run, and append a row to one series with a mode column.

## 0.3.12

A patch release about two fuzz targets and about where the encoder spends its seconds. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here changes what a block looks like on disk, only which encoding gets chosen for it and how long the choosing takes.

- `SELECT 1 HAVING FALSE` came back with a row and should come back with none. Filter pushdown was moving a `HAVING` predicate below an aggregate with no group keys, and an aggregate like that answers exactly one row whatever arrives at it, so a predicate under it decides what that row is computed from and a predicate over it decides whether the row is there at all. Those are different questions and the pass had been swapping one for the other since it was written. Nothing pushes below an ungrouped aggregate now. It is filed as #554 and it was found while reducing a fuzz failure rather than by the fuzzer, which reported only that the passes would not settle.
- The binder and the optimizer are fuzzed on generated ASTs. The target builds a tree out of the fuzzer's bytes bottom up, so a child exists before the node that names it and an index that points at nothing cannot be spelled, which is why it does not derive `Arbitrary` on node types that are arenas of `u32`. It found five bugs and all five are fixed here: a binder panic on `USING (id, id)`, and four ways for the pass sequence to stop settling, which were two folded filters meeting as `NULL AND NULL`, a substituted group key leaving a `CAST(NULL AS BOOLEAN)` behind the rewriter's turn, the distinct aggregate rewrite running ahead of folding and seeing two arguments where a second run sees one, and a predicate derived from an equality being put back next to the copy an earlier run derived. Folding now runs first in `PASSES`, filter pushdown folds what its own substitution builds, and a filter drops a conjunct it already holds unless the expression is volatile. An hour on sixteen cores after the fixes came to 101,294,027 executions with no crash and no artifact.
- The front end is fuzzed on raw bytes, through the tokenizer, the matcher over the 1088 rule table and the transformer rather than stopping at the first of the three. It also checks what the tokenizer promises its callers, which is that spans arrive in order, do not overlap, stay inside the query and land on character boundaries. Half an hour on sixteen cores came to 40,555,581 executions and found nothing, which is the outcome to expect for a front end a corpus and a round trip test already cover. Anything a later run finds goes in `crates/rudb-parse/tests/crashers/` and is replayed by a test that needs no nightly and no fuzzer.
- `cargo xtask encode` says what the encoder costs, per column and per candidate. The chooser is exhaustive, so it encodes every candidate that applies in full and keeps the smallest, and the second table it prints splits the seconds across candidates with how many chunks offered each one and how many kept it. On the committed fixture most of the time is search: string FRONT is a quarter of the seconds and is kept zero times at the top level, integer DICT is another fifth and is kept three times out of seventy, string FSST is an eighth and is kept none.
- The chooser has a sampled alternative, which searches on 8,192 values and encodes the whole chunk with what that picked. `cargo xtask encode --ablate` runs both over the same columns in the same process and prints what the sample saves and what it gives up, and on a million rows of ClickBench `hits` it is 3.61 times faster for half a percent of size. It is a plain trait with two implementations rather than a `rudb-seam` registration, because `SeamId::StorageEncoder` belongs next to a write path and there is no write path yet.
- The sampled chooser does not sample a narrow chunk. What the search costs scales with the bytes in a chunk rather than with how many values they are spread over, so on a narrow column there is nothing to save and a sample that guesses wrong gives up real size. `Params` is a million values that are nearly all the empty string and it encoded to 21,782 bytes exhaustively and 128,455 sampled, which is 490 percent bigger for no time. There is a floor of 256 KiB on bytes now as well as the floor on values, and 256 KiB is one page, which is the smallest unit the format moves.
- A dictionary is built once rather than once per value. The encode sorts a permutation of indices instead of the values and walks it one time, and the codes come out of that walk for free because the position a value sorted to is the position its code was assigned at. On the committed fixture the whole file goes from 26.6 MiB/s to 43.5 and the output is 130,485 bytes either way, so it is the same file written faster rather than a different trade. The dictionary is still in sorted order, which is what turns a range predicate into a code range rather than a code set.
- `cargo xtask encode --threads N` sweeps the encode across one, two, four and so on up to N, handing out one chunk of one column at a time off a shared counter, and `--sampled` runs the same sweep through the chooser the encoder would ship with. The arithmetic behind F2's first exit criterion multiplies the exhaustive chooser's scaling by the sampled chooser's speedup, and the two were measured separately, so this is the measurement that says whether multiplying them is allowed. Sampling adds an allocation and a pass per chunk, and memory bandwidth is already what stops the sweep scaling past eight threads on a machine with more threads than cores.
- `spec/sql/duckdb/10-generation-and-fuzzing.md` says what the structure aware target came out as, why its findings are regression tests in the ordinary suite rather than committed artifacts, and why there is no crashers test for it. An artifact there means something only to the generator that produced it, so it stops testing what it tested the moment the generator changes.

## 0.3.11

A patch release, mostly about where instructions go in a Parquet scan and about testing the plan format against plans nobody wrote. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The snappy decompressor copies an element without calling libc for it. Two of its three copy paths do not overlap and now say so, which is what lets the compiler inline them instead of leaving a call to memmove. A callgrind profile of `SELECT count(*) FROM hits WHERE URL LIKE '%google%'` over a million rows made 7,022,549 calls into libc and now makes 46,380, and the same query went from 1,291,094,134 instructions to 1,201,328,284. The third path is the repeating pattern, where a run of identical bytes arrives as an offset of one, and that one really does overlap and is untouched. Seventy two percent of the copy elements on a real ClickBench page are four to seven bytes long, so the call and the size dispatch inside it cost more than moving the bytes does.
- A Parquet scan checks whether a string is ASCII before it calls the UTF-8 validator. `bytes.is_ascii()` answers a word at a time with nothing in front of it and settles nearly every string in a column of this kind, and the validator only sees what is left. The same scan went from 1,201,766,641 instructions to 1,151,398,237. The validation itself stays: DuckDB throws `Invalid Input Error: Invalid string encoding found in Parquet file` on bytes that are not valid UTF-8, so a reader that let them through would disagree with them about which files are readable at all.
- The plan printer and the plan reader are checked as a fixed point over generated plans. A plan comes out of a seeded generator, gets printed, read back and printed again, and the two dumps have to be the same string. Two thousand seeds run in half a second so it sits in the ordinary test run, and two million pass. The generator has two tests of its own, one over the operators and expression forms it reaches and one over the pieces of text the printer has to escape, because a generator that stops reaching something does not break, it just quietly stops testing. `Plan::operator`, which is the line `EXPLAIN` walks, is checked against the line the whole dump has for the same node, since two printers that drift are two formats and the reader only knows one.
- A `LIKE` pattern is no longer 288 bytes. A `memmem::Finder` carries the packed pair prefilter on x86_64 and is 288 bytes there and 24 on aarch64, so the enum added in 0.3.10 was as wide as its widest arm everywhere it was held, and the lint that says so only fires on the machine nobody wrote it on.
- `spec/sql/duckdb/10-generation-and-fuzzing.md` says what the other two TLP forms came out as, why the aggregate form folds its three answers in the harness rather than asking the engine for the paper's union inside a subquery, and why the HAVING form's sharp count is half what the other two get.

## 0.3.10

The release where the serial half of a grouped aggregate goes away and where the compatibility harness started finding wrong answers rather than only missing features. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file. There is no suite wide number in this entry, because the suite has not been run end to end since 0.3.9 and the per query numbers below are the ones the pull requests that made them measured.

- A grouped aggregate is radix partitioned. Every instance splits its rows across sixteen partitions before it updates a table, the hashes it already computed for the split are reused for the probe, partition p of one instance only ever meets partition p of another so a group is probed once however many instances ran, and the sweep starts at a rotating partition so thirty two threads do not all begin on the same one. Partitioning only starts once a table is large enough to be worth it, since a query keeping a hundred groups pays the split and saves nothing.
- The partitions are finished on every thread rather than on the one that called finalize. That last step was half of some queries and did not move between eight threads and thirty two, because nothing about it was parallel. On ten million ClickBench rows q34 went from 0.92s to 0.50s, q17 from 0.62s to 0.32s, q13 from 0.25s to 0.14s and q16 from 0.25s to 0.19s. The results come back in partition order, so the answer is the same rows in the same order one thread produced.
- Merging instances in a tree was tried, measured and taken back out. The arithmetic looked good and is wrong when the tables do not overlap, because the union doubles at every level and the critical path is the same 31g a line already had with the extra work still paid. Count distinct over a high cardinality column is the worst case and q5 at ten million rows went from 211ms to 956ms. What was kept is the half that pays, which is an instance combining on the thread that built its state.
- `COUNT(DISTINCT x)` is rewritten into a grouping with an aggregate over it, which is the pass DuckDB calls `distinct_aggregate_rewrite`. The counting then runs on the machinery grouping already has, which hashes a vector at a time and partitions, rather than on a hash set per group filled a row at a time. `GROUP BY RegionID` with `COUNT(DISTINCT URL)` went from 2.96s to 0.59s and with `COUNT(DISTINCT SearchPhrase)` from 0.55s to 0.15s. A grouped distinct over a single BIGINT is left to the row loop it is already good at, because the specialised set there is a wash and the wider key makes it slightly worse.
- The group table probes a batch of rows at a time and compares the key a column at a time inside the batch. A probe used to be three dependent loads per row, the bucket then the stored hash then the key, and on a table larger than the cache all three are misses, so a row cost three trips to memory end to end. Rows are independent, so the batch does one kind of load at a time across all of them and the misses are outstanding together.
- Parquet row groups a filter has already ruled out are not read. Every row group carries the smallest and largest value of each column in the footer and this reader has parsed them since the footer parser was written, and nothing read them, so a query with a WHERE clause read every page and threw the rows away one chunk later. The decision is conservative in one direction only: a missing statistic, a type that cannot be ordered and a comparison that is not understood all mean read the group. Timestamps and times are left out entirely until the unit travels with the type, because a rudb timestamp is microseconds and a file may store the same column in milliseconds or nanoseconds.
- A wide Parquet fetch is read in parallel, with the workers balanced by bytes rather than by column count.
- A `LIKE` builds its substring searcher once per plan rather than once per row, and answers once per dictionary entry rather than once per row when the column arrives as a dictionary shorter than the chunk. A profile of `SELECT count(*) FROM hits WHERE URL LIKE '%google%'` said the match was a quarter of the whole query and almost none of that was searching, since `memmem::find` scans the needle for its two rarest bytes before it looks at the haystack and the needle is fixed for the query. Instructions on that query went from 1,668,648,718 to 1,291,298,392, which is twenty two percent of the whole query with the filter and the scan and the aggregate in it. The per entry pass is guarded on the dictionary being shorter than the chunk, because a dictionary serving a whole row group has more entries than the first chunk has rows and answering per entry there would search values nobody asked about.
- `SET disabled_optimizers` takes all forty four names the pinned binary takes rather than the seven rudb has built. Forty five files in the upstream corpus run that statement, most of them naming a pass rudb has not written, and a failed SET ends a sqllogictest file, so every record after it went unasked. The name is matched without regard to case and the setting reads back as what was understood rather than as what was written, which is what the binary answers.
- A group by on a column key put every null row in a group of its own whenever a filter below it had dropped a row. That is the first wrong answer the compatibility harness found on its own, through the GROUP BY with HAVING form of the TLP oracle, and it is filed as #540 with the narrowing that says how tight the trigger is. A filter that drops a row hands its columns on as dictionary vectors, a dictionary keeps its nulls in the values its codes point at rather than in a mask of its own, and the grouping table read the mask at the wrong level. `Vector::is_null_at` answers the question for every form and `Vector::validity` now says in its own doc comment that it is not the one to ask.
- Dictionary nulls group as one value, which is the same class of mistake caught earlier and from the other direction.
- Reserved morsels stay behind the source order watermark, so a plain SELECT with no ORDER BY still comes back in file order with several morsels in flight.
- `spec/sql/duckdb/10-generation-and-fuzzing.md` says what TLP and NoREC came out as, which decisions in each went against the paper and why, and how small a claim a green run of twenty thousand predicates is. `spec/sql/duckdb/09-the-harness.md` says which corpus the three resource ratios are measured over.

## 0.3.9

The release where one query runs on more than one core. Everything from 0.3.5 onward was groundwork for this and none of it made a query faster on its own, so this is where the number moves: 1.92 times on the ten million row ClickBench suite, and between six and nine times on the queries that are mostly a scan. It also costs more CPU and more memory than it saved, which is written down here rather than left for somebody to find, because two of the four things this project is measured on went the wrong way. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A plan is cut into pipelines and run as pipelines. `build` now returns a `Query`, which is the list of pipelines and the order they depend on each other in, and the pull tree that used to drive execution from the root is gone along with `adapt.rs`, `operator.rs` and `cancel.rs`. There is no pull left inside the engine and the one at the edge is the root, which is what an embedded library owes a caller that owns its own loop. It cost 1.3 percent on query time and 1.9 percent on CPU rather than nothing, measured interleaved at a million rows, because the result now sits in the root's queue before the caller drains it and a breaker reads a clock on the way back out. That was worth paying, since nothing could be scheduled while execution was a tree of `next` calls.
- One pipeline runs on several threads. `run_parallel` starts N instances of the same pipeline the serial driver starts one of, each with its own local state, and the calling thread runs one of them rather than waiting on the others. The first error wins and stops the rest, then every local is combined and the sink is finalised once. How many instances is `Pipeline::degree`, which is the pool's ceiling capped by how many morsels the source says it has and dropped to one if any operator in the pipeline says it will not run twice. The floor comes from counting rather than from estimating, which matters more than it sounds: a small query is serial because it has one morsel, not because a rule fired on a row count that could be wrong.
- The thread budget belongs to the database and not to the query. `Pool` is built from the `threads` setting, hands out a count rather than running closures, and takes it back when the lease is dropped. Lending a number is what keeps a query that is already running inside a worker from waiting on the pool it is inside, which is the deadlock every engine that hands closures to a pool has to work around. `SET threads` moves the pool as well as the setting, so it is a number something obeys rather than a number something reports.
- Every sink was audited for whether a second instance of it changes the answer, and the ones that say no say so in code. A limit, a gather, a keep, a join, a set operation, a `DISTINCT ON` and the plain root all produce their output in the order their input arrived, so they refuse. An aggregate refuses when one of its calls has a `DISTINCT` in it, because the merge refuses those anyway, and when a limit has been pushed into it, because each instance would stop at its own tenth group while rows of the groups it dropped were still arriving. A refusal anywhere in a pipeline drops the whole pipeline to one thread, which is correct and is also why nine ClickBench queries did not get faster.
- A sort and a top N accept a second instance and pay sixteen bytes a row for it. Each row carries the morsel it came from and its place inside that morsel, and a tie between two equal sort keys is broken with that pair, so the answer on thirty two threads is the same answer as on one rather than merely a valid one. This was not theoretical. An `ORDER BY s DESC NULLS LAST LIMIT 10` over rows that all tie on the key returned a different ten on eight threads until the arrival key went in, and the alternative was making top N serial, which would have dragged the scan under it back to one thread on nearly every ClickBench query.
- CPU is measured across the threads that burned it. A span reads the calling thread's CPU clock, so a driver could only ever see one thread's share of a pipeline that ran on thirty two. The parallel driver measures each worker and reports the total, the driver records it along with how many instances ran, and a pipeline's CPU is now what it cost rather than the fraction of it that happened on the thread that started the others. Wall time is deliberately not summed, because a worker ran at the same time as its caller and adding the two would make a pipeline that got faster look slower.
- The run document has an instance count per pipeline. It is the one number in there that cannot be worked out from the others, since a pipeline that ran four instances for a tenth of a second each and one that ran one for four tenths report the same CPU.
- What this cost. At ten million rows the suite went from 36.482s to 18.976s of query time, and from 36.230s to 72.420s of CPU, with peak memory from 1.28 GiB to 2.18 GiB. The memory is N instances holding N hash tables and radix partitioning is what fixes it. The CPU is partly a machine with sixteen of its twenty four cores being small ones, partly memory bandwidth, and partly ours, and the fact that the multiplier grew from 1.38 at a million rows to 2.0 at ten million says at least some of it is ours. Both are filed with the measurements in them rather than carried as a footnote.

## 0.3.8

Everything here is groundwork for running one query on more than one core, which is the next thing the benchmark needs and the largest single number still on the table. Nothing in this release makes a query faster on its own, because there is still one thread taking one morsel at a time. What changed is that the two things which would have gone wrong the moment a second thread appears no longer go wrong. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A grouped aggregate stops refusing a second instance. Until now the operator built one hash table and a second instance of it was an error, which meant a plan containing a group by could not be split across threads at all. `Accumulator::combine` folds one aggregate state into another for count, sum, min, max and average, and `Aggregate::merge` walks the incoming table's groups and probes them into the one being kept, reusing the hash the incoming table already stored rather than hashing the keys again. The finish moved out of the combine and into the finalize, because a combine now happens once per instance and there is one table's worth of answer however many instances ran. A `DISTINCT` aggregate is still refused, because merging two groups there needs a set union rather than a fold, and a spilled instance is still refused, because spilling rests on a key being either finished in this pass or absent from it, which holds inside one instance and not across two. Radix partitioning is what fixes the second one and it is the next item but one.
- Averaging over an integer column gives the same answer however the rows were divided. The accumulator keeps an exact 128 bit total and divides once at the end, so the split does not move the result. Summing or averaging a FLOAT or a DOUBLE can move it, because adding doubles is not associative and a different division of the rows adds them in a different order. That is stated in the code rather than hidden, since it is the same answer any parallel engine gives and pretending otherwise would mean giving up the parallelism.
- A plain `SELECT` with no `ORDER BY` will still come back in file order once several morsels are in flight. A chunk arriving at the sink came from exactly one morsel, so the morsel number and the position inside it totally order the output, and the new `Sink::at` tells a sink which morsel it is being fed from. `root_in_order` holds chunks whose turn has not come and releases them once no instance is still reading an earlier morsel. SQL promises no order for that query, but every engine anybody uses returns the file order and a user who sees rows move between runs reports it as a bug. The bound on how much can be held has one rule so it cannot deadlock, which is that the instance holding the earliest morsel is never told to wait, so somebody can always make progress.
- A compact group key was built, measured and taken back out again. The idea was to pack a group by over one or two narrow integer columns into a fixed width row and compare it with a word compare. It landed and then measured slower on every query it was meant to help, by 1.5 to 3.1 percent, and worse on the one query that could not use it at all. The reason is that the two changes in 0.3.6 and 0.3.7 had already taken the win: keys were already stored a column at a time in typed runs, so there was no memory to save, and the probe already read them in place, so only the per row type dispatch was left to buy. Flattening into a scratch buffer costs more than that. The finding worth keeping is the second half, which is that the shape enum sat in the probe on every group by in the suite, so queries that could not use the abstraction paid for it while the ones that could saved nothing.

## 0.3.7

Two pull requests aimed at the same thing from opposite ends, which is the grouped aggregate and the Parquet scan, and both of them came out of a profiler rather than off the work list. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A Parquet scan hands out one morsel per row group instead of one morsel covering everything. It also held a mutex around one reader for the length of a scan, so a second thread asking it for work got none and would have queued behind the lock if it had. A row group is the unit the format stores and the smallest unit a reader can be positioned at without having read what came before it, which makes it the natural morsel; a CSV file cannot be positioned at all, because nothing in it says where a row begins until every byte before it has been parsed, so a CSV morsel stays a whole file. `Reader::split` is what makes it cheap: it hands back another reader over the same open file and the same parsed footer, positioned at a stretch of row groups and reading nothing outside it. Sharing works because `rudb_io::File` is addressed by offset and every method on it takes `&self`, so two readers on one file do not interfere. That is nine morsels at a million ClickBench rows, eighty one at ten million and two hundred and twenty six at a hundred million. There is no speed change in this release, because there is still one thread and it takes the morsels in the order they were cut. What changed is that there is now something for a second thread to take.
- A group by stops copying its keys in and out of tagged values. The keys have been stored a column at a time since 0.2, with a hash per slot and a typed run per key column, but everything around that storage still went through `Value`: a callgrind profile of `GROUP BY UserID` over a million rows counted one `Vector::value_at` call per input row inside the fold at 131 instructions a call, which is the equality check after a probe building a sixty four byte tagged value to compare an `i64` that was already sitting in the vector it arrived in, and put another 14 percent of the query in `Vector::from_values`, which is every finished group copied into a `Vec<Value>` and straight back out of one. `Vector::signed_at` is new and is the integer sibling of the `text_at` and `bytes_at` that already existed for exactly this caller, and the three places a group key crossed that boundary stop crossing it. Aggregate went from 69.5 ns a row to 59.1 at a million ClickBench rows, hot CPU over the suite from 4.220 s to 3.950 s, and the gap to DuckDB on the 41 shared queries from 8.84 times to 8.16.
- The fallback in that last change is load bearing rather than decoration. A bit packed column cannot hand its rows over as integers, so `signed_at` answers nothing for one, and reading that as a key that did not match would have quietly put every row of a packed column in a group of its own. Both call sites build a value instead, and there is a test that groups a packed column and the flat column it stands for and asserts the two produce the same slots.

## 0.3.6

The release where we finally ran the benchmark we keep talking about. ClickBench on the benchmark machine, at 1k, 10k, 100k, 1m and 10m rows, against DuckDB on the same files, and the numbers said the largest thing wrong with the engine was not in any of the places the work list had. Most of this release is what came out of that, and the rest is the instruments that made it readable. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A top N over a wide file no longer reads the columns nobody looks at. `SELECT * FROM hits ORDER BY EventTime LIMIT 10` needs one column of every row to decide which ten rows win and a hundred and five columns of the ten that did, and the plan was carrying all hundred and six through the top N, which meant decoding the whole file to throw almost all of it away. The new pass is `late_materialization`, which is the name DuckDB gives the same rewrite, so `SET disabled_optimizers` takes it by that name. A top N over a wide projection of one Parquet file becomes a scan of the ordering columns plus the row's ordinal inside the file, the top N over those, and a `Fetch` node that reads the rest of each surviving row back out. At ten million rows that query went from 26.58 seconds to 1.79 and its peak memory from 322 MB to 43 MB, and ClickBench q24 went from 9.04 seconds to 1.96 with memory from 314 MB to 97 MB. DuckDB uses 615 MB and 1706 MB on the same two, so the memory half of the goal is met on this shape with room over. The latency half is not, and what is left is one column of ten million rows through a decoder, which is the work that was already on the list.
- The pass refuses more often than it fires, and the conditions are a cost model rather than a rule of the language. A limit over 1024 rows, a projection under eight columns wider than its ordering, an ordering key that is not a bare column, more than one file, or a scan that already produces `file_row_number`. Fetching by ordinal reads a page per column per row group the winners land in, so two rows out of a million still cost 31 MB of a 220 MB file, which puts a floor under the saving that a narrow projection or a large limit cannot clear.
- The reader can be asked for a handful of rows instead of scanned. `Reader::rows_at` takes sorted ordinals and hands back a chunk of just those rows, skipping row groups that hold none of them and pages whose header says nobody wants a row inside. The dictionary page of an opened column chunk is read whatever happens, because a code is not a value without one, and that is the floor the paragraph above is about.
- `file_row_number` is a column the planner can ask a Parquet scan for, spelled the way DuckDB spells it. It counts from zero in each file of a glob rather than across them, since an ordinal that does not say which file it belongs to does not identify a row. It is there only when the call asks for it, which is what keeps a file that happens to have a column of that name from being ambiguous.
- Query metrics say more about where a scan went. The time is split into the stages of a read, which are opening, seeking, decompressing and decoding, so a profile points at a stage rather than at a file. Each operator records which seam implementation it actually ran, which is the difference between a sweep and a guess. And the bytes a scan decoded are counted and totalled per query, which is the number the fetch above is measured with.
- Five decoder fixes, all of them from the same profile. A Snappy page is now allowed to be as large as its container says it is, which was a hard failure on the wider ClickBench columns. The decompression buffer is kept between pages instead of being allocated per page, and a real page is copied wide rather than byte at a time. A scan no longer clones a column it is about to hand on unchanged. A packed stream is read once rather than once per value. And a chunk shares its dictionary rather than copying it for every page, which on a ClickBench string column is the dictionary copied once per two thousand rows.
- A dictionary page with a bad code is rejected by checking the highest code rather than stopping at the first bad one, which is one pass over the codes rather than a branch per value and gives the same answer.
- The DuckDB compatibility plan is written down under `spec/sql/duckdb`, which is the list of what being compatible actually requires rather than a claim that we are.

## 0.3.5

Two more physical forms, both of them for text, and both of them a way for a kernel to answer a question without turning the column back into strings first. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A vector has a seventh physical form, which is the string view. A flat varchar vector owns the arena its long strings live in, so cutting a chunk out of it copies every byte of every long string in the range, and a page read as chunks copies the whole page once per chunk. The view form splits the ownership: the views are owned and the arena sits behind an `Arc`, so a cut is sixteen bytes a row and the bytes are not touched. That is the other half of the finding recorded on `Chunk::compact`, which measured the same problem for selecting rather than for cutting. A gather keeps the form, which means the arena a selection holds is the whole page rather than the rows it kept, and a caller that wants the bytes narrowed calls `flatten` and gets a copy of just those rows. Both halves of that trade are written next to the code. The comparison kernel reads either string form through one accessor, so the ordering rule lives in one function and the flat form did not grow a second one.
- A vector has an eighth physical form, which is FSST. The symbol table moved from `rudb-encoding` down to `rudb-vector`, because a vector that holds codes has to be able to read one and the encoding crate sits above the vector crate in the layer rule. There is still one implementation and one test suite, and the old path re-exports the new one. `compressed` trains a table on the column and keeps the result when it is at most half the plain bytes, the same shape of rule bit packing uses. The comparison kernel answers equality in code space: compressing is a function of the table and the bytes, so two rows have the same codes exactly when they are the same string, and the literal gets compressed once before the loop. Ordering gets nothing from this, because a symbol code says nothing about where its symbol sorts, so `<` and friends decompress and count themselves on the fallback table. Three forms now answer a filter in the space they are stored in, which is bit packing for narrow integers, the string view for a prefix, and this for equality on text.
- Two fixes for things that only broke once two branches were both in. `Vector::bytes_at` and the FSST form landed in the same window and each was exhaustive against the other's absence, so the merge did not build, and the new arm returns the `None` the text accessor already returned because a compressed row is not anywhere in its plain bytes. The other is the affine sum marker in the aggregate, whose loop over aggregate calls tripped the row loop lint on a `Value::Integer` pattern, since a pattern and a construction are the same text and the lint reads text. The literal it was reading moved into a function of its own rather than the loop claiming to be row at a time, which it is not. Nothing released was affected by either.

## 0.3.4

Two more pieces of F1, both of them in the data plane rather than around it. One is a decision the engine was taking with a constant and now takes with a gain function, and the other is a physical form a narrow integer column has been asking for since the first ClickBench profile. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Whether a filter copies the rows it kept is a seam with three implementations. A filter hands on a selection over the chunk rather than a copy, which costs nothing now and one redirection on every later read of every kept row, and compacting is the other side of that trade. The three are `never`, which always selects and is the reference, `fixed-threshold`, which copies when the filter kept under a fifth and is the rule most engines have some version of, and `learned-gain`, which is the gain function from Data Chunk Compaction in Vectorized Execution, SIGMOD 2025. Writing that function out is the finding: the kept row count appears on both sides and nearly cancels, so what decides it is row width against how many more times the rows get read, and not the selectivity the fixed rule is written in terms of. That agrees with the measurement already in the doc on `Chunk::compact`, where compacting loses at every selectivity with one later pass and wins at every selectivity with sixteen. The pass count comes off the plan and the copy rate is measured while the query runs, both in a gauge that belongs to the pipeline instance rather than to the strategy, since a strategy is shared by every query in the process. `EXPLAIN` prints which one a filter chose, a session pins one with `SET seam_chunk_compaction`, and a statement pins one with a hint. The paper's other half, a buffer that merges sparse chunks into full ones, is not here, because `Stream::push` transforms one chunk in place and has nowhere to keep a remainder.
- A vector has a sixth physical form, which is bit packing. A narrow integer column stored flat spends most of its bytes on zeros: ClickBench `ResolutionWidth` is a `SMALLINT` holding numbers under 2561, which is twelve bits of the sixteen it is given. A packed vector is codes of one width laid end to end, each an offset from a base, and `bit_packed` is the counterpart of `run_encoded` and packs when the result is at most half the flat size. A cut stays packed by remembering which row it starts at, which is one addition per read against a pass to repack, and that matters because a page is packed once and cut into chunk sized pieces. The comparison kernel reads the form without unpacking: the literal becomes a code once, so `column > 900` over a column based at 40 is `code > 860` and the loop compares two `u64` with a shift and a mask, and a literal outside what the width can hold answers the whole vector without a bit of it being read. Nothing in the engine builds a packed vector yet, the same as run length encoding, because which columns a storage layer packs is a storage format decision and the form landing first is what lets that decision be made with a kernel already able to read the result. The fallback table also stopped calling run length encoding and bit packing both `other`, which was hiding the counts F1 asks for.

## 0.3.3

More of F1, all of it in the expression evaluator and all of it the same idea: work that depends on the query rather than on the rows should happen once for the query. A pipeline over `hits` runs a hundred thousand chunks, so anything decided per chunk is decided a hundred thousand times for one query, and three things that were being decided there did not need to be. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A pattern is compiled once for the query. `LIKE` and the four regular expression functions read their pattern out of a vector and compile it, which for a literal pattern is the same work on every chunk. The prepared form now hands the kernels what it can read off the plan, and the kernels keep the per chunk path for a pattern that is not a literal, which is legal SQL and vanishingly rare. The rule the seam is built around is that preparing must not move an error: a pattern that does not compile stays unprepared, so the sentence the user sees still comes out of the chunk that reached the call, which matters most under a `CASE` arm where compiling early would raise on a query whose rows never reach it. `call` and `call_prepared` are one body with one extra argument, so there is no second path to keep in step.
- An `IN` list is one lookup again. The binder lowers `x IN (1, 2, 3)` into three equalities under an `OR`, which is right for a binder and wrong to run: three passes over the column, three output vectors and then an `OR` to put them back together. The prepared form recognises that shape and replaces the whole connective with one set lookup, and `NOT IN`, which arrives as an `AND` of inequalities, is the same lookup read the other way. Only lists of whole numbers and strings fold. Floats do not, because DuckDB says two nans are equal and a hash set does not, decimals do not, because the same number can arrive as two unscaled integers at two scales, and intervals do not, because interval equality is by length. Nulls in the list are kept rather than dropped, since a miss against a list holding a null is null rather than false, and that is the rule that makes `IN` not a plain set lookup.
- The literal side of a comparison is built once. The comparison loops read both sides through a slice, so a literal has to become a one row column before a loop can start, and for a string that column is where the four byte prefix lives, which is what almost every row of a string comparison is decided by. It helps most where it looks least likely to: a full chunk spreads the setup over two thousand rows, while a conjunct after the first is handed the rows the earlier ones kept and can be an eleven row loop with full chunk setup in front of it. A prepared literal that does not match the side a kernel is about to read is ignored rather than believed, which costs one comparison of two literals per chunk and is the difference between slow and wrong.

## 0.3.2

Three more pieces of F1. The first one is the one that makes a query faster: a run length column stopped falling through to the row at a time path, which is where the form landed in 0.3.1 with nothing reading it. The other two are the filter, which now runs an `OR` the same way it runs an `AND` and works out for itself which side of either to run first. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Every kernel reads a run length column. A dictionary and a run length vector look the same from inside a kernel, which is a position per row and a vector to read them out of, and the only difference is that one stores the positions and the other works them out. `Vector::positions` is that question asked once, and the eighteen dictionary specializations in `rudb-kernels` ask it instead of asking for a dictionary, so the aggregate, the comparisons, the scalar functions, three valued logic, the casts, the selection and the regular expressions all took run length without gaining an arm. The one that had to be right is `nulls_of`, because a form that reads through positions has two places to keep a null and a kernel that checked only the vector's own validity would hand back whatever sits at position zero instead of the null. That function asks for positions now too, which is the whole reason for having the question in one place. This is not the per run specialization, which would do one multiply per run instead of one add per row and is still worth writing for the aggregate. It is the step that gets a run length column off the path that builds a `Value` per row, and that path was measured at between fifty and two hundred times what the loop should cost.
- A filter threads the branches of an `OR`. Threading an `AND` is old news: each conjunct is handed the rows the one before it kept, so the second one never looks at a row the first one rejected. The same narrowing is wrong for an `OR` and the complement is right, because a row the first branch accepts is kept whatever the later branches say, so every branch after the first only has to decide the rows no earlier branch accepted. Three valued logic does not break this, since a filter keeps only the rows that are TRUE, and a test with a null branch beside a true one guards the one way it could go wrong. Both connectives are one walk now, carrying the rows every operand kept for an `AND` and the rows no operand accepted for an `OR`, each stopping early for its own reason. A connective inside a connective recurses rather than falling back, so `(a AND b) OR (c AND d)` is four narrowed comparisons where it used to be two narrowed ones and two full passes.
- A filter works out which conjunct to run first. The plan writes the conjuncts in the order the SQL had them, and the order that matters is the one where the conjunct that rejects the most for the least work goes first. So each connective keeps a count of what it was given and what it kept over the last sixteen chunks, ranks its operands by how much they reject over what they cost, and sorts before the next chunk. An `OR` ranks the other way, since there the branch that accepts the most is the one that leaves the least for everybody after it. Selectivity is measured and cost is estimated from a weight table over the subtree, because a clock read per operand per chunk is a real fraction of the work in a 1024 row chunk, and the rank function sits alone in `crates/rudb-exec/src/ordering.rs` so that timing can replace the estimate later without touching the walk. Reordering is free because the steps are in post order, so an operand's subtree sits between the operand before it and the operand itself whatever order they run in. A test runs forty rounds and asserts the surviving rows never change.

## 0.3.1

The first three pieces of F1, which is the milestone that makes the data plane fast. None of them makes a query faster yet. Two of them are instruments and the third is a form nothing reads, and that order is on purpose: F1's exit criterion is a ratio against F0, and a ratio nobody can attribute to a line of code is a number you cannot act on. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Every path the engine takes because it was written to be correct rather than fast now counts itself, and the count lands against the operator that took it. There are seven causes, which are flattening a compact column, a comparison, a scalar function, three valued logic, a cast, an aggregate and a selection, and the counter sits in `rudb-common` at rank zero because the two things that increment it are at rank one and rank three and the thing that reads it is at rank four, and no two of those can see each other. It is per thread rather than process wide, deliberately: the shim around an operator call takes a difference, and with a shared counter two threads on one pipeline would read each other's work into their own differences and per operator attribution would be noise. A thread cannot race with itself, so the number stays exact when F4 adds parallelism. The totals go into the metrics document per operator, `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` prints the worst cause on the operator's line, and a query that took one of these paths says so in the warnings without anything having to call a warn function. On a `count(*)` with a `min` over fifty thousand varchar rows the aggregate line reads `49 fell back, most of it aggregate` and the scan and the filter read nothing, which is the shape the instrument is supposed to have.
- `cargo xtask flatten` keeps every place a compact column is copied out flat in a reviewed list, and it runs in the gate. `Vector::flatten` throws away whatever the encoding was buying and it is the most expensive single thing in `rudb-vector`, but the risk is not the calls that are there, it is the ones that arrive later one at a time as the shortest way to make something compile. Three call sites say why today: the top of a query where a chunk becomes a result set somebody outside the engine reads a value at a time, the Arrow export whose whole job is handing data over in somebody else's format, and the chunk wide helper the first one calls. The awkward part is that `flatten` is also an iterator adapter and an `Option` method and the workspace calls those far more often, so the rule is what sits in front of the dot: a column flatten is called on a name and the other two end a chain. A chain ending in something the lint has not been told about is reported rather than guessed at either way, because a lint that guesses stops being true without anybody noticing.
- A vector has a fifth physical form, which is run length. Dictionary and run length answer different questions about the same column: a dictionary says which distinct values there are and wins on low cardinality however the rows are ordered, and run length says where the values stop and wins on a clustered column however many distinct values it has. `hits` is written in time order, so `EventDate` is a few hundred runs over ninety nine million rows. The body is the row each run ends at, exclusive and increasing, plus one value per run, and ends rather than lengths because every reader wants to know which run holds a row. `run_encoded` is the way to make one and it costs a pass over the column to decide, since a run costs its value plus four bytes and a row costs its value, so runs are smaller once a run covers more than about two rows. Slicing cuts runs as runs. Gathering and flattening needed no new code, because a run length body is a dictionary whose code is worked out from the position rather than stored. No kernel reads the form yet, so a vector in it falls through to the row at a time path and counts itself there, which is the first instrument doing its job.

## 0.3.0

F0 closed, so the minor version moves, and this is also the release where the minor version changes what it counts. It counted the M series and it counts the F series now, which the paragraph at the top of this file explains.

F0 is the skeleton, the seams and the metrics, at https://github.com/tamnd/rudb/issues/345. It is the only milestone in the plan with no performance gate, and what it asked for instead was that everything be in place and nothing be partial. Three things it built that were not there before: every operator behind one of the three push traits with a driver around the loop that runs them, twenty seven named seams with a reference implementation registered at each and `rudb_strategies()` listing them, and a metrics document per execution that `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` renders and the harness reads.

The base is on gamingpc-wsl, fifteen hot runs after a cold one at load average 1.16 on 32 threads, against the 2a baseline for the same suite and the same machine. rudb ran five of the six smoke queries over ten million rows in 1.025s of wall clock and 970ms of CPU, against DuckDB's 206ms and 440ms, holding a peak resident set of 7.73 MiB against DuckDB's 154 MiB. Five times slower and twenty times smaller. The goal is ten times faster and ten times less resource, so on this suite the resource half is already past the target and the speed half is fifty times away from it. Every operator on every query ran the reference implementation of its seam, which is what a base is supposed to look like and what F1 onwards exists to change.

The cross check reads 0.0 percent apart on all five queries, and that is written down as close to an identity rather than claimed as a passing check, because the tree is serial and runs inside one loop. What it still catches is a pipeline nobody drove, time inside the execution and outside every driver, and real disagreement once pipelines run on their own threads.

What has been measured of the exit criterion, and what has not. rudb runs 41 of the 43 ClickBench queries over the full hundred million rows, in 630s of wall clock against DuckDB's 26s on the same machine, with two documented abstentions on q19 and q33 because the hash aggregate does not spill and both group by a key that is close to unique. `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` renders the document and `rudb_strategies()` lists all twenty seven seams. The answer comparison against DuckDB over the full file was still running when this was cut, and at one row in five hundred it came back clean except for q23, where every group has a count of one, so `ORDER BY c DESC LIMIT 10` is a tie across the whole result and the ten rows either engine hands back are as good as each other. TPC-H is not measured at all, because the SF100 corpus is not on the machine the rest of this was run on. Neither gap is a reason to hold the release, since F0 has no performance gate and a milestone that is the base for a ratio is worth more in the ledger than in a queue, but neither is a reason to say the criterion passed either, so it does not say that.

One change since 0.2.37, which is `date_part` answering `epoch` and `julian` as doubles. Upstream declares the function as a `DOUBLE` and narrows it to a `BIGINT` while binding when the specifier is a constant naming a part that is whole, so `typeof(date_part('minute', ts))` is `BIGINT` and `typeof(date_part('epoch', ts))` is `DOUBLE`, and a specifier that is a column stays a double whatever that column holds, since nothing at binding time can look at it. The arithmetic was read off the pinned binary rather than worked out: seconds are the microseconds over a million on a moment and the days times 86400 on a date, julian days add 2440588 in double arithmetic so that far enough out the microseconds stop fitting and the digits that fall off are upstream's digits too, and over a length a year is 365.25 days and a month is 30, which is why twelve months and one year are the same number of seconds and eleven months are not eleven twelfths of one. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

## 0.2.37

Four changes. One is the last piece of measurement work in F0, which is measuring the loop that runs a pipeline, and three are the interval, which is arithmetic on one, date arithmetic without one and reading a part out of one. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

This was cut as a patch because the version tracked the M series at the time and M2 is open. That was reconsidered the same day and the version moved to the F series instead, so the release that marks F0 is 0.3.0 and the paragraph at the top of this file says how the handover works.

- The loop that runs a pipeline is measured. An operator is measured around its own call, so a chunk that goes through four operators is charged to four operators and nothing charged the loop that made the four calls. On a scan of ten million rows that loop takes a morsel, allocates the chunk the source fills, hands it up the tree and drops it again ten thousand times, which is about a microsecond each and between a third and a half of `SELECT count(*) FROM smoke`. Every pipeline has a driver now, a pipeline's time is its driver's time rather than the sum of its operators, and the difference between the two is what the driving cost. Pipelines run inside each other in a pull tree, because a sort is drained by the loop above it while that loop is still running, so a driver charges itself its span minus whatever was charged while its span was open and every driver of one report shares the running total that makes the subtraction work. A pipeline nobody registered a driver for still falls back to the sum of its operators. `build_cpu_ns` splits out of `resource.cpu_ns` at the same time, since opening a file, reading its schema and allocating a tree all happen before there is a pipeline to charge, and the span the pipelines sit inside is the CPU without it. That is the right hand side of the cross check on both sides of the wire now, and all five smoke queries read 0.0 percent apart where one of them was 43 percent apart before.
- An interval has its own arithmetic. An interval plus or minus an interval, a minus in front of one, and an interval times or divided by a number. Addition and subtraction are field by field and not by length, so `INTERVAL '1 month' + INTERVAL '30 days'` is a month and thirty days rather than two months, even though the order over intervals calls those two the same value, and the two directions fail with two different upstream sentences. Multiplication has two overloads and the difference shows: a whole number goes in as a `BIGINT` and multiplies the three fields where they stand, so `INTERVAL '25 hours' * 2` is `50:00:00`, and everything else goes in as a `DOUBLE` and moves what is left over on a field down to the next one at thirty days to a month and twenty four hours to a day, so `INTERVAL '1 month' * 0.5` is fifteen days. Dividing has only the double, which is why `INTERVAL '1 month' / 3` is ten days rather than nothing. The month leftover carries a quirk that looks like a bug and is not: upstream turns it into a whole number of millionths of a day before it becomes microseconds, so `INTERVAL '1 month' / 7` is `4 days 06:51:25.6896` where the exact division would be `06:51:25.714286`, and a day leftover has no such step. Both are copied, because a query that answers one way here and another way there is the bug this project exists to not have.
- A date takes a count of days, and two moments subtract. `DATE '2020-01-01' + 1` is a date, `DATE '2020-03-01' - DATE '2020-02-01'` is a count of days, `TIMESTAMP '2020-01-02 10:00:00' - TIMESTAMP '2020-01-01 08:00:00'` is an interval and `DATE '2020-01-01' + TIME '10:00:00'` is a timestamp. All four were binder errors before this. The count of days is an `INTEGER` and nothing wider, which is upstream's overload, so `DATE '2020-01-01' + 1::BIGINT` has no function to reach while the four narrower integer types widen into it. A date on either side of a subtraction between two moments becomes a timestamp first, which is why `DATE - TIMESTAMP` answers an interval rather than a count. An untyped null next to a date takes the count of days and next to a timestamp takes the interval, both measured rather than assumed.
- `date_part` and `extract` read an interval. A part of an interval is one of its three fields and never a sum of them, so thirty six hours has no days in it and asking for the days says zero. The years and everything longer divide the months and truncate towards zero rather than flooring, which is the opposite of what a timestamp before the epoch does, and both were measured. The minutes and everything shorter take the remainder of the microseconds the way the clock parts of a timestamp do. An interval has no calendar in it, so the week, the three ways of numbering a day, the ISO year and the era have no answer over one, and upstream refuses those at run time with a sentence that echoes the specifier exactly as it was written rather than at binding. The check that a part exists happens once per vector rather than once per row, since the specifier is a literal in every query anybody writes.

## 0.2.36

Five changes. Three of them are the measurement work in F0, which is `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` and the document behind it, and two are the interval, one comparing them and one doing arithmetic with them. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The operator numbering moves into the walk that works out the pipelines. The builder used to allocate an id as it went and ask `rudb-plan` separately which pipeline a node runs in, which left the numbering as the one part of the shape of an execution that did not exist until the execution had been built, and that is exactly the part a plan printed with measured numbers on it needs. `Pipelines` becomes `Shape` and answers three questions about a plan rather than one: which operator a node becomes, whether it has a second one holding the side that finishes first, and which pipeline runs it. A node with two inputs is two operators, since the gather under a join or a set operation and the kept chunks under a cross product have their own counters and their own row in a document, and the side that runs first is walked first so the ids go in the order the work happens. No behaviour changed. Same plans, same output, same numbers, with one walk deciding all of it.
- Two intervals that are the same length are one value. An interval is three counts, of months, of days and of microseconds, and the comparison was the derived one over the triple, so `INTERVAL '1 day' = INTERVAL '24 hours'` was false, a day and twenty four hours grouped apart, and `min` of a day and twenty five hours picked the wrong one. Upstream flattens the triple into one count of microseconds, at thirty days to a month and twenty four hours to a day, and compares that, so the flattening is one function next to the civil date arithmetic and the four places that need it read it: the row at a time comparison, the vectorized one, the grouping key and the aggregate's fold. It is an `i128`, because the longest interval is about six hundred times too long for an `i64` of microseconds and upstream answers those cases rather than refusing them. The vectorized arm is written out by hand instead of generated, because the generated arms compare the value as it is stored and this comparison is over a number computed from it, which is also why the interval leaves the ordered layout group. The three counts stay apart in storage and in printing, since adding a month to a date is not adding thirty days to it.
- `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` runs. It is the same three sections `EXPLAIN` prints with what happened written next to what was expected: rows and time on every operator line, wall and cpu on every pipeline line, then the totals for the query and whatever the document has to warn about them. `ANALYZE` stops being a refusal in the transformer and becomes a flag on the statement, so a plan that is explained and a plan that is analyzed are the same plan and a number measured under it is a number about the plan somebody was shown. Every number is looked up by the operator id the shape gives a node, which is the id the builder tagged that operator's counters with, so a document from a different query lines nothing up rather than lining the wrong things up. The estimate goes into the document as well as onto the line, because the warnings are generated from the document and cannot compare an estimate that is not in it, and an estimate an order of magnitude away from what happened is how a bad plan explains itself. One bug came out of reading the output: the rows a pipeline breaker finally hands out come back through a buffer that is not the sink and was not wrapped in anything that counts, so a sort over a hundred thousand rows reported that it produced nothing.
- The shell writes the metrics document. `--metrics FILE` appends one document per statement, one per line, rather than one array over the whole run, because the document is written the moment its statement finishes and a run that dies half way through still leaves the ones that did finish. It is also what a harness wants to read, since a benchmark runs a setup statement and then the query it timed and reading the second record should not mean parsing the first. That needed a one line form of the document, so the JSON writer grew an indent flag: same keys in the same order, whitespace the only difference, and a test that says so by comparing the two with the whitespace taken out. A statement that measured nothing writes nothing, the file is created on the first document rather than at startup, every run starts a new file rather than appending to whatever was there, and a file that cannot be written is reported once and fails the run rather than once per query.
- An interval can be added to a date, a timestamp or a time, and taken off one. That was a binder error until now, so none of the date arithmetic anybody writes would run. A date plus an interval is a timestamp and not a date, because the interval can carry a time of day, and a time plus an interval is a time, since the months and the days have nowhere to go and it wraps at midnight. The three fields are applied one at a time, months then days then microseconds, and the order shows whenever a month lands on a day the next month does not have: a month added to the thirty first of January is the twenty ninth of February and a month and a day added to it is the first of March rather than the second. The signature table gets its first overload by argument type, with the rule written down next to it, which is that an interval beside a date, a timestamp or a time is date arithmetic and everything else is the numeric row. There are three ways out of range and upstream has a different sentence for each, one for the months leaving the calendar, one for the days leaving the date range and one for the answer leaving the timestamp range, and a date becomes a timestamp before anything is added to it, which decides which of the three a date at the end of the range gets. The timestamp range also stops one short of the top of the `i64` now, because that value is `infinity` upstream, the same reservation the date range already had.

## 0.2.35

Six changes. Three of them are the measurement work in F0, one finishes moving the operators behind the engine traits, and two are casts from a string that upstream reads and we did not. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Every operator in the engine sits behind one of the three traits in `rudb-pipeline`. The six that were still pulling are the leaf sources, the table scan, the dummy scan, the series, the file scan, the values list and the strategies table, and a source is the one of the three that is shared rather than instanced: every thread calls the same read and the position it is up to is an atomic, so a morsel goes to whoever asks for it first and no lock is held while anything is read. What a morsel covers is each source's own business. A table scan hands out one stored chunk, because reading half of one costs what reading all of it costs. A series hands out sixteen chunks at a time, since its rows are worked out rather than read and a morsel per chunk would make the counter most of the work, and the value at a position is computed from the position because the thread that gets the second morsel never saw the first. A file scan hands out one morsel for the whole list, which is not a gap in the operator but what the readers are, a position in a file with a buffer beside it that cannot be asked for the tenth chunk without having read the nine before it. A file per morsel and then a Parquet row group per morsel are changes to the readers rather than to this.
- There is a metrics document, one JSON object per execution, written whether the query succeeded, was cancelled or failed.
