The query and its hash, the engine and machine it ran on, the settings, how it ended, the timings, the resources, the strategy chosen at every seam, a row per pipeline with its blocked time split by the four reasons, and a row per operator with rows in and out, wall and CPU time, bytes read and decoded and spilled and a memory high water mark. The warnings are generated rather than written: a spill, a reference implementation, an estimate an order of magnitude out, a pipeline that spent most of its time blocked, a peak within a few percent of the limit and CPU the operators do not account for are all rules over numbers that are in the document anyway, so nothing calls a warn function and nothing can forget to. `schema/1.json` is committed, so a change to the shape is a diff somebody has to look at rather than a surprise in a harness three repositories away.
- An operator is measured by being wrapped rather than by mentioning a clock inside itself. Two clock readings and a row count per call, which is once per chunk, which is the granularity rule, so an operator written next year is measured the day it is written by somebody who never read the module. The counters live with the document rather than next to the operators, because the file readers and the buffer manager have numbers for the same operator and none of those crates can see each other, and every field is an atomic read once at the end by the thread that writes the document. Wall time is an `Instant` and CPU time is per thread, which is the axis the project's resource claim rests on and the one that cannot be bought with threads, and where that clock is not available the field is empty rather than a wall time reported twice under two names. A sink is measured in `finalize` too, because a sort does its sorting there and an operator whose pushes look free and whose finish takes a second is the shape the document has to show rather than hide.
- Every operator the engine builds is wearing one, since the walk that builds the tree is the only one that sees all of them and the arm that builds an operator is handed an id it has to put somewhere. The ids and the pipeline numbers come from that walk: a parent is numbered before its children, so reading the list top to bottom is reading the tree top to bottom, and every arm with a sink in it starts a new pipeline below itself and writes down the edge, so a sort is two pipelines with the one above waiting and a join is three. Nothing schedules off those edges yet. They are written down now because they are known now, and an edge reconstructed later from a tree somebody has already flattened is an edge somebody has to guess at. The document hangs off the result rather than off the connection, because a result outlives the query and a program can hold two at once. Everything built here is marked as a reference implementation, which is what stops a number measured against the simplest correct version of an operator from being quoted as if it came from a fast one, and that marker comes off an operator on the day it gets a second tier.
- An interval written as a string is read. `SELECT INTERVAL '1 day'` was already turned into a cast of the text behind it, so the whole of it was the missing cast, and almost none of the grammar is guessable: a string is a run of items that add up without being normalised, each item carries its own sign, a bare number is seconds only when it is the first item, `ago` at the end negates the whole thing once, and a colon after the first count turns the rest into a clock that ends the parse and swallows whatever follows. The fraction behind a count lands somewhere different for almost every unit, a year drops what does not fit in months, a month spills into days and then into the clock, a quarter spills into days and rounds there and stops, an hour and everything shorter rounds into microseconds, and a microsecond ignores its fraction outright. Sixty nine written intervals were compared statement by statement against the pinned binary and every one agrees.
- A date at or before the year zero reads and prints in the era before Christ. The era is a suffix where the time would start, the sign is a prefix on the year, and the two say the same day, so `'0000-01-01'` and `'0001-01-01 (BC)'` are the same date and both print the second way. Printing was already half there and was off by one, because the astronomical calendar has a year zero and the era does not. The era counts from one and the two spellings do not mix, so a year of zero written with the suffix and a sign written with it are both refused. Three things next to it were measured at the same time and fixed with it: the oldest date is `5877642-06-25 (BC)`, one day later than the old range check allowed, a field with a sign of its own like `'+2021-01-01'` is not a date, and more than one space between the date and the time is read rather than refused.

## 0.2.34

Six changes. Two of them are the rest of the operators moving behind the new engine traits, one is `EXPLAIN`, and three are planner, cast and embedding work that landed beside them. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The distinct, the set operations, the aggregate and the join sit behind the sink trait. That is every pipeline breaker there is, so the only thing still pulling is the leaf sources. Each one takes `&self` and is handed its mutable part beside the chunk, which is what lets one plan node run on as many threads as F4 wants. Two of them needed a shape the trait does not have on its own, because a set operation and a join both read one whole side before they can decide anything about a row of the other, which in push terms is two pipelines with a dependency edge between them. The side that has to finish first now ends in a sink that holds its rows and does nothing else, and the side that uses them reads them through a handle. That edge is the one the scheduler will read off the plan, and for the join it is where the hash table goes when #62 replaces the nested loop. The aggregate is the one place where being in the shape is not the same as being parallel: it holds its hash table in the instance, which is where it has to be, and merging two of those needs a serialize and a combine per aggregate that nothing implements yet, so a second instance is refused rather than answered wrongly. No answer changed anywhere in this, which is the point of moving the operators before anything replaces them.
- The cross product is a stream, and it is what made progress grow a fourth answer. One of its input chunks becomes as many output chunks as its right side has, which a stream could not say with `More` and `Done` and a sink could only answer by holding the whole product. `Progress::Again` is an operator saying it has more output for input it already has, the driver asks it again with the operator nearest the sink going first so that nothing above it is handed a chunk built from an input it was half way through, and the operator keeps whatever it still needs itself rather than expecting the chunk back. The right side is kept as the chunks it arrived in rather than taken apart into rows, because that side is replayed once per left row and rebuilding chunks out of rows would be a copy of the whole side for nothing. Unnest and the window functions answer the same way, so this is not a special case built for one operator.
- `EXPLAIN` prints the plan the query would have run, with a row count estimate on every line. It parses to its own statement, binds to the same plan the query binds to, runs the same optimizer passes with the same context, and then prints instead of executing, so what it shows is what would have run, down to which filters were pushed and which projections were dropped, and a test pins the text against the planner to keep it that way. A plan built a second way for display is a plan nobody executes. The two columns are called `explain_key` and `explain_value` because that is the shape a client reading a result set has to cope with, and the text inside them is ours: `spec/12-duckdb-compat.md` section 12.5 excludes explain text from the guarantee, since DuckDB's vocabulary is a physical one with `HASH_JOIN` in it and matching their text would pin our optimizer to their operator names. An operator nobody can estimate says `rows unknown` rather than being left blank, because blank reads as zero and the difference between no rows and nobody knows is the whole of what the estimate is careful about. `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`, the option list and the twenty explainable statements that are not a query are refusals that name what they refused. Two things came out of getting there: the query path binds the statement rather than assuming it is a query, since `EXPLAIN` answers with rows, and a statement that writes is refused there rather than run under a read lock.
- An expression can say whether its value for a row is decided by that row on its own, and whether it is decided before any row is read. Those are the last two of the four analyses #102 asks for, and they sit beside the volatility question rather than in files of their own, because they are the same question asked the same way, one match arm per expression. Filter pushdown asks the first one of a projection source before substituting a predicate into it, and limit pushdown asks it of every projection expression before trading places with the projection. Neither can fail today, because the binder puts every aggregate in an aggregate node and leaves none in a projection, and both are asked anyway, since a window function is the next expression that reads more than its own row and the pass that believes an invariant without checking it is the one that pushes a filter through a window and answers a different query. The third caller changes a number: a conjunct that reads no column is no longer counted as a condition by the estimate, because the selectivity constant is a guess about a predicate over data and a condition that does not touch the data keeps every row or none of them rather than a fifth of them.
- A cast from a string to a number reads the five spellings it used to refuse. A point rounds half away from zero, so `'1.5'` is 2 and `'-2.5'` is -3. An exponent shifts the digits, so `'1e3'` is 1000. An underscore between two digits is a separator, so `'1_000'` is a thousand while `'_100'`, `'1_'` and `'1__0'` are none of them a number. `0x` and `0b` are read and `0o` is not, which looks like an accident upstream and is reproduced rather than tidied up. The reading is one pass that pulls the string into a sign, the digits, how many of them were behind the point and what the exponent said, and it is done on the digits rather than through a double, which is what makes `'9223372036854775807.4'` the largest BIGINT instead of the number above it that a double would have rounded to first. Because an integer is a decimal whose scale is zero, that same pass is the decimal parser with the target's scale in place of the zero. The CSV sniffer needed two rules of its own on top of that, since it defers to the cast for the number rungs and the cast now reads more than it did, so a point or an exponent keeps a column off the BIGINT rung and a separator keeps it off both. All of it was measured one statement at a time against the pinned `v2.0.0-dev84237 (cc7e7bac7f)`.
- The optimizer pass names are something an embedder can read. `rudb::optimizers()` hands back every pass by the name `SET disabled_optimizers` knows it by, in the order they run, so joining that list with commas and setting it is every rewrite off and the plan that runs is the one the binder produced. That is what the corpus in `tamnd/rudb-compat` needs in order to run each file twice and require the two answers to match, and it needs no new expected outputs, because the unoptimized answer is the right answer by construction and a file that answers differently with the passes on is a pass that changed an answer. A test says that every name the list publishes is a name the setting accepts, one at a time and all of them at once, so a pass added without one fails here rather than in the other repository.

## 0.2.33

Five changes, two of them the next two operators moving behind the new engine traits, one the first row count estimate, and two the written time and the zone on the end of it. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The three streaming operators sit behind the push trait. The filter, the projection and the limit are `Stream` implementations, each taking `&self` with its mutable part handed in beside the chunk, so one operator can run on as many threads as F4 wants without copying its predicate and the state that will have to be spilled later is named rather than buried in a field. The tree is still pulled, with an adapter turning a pull into a push, and that adapter is the whole of the temporary part. One thing that is not a refactor came with it: a limit says it is finished on the chunk that fills the count rather than on the call after it, which stops the scan instead of reading rows in order to throw them away.
- The sort and the top N sit behind the sink trait. Both are pipeline breakers, so rows arrive through `sink`, an instance hands what it holds over through `combine`, and `finalize` orders once at the end, which on one thread is the same work in the same order as the loop that was there before. The finished chunks go into a shared buffer that is its own source rather than something `finalize` hands back, because a hash aggregate has to finalise into a structure that a separate source then reads in parallel and fusing the two would make that impossible. The top N trims in two places now, once per instance as it goes and once over what the instances brought, which is what keeps the bound the bound rather than the bound times the number of threads.
- A plan node can say how many rows it thinks it produces. The constants are the textbook ones, which is to say DuckDB's, and they are wrong for any particular query in a direction that does not depend on the query, which is the property that makes them usable for choosing between two sides. The answer is optional rather than defaulted, so a scan of a table nobody measured is unknown and so is everything above it, because a made up number that looks like a measurement is how an optimizer talks itself into the wrong plan. Three of the answers are facts rather than guesses: an ungrouped aggregate is one row over any input, a `VALUES` is the length of its row list, and a limit is a ceiling even when what feeds it is unknown. No pass reads any of this yet, so no plan changed and nothing got faster or slower.
- A written time can be read. `TIME` was a type you could declare a column of and never put a value in, because there was no cast from a string and nothing else produced one, so the declaration succeeded and the failure arrived at the insert. Upstream reads a time with a different parser from the one it reads a date and a timestamp with, not the same one with another message, and this reproduces it: one sentence for every way of failing, the seconds and the fraction both optional, the fraction cut at six digits rather than refused, and whatever follows the numbers ignored, so `'12:34:56 UTC'` and `'12:34:56abc'` are both twelve thirty four and six. The CSV sniffer gets the rung it has been missing plus a rule of its own, since a rung that deferred to a cast that lenient would take a whole timestamp column and drop every day in it.
- A written timestamp can carry the zone it was written in. rudb read the zone as part of the seconds field and refused the whole string, where upstream reads it, checks it and throws it away, because a TIMESTAMP has nowhere to keep one. Splitting the clock from the zone before either is read is also what fixes `'2020-01-01 -05:00'`, which used to be reported as an hour of minus five that is out of range when the minus is the start of a zone. There is a third sentence up there that rudb did not have, for a sign that starts an offset and does not finish one, and a date has no such sentence and says the format instead, which was measured rather than guessed. So was the rest of it: the offset is not range checked, the zone name is not looked up, exactly one space in front of the name is required, an upper case `Z` is UTC and a lower case one is not.

## 0.2.32

The first release with the F0 engine work in it. Seven changes, four of them are the skeleton the new engine hangs off and three of them are compatibility fixes that came in beside it. Nothing in the skeleton changes an answer yet, which is the point of doing it before there is anything to move. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- There is a place to hold two implementations of one thing. `rudb-seam` is a registry keyed by a closed list of twenty seven seams, a seam being a point where two or more published designs disagree about how to do the same job, and a hash table is one while a type is not, since there is one right answer for what `DECIMAL(18,3) + INTEGER` produces and it is DuckDB's. Every implementation carries a name, a sentence saying what it is, where it came from, whether it is bit for bit deterministic, and whether it applies to the query in front of it. The rule that makes this affordable is that a seam is crossed once per chunk and never once per row, so every trait method takes a whole chunk, a whole column, a whole morsel or a decision made at plan time, and the dispatch is at the door of the loop rather than inside it. One indirect call every hundred and twenty thousand rows is not measurable and one per row is an order of magnitude, and that difference is the reason a design with this many swappable parts can be fast at all.
- The operator interface is settled on push before there are more operators to move. `Source`, `Stream` and `Sink` all take `&self` with the per instance state handed in beside it, so one plan node is shared across however many threads run it and the thing that is not shared is the small mutable part. Progress is `More`, `Done` or `Blocked`, and blocked carries one of exactly four reasons, waiting on io, on memory, on something upstream or on something downstream, because a scheduler that cannot tell those apart cannot do anything useful about any of them. A pull adapter sits at the root so the existing query path runs unchanged over the new traits, which is what lets the operators move one at a time rather than in one commit.
- The list of swappable parts is something a query can read. `rudb_strategies()` is a table function over every seam, every registered implementation, which one is the reference, and what the current session would choose, so the answer to what this build can do comes out of the build rather than out of a document that was true last month. It is also what an error message points at when somebody names a seam that does not exist.
- Every seam is reachable three ways and the three are one mechanism. `rudb --set hash.table=unchained` before the process starts, `SET seam_hash_table = 'unchained'` for a session, and `SELECT /*+ hash.table(unchained) */ ...` for one query, all ending in the same function, so they cannot come to disagree about what a name means or about what the message is when the name is wrong. The DuckDB grammar we vendor has no dot inside an identifier, so the dotted spelling has to be quoted and the underscored one does not, and both are accepted along with the bare seam name. Hints are collected by the tokenizer rather than by a second scan over the text, so a `/*+` inside a string literal stays a string literal. A mistyped seam name fails the statement and lists the seams, because the failure it is guarding against is a benchmark run that silently measured the wrong thing. The resolved choice does not change what runs yet, since no seam has a second implementation in the tree before F1, and that is written down where the value is dropped so it reads as waiting rather than as dead code.
- A failed cast says what DuckDB says. There were two sentences here and eight upstream, so the same query failed with different words on the two engines even when it failed for the same reason, and the compatibility record had been cut back to a prefix both sides could agree on. All eight were measured one statement at a time against the pinned `v2.0.0-dev84237 (cc7e7bac7f)` and are reproduced word for word, including the part that is not the split you would guess: a string that will not parse and a string that parses and does not fit are the same sentence, so `'300'::TINYINT` says it could not convert rather than that it is out of range. The names in these are the physical ones, so an overflow says INT32 and a decimal carries the width of the integer it is stored in. Two of them were wrong answers and not only wrong words, since the thirty first of April used to come back as the first of May, and a date written with a time now has to have written a real time.
- An interval literal is rewritten into the call DuckDB rewrites it into. `SELECT INTERVAL 1 DAY` raised out of the transformer, and there is no interval node in the fix because upstream has none either: it turns the literal into an ordinary function call and names the column after the call, so doing the same rewrite means the literal and a handwritten `to_days(1)` are the same expression from the transformer on. Eleven of the thirteen units truncate to a whole number and the two that can carry a fraction stay a double, which is the difference between `INTERVAL 1.5 DAY` being one day and `INTERVAL 2.7 SECOND` being two and seven tenths of a second.
- Two `xtask` tests build the paths they compare instead of writing them out. Both had a forward slash in the expected string and both were comparing against a sentence printed with whatever separator the platform uses, so Windows had been red on every pull request since the conformance check went in.

## 0.2.31

The release where a column name that DuckDB writes in quotes comes back in quotes here too. Three changes, and the middle one is the reason to believe the other two, because it found that the conformance corpus had spent some number of runs checking a published build rather than the tree it was pointed at. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- An identifier inside a generated name is quoted wherever DuckDB quotes one. `SELECT min(name) FROM t` comes back from the pinned `v2.0.0-dev84237 (cc7e7bac7f)` in a column called `min("name")` and came back here as `min(name)`, and the same went for `substring`, `position`, `nullif`, `trim` and every other word upstream treats that way, so a tool that reads column names off a result saw a different name than it would have. There are two rules and they were both read off the binary rather than guessed. One is that the folded word is in the keyword table with a class mask that is not zero, which is why `name` and `action` are quoted as unreserved words, `left` and `right` as function or type names, `nullif`, `overlay`, `position`, `row`, `substring` and `trim` as column names, and why `alias` is not quoted at all, since it is spelled by a rule and is in no class, and neither are `banana`, `substr`, `strpos`, `lower`, `length`, `abs`, `chr` or `concat`, which are not keywords. The other is that the text is not one word the tokenizer would read back, so `my col`, `9x` and `é` keep their quotes and an embedded quote is doubled. What upstream does not quote on is case, so `min(UserID)` stays exactly like that even though reading it again would fold it, and that is the whole reason the ninety nine column ClickBench names agree between the two engines, so this does not quote on case either. The rule is the parser's own keyword table through `classes(lookup(text))` and not a list in the deparser, so a keyword somebody adds later is covered the day it is added.
- The conformance corpus says which rudb it conformed, and refuses to run if the answer is the wrong one. `cargo xtask conform` builds the harness in tamnd/rudb-compat against this tree through a `[patch]`, and the harness lock file pins a version, so once this tree's version moved past the pinned one cargo quietly dropped the patch, printed `warning: patch for rudb ... was not used in the crate graph` in the middle of a few hundred lines of build output, and ran the whole corpus against rudb 0.2.29 downloaded from git. Every run was green and none of them were measuring anything. It was proven with `cargo tree --invert rudb --depth 0` under the same config, which printed the git checkout and not the path. The step now reads that line before it runs anything, repoints the harness with `cargo update -p rudb` when the line is not this tree, reads it again, and stops with an error naming what it resolved to rather than running a corpus whose result would mean nothing. The harness lock file is the one thing conform writes, which the module documentation now says, because it used to say nothing is written. Verified by putting the compat lock back to 0.2.28, watching conform repoint it and run against 0.2.30 from the tree, running it again to see it do nothing, and watching the count of that warning go from one to zero.
- `chr`, `left`, `right`, `concat` and `replace` have signature rows, so they resolve. All five were kernels with nothing to bind them and answered `Not implemented Error`. Every rule in them was measured one statement at a time on the pinned binary rather than taken from the documentation. `left` and `right` count characters and not bytes, a negative count means all but that many from the other end so `left('abc', -1)` is `ab` and `right('abc', -1)` is `bc`, and a count past either end clamps instead of raising. `replace` with an empty needle changes nothing and returns the text, and any null argument makes the whole thing null. `concat` skips its nulls instead of propagating them, so `concat('a', 1, NULL)` is `a1` and `concat(NULL)` is the empty string, which is the one function here where a null does not win. `chr` is a code point and not a byte, so `chr(233)` is one `é` and `chr(0)` is a string of length one, and the three ways to be outside the range all raise in upstream's words, `Invalid UTF8 Codepoint` followed by the number, including the surrogate half at 55296. Argument types are checked the way upstream checks them, so `chr(65::BIGINT)`, `chr(65.9)`, `chr(TRUE)` and `left('abc', 2::DOUBLE)` are binder errors here as well. All twenty six answers and seven error messages are the same text on both engines, with two known gaps left over: `chr('a')` raises a binder error where upstream raises a conversion error, which is the untyped string literal this parser does not have yet, and a list handed to `concat` or `||` comes back as the text the list prints as where upstream refuses the call, filed as #358.

## 0.2.30

The release where a string written with a letter in front of it stops answering with the query. Four changes, the first two are the two halves of one wrong answer, and the third is the first time this repository has written down a place where DuckDB is wrong and we are not. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A prefixed string literal is decoded instead of being handed back as its own source text. `SELECT E'a'` answered with the four characters `E'a'`, `SELECT B'101'` with `B'101'`, and a filter written against `E'\n'` found nothing and reported, accurately and uselessly, that it had found nothing. The cause was one arm: `string_value` stripped the quotes off a token and fell through to pushing the text when the token did not start with a quote, which is what every prefixed spelling does. That arm is gone and there is no replacement for it, because a default that silently answers with the query is a default that will do this again with the next spelling somebody adds, so a spelling the transformer does not know raises. `E'...'` is a C style escaped string and every rule in it was read off the pinned `v2.0.0-dev84237 (cc7e7bac7f)` one escape at a time through `hex()`, so that nothing could be hidden by the rendering. The named escapes are `\n`, `\t`, `\r`, `\b` and `\f`, and `\v` is not one of them however much it looks like one. `\x` takes one or two hex digits and `\0` through `\7` take one to three octal digits, and both write a byte rather than a character, so `\xc3\xa9` is one `é` and `\377` is not a string at all. `\uHHHH` wants all four digits and writes the character they name, and a short one, a non hex one, a surrogate half and a NUL all fall back to the rule that covers everything else, which is that the backslash is dropped and the character is kept. So `\q` is `q` and `😀` is the ten characters it was written as. The two ways the result is not a string raise in upstream's own words, `Null character not permitted in escape string literal` and `Invalid UTF-8 in escape string literal at byte offset 1: byte mismatch`, where the offset is where the bytes stop being a string and not where the escape was written. `N'abc'` is a cast of the string to VARCHAR and not a plain string, which the column name proves, since the binary answers it in a column called `CAST('abc' AS VARCHAR)`, so it is built as that cast. `B'101'` is the one the issue asked to measure rather than assume, and it is not a bit string: upstream answers the four characters `b101` as a VARCHAR, `B'abc'` is `babc` and `B''` is the single letter `b`.
- A hex string is read as the blob it is. `x'4142'` is a BLOB and not a VARCHAR, so the half above left it raising rather than answering it as a string, which would have been a second wrong answer of the same kind as the one it fixed. What goes in the AST is the text a blob prints as and not the text it was written as, because the binary names the column after the value: `x'4142'` is `'AB'::BLOB` and `x'ff41'` is `'\xFFA'::BLOB`. That printed form is also the form the string to blob cast reads bytes back from, so one text serves the name and the value and the two cannot disagree, and it is `Value` that prints it, so this cannot drift from the way a blob prints anywhere else. The errors fall out of the same arrangement. Upstream writes `\xHH` for every pair without looking at the digits and lets the cast refuse the ones that are not hex, which is why `x'4'` is `Parser Error: Hex string literal must have an even number of hex digits` and `x'41zz'` is a conversion error one step later naming `\xzz`, and doing the same thing here gives both messages in the same words for nothing. The pairs are bytes and not characters, which is how upstream counts them, so `x'éé'` is four bytes and two pairs and says so in its own error. Every statement was run through both shells on server2 and the answers, the column names and both errors are the same text on both engines. The four spellings went into `corpus/dialect.sql` in the harness at tamnd/rudb-compat#34, which is fifteen of fifteen through the shells now.
- The specification has somewhere to write down that DuckDB is wrong. The ClickBench sweep over the full ninety nine million row file has been reporting that query 4 differs, and the difference is not ours: `SELECT AVG(UserID) FROM hits` is wrong on the pinned binary and right here. Document 02.4 pointed at 14.4 for the list of permitted divergences, 14.4 is about function level differential testing and there was no list, so 14.10 is the list and the pointer aims at it. An entry has to carry a reproduction with both answers and an argument for which is right that does not appeal to our own implementation, a reason for not matching, since matching is the default and somebody migrating depends on the behaviour they have, somewhere upstream it is tracked, because an entry with no upstream report is one nobody has finished, and a note that it is temporary, since the ledger getting shorter is the healthy direction. The first entry is the ungrouped sum of a BIGINT column off a parquet scan dropping carries out of the high word of its 128 bit accumulator, short by exactly `282 * 2^64` over that file, which is not what a wrong value looks like. The binary disagrees with itself four ways, since `SUM(UserID::HUGEINT)`, `SUM(UserID::DECIMAL(38,0))`, the same column summed out of a table and the sum of its own per counter group sums all give the answer we give. It reduces to two statements over a 1.3 MB one column file, and narrowing says it is a function of how far the accumulator has gone rather than a bad row, is identical at one and sixteen threads so it is not the parallel combine, and follows the encoding rather than the order, since every file that loses a carry is dictionary encoded.
- The gate stops measuring against whatever pull request was merged an hour ago. `scripts/gate` worked out its base as the merge base with the trunk and fell back to `HEAD~1` when that came out equal to HEAD, and the base equals HEAD whenever nothing is committed on top of the trunk, which is the state every branch starts in and the state the gate is run in most often, because the gate is what you run before you commit. So editing two markdown files produced a changed list of seven paths, five of them from somebody else's merge, and the focus correctly followed those into six crates and compiled and tested all of them to check that a specification paragraph was still a specification paragraph. The two cases are now split: with a dirty tree the uncommitted work is the whole of the change and HEAD is what to measure against, and with a clean tree there is no other reading than what the last commit did. The other half is a line per part saying how long it took, because there was no way to answer why the gate is slow except by guessing. On server3 with a warm target the parts are clippy at 77.3s, test at 82.3s, doc at 76.3s and corpus at 84.4s, and the repeat with nothing changed is the interesting column: clippy goes to 3.2s, so its time is compilation and cargo caches it, and test does not move at all, so its time is the tests running, which is `rudb` at 25.0s and `rudb-cli` at 15.1s against three to four seconds each for the rest.

## 0.2.29

The release where a group by on a string that came through a join stops answering one group per row. Three changes, and the first one is a wrong answer. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Grouping a string that arrived as one value for the whole chunk finds its group again. `SELECT s, count(*) FROM a, b WHERE a.k = b.k GROUP BY s` over nine rows and three distinct strings answered with nine groups, each with a count of one. A cross product hands the left row down as constant vectors, one value standing for the whole chunk, which is what makes pairing one left row with a thousand right ones cost one value per left column rather than a thousand. `Vector::text_at` has nothing to hand back for that form and says so with `None`, and `Table::holds` was reading that `None` as a difference, so the probe hashed the row into the right bucket, found the group already sitting there, compared it, decided it was not the same string and opened another group, every row, forever. The hash was never wrong and neither was the bucket. `fold` in the same file already falls through to `value_at` for the forms that have no run of bytes to walk, and so does `set` in `group.rs`, and this was the one caller that did not. Found by running TPC-H at SF 0.01 against the pinned `v2.0.0-dev84237 (cc7e7bac7f)` on server2, where q05 came back with 103 rows against 25 nations and numbers a twelfth of the right ones, q07 with 46 against 4, and q10 with duplicate customer keys. All three are byte for byte identical with the reference now. Nothing on ClickBench ever showed it, because ClickBench has no joins, and a `JOIN ... ON` never showed it either, because that path materializes its output and hands down flat vectors.
- The regular expression engine gets a backtracker and a prefilter. Pike's machine runs every possibility at once, which is what makes it linear and also what makes it slow on the patterns people write, because `^https?://(?:www\.)?([^/]+)/.*$` over a URL has exactly one way on at nearly every position and the machine is paying for a thread list to hold one thread. `bitstate.rs` is RE2's `BitState`: it walks the one way on, keeps the capture slots in one array with an undo record beside every branch that writes to it, and reuses its three buffers out of a thread local, so a column is not a hundred million allocations of the same three things. What keeps it linear is the memo, one bit per instruction per position, because whether the rest of the program can match from a position does not depend on where the groups have been on the way there, so `(a+)+b` against two thousand `a`s is still answered at once. A program times a text over a quarter of a megabyte of bits goes to Pike's machine instead, which needs no memo because it never visits a pair twice. The prefilter is the other half: `compile.rs` works out the set of bytes a match may begin with and an unanchored search skips to one of them rather than running the whole program from every byte. On server2 over the one million row ClickBench partition, against the pinned binary's 1.02s, ClickBench query 29 as written goes from 9.28s to 2.26s, a global replace from 17.04s to 1.89s, and `count(*) where regexp_matches(Referer, 'yandex')` from 6.79s to 0.53s against the binary's 0.16s. Every answer is byte identical in all four.
- A type in front of a string is read as a cast of that string. `DATE '1995-09-01'` said `is not supported yet, the grammar rule is TypeLiteral`. It is the cast written the other way round and upstream it is the same node, which the column name proves: the pinned binary answers `date '1995-09-01'`, `CAST('1995-09-01' AS DATE)` and `'1995-09-01'::DATE` all three in a column called `CAST('1995-09-01' AS DATE)`. So the transformer builds the cast node and nothing else, which means every type the cast already takes is a typed literal from today, parameters and all, and the two spellings cannot drift apart because there is only one of them below the parser. TPC-H q14 is the query that wanted it, and at SF 0.01 it now runs and matches the reference byte for byte, which takes TPC-H from twelve of twenty two to thirteen.

## 0.2.28

The release where a query that is told to stop stops. Three changes and the one worth reading is the third. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The nested loop join checks the cancellation token inside its own loop. Every operator is wrapped in a check that runs between the chunks it produces, and the argument for putting it there rather than in each operator is that one line in one place is not a decision somebody gets wrong when they add the twentieth operator. That argument is still right and it had a hole in it, which is an operator that produces no chunks until it has finished, because for that one a check between chunks is a check at the end. The join is that operator: it reads both sides, runs the whole join, and hands back its first chunk when there is nothing left to do. The compatibility harness measured a hundred thousand left rows against thirty thousand right ones running for sixty two seconds after being told at one second to stop, and then reporting, accurately and uselessly, that it had gone past a one second limit. So the join holds the token and checks it once per left row, in the same place and at the same granularity as the reservation it already grows there, which means a query can overshoot its clock by one pass over the right side and no more. Nothing else needs it, because every other operator that buffers reads its input through a child operator and the child is wrapped, so it is checked all the way through the reading. Measured on server2 against four upstream corpus files the harness had been killing, with a ten second clock on a statement, `hash_join_dict_surviving.test` goes from 329 seconds to 32, `test_prefix_range_filter_pushdown.test` from 124 to 20, and the two that barely move are files where several statements each run out their own ten seconds, which is the limit working rather than the limit being missed.
- The committed corpus is a gate in this repository too. `tamnd/rudb-compat` owns two corpora and they are two different things: the upstream sqllogictest files are a measurement that moves when somebody else adds a file, and the files under `corpus/slt` are ours, they say what rudb can do today, and they are never allowed to go down. The second one ran in that repository only, against whatever rudb the pin there points at, so a regression was found days after the commit that caused it and by somebody moving a pin. Now `cargo xtask conform` runs it against the tree in front of it, the shell built out of this working tree and the library patched to the same tree rather than to the pin, with nothing written to either manifest to do it, because a gate that edits the tree it is checking leaves a dirty checkout behind on the run that fails. `cargo xtask ci` runs it last and says so when there is no harness checked out beside this repository, and the workflow has a job for it in the required list.
- The release job stops retrying the crates.io daily cap. A publish that is refused because the account has hit its limit for the day is not going to succeed in forty minutes, so the retry loop sat there burning a runner and then failed anyway.

## 0.2.27

The release where the second half of the hash aggregate stops working a row at a time. One change, and it is the half #308 said it was deliberately leaving behind. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A chunk folds into many accumulators in one pass. #308 took the row out of grouping and left it in the aggregate, so what the operator did after it was hash a chunk a column at a time, probe with the hash in hand, and then, for every row and for every call on that row, build a `Value` for the argument and go through an update that matches on which aggregate it is and on what type the value holds before it adds anything to anything. Which aggregate this is was settled when the operator was built and what layout the argument arrives in was settled when the chunk was produced, so two of those three matches are answered before the loop starts, and the `Value` is pure loss: a copy of a word into a wider enum for a fixed width column and a malloc and a free for a string. `update_scattered` is the grouped form of the `update_run` that has folded a whole vector into one accumulator since 2b. It takes a vector, one slot per row saying which accumulator that row belongs to, and a stride and an offset saying where the calls of one group live, which is the layout the hash aggregate already keeps, so nothing is copied to call it and the slots are the probe results the operator has in hand. It cannot reduce the way the ungrouped form does, because two adjacent rows are usually two different groups and there is nothing to add up before the scatter, but everything else goes. A row that went to a spill file and a row a call's `FILTER` threw away point at the same sentinel, so the filter is folded into a copy of the slots rather than read inside the loop that walks them and there is no second mask to hand over. The loops cover the shapes a scan produces, which is flat and dictionary over the fixed width layouts, and they are chosen by the same rules the ungrouped form already uses, with the arms in the same order so the two cannot drift into disagreeing about what a decimal at the wrong scale means. Both counts are answered by the validity mask alone. A shape with no loop falls through to the row at a time update and records itself in the fallback counters the way every other kernel does, so a column that misses shows up as a number rather than as an opinion. Measured on server2 over one million rows of the real ClickBench file, one query per process, three runs, against 0.2.26 and the pinned binary: `GROUP BY RegionID` with `count`, `sum` and `avg` goes from 280ms to 130ms where the binary is 110ms, `GROUP BY SearchPhrase` with four aggregates from 520ms to 340ms, and `GROUP BY UserID` with two from 420ms to 330ms. The low cardinality case is where this shows most, which is the opposite of #308: there the cost was per group and here it is per row, so a thousand groups over a million rows pays it a million times. On the full forty three, q34 goes from 2.70s to 2.18s, q25 from 3.16s to 2.84s, q29 from 0.54s to 0.40s, q24 from 0.41s to 0.28s, q11 from 0.36s to 0.26s and four more move, with every answer byte identical. The ungrouped row does not move, which is right, since that path is untouched. What is left in the operator is `DISTINCT` inside an aggregate, which is still a hash set per group per call and is a grouping of its own keyed on the type grouping used before #237, and giving it the table #237 built is a change of its own.

## 0.2.26

The release where a function that was never implemented stops answering anyway. Two changes and they are the same corner of the transformer seen twice, first as the bug and then as the work it was hiding. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A grammar rule that wrote a keyword is not stepped through on the way to its one argument. `SELECT trim('  a  ')` answered the untrimmed string and `SELECT length(trim('  a  '))` answered 5 against the pinned binary's 1, with nothing said about it. The expression dispatch stepped through any rule with exactly one child, on the theory that such a rule is a precedence level that said nothing about the value, and that is true of the ten chain rules it was written for and false of any rule that spells a keyword, because a keyword is not a child of the node that spells it. `TrimExpression <- 'TRIM' Parens(TrimArguments)` has one child, so the loop walked past the `TRIM` and transformed the argument. Three other rules had the same shape and all three were being walked through too: `row(1)` answered 1 where upstream answers the struct `(1,)`, `try('a'::INT)` raised the conversion error upstream answers NULL for, and `unpack([1])` tried to build a list. `trim` is the one worth a p0 of the four, because it is the only one that answers a row rather than raising something, and because a query that trims a column and groups by it got the wrong groups. Two arguments hid it, since `trim('xxaxx', 'x')` has two children and has always said it is not supported. What replaces the count is a comparison of the spans: a precedence rule with one child covers exactly the text its child covers and a rule that wrote a keyword or a bracket covers more, so the step is taken only when the two are equal. That is a rule rather than a list of the names that happened to be wrong, which matters because the vendored grammar has eleven hundred rules and the ones with a keyword above a single child are not enumerable by reading the ones somebody noticed. `ParensExpression` is the one rule that covers more text and still says nothing, since its brackets are grouping, and it has an arm of its own.
- `SUBSTRING`, `POSITION`, `TRIM` and `OVERLAY` are calls. Each of the four is a grammar rule of its own, the transformer had an arm for none of them, and the plain spellings behind them were missing from the signature table as well, so neither `substring('abcdef', 2, 3)` nor `substr('abcdef', 2, 3)` could be written. Each rule lowers to the call DuckDB's own parser writes, read off the pinned binary as the column name of each statement rather than worked out from the grammar, and three of the lowerings move something: `POSITION` swaps its arguments so the haystack comes first, `TRIM` turns a direction into a different function so `LEADING` is `ltrim` and `TRAILING` is `rtrim`, and `substring('abcdef' FOR 3)` is three arguments with a 1 filled in rather than the two argument call it looks like. The index rules were measured one statement at a time, the way the subscripts were, because not one of them follows from the others. The window starts where the index says and is clipped afterwards, so `substring('abcdef', 0, 3)` is `ab` and not `abc`. A negative start counts back from the end, and a start still off the string after counting back leaves nothing when there is a length and the whole string when there is not. A negative length runs the window backwards from the start rather than being empty, so `substring('abcdef', 4, -2)` is `bc`. An index has to be a whole number already and is not rounded, which is upstream's rule and the same one the subscripts follow. `trim` with no characters named strips the space alone and leaves a tab where it is, which was measured rather than assumed, and with characters named it strips any of them as a set. `overlay` takes the replacement's own length when it is told a negative one, which is the one place it parts company with `substring`. Everything counts characters and not bytes, so `substring('héllo', 2, 2)` is `él`. Ninety seven statements through both shells agree on every value and every error message, and the only disagreements left are the column names upstream quotes because the name is a keyword, which is #251.

## 0.2.25

The release where three things that could not be written at all can be written, and the hash aggregate stops being the largest cost left in ClickBench. Brackets, `typeof` and the three spellings of a null check share a common thread, which is that every one of them was a grammar rule the transformer walked past rather than a function nobody had implemented. The fourth change is unrelated to those and is the p0. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Grouping hashes a chunk a column at a time and keeps its keys in columns, which is the shape a chunk wants them back in. The finding, when the operator was taken apart, was that the hashing was never the problem. A group cost the allocator four times: a `Vec<Value>` built per row to ask the question, a clone of that vector into the map on a miss, a second `Vec<Value>` per group to hand the answer back, and a clone of every key value while transposing those rows back into columns to make a chunk. Two of those are per row and two are per group, and the ClickBench queries that group on something close to one group per row were spending most of the operator there rather than in the table. What replaces it is a purpose built open addressed table that never materializes a row: one pass per key column folds the whole chunk into one hash per row with the type matched on once instead of once per value, the probe walks the buckets with the hash already in hand, and a stored key is compared against the vector in place, through `text_at` for a string so that a dictionary column is not copied per row. The keys already live one column per vector, so the answer is built straight out of them and both transposes are gone. The one invariant worth stating out loud is that the same column arrives in more than one form, because a `VARCHAR` read out of a parquet file is a dictionary in one chunk and flat in the next, and if the typed path and the general path disagreed by so much as a discriminant then a group by would answer with the same string twice, so the general path does not mix a discriminant either, since a typed arm over a run of `i32` has none to mix. The rule that folds one word and the rule that spreads the result are free functions used by both the digest and the table, because two hash functions that are meant to be the same one are two hash functions that will eventually differ. Measured on server2 over one million rows of the real ClickBench file, one query per process, against the pinned binary: `GROUP BY WatchID` at a million groups goes from 1201ms to 369ms where the binary is 192ms, and `GROUP BY WatchID, ClientIP` from 1515ms to 693ms where the binary is 166ms. Low and mid cardinality grouping was already at parity and stays there, which is what said the remaining cost was per group rather than per row. On the full forty three, q34 goes from 3249ms to 1959ms, q41 from 2253ms to 1722ms, q20 from 1032ms to 692ms, q25 from 2522ms to 2182ms, q35 from 1116ms to 886ms and eight more move, with every answer byte identical to 0.2.24. What is left in the operator is the per row aggregate update, a dynamic dispatch per row per call with a key built per row per call for the argument, and a `DISTINCT` argument that is still a hash set per group per call, both deliberately untouched here. Four queries looked worse on the first sweep and none survived being run three times, and the one that took longest to dismiss, q30, turned out to be spending ten of its eleven seconds inside `REGEXP_REPLACE` over 921,225 rows where the binary does the whole query in one, which is filed as #310 against the regex engine rather than against the aggregate.
- A bracket indexes and slices, which is two function calls and is how DuckDB's own transformer reads it. One index becomes `array_extract`, a range becomes `array_slice`, and the bounds the query left out are filled in before the binder sees them: a missing begin is 1, a missing end is -1, and the lone hyphen in `x[1:-]` is a range with no end rather than a subtraction of nothing. `x[]` is the one subscript the parser takes and the transformer refuses, in upstream's words. Every rule under this was measured against the pinned binary case by case, because almost none of it is what a reader would guess. Indices count from one. An index off the end of a list is null and an index off the end of a string is the empty string. A range clamps rather than raising and the clamping is not symmetric, so a begin below one moves up to one and an end past the length moves down to it, while an end that is still negative after counting back from the end stays where it is, which is why `'abcdef'[-99:-98]` is empty. A step is a list only feature and a step on a string is a not implemented error with a suggested rewrite in it. A slice of a list answers a list, and there is no LIST vector yet, so those stop with the same message `SELECT [1, 2, 3]` already stops with, which is #302. What this closed in passing is a statement in `corpus/dialect.sql` that had nothing to do with subscripts: `SELECT x[1:2] FROM t` has no table `t`, and rudb refused the slice before it ever went looking for the table, so the two engines disagreed about a catalog error.
- `typeof` is answered in the binder rather than by a kernel. The type of an expression is settled the moment the expression is bound and nothing about it changes per row, so the fold happens in `bind_call` and the executor never sees the call. The argument still goes through the aggregate rules before it is dropped, which is not a detail: upstream refuses `SELECT typeof(x), count(*) FROM t` for the same reason it refuses a bare `x` there, even though neither of them reads a value, and folding without that check would have accepted it. The names are the ones `LogicalType` already prints and they turned out to be right for every case measured, including the one the issue had guessed wrong. `typeof(NULL)` is six characters and not four, because the quotes are part of the name upstream prints, which is visible in `typeof([])` coming back as `"NULL"[]`.
- `COALESCE`, `NULLIF` and `IFNULL` can be written. The first two are grammar rules of their own and the transformer had an arm for neither, so neither could be written at all, while `coalesce` had a signature row, a kernel and its exception to the null in null out rule underneath the whole time. `COALESCE` is written with the canonical name and the binder prints the column in capitals, because upstream calls the column `COALESCE(NULL, 1)` whatever case the query used: it is an operator there rather than a function name its parser folded. `IFNULL` is a plain call that upstream's parser rewrites into that same operator, qualifier and all, and the wrong number of arguments to it is a parser error there rather than the binder's arity message, so it is one here too. `NULLIF` is a macro upstream, `CASE WHEN a = b THEN NULL ELSE a END`, and it is a function here because the column is named after the call and not after the expansion. Both things that follow from that macro were measured. A null on the right hands back the left value, since `1 = NULL` is null rather than true, which makes this the second function after `coalesce` that has to sit above the null rule. And the answer keeps the first argument's own type while the comparison happens at the type the two promote to, so `nullif(2, 2.5)` is the INTEGER 2 rather than null or a decimal.

## 0.2.24

The release where a literal is worth the trouble of reading properly. Four changes, two of them text that came back as something nobody wrote, one an optimizer pass that makes the plan the binary makes, and one the gate finally doing the narrowing it has claimed to do since it was written. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A number literal means what DuckDB means by one, which is three different refusals and two values that could not be written at all. `number()` in the binder gave one conversion error to everything it could not read, and the pinned binary answers `Invalid Input Error: Could not convert string '1e' to DOUBLE` for an exponent with nothing behind it, `Parser Error: Already found scientific notation` for a second exponent marker, and `Invalid Input Error: Failed to cast value: Could not convert string "1.2.3" to DECIMAL(4,1)` for more than one dot. That last one was not a message problem. `SELECT 1.2.3` answered 0.123 here, silently, because the scale was counted from the first dot and the width from the digits alone, where upstream counts the scale from the last dot and every dot after the first as a digit of the width, which is how three digits ask for `DECIMAL(4,1)` and `1.2.3.4` asks for `DECIMAL(6,1)`. Digit separators were refused outright, so `1_000` was an error even though the tokenizer had gone to the trouble of keeping it as one token, and they are dropped before anything parses or counts now, which makes `1_000` a thousand, `1e1_0` ten billion and `1_0.5_0` a `DECIMAL(4,2)`. The issue this closes was wrong about the case it was named for, which is the part worth remembering: `SELECT 1e` refuses upstream too, because the tokenizer only gives the exponent marker back when there is a byte behind it to give it back into, so `SELECT 1e;` and `SELECT 1e ` answer a row and the bare statement does not. rudb's tokenizer agrees about all three. What loses it is the statement splitter trimming a statement to its last token before the parser tokenizes the slice again, which is left open as #297, and a decimal whose width is all scale printing a leading zero upstream leaves off is #298.
- A dollar quoted string is the text between the tags rather than the text with the tags still on it. `SELECT $$dollar quoted$$` came back holding `$$dollar quoted$$` and `$tag$body$tag$` came back whole, so a comparison against the same string written the ordinary way was quietly false. The tokenizer was right about all of it, since finding the closing tag is the part that takes work and it does that, and the transform had a case for `'...'` and a fallthrough that pushed the token's bytes unchanged, which is where a dollar quoted token landed. Nothing inside the body is escaped, which is the whole reason the spelling exists, so a doubled quote stays doubled and a dollar that is not the closing tag stays a dollar. The column name comes right for free, because a name is rendered from the value rather than read off the source text.
- A limit moves below the projection above it, which is the pass DuckDB calls `limit_pushdown`. A projection produces one row for each row it is given, so the rows are the same either way and below is cheaper, because the expressions are evaluated for the rows that come out rather than for the rows that were about to be thrown away. It is worth one chunk of work rather than one table of it and the module doc says so: every operator is pulled a chunk at a time and the limit stops as soon as its count is met, so `LIMIT 10` over a hundred million rows already read one chunk and stopped. The reason to do it anyway is that it is the plan the binary produces and `EXPLAIN` is meant to agree with it. Everything that is not a projection is refused, because a filter, a `DISTINCT` and a set operation produce fewer rows than they are given and a sort chooses which rows the limit gets, and all of that was checked against the binary with `SET explain_output='optimized_only'`. A volatile call is not refused, since nothing above the projection is reading the value to decide anything, which is the difference from filter pushdown. The rewrite cannot append, because a node may only point at one behind it in the arena, so the limit and the projection trade the slots they already hold and no node is added, and a projection that more than one node points at is refused rather than traded.
- The focused gate computes its diff on the machine that has the repository, so it focuses for the first time. Every `scripts/gate` run since the focus was written printed `running everything: no git base to compare against` and then compiled and tested all thirty crates. The reason is that the gate copies the working tree to another machine and `cargo xtask ci` worked the diff out there, and this checkout is a git worktree, whose `.git` is a file naming a directory under the main clone that does not exist on a Linux box. So git failed, there was no base, and the only correct thing to do with no diff is everything. The base and the changed paths are worked out on the side that has the repository now and handed over in `RUDB_CI_BASE` and `RUDB_CI_CHANGED`, and `focus::detect` prefers those when they are there and runs git exactly as before when they are not, so a normal clone and the workflow are unchanged. On server2 warm, a one crate change goes from 102s to about 30s and a markdown only change from 102s to about 5s with nothing compiled.

## 0.2.23

The release where a dictionary encoded column stops being read one value at a time. Three changes, two of them the same mistake in different kernels, and the mistake is worth a release of its own because every string column in the published ClickBench file is `PLAIN_DICTIONARY`, so the fast paths those kernels exist for were the paths the real file never reached. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- `LIKE` reads a dictionary through its codes. `like_of` asked the text vector for `data()`, which answers only for a flat vector, so a dictionary encoded text fell out of the compiled path and took the row at a time walk that builds a `Value` per row. The loop is a generic function over an index mapping now, with one arm per form the text can arrive in, which is what `by_form!` and `cast.rs::swept` already do for the two operand kernels, and the mapping is a generic parameter rather than a function pointer for the reason `by_form!` records, which is that an indirect call per row was measured at ten nanoseconds a row. On server2, twenty million rows of a URL shaped string in a cached file, release, single threaded, `count(*) WHERE s LIKE '%google%'` goes from 13.8s to 1.30s when every page is `PLAIN_DICTIONARY`, and stays at 6.4s when every page is `PLAIN`, which is the path that was already right. The dictionary file reads faster than the plain one now, holding 6 MB resident against 19 MB, which is the way round it should always have been, since eight hundred distinct values stay in cache and twenty million separate strings do not. There is no constant arm and there cannot be a useful one: this path needs the pattern to be constant to get as far as the loop, so a constant text as well would be a call whose every argument is constant, and `call` answers one of those in a single row before any kernel sees it.
- The one argument kernels read a dictionary through its codes too. `unary` and `date_of` opened the same way, by asking for `Vector::data`, so `length`, `strlen`, `lower`, `upper`, `not`, unary minus, `abs`, `make_date`, `epoch_ms`, `date_part` and `date_trunc` all fell out on a dictionary and took the row at a time path. `one_of` and `date_runs` hold the loop bodies, so the form dispatch is written once rather than once per function. Measured the same way as the one above: `avg(strlen(s))` 2.49s to 0.50s, `avg(length(s))` 2.80s to 1.00s, `count(*) WHERE upper(s) <> 'nothing'` 5.35s to 2.00s, and `count(*) WHERE lower(s) LIKE '%google%'` 6.89s to 2.50s, that last one being the query the `LIKE` change could not move, because `lower` flattened the dictionary before the predicate ever saw it. `strlen` is the one the board cares about most, since ClickBench q28 and q29 are `AVG(STRLEN(URL))` and `AVG(STRLEN(Referer))` over a hundred million rows and both of those columns are dictionary encoded. What is deliberately not done is evaluating once per distinct value rather than once per row, which is the other factor available here and needs a cache keyed on the dictionary page, because a `PLAIN_DICTIONARY` page can hold tens of thousands of entries against a chunk of 1024 rows. That is state the kernels do not have, so it is written down on the issue instead of half attempted.
- Negating the smallest value of a signed integer type widens by a step instead of raising, and the overflow it does raise says the sentence the binary says. `-((-128)::TINYINT)` is the SMALLINT 128 upstream, and a SMALLINT goes to INTEGER, an INTEGER to BIGINT and a BIGINT to HUGEINT the same way. It is a rule about the one value in each type that has no negative rather than a rule about the type, so `-((-127)::TINYINT)` is still a TINYINT, and the constant folder is the only place that can apply it, because the type of the answer depends on the value and the binder has a cast rather than a value in front of it. Over a column there is nothing to fold and upstream keeps the type and raises, and a HUGEINT has nowhere wider to go, so both of those still raise, and what they say is `Overflow in negation of numeric value!` where it used to be `Overflow in subtraction of INT32 (0 - -2147483648)!`, a sentence that named an expression nobody wrote and came of negation borrowing the binary subtraction path. One case in the same corner is left open as #291 rather than stretched into this one: `-((-128)::TINYINT) + 1::TINYINT` is the SMALLINT 129 upstream and raises here, because the addition was typed TINYINT before its operand widened underneath it.

## 0.2.22

The release where a group by that does not fit in the memory limit finishes anyway. One change, and it is the only reason any ClickBench query fails to produce an answer at all. The storage format version is unchanged, because the spill file is temporary, is read back by the process that wrote it and is removed when the query ends.

- The hash aggregate spills to a file when its table fills the budget, and it splits the input by key rather than by row. A pass that runs out of room keeps the groups already in its table and writes any row whose key is not one of them to a file, whole and unfolded. Nothing already in the table ever goes out, so a key is either finished in this pass or absent from it entirely, and the next pass can aggregate the file knowing nothing about the rows that came before. That is the whole of why no aggregate state is written. Splitting by row would leave a partial state on each side to be merged, and a merge needs a serialize and a combine per aggregate, which `spec/engine/07-aggregate.md` section 7.8 names as debt not yet paid, so every aggregate would have to be touched before anything could spill. Splitting by key means there is nothing to merge and `count`, `sum`, `string_agg`, a `DISTINCT` argument and a `FILTER` all keep working with no change to any of them. The file is one tag byte per value and then the payload, little endian, with the element type of a list and the field names of a struct coming off the schema rather than off every row. Three memory problems turned up on the way and all three are fixed here, because a spill that spills and then dies converting is no better than one that never spilled. The table charge was still held while the table was being turned into rows, even though the map had already been consumed by that loop, so a new `Reservation::shrink` gives part of a reservation back and the aggregate gives the table back and keeps holding the accumulators. The conversion asked for one block of twenty four bytes per group plus sixty four per aggregate per group, which for a million groups is the largest single allocation the query makes, and it now converts one vector of groups at a time and gives the charge back after each one. Finished chunks were carried across passes, which turned the second pass into the first pass plus everything the first pass produced, and each pass now builds its chunks at its own end. On a four thousand group test the peak went from 2.46 MB to 2.03 MB before any spilling happens at all. What is left undone is named rather than hidden. The answer is still held in memory, so a query whose output alone fills the budget still runs out, correctly. Each pass rereads the whole file from the start, so a query needing many passes reads it many times, and hash partitioning the spill so a pass reads one partition is the obvious next thing. A file that would take more than sixty four passes is refused early with an out of memory error rather than run for an hour, using the rows already written divided by the groups a pass finished as a floor on the passes left.

## 0.2.21

The release where a percent sign in a string stops being read as a wildcard. One change, and it is a wrong answer on ClickBench rather than a corner of the grammar. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A `%` in a `LIKE` pattern is tested as a wildcard before it is tested as a literal. The backtracking walk asked whether the pattern character equalled the text character first, so a `%` in the pattern landing on a `%` in the string matched it as itself and consumed one character of each instead of opening a wildcard. `SELECT 'a%b' LIKE '%a%'` was false and is true. What made this worth a p0 rather than a curiosity is where it was reachable from. A constant pattern over a flat vector is compiled once and `%abc%` becomes `str::contains`, which was always right, and that path only engages on a flat vector, so a dictionary vector fell through to the row at a time path and the walk. `URL` in ClickBench is dictionary encoded in most of its pages and encoded URLs are full of percent signs, so `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM hits WHERE URL LIKE '%google%'` over the published ninety nine million rows returned 15902 against the binary's 15911, with the nine missing rows all holding a percent sign after the word google. That was filed as a second bug on the theory that a pattern compiled to a substring search could not be reaching the walk at all, and the theory was wrong about which vectors reach it rather than about what the walk does. Both counts are 15911 now. The test that keeps the two implementations from drifting again crosses them over each other: `%a%` compiles to a substring search and `%a%%` does not, they ask the same question, so they have to give the same answer on every string.

## 0.2.20

The release where the memory limit tells the truth about a group by. Two of the three changes are the same operator making opposite mistakes, one charging forty times what a group cost and one charging nothing at all for half of what the operator holds, and neither was findable without running the real ninety nine million row ClickBench file on a machine where being wrong about memory shows up as a dead process. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- A group key is charged for the copy the table keeps rather than for the buffer it was read into. The row loop fills one `Key` buffer per input row and copies it out only when the group turns out to be new, which is what keeps a group by over a string column from asking the allocator for a buffer on every one of a hundred million rows. A `String` in that buffer keeps whatever the longest string it has ever held needed, and the charge counts capacity rather than length for good reasons of its own, so the charge taken off the buffer charged every group in the table for the longest key in the table. On ClickBench q15 that is a factor of forty: the process held 4.45 GiB and the budget had been told 26.1 GB, so the query was refused by its own accounting with twenty gigabytes of the machine still free. Seven of the eight ClickBench queries that could not finish were this and not a table that did not fit. The fix is to charge the clone that goes into the table, which needs no assumption about what capacity a clone ends up with, because the thing measured is the clone.
- The rows a group by builds out of its table are charged, and the table is given back before the chunks are built rather than after. The end of the build turns the hash table into rows: one vector header per group, and each row is the key's own vector with the aggregate results pushed onto it, which asks the allocator for a block wider than the key was given. For a million groups that is seventy two bytes each, about a quarter of what the query holds, and none of it was charged. On ClickBench q19 it is the gap that let the query finish while holding 30.4 GiB on a machine with 31.4, swapping for most of its hundred and sixty eight seconds, with the budget saying it was under 25.1. It now says out of memory at 20.7 GiB, which is the truth about it, and it goes back beside q33 on the list of two that wait for the hash aggregate to spill. The other half is that the table, the accumulators and the sets behind a `DISTINCT` were held against the budget until the operator finished, which is after the chunks have been made out of the rows, and they are dead as soon as the last group is. Giving them back there is real memory and not only accounting: the six ClickBench queries that do run hold a fifth to a quarter less than they did, q17 and q18 going from 12.3 GiB to 9.6, q34 from 15.4 to 11.3 and q35 from 16.5 to 13.5.
- A nan keeps its sign when it prints. A nan carries a sign bit, DuckDB prints it, and an invalid operation on x86 produces the one with the bit set, so `0 / 0` is `-nan` on the pinned binary and was `nan` here. Rust prints `NaN` whatever the sign and the lowercasing kept that for both. The branch is on the sign bit rather than on a comparison, since no comparison against a nan says anything, which is why the infinity beside it never had this bug.

## 0.2.19

The release where arithmetic stops being approximately what the binary does. Three changes, all of them measured against `v2.0.0-dev84237 (cc7e7bac7f)` rather than reasoned about, and all three found the reference behaviour to be something other than what the issue had guessed. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Dividing by zero raises where DuckDB raises, which is two of the three operators. `/` never raises: it promotes both sides to a double and is IEEE arithmetic from there, so `7 / 0` is `inf`, `(-7) / 0` is `-inf` and `0 / 0` is a nan. `//` always raises. `%` raises on integers and on decimals and follows IEEE on floats, so `7.5::DOUBLE % 0.0::DOUBLE` is a nan. rudb answered NULL for all of them, and a null is the worst of the four answers to be wrong with, because it is the one that does not stop anything: a query that divided by zero inside a large aggregate got a smaller number back here and an error there, with nothing in the output saying which rows had gone missing. The message names the expression rather than the values, and the expression it names is the bound one, so the casts the binder put in are in the text, a column is the name its table gives it, a literal is the value it was folded to, and a binary operator is written between its operands where every other call is written in front of them, unary minus included, which comes out as `-(a)`. A kernel has the two numbers and not the expression, so the caller that has the plan hands down a closure that renders it and the kernel calls it on the row that fails, which is a cost nothing pays until something divides by zero. The prepared form has no plan to consult once a chunk arrives so it renders when the pipeline is built, and the constant folder passes nothing, because a call that fails there is a call that does not fold and the executor raises the same failure later with the expression to hand.
- `//` is integer division when there are integers on both sides of it and it is `/` under another spelling everywhere else. `7.5 // 2.5` was a `DECIMAL(4,2)` holding 3.00 here and is a `DOUBLE` holding 3.0 upstream, which looks like formatting until something downstream depends on the type, at which point a column of these lands as `DECIMAL` in a `CREATE TABLE AS SELECT` and the shell prints the wrong number of digits. Nothing is truncated once a side is not an integer: `7.0 // 2.0` is 3.5, `7.9 // 1.0` is 7.9 and `7 // 2.5` is 2.8. So the float path was wrong as well as the decimal one, and it was wrong in the direction that silently loses digits. A `FLOAT` stays a `FLOAT`, so the rule is about decimals rather than about width.
- The arithmetic overflow errors say what the binary says, down to the punctuation. The type name is the integer the value is stored in rather than the type it was written as, so an INTEGER is INT32 and a USMALLINT is UINT16, and a decimal is named by the width of that integer with no scale on it, so a DECIMAL(18,8) and a DECIMAL(11,0) are both DECIMAL(18) and their operands print unscaled. An integer ends the sentence on an exclamation mark and a decimal on a semicolon, except that a decimal multiplication ends on advice about what to cast to. A decimal subtraction is called `subtract` where every other message uses the noun, which looks like an oversight upstream but is what the binary prints. `abs` is not in this family at all: it says `Overflow on abs(-2147483648)`, with no type in it and no mark on the end, where this engine was reusing the binary subtraction message and claiming that zero minus the value overflowed.

## 0.2.18

The release where two of the passes stop a query from doing work it will throw away, where decimal arithmetic answers the types the binary answers, and where `DESCRIBE` works. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- `DESCRIBE`, which is the statement the differential harness is built on. `rudb-compat` asks DuckDB what types a result has by running `SELECT column_name, column_type FROM (DESCRIBE <statement>)`, and rudb refused it, so the corpus has a comment where two records should be and the harness can only drive one engine. It is a query body rather than a statement kind, which is what the vendored grammar says and is why `FROM (DESCRIBE SELECT 1)` needs no rule of its own and `DESCRIBE DESCRIBE SELECT 1` falls out. The two spellings that name a thing instead of writing a query are rewritten into the query form, which is not a shortcut: on the pinned binary both print the same six columns and the same rows.
