The answer is known once the described query is bound, so the binder binds it, reads the names and the types off the scope and throws the plan away, and what comes out is a `Values`, because a plan that still had a `Get` under it would open a hundred million row file to answer a question about the file's shape. The `null` column is the only part that needed anything new: it has to survive a projection that passes a column through and be lost by one that computes anything, which is a fact about where a column came from rather than about the plan, so the scope carries it. Eleven statements captured off the pinned binary are matched byte for byte.
- A query that cannot match a row stops reading the table. `WHERE false` asks for nothing and rudb answered it by reading the whole table first. `empty_result_pullup` replaces the highest subtree that cannot produce a row with an empty `VALUES` bound to the same table index and the same columns, so the scan under it is gone from the plan rather than run and thrown away. What counts as empty is a filter over a false or null constant, a limit or a top N of zero rows, a `VALUES` with no rows, and anything above one of those that passes its input through. It stops at a group by, which is the case worth being careful about, because an ungrouped aggregate over no rows produces one row and `SELECT count(*) FROM t WHERE false` is still `0`. It stops at a join, a cross product and a set operation too, since an empty relation here is bound to one table index and those three produce columns bound to two. The other half is in the pushdown pass, where a conjunct that is a true constant is not put back as the filter is rebuilt, so `WHERE true` leaves no filter node at all.
- Decimal arithmetic gets the result types DuckDB gives it. A product of two decimals was as wide as the wider operand, where the binary makes it as wide as both of them together, so `1.5 * 1.5` was `DECIMAL(3,2)` here and `DECIMAL(4,2)` there, and a product that needs more than thirty eight digits of scale is refused rather than silently narrowed. Fourteen expressions were compared side by side against the pinned binary on server2 and all fourteen agree now.
- The hash aggregate stops allocating once per input row. Part of the kernel work in #237.
- `read_csv` takes `header`, `delim`, `sep`, `quote`, `escape` and `all_varchar`. It took no named parameters at all, so every call sniffed the file and there was no way to tell it what it already knew, and these six are the ones that change what the sniffer decides, which is where guessing wrong changes the answer rather than the speed. The binder builds what the call said to sniff the schema with and writes it into the plan, and the executor builds the same thing back out of the plan to read the rows with, so the columns a query was planned against are the columns it reads. `all_varchar` is answered in the binder the way `binary_as_string` already is, with the sniffer still running because the names come out of the same pass over the front of the file. The refusal for a name `read_csv` does not take now lists the ones it does, in the binary's own layout, and it reads off the function's parameter table rather than off a list written next to the message.

## 0.2.17

The release where the shell's flags and the string functions stop being approximately what the binary does. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- `strlen`, which is the function that took all forty three ClickBench numbers away. The last full comparison on gamingpc had no rudb row in it at all, because the report said `rudb rudb 0.2.14 did not run: rudb failed: Catalog Error: Scalar Function with name STRLEN does not exist!` and the harness stops an engine at its first failure. It is not another name for `length`: on the pinned reference binary `strlen('héllo')` is 6 and `length('héllo')` is 5, so it counts bytes where `length` counts characters, and it gets a row of its own in the signature table, a vectorized kernel beside the one `length` has, and a test asserting it is not in the alias list, because folding it in there is the obvious wrong fix and it would be wrong by one on every string with a multibyte character in it. Five real aliases arrived with it, for functions the engine already had: `len`, `char_length` and `character_length` are `length`, `lcase` is `lower`, `ucase` is `upper` and `mean` is `avg`. They canonicalize inside the lookup rather than adding a second row to the table, so the plan and every kernel under it sees one name per function, and a test holds that every alias names something the engine really has. The reason the front end test never caught any of this is that `crates/rudb/testdata/clickbench.sql` said `length` in q28 and q29 where DuckDB's published file says `STRLEN`, which is the ClickHouse spelling, and the two engines mean opposite things by those two names. So the test that asserts all forty three queries plan was green while the real benchmark could not get past query 28.
- A string function refuses anything that is not a string. `length([1,2,3])` was 9 here and 3 upstream, 9 being the length of the text `[1, 2, 3]`, because one helper in the signature table built every string function as a cast to VARCHAR. Twelve entries used it and eleven of them should not have. DuckDB refuses `lower(123)`, `length(DATE '2020-01-01')`, `123 LIKE '1%'`, `regexp_matches(123, '1')` and even `length('abc'::BLOB)`, so the rule is VARCHAR already rather than anything a cast can reach, and `||` is the genuine exception since `1 || 'a'` is `1a` upstream. An untyped null is still allowed, because `length(NULL)` is NULL upstream rather than an error. The refusal carries the candidate block, copied off the pinned binary byte for byte including the tabs and the trailing blank line, because those blocks are not derivable from what rudb has: the parameter names are `col0` for some functions and `string`, `regex` and a quoted `"options"` for others, an operator is quoted where a plain name is not, and `length` lists three overloads of which this engine implements one. The one remaining difference is that DuckDB prints `INTEGER_LITERAL` where rudb prints `INTEGER`, which is a separate type for a literal whose type nothing has pinned down yet, is older than this change and affects every binder error the engine prints.
- The command line mode flags are copied from upstream's table rather than derived from the mode list. Every mode here was also a flag, because the argument parser handed anything starting with a dash that it did not recognise to the mode table, and DuckDB has a flag for twelve of its sixteen modes and no flag at all for the other four. So `-duckbox`, `-insert`, `-tabs` and `-trash`, and the three aliases `-lines`, `-tsv` and `-ndjson`, worked here and are an `Unrecognized option` and a non zero exit there, while the modes themselves are fine on both. The flags that do exist do not all do the same thing either: the binary sets both separators for `-ascii`, sets the column separator and leaves the row separator for `-csv`, and sets neither for the other ten, where this shell reset the column separator for all of them. So `rudb -quote` wrote `'a','b'` where `duckdb -quote` writes `'a'|'b'`, and `rudb -ascii` ended a row with a newline where the binary ends it with `\036`. It was measured by reading `.show` after `-separator ';' -newline '@'`, which is the part that makes a flag that leaves a separator alone distinguishable from one that sets it to the value it already had. Twelve new captures and the list of names the binary refuses both come off the binary rather than out of the change, so recapturing on a newer DuckDB is what finds out that upstream changed its mind.
- The command line takes as many statements as DuckDB takes. The first positional argument is the database and every one after it is SQL, run in the order it was written, with no limit on how many, where rudb took two of them and refused a third with `too many arguments, starting at extra`. Asking the pinned binary about a third argument gets no complaint about the command line at all: `duckdb a.db "SELECT 1" extra` runs `SELECT 1`, then runs `extra`, and fails with `Catalog Error: Table with name extra does not exist!`, which is the error of an engine that tried rather than of a shell that counted.

## 0.2.16

The release where the optimizer starts changing what the operators have to do rather than only where they do it, and where a database opened with no settings has a memory limit. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Top N, so an order by with a limit holds the limit and not the input. The plan for `ORDER BY x LIMIT 10` was a limit over a sort, and a sort has to see every row before it can emit the first one, so it held everything and the limit above it threw almost all of that away. `Node::TopN` is the one operator the pair collapses into, under the name `top_n`, which is what `duckdb_optimizers()` calls the same rewrite. The offset is on the node rather than left above it, because the rows that get skipped still have to be found before there is anything to skip them from, so what is kept is `count + offset` rows. The operator collects until it holds twice the bound, then sorts and keeps the better half, which is not the textbook heap and is chosen for a reason: the sort promises that rows tying on every key come out in the order they went in, and a heap does not, because sifting moves equal elements past each other. The cost is the same either way. On one million rows of the ClickBench table, `ORDER BY "EventTime" LIMIT 10` goes from 2.29 seconds to 0.48 and `ORDER BY "URL" LIMIT 3` from 4.85 to 0.88, with the answers byte identical to what the sort produced.
- Null rejecting predicates, so an outer join becomes the join it really is. An outer join exists to produce rows padded with nulls, and a predicate above it that cannot be true of a padded row means those rows were never wanted, so the join that made them becomes a join that does not. `WHERE b.z > 5` over a left join is an inner join, and a full outer join with a rejecting predicate over one side becomes the one sided join that side leaves. The analysis is a six element lattice over one expression, which answers null, true, false, dropped, present or unknown for a row whose padded side is entirely null, and the sixth element is the one that matters: `unknown AND null` is not unknown, it is an expression that is never true, and a five element version of this quietly loses that. Only `coalesce` is tolerant of a null argument, everything else in the function set is strict, and `CASE` and aggregates are always unknown. It is worth an order of magnitude on its own, since the converted join then takes the predicates and the transitive predicates that a left join refused: two forty thousand row tables joined and filtered went from 37.0 seconds to 0.86.
- Transitive predicates, so a join key carries a filter to the other table. An equality between two columns means anything said about one of them is said about the other, and without that a query that restricts one table and joins on a key gives the other table nothing to narrow with. The derived predicate goes through the pushdown rules like any other, so it lands wherever it can land, and it is compared by structure rather than by arena position before being added, because a predicate the query already had would otherwise be written down twice and the pass would produce a different plan every time it ran.
- A memory limit by default, which is eighty percent of physical memory, the fraction DuckDB uses. Eight of the forty three ClickBench queries used to end in `memory allocation of 128 bytes failed`, which is the Rust allocator's abort handler and not something a program can catch. The accounting that raises `Out of Memory Error` instead has worked since 0.2.12 and had no limit to check against. Reading how much memory there is is per platform and lives in `rudb-io`, because two of the three calls are C calls: Linux takes the smaller of `MemTotal` and the control group limit, macOS asks sysctl for `hw.memsize` and Windows calls `GlobalMemoryStatusEx`. The control group half needed a second pull request to work at all, since it read the file at the root of the cgroup mount and a process is almost never in the root group. The group to ask is the one in `/proc/self/cgroup` and every parent of it, because a limit on a parent binds a child as much as its own does and systemd routinely puts the limit on a slice rather than on the scope inside it.
- A hash table is charged for the room it took rather than for what it put in it, which is what makes the limit above stop anything. The aggregate accounted for about two fifths of what it really held, so on server3 with a twelve gigabyte cap and the budget correctly set, ClickBench q16 was still killed by the kernel at 12.5 GB resident and never raised `Out of Memory Error`. What was missing is everything that is not the entries: a `Vec` doubles and so sits between half empty and full, a `HashMap` fills to seven eighths and then doubles as well, and a group allocates four separate blocks each of which costs the allocator a header and an alignment, which on seventeen million groups is seventeen million of those. The containers are now charged for their capacity, once per input chunk, and the per group charge covers only the blocks.
- `SELECT count(*) FROM (SELECT DISTINCT region FROM sales)` came back as 1 on any table with a row in it. Column pruning narrows a projection to what something above it reads, nothing above a `DISTINCT` under a `count` reads anything, so the projection lost every column and the operator underneath was left deduplicating rows with nothing in them to tell them apart. A plain `DISTINCT` is distinct on everything its input produces and names none of it, which is the same shape a set operation has, and it now gets the same treatment: its input is left alone. A `DISTINCT ON` binds to what it reads and still narrows underneath. Found by the committed corpus in tamnd/rudb-compat when the pin there moved to 0.2.15.

## 0.2.15

The release where a query's plan gets rearranged before it runs, and where a pass can be turned off from SQL to prove it. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Filter pushdown, in two pull requests because the second one is where a wrong answer would come from. The first moves a predicate through the operators that have one input: the conjunction is split so half of an `AND` can reach the scan without the other half, a predicate goes through a projection rewritten in terms of what the projection computes from, through a group by when it is over group keys only, and through a sort and a plain `DISTINCT` unchanged, and it stops at a limit, at `DISTINCT ON` and at a set operation. It refuses to cross a projection that would make it copy a volatile call, because `WHERE r > 0.5` over `random() AS r` would call `random()` once to decide and once to report and the row that passed is not the row that comes out. The second crosses a join, which needs to know which tables an expression reads, so the table set analysis this milestone asks for arrives with it as a bitset cached by expression. A predicate moves into a side when what it reads is a subset of what that side produces and the join keeps every row of that side, which is true of both sides of an inner join, of the left of a left, semi, anti or single join, of the right of a right join and of neither side of a full or positional join. A predicate that reads both sides becomes a condition on an inner join and stays where it was over anything else. On the ClickBench partition, counting the groups of `GROUP BY UserID` with a `HAVING` nothing satisfies goes from 0.28 seconds to 0.017, because the grouping stops seeing rows, and the same query with a `HAVING` most rows pass does not move, which is the pass being a pass: it moves work rather than removing it.
- A cross product with an equality over both sides does not become an inner join, which is the one rewrite anybody would expect to find in the paragraph above. It was written, measured and taken back out before the merge. On server2 with ten thousand rows against fifty thousand, the cross product with a filter over it is 1.25 seconds and the inner join with the same predicate as a condition is 10.7, and the same cross product with every column pruned away is 0.060, so producing the pairs is not what costs. Every join is a nested loop that runs its condition over one left row against a chunk of the right side, where a cross product hands whole chunks on and lets the filter run over a chunk of pairs at once. Writing the better plan for the slower operator is how an optimizer makes a query slower while every test still passes, so the rewrite waits for the executor and the pass says so in its own documentation with the numbers in it.
- `SET` and `RESET` as statements, with `disabled_optimizers`, `memory_limit` and `threads` behind them. The first is the one this milestone needed, because turning a pass off from SQL is how a wrong answer gets attributed to a pass, and a corpus file that does it is already written in DuckDB's spelling. A pass name nobody has fails at the `SET` rather than at the next query. Moving `memory_limit` moves it for the queries already running, and a query already holding more than the new limit allows keeps what it has and is refused the next time it asks, which is what the binary does and is the only reading that does not turn a setting into a way of killing whatever happens to be running. `threads` is recorded and not obeyed until the executor has more than one. A `RESET` goes back to what the database was opened with rather than to a compiled in default. All three are global, which is the scope DuckDB gives them, and `SET LOCAL` and `SET SESSION` are refused with the two different sentences the binary prints for them.
- `1GB` and `1GiB` are two different numbers, which corrects the 0.2.10 notes. That entry said they are the same number here for the same reason DuckDB makes them the same number, and DuckDB does not make them the same number. Measured on the pinned binary, `SET memory_limit='1GB'` reads back as `953.6 MiB` and `SET memory_limit='1GiB'` reads back as `1.0 GiB`, so `KB`, `MB`, `GB` and `TB` are powers of a thousand and the `i` spellings are powers of 1024. The stated reason for the old behaviour, that a script saying `10GB` has to get the same number from both engines, is the reason for the new one. A fraction is allowed because the binary allows one, and a limit prints back in the family it was written in.

## 0.2.14

The release that actually goes out. 0.2.12 and 0.2.13 are both tagged and neither one published, so the registry is still at 0.2.11 and everything written under those two headings arrives here. Both failures were in the release workflow's own gate, after the tag was pushed, which is the workflow working as intended and is also the worst moment to find out. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The fall through counters in `rudb-kernels` are counted per thread in a test build. That is what took 0.2.12 down: five tests failed at once, four of them only because the first poisoned the lock the five of them shared. The counters are one table for the process, the test harness runs a hundred and twenty tests of that crate in parallel, fourteen of them read a count and most of the rest call kernels, and a kernel call on a form pair with no specialized loop increments the table, which is the point of the table. So a test resetting the table and asserting a count of one fails whenever one of the other hundred falls through at the same moment. The lock that was there only serialized the fourteen, and the tests that would have had to take it were the hundred. A release build keeps one table for the process, which is what a harness reading the counts at the end of a run wants.
- Cargo.lock has the optimizer's new dependencies in it. That is what took 0.2.13 down. Constant folding gave `rudb-opt` a dependency on `rudb-vector` and `rudb-kernels`, the lock file did not follow, and an ordinary build papers over that because cargo updates the lock as it goes. The release is the one place it does not, since publishing runs with `--locked`.
- `duckdb -csv` ends a row with a newline and `duckdb -cmd ".mode csv"` ends it with a carriage return and a newline, on the same build in the same run, and rudb now does the same. A mode flag sets the column separator and leaves the row separator where it was, and the dot command sets both. It reads like an oversight upstream and copying it is still right, because somebody who reaches for the flag is piping the output into a program that counts bytes. Found by diffing rudb against the binary over ClickBench, where it made thirty six of the forty three queries look wrong when the only thing wrong was one byte at the end of every line. The same query is captured through the flag and through the dot command from the same binary in the same run, and the pair of goldens is the argument that this is the binary's behaviour rather than a capture accident.

## 0.2.13

The release where DuckDB's own ClickBench entry runs against rudb with nothing about it changed, and the first release with a correctness number over the real file to go with it. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Named parameters on table functions, and `binary_as_string`. The published entry reads the file with `read_parquet('hits.parquet', binary_as_string=True)` and rudb had nowhere to put the name, so an argument of a table function stopped being a bare expression and became a name and an expression. The grammar has `:=` and `=>` and it has no rule at all for `name = value`, which parses as an equality and is picked apart afterwards the way DuckDB's own transformer does it, and that third spelling is the one the entry uses. The binder asks the function which parameters it takes, so an unknown name is `Invalid named parameter "nonesuch" for function read_parquet` with the candidates on the end of it and a NULL is refused by name. `binary_as_string` is the only one acted on, as one pass over the file's schema turning each projected BLOB column into a VARCHAR, and the other sixteen names the binary prints say they are not implemented rather than being quietly ignored, because a name the engine knows and silently drops is worse than one it does not know. Without this the twenty eight unannotated byte array columns of `hits.parquet` arrive as BLOB, query 40 fails comparing one against a VARCHAR, and the rest of the string work is wrong more quietly.
- The CSV writer quotes a field on the bytes the binary quotes it on. This was found by the first differential run of all forty three queries, which matched on two of them with nothing wrong with any of the answers. The writer quoted on the separator, a double quote and a line break, which is RFC 4180, and the shell does not follow RFC 4180. It carries a table and quotes on every byte under `0x20`, both quote characters, `0x7f` and the whole of `0x80` to `0xff`. Thirty two of the forty three queries return Russian text, so almost every output differed. The rule is copied here as a table rather than rewritten as a condition, because the apostrophe and the delete are the two entries nobody guesses and a condition is how they get left out. Being right under the standard and different from the reference is still different, and a drop in replacement is judged by a byte comparison of its output.
- Constant folding, and the machinery a sequence of optimizer passes needs. The pass is `expression_rewriter`, in DuckDB's spelling, because a corpus file that says `SET disabled_optimizers = 'expression_rewriter'` was written to turn this off. It folds an expression whose operands are all constants and then applies what folding exposes, so a true operand drops out of an `AND`, a comparison against a null constant is a null boolean, and a `CASE` arm with a false condition is gone. Everything it computes goes through the same kernels the executor calls for the same expression, so a folded answer and a computed answer are the same answer by construction rather than by testing every function twice. Three things stop a fold: a kernel that returns an error, which leaves `CAST('abc' AS INTEGER)` alone so the error still comes from running the query, a volatile function, of which rudb has none yet so the list is defensive, and a value whose type is not the type the plan recorded. The passes run in a fixed sequence rather than to a fixed point, because a pair that undo each other runs forever and the version that stops after a few rounds has a plan that depends on how many rounds it was given, and in a debug build the whole sequence runs a second time and the two plans have to print the same. Folding comes before pruning, since folding removes column references and pruning drops the columns nothing refers to.
- Parquet files written in zstd read. Snappy is what a writer emits when nobody chose and zstd is what somebody chose, so a reader that only does Snappy cannot open most files written in the last few years, including the corpus the benchmark harness writes for itself. The decoder is written in `rudb-compress` rather than pulled in, for the reason that crate already gives for Snappy, and it costs more here because zstd carries a Huffman coder and a finite state entropy coder. Dictionaries are refused by name rather than decoded without one, since Parquet does not use them and decoding without one produces bytes rather than an error. Eleven frames from the zstd command line tool are committed and run through, chosen for what a frame contains rather than what it looks like, and the two real bugs both turned up there and were silent wrong answers rather than crashes.

With named parameters in, and with the star replace list and the two date functions out of 0.2.12, the published load recipe runs unmodified over the one million row partition of `hits.parquet`. Measured on server2 against `v2.0.0-dev84237 (cc7e7bac7f)`, one process per engine loading once and then running all forty three, the load takes 3.65 seconds of wall time against DuckDB's 2.09 and 2.63 CPU seconds against its 4.61, and the forty three queries take 42.3 seconds against its 2.6. Thirty two of the forty three outputs are byte identical. The other eleven are every one of them a `LIMIT` over an `ORDER BY` whose key ties across the cut, or a `LIMIT` with no `ORDER BY` at all, the ordering key multiset is identical on all of them, and which ten rows come back is not something the SQL decides. That is the base number the kernel work has to move and it is the first one that means anything, because it is the same SQL over the same file compared byte for byte.

## 0.2.12

The release where a query can be stopped. Both of the limits the configuration object has been remembering and ignoring since 0.2.10 are now enforced, and the rest is the last of what DuckDB's own ClickBench entry needs in order to run against rudb with nothing about it changed. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The memory limit is real. `Memory` is a budget shared by everything running against one database, so two queries at once are held to one limit between them rather than to one each, because a limit each query gets a fresh copy of is not a limit on the process. Memory is taken through a handle that releases what it took when it is dropped, which is what makes the error path correct without anybody writing the release, and the error path is the one that matters, since running out of memory is itself an error and it unwinds through every operator below the one that reported it. Everything from a value up to a chunk can say what it costs, counting capacity rather than length. The operators that charge are the ones that buffer without bound, which is sorting, grouping, duplicate elimination, joining, set operations and the result a query hands back, because a streaming operator holds one chunk and gives it away again and charging it would count the same megabyte once per level of the tree. Nothing hooks the allocator, so the number is what the operators reserved and not the resident size of the process, which under reports and is the safe direction for a first version. The message is DuckDB's shape, `could not allocate 2.0 MiB (9.0 MiB/10.0 MiB used)`, without the "Possible solutions" tail, which names SET options rudb does not have.
- A query can be stopped while it is running, by `Connection::interrupt` from another thread or by the query timeout the configuration object holds. The error says which of the two happened, because a timeout reported as an interrupt sends whoever reads the log looking for a person who was not there. The executor checks the token by wrapping every node in the tree rather than by putting a check inside the six operators that can loop, which makes "every operator checks before it produces a chunk" a sentence a reader can believe where "these six check" is a claim that has to be re-audited every time an operator is added. The cost is an atomic load per chunk per level of the tree.
- `SELECT * REPLACE (expression AS column)`, which is how DuckDB's ClickBench entry converts four of the hundred and five columns in `hits.parquet` without listing the other hundred and one. A replacement takes the position of the column it names, so the output is still in table order, and it is named the way the replace list spells it rather than the way the table does. Naming one column twice is a Parser Error and not a Binder Error, which is where the binary puts it and is one of the few things about a star that can be decided without knowing what the star stands for.
- `make_date` and `epoch_ms`, the other two functions that entry is written in terms of. `hits.parquet` stores the day as days since the epoch and the three times as seconds since it, all four as integers, and without these two, six of the forty three queries fail comparing an integer column against a date literal while query 19 fails asking for the minute of a BIGINT. `make_date` takes one argument or three and not two, which is the first arity in the table with a hole in it, so an arity can now be a choice of two counts beside an exact one and a range.
- A column is named the way DuckDB names it. `SELECT -i FROM t` came back from rudb with a column called `-i` and from duckdb with one called `-(i)`, and a client keys a row by that name, so it is a compatibility gap even though every value under it was right. Eighty three expressions were swept through both engines and compared header by header. Thirty six of them matched before and all eighty three match now.
- The five sentences about views and collisions that were measured against v1.5.1 before the reference binary was pinned to the v2.0 alpha. A collision now names what is already there rather than what is being created, dropping an entry as the wrong type quotes the name, and `DROP VIEW gone` says View where it used to say Table, which it said because the binder resolves a written name before anything looks at which type the statement said.

## 0.2.11

One change, and the largest number this project has moved so far. Column pruning stopped at projections, and a view expands into one, so every ClickBench query through `CREATE VIEW hits` was reading all hundred and five columns of the file. The forty three published queries over the one million row partition go from about 160 seconds to about 58. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- Column pruning narrows a projection and not only a scan, which it had to start doing the day a view began expanding inline. `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM hits` over a view of `read_parquet` is a count over a projection of a hundred and five columns, and a projection that reads every column keeps every column alive in the scan under it, so the pruning that made the same query over a file name read nothing at all was doing nothing at all through a view. The pass now walks the plan from the root down carrying the set of columns each table index is read for, and a projection nothing reads prunes to no expressions and passes its input's row count through. The queries that barely move are the ones whose cost is the string work rather than the scan, which is the right shape for the pass to have. Neither side of a set operation is narrowed, and that one is a wrong answer rather than a slow query if it is got wrong, because the two sides line up by position and nothing binds to either of them.

## 0.2.10

The release where a program can embed rudb without reaching into it. Everything here is about the surface between the engine and the things that drive it, which is the harness, the benchmark adapter and anybody writing a program against the library. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- An Arrow result path. `QueryResult::to_arrow` gives a `RecordBatch` per chunk and `QueryResult::arrow_schema` gives the schema on its own, so an empty result still says what its columns were. The new `rudb-arrow` crate holds the mapping from a logical type to an Arrow one, the format strings of the C data interface, and the three buffers of an array in the order that interface wants them. The buffers are copied rather than borrowed today and the module documentation says exactly which two stop being copies later, which is the validity bitmap and the fixed width values once a buffer manager owns the pages, and which one never will, which is VARCHAR, because a view into an arena and a run of offsets over one contiguous block are different data structures. The FFI boundary is deliberately not in there: the crate forbids unsafe, so the mapping is testable without a raw pointer, and `ArrowArray` and `ArrowSchema` belong in the C API.
- A configuration object. `Config` carries the memory limit, the thread count and the query timeout, `Database::with_config` and `Database::open_with` take one and `Database::config` reads it back. It is set at open time rather than changeable by `SET`, which is narrower than DuckDB on purpose: a setting that can change under a running query is a setting every operator has to re-read, and there is no operator yet that would honour a change. None of the three is enforced yet and the documentation on each says which milestone makes it real, because a setting that is stored and ignored is easier to find than one that was never accepted. `rudb --print-config` prints them from the object rather than from a literal in the shell, so what it prints is what the engine was opened with. A size is written the way a person writes one, and `1GB` and `1GiB` are the same number here for the same reason DuckDB makes them the same number: a script that says `memory_limit='10GB'` has to get the same answer from both.
- `CREATE VIEW`, which is what lets a published ClickBench query that says `FROM hits` run against rudb with nothing about it changed. The catalog keeps the body as text and the column aliases rather than a plan, so a view follows the tables underneath it the way DuckDB's does, and the body is bound again inline at each reference, so column pruning still reads no columns at all for a `COUNT(*)` through a view. Fourteen tests, every sentence measured off the binary, including the collision message that names the type being created rather than the type already there.
- The questions a harness asks about a statement, answerable without a database. `rudb::split` is the statement splitter for a tool reading somebody else's SQL, `rudb::accepts` and `rudb::parses` ask whether the grammar takes a statement, `rudb::row_order` says whether a query fixed its own order as three states rather than two, and `rudb::line_and_column` and `rudb::where_it_happened` turn a byte offset into the position a person reads. The error type is re-exported with them. Between them they are every reach `rudb-compat` had into `rudb-parse` and `rudb-common`, and that harness now depends on `rudb` and on nothing else in this workspace. That is the point of the change rather than a side effect: the harness is the closest thing this project has to somebody embedding rudb, so anything it needs and cannot get from the one crate is something a user could not get either.
- The reference binary is the v2.0 alpha at the commit the grammar is vendored from, on all four machines, rather than whatever 1.4 or 1.5 a package manager had put there. A published 1.5 is a different language and not a near miss: it rejects `ORDER BY x ASCENDING`, which the vendored grammar accepts, and it accepts `[1, 2] <-> [3, 4]`, which the v2.0 tokenizer cannot read as one token, so two of the eleven dialect checks in the harness were failing on upstream drift rather than on anything rudb does. `scripts/oracle` is what puts that binary on a machine and `VENDOR` now records where it came from next to the commit, so the grammar and the binary cannot drift apart quietly again. The shell goldens under `crates/rudb-cli/testdata` were captured from it, and one of them changed shape in the move: `.mode csv` ends its lines with a carriage return and a newline, and the file that was there had been captured on macOS and had neither the carriage returns nor a reason for not having them.
- `cargo xtask differential` runs the forty three ClickBench queries through rudb and through duckdb over a Parquet file somebody downloaded, compares the answers byte for byte and prints both times beside them. It is a command rather than a gate because the committed fixture is ten thousand rows and every difference found so far needed a million, which is a file too big to commit. Over the one million row partition it is twenty one the same, eight different and six that only rudb answers, and seven of the eight differences are a LIMIT over rows that tie on the ordering column or no ORDER BY at all, so neither answer to those is wrong.
- The reference DuckDB is downloaded rather than built. Upstream's CI keeps a command line binary for every commit to the branch as a public Actions artifact, so `scripts/oracle` takes a minute instead of forty on a four core box, and the binary is the one upstream produced rather than one built here with whatever flags we happened to pick. The build is still there behind `--build`, because an artifact expires after ninety days and because a machine that cannot reach GitHub needs it. The install now checks that `duckdb` on the path really is the file it just wrote and fails saying which file it found instead, which is the part that was silently not happening on three of the four machines, and a `duckdb` that was already there is moved aside under its own version rather than written over.

## 0.2.9

The release where the output is duckdb's output. 0.2.8 could read a directory of files and answer over it, and this one is about what the answer looks like when it comes back and about who is allowed to ask. Twenty one of the forty three ClickBench queries now match the duckdb binary byte for byte over a million rows, up from nine, and seven of the eight that still differ have a LIMIT over rows that tie on the ordering column or no ORDER BY at all, so neither answer to those is wrong. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The counts under a `duckbox` table, which rudb did not write and which were the only difference between rudb and duckdb on fifteen ClickBench queries. Which counts appear depends on how many rows there are and how wide the box is, the row count and the count of what was shown merge onto one line as soon as they both fit next to the column count, and a box with no rows or with rows left out is grown until the count fits under it. None of that is written down anywhere, so all of it came off the binary by sweeping, and a sweep of 3840 shapes over columns, rows, name lengths and types is now byte for byte identical.
- The row of dots that stands in for the rows a box left out, which was centred in the column and should not be. The dot lines up with the middle of a value, and the value it picks is the shorter of the two either side of the gap rather than the shortest one on show, so a column with one short value at the top keeps its dots out to the right. That last part is why a column of small integers has them hard against the right edge and a column of `true` and `false` has them one in from the left.
- A `Database` is a cheap clonable handle rather than a thing with `&mut self` on every method, so a program can use it from more than one place. `Database::connect` gives a `Connection`, which is the same sharing with a name that says what it is for. `Database::open` says what a database name means in one place: the empty string and `:memory:` are in memory, which are DuckDB's two spellings of it, and anything else needs a storage format and so is an error.
- Prepared statements, with all four ways DuckDB lets a parameter be written: `?` numbered by where it is, `?1` and `$1` numbered by hand, and `$name`. The statement is bound again for each set of values rather than planned once and filled in, which is the opposite of what a transactional database does and is the right way round here, because a plan built without knowing whether `$1` keeps one row in a million or half the table is a plan built blind. Parsing is the part that is pure overhead and that happens once.
- `read_parquet(['a.parquet', 'b.parquet'])`, the second overload of both file readers. A list is not a glob written differently, which is the part worth testing. A glob is sorted and deduplicated because it comes from a directory and nobody chose the order, and a list was written by somebody, so it keeps their order and reads a file named twice twice.
- A CSV read over several files sniffs every one of them rather than taking the first file's answer. That is right for Parquet, where a file states its schema, and wrong for CSV, where a file states nothing and a directory of daily exports would come out BIGINT or DOUBLE depending on which day sorted first. Measured at two, three, four and six files. Each file is then told the types the whole read settled on before it parses a row, including the first one.

## 0.2.8

The release where a query reads more than one file. 0.2.7 checked the answers over a single Parquet file, and the two things a real dataset needs after that are the other text format and a way to name a directory of files. Both are here. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- `read_csv` and `read_csv_auto`, with no schema argument, because every benchmark loader in the world relies on the sniffer and a required schema means rewriting all of them. The delimiter, the quote, the header and the column types are all worked out from the file, and every rule in that sniffer was measured against duckdb v1.4.1 rather than reasoned about. The candidates are comma, pipe, semicolon and tab, picked by which one gives a consistent field count with the most columns and comma breaking a tie. A quote only counts at the start of a field. A header is one when every body column is a VARCHAR or when the first row does not fit the types the body has. The type ladder is BOOLEAN, BIGINT, DOUBLE, DATE, TIMESTAMP with VARCHAR at the bottom, the sample is 20480 rows, six spellings of a boolean are taken and `on`, `off`, `y`, `n`, `1` and `0` are not, a leading plus or a zero pad makes a VARCHAR, and a file with no header gets `column0`, `column1` and so on.
- `.csv` and `.tsv` in the replacement scan, so `SELECT * FROM 'data.csv'` binds the same way the Parquet form does. The extension picks the reader and nothing more: a `.tsv` file full of commas is read as commas, which is what duckdb does with one.
- TPC-H SF1 loads through `CREATE TABLE AS SELECT` with no schema argument, all eight tables, about a gigabyte of pipe separated text, and every count and checksum matches the duckdb binary over the same files.
- A pattern where a file name goes, so `SELECT * FROM 'parts/*.parquet'` reads the whole directory, for both readers and through the table function as well as the replacement scan. The syntax is the four pieces duckdb v1.4.1 takes: a star inside one path segment, a question mark for one character, a class with ranges and both spellings of the complement, and a double star for any number of directories including none. Brace expansion is not there because duckdb does not have it either.
- Three rules in that walk that are wrong answers rather than preferences, so all three were measured. The files are read in the sort order of the whole path, because they are concatenated and a directory order would make the same query with a LIMIT answer differently on two machines holding the same files. The first file decides the schema and later files are cast to it. A column missing from a later file comes back with DuckDB's own sentence about it, candidate names and the `union_by_name` hint included, word for word.
- `is_dir` and `read_dir` on `rudb_io::Filesystem`, which the walk needed. The simulated filesystem keeps a listing out of its operation log on purpose, because the crash tests replay that log and a listing changes nothing a replay has to see.
- The two file scans in the executor became one, over a list of paths, with either reader behind a small enum. The comment left when `read_csv` landed said they would stay apart and it was wrong, because the loop over files is the same loop for both formats.

## 0.2.7

The release where the answers are checked against duckdb's answers. 0.2.6 made a query read a file, which is a claim about what runs rather than about what comes out. All forty three ClickBench queries now run end to end over a Parquet file and every cell they settle is compared with what the duckdb binary says for the same file. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- All forty three ClickBench queries, unmodified, answering what duckdb answers. The fixture is committed: `crates/rudb/testdata/hits.parquet` is a hundred and five columns and ten thousand rows in five row groups, and the duckdb script that builds it is committed beside it so the file can be rebuilt rather than trusted. The queries reach it through the replacement scan and `CREATE TABLE AS SELECT`, which is how ClickBench loads duckdb, so the Parquet reader is under every one of them.
- The fixture is shaped rather than random, which took two attempts. The first one had ties everywhere, so `ORDER BY count DESC LIMIT 10` had no single right answer and duckdb disagreed with itself between two shuffles of the same rows. Every group key is now built out of the trailing zero count of the row number, which gives groups of distinct sizes, and the tail folds back into group zero rather than being capped, because capping makes the last group exactly the size of the one before it and puts the tie straight back.
- Three of the queries still have no single right answer and the generator finds which three rather than anybody deciding. It runs duckdb three times, once over the file and twice over the same rows shuffled, and records only what the three runs agreed on. q17, q19 and q41 settle the ordered column and the shape, q18 has a LIMIT with no ORDER BY at all so it settles the names, the types and the row count, and the other thirty nine settle every cell.
- Two compatibility defects in result column naming, which is what a driver keys a row by. An unaliased function result kept the spelling from the query where duckdb folds a function name to lower case as it parses it, so `SELECT SUM(x)` came back as a column called `SUM(x)`. And DISTINCT was dropped from that name, so `count(UserID)` and `count(DISTINCT UserID)` were both reported as the first one, which is a result that names the wrong answer. A column name is still not folded, because that one comes from the catalog rather than from the query.
- A wrong answer in `avg`. It added into a double as it went, and past 2^53 every addition rounds and the roundings do not cancel, so `AVG(UserID)` answered `435091026172918.3` where duckdb answers `435091026172920.25`. An average of whole numbers now totals in an `i128` and divides once, which is the one rounding there should be, and it gives that up for the float path on the first value that is not whole or the first total that would overflow, so a column of doubles lands on the same additions in the same order it did before.
- Column pruning checks that it did not change the answer. The pass records how wide the plan is on the way in and checks it again on the way out, next to the validate that was already there, because a malformed plan is found by whatever runs next and a query that quietly returns one column fewer is found by nothing.
- `CREATE TABLE AS SELECT` reaches the optimizer, which it did not before. `Database::query` and `Database::execute` both ran their plans through `rudb_opt::optimize` and the create path did not, so loading three columns out of a hundred and five decoded all hundred and five and threw a hundred and two away.
- The column naming rules on that statement, every one read off duckdb v1.4.1 rather than reasoned about, because three of them were wrong. A column list shorter than the query renames a prefix and leaves the rest alone, a list longer than the query is an error whose sentence carries DuckDB's own typo, and a query producing two columns of one name is renamed to `a`, `a_1`, `a_2` counting until the name is free rather than refused.

## 0.2.6

The release where a query reads a file. The two halves that 0.2.5 left apart are joined: SQL reaches the Parquet reader, through the function or through the file name on its own, and it asks for the columns the query needs rather than all of them. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- `read_parquet` and `parquet_scan` as table functions. A table function that scans a file cannot be resolved out of a table of names the way `range` can, because its columns are in the file, so binding one means opening it and reading the footer. That is the only I/O in `rudb-functions` and it is why `rudb-io` and `rudb-parquet` are dependencies of a crate that is otherwise pure. An alias renames the call and not the columns, the path has to be a string and a `read_parquet(3)` says so with the signature, and a file that is not there fails at bind time with DuckDB's message rather than at run time with ours.
- A wrong answer under that, found by the first query that ever built the vector. `nulls_of` read a dictionary's values for validity and not the dictionary's own, so a dictionary column with nulls in it counted them wrong. Nothing before this could have failed on it because a dictionary vector with nulls arrives from a file and from nowhere else.
- The replacement scan, so `SELECT count(*) FROM 'hits.parquet'` runs with no function name written. Every rule in it was read off duckdb v1.4.1 rather than guessed. The catalog is asked first and the file is the fallback, so a table really called `mixed.parquet` wins over a file of that name next to it. The extension decides whether a name that is not a table is worth opening. The columns answer to the stem of the file name, since the path has a slash and a dot in it and could not be written as a name, unless an alias says otherwise. A name that is not a Parquet file is the ordinary missing table error and a Parquet file that is not there is the file error, which are different sentences and DuckDB picks between them the same way.
- A parser bug that one turned up. A string where a table name goes kept its quotes, so `FROM 'hits.parquet'` bound a table whose name included the quote characters and the error printed a name nobody had written. The fix is in the transform rather than in the binder, because the matcher only takes a string in two positions and both of them want the text without the quotes, which is also what makes `FROM "hits.parquet"` and `FROM 'hits.parquet'` the same file the way DuckDB reads them.
- The first optimizer pass, which is column pruning. `rudb-opt` was nine lines and a scan read every column in the file whatever the query asked for, which on ClickBench is 20 GB read to answer a question about 200 MB, per `spec/09-optimizer.md` section 9.2. Now a scan reads the columns something above it reads, and pruning to nothing is the right answer rather than a degenerate one: `SELECT count(*)` reads zero columns, which makes it a read of the footer, and both scan operators already handled that before anything relied on it.
- Two narrow rewriters on `Plan` rather than a general `expr_mut`, with the reason written next to them. The type of an expression is stored beside it, so a pass that wants to change what an expression computes adds a new expression and points at it. `rebind` changes which column a column reference names and `node_mut` hands back a node, and neither can get a type out of step because a binding does not carry one and a node does not have one.
- The guard on that pass is computed rather than stated. Instead of never pruning the root, `exposed` walks down from the root and stops at the first node that introduces a table index, which marks every scan whose columns are the query's answer, so a `SELECT *` through a chain of limits and sorts and filters is left alone and a scan under a projection is not.
- An `optimize` seam in `Database`, so both ways in go through one function and a plan dump is the plan that runs rather than the plan the binder built. Verified on a 105 column ClickBench shaped file against duckdb v1.4.1: the count, a filtered count and a three column aggregate all match through the replacement scan with pruning on.

## 0.2.5

The release where you can run it. There is a shell, the Parquet read path goes from a file on disk to a chunk, and the front end plans every ClickBench query from the unmodified SQL. What is still missing is the join between those two halves, which is a table function, and it is the only thing left before there is a benchmark number. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The shell. `rudb -c "SELECT 1"`, a file argument, a pipe on stdin and an interactive prompt, with sixteen output modes and eighteen dot commands, all of them DuckDB's down to the single dash long options it inherited from SQLite. Statement splitting lives in `rudb` rather than in the shell, as `rudb::statements` and `rudb::is_complete`, so it goes through the tokenizer and a semicolon inside a string literal or a dollar quoted body is not a statement boundary. Every output mode is diffed against a real duckdb binary rather than against a description of one, and running that diff found four things that were written down wrong: box, table and markdown left align numbers where only duckbox right aligns them, line mode right aligns the column name in a field at least five wide, csv ends its rows with CRLF, and `.mode` resets the separators but deliberately does not reset `.headers`.
- A bug two layers under the shell, which no existing test could have caught because nothing printed a float where the answer was known. `Value` printed a DOUBLE of -3.0 as -3, which is not a formatting preference, it is a value that reads back as an integer. DuckDB keeps the point, switches to exponent form outside a decimal exponent of -4 to 16, and formats a FLOAT from the float rather than from an f64 made out of one, which is the difference between printing 0.1 and printing 0.10000000149011612.
- The Parquet read path is end to end. The pages of a column chunk are found by walking, because there is no index inside a chunk: decode a header, take the body it sized, start again at the byte after. Then a page becomes a vector, with the wire type deciding how to read and the logical type deciding where to put it, so a `UTINYINT` that arrives as four byte integers lands one byte a value and is checked per value rather than assumed. Then `Reader` works a row group at a time, reads only the projected chunks, and zips them into chunks of `VECTOR_SIZE`.
- Two things in that path are worth recording because both are wrong answers rather than slow ones. A chunk that runs out of buffer with values still owed is an error and not the end of the chunk, since a column with fewer rows than the one beside it is a wrong answer, and that is exactly what a short read from the I/O layer will look like. And a dictionary page does not count against the chunk's value total, so a reader that decremented on every page stops one page early on most of `hits`.
- `Vector::slice` exists because of that reader. Two columns of one row group are free to page differently, so a chunk ends wherever the first projected column runs out of its page, and the first version cut with `Vector::gather` and got a flat vector back every time. A writer puts 2048 rows in a page and a chunk holds 1024, so almost every page of every dictionary column gets cut, and cutting with a gather means no dictionary column ever reaches an operator as a dictionary. A slice keeps each form as itself: a dictionary slices its codes and keeps its dictionary, a sequence moves its start along, a constant stays a shorter constant, and a flat body is the only one that copies.
- The four encodings DuckDB does not write and pyarrow, parquet-java and parquet-cpp do. ClickBench files come from all of them, so a reader that only reads what DuckDB writes is not a Parquet reader. Delta binary packed has two quiet failures in it and there is a test for each: the block minimum is signed and negative throughout a decreasing column so it is added rather than subtracted and the addition wraps, and the header's value count includes the first value, which lives in the header and not in any block. Byte stream split's expected answer is written out by hand in the test rather than computed, because a transpose computed the same way in the test and in the code agrees with itself whichever way round it is wrong.
- `EXTRACT` and `DATE_TRUNC`, with the calendar they are made of, written out rather than pulled in from a date library for the same reason `civil_from_days` already gives: a library that disagrees with DuckDB about a week number is a compatibility bug we would then own without being able to fix. Every number in it was read off the binary first, including the two that look like mistakes. `date_part('millisecond', ...)` carries the seconds with it, so 59.654321 seconds is 59654 rather than 654, and `date_part('century', ...)` of the year 2000 is 20 while `date_trunc('century', ...)` of the same timestamp is the year 2000 rather than 1901, because a truncation drops digits instead of counting centuries.
- `rudb-regex`, at rank 1 in the layer rule, with a regular expression engine written rather than depended on. A parser, a compiler and a Pike virtual machine, RE2 compatible in syntax, in leftmost first semantics and in error messages, because RE2 is what DuckDB links. It is a Pike VM rather than a backtracker on purpose: a pattern that comes out of a column is a pattern an adversary chose, and `(a+)+b` against a long line of `a` is the difference between microseconds and never finishing.
- Two more places where the binary contradicted the documentation. The `never_nl` options do not stop an explicit newline or a negated class from matching a newline, they only cancel `dot_nl`. And `sm` and `ms` answer differently, which means the two are one flag where the last letter written wins rather than two flags that are both on. Both are implemented as measured, with the probe written into the test.
- `regexp_replace`, `regexp_matches`, `regexp_full_match` and `regexp_extract` on top of it, compiling the pattern once per vector rather than once per row. Query 29 runs one pattern over a hundred million referers and compiling it per row would cost more than matching it. A pattern that varies from row to row is legal SQL, falls through to the row at a time path, and counts itself there, which is the same shape `LIKE` and `date_part` already have.
- All forty three ClickBench queries plan, against the official DDL, with the SQL unmodified. The list of gaps in `crates/rudb/tests/clickbench.rs` started at nine entries two releases ago and is empty, and the assertion is equality rather than a subset so it cannot drift in either direction. That is the front end finished as a measure and it is only the front end: planning a query is not answering it, and what a number needs next is `read_parquet` under a table function.

## 0.2.4

Layer three starts arriving. The path from a file on disk into a chunk does not exist yet and two thirds of it are now written: the reads, the footer and the codec. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- `File` has a submission interface. `fn submit(&self, requests: Vec<Request>) -> Completion` takes every range a scan wants in one call, per `spec/engine/05-scan.md` section 5.3, which is what lets a backend issue them concurrently and coalesce the adjacent ones. A `Request` owns its destination buffer and hands it back inside the completion, which is the ownership shape io_uring needs and the reason the interface can grow an io_uring backend underneath without a caller changing. io_uring itself is deferred by name.
- There is an I/O thread pool behind it, sized separately from the execution pool, because the right depth for a local NVMe and the right depth for object storage are an order of magnitude apart and one number cannot be both. `cargo xtask io` is the table that says so, and it set the default rather than confirming it: coalescing is on for a local disk but over a 16 KiB gap, which is small enough not to merge a column projection and still nearly halves a scattered page read. Thread count stays at cores clamped to 2 and 8 because 16 was inside the spread of 8 on every pattern.
- Two measurement bugs in that table are worth recording because both produced numbers that looked like results. The pool was being built inside the timed region, so every sample paid for spawning and joining its threads and the pool lost to the plain `read_at` loop on every pattern. And a cold row measured once is not a cold row: rows doing provably identical work differed by 40 percent and the run to run spread was a factor of three, so the cold path now drops the page cache before each of five samples.
- The Thrift compact protocol decoder and the Parquet footer above it. It is a pull decoder rather than a deserializer, with no derive and no intermediate tree, because a tree allocates a node per field of a footer that on `hits` has a hundred and five columns in every row group and every node would be read once and dropped. An unknown field id is skipped by type, which is what keeps a reader written against one version of `parquet.thrift` working against a file written by a newer one. The bounds it keeps are `min_value` and `max_value` rather than the deprecated pair, since writers disagreed about how they ordered strings and a row group skipped on a bound whose ordering is in doubt is a wrong answer rather than a faster query. A nested schema is an error naming the column, because a repetition level this reader ignored would also be a wrong answer.
- `rudb-compress`, at rank 1 in the layer rule, with Snappy decompression written rather than depended on. `Codec` is Parquet's `CompressionCodec` enum as a type, and `Codec::decompress` treats the uncompressed length from the metadata as a requirement rather than a hint, since a page that decompresses to a different size than the metadata claims is a disagreement that produces a short answer if nobody checks. zstd, gzip, LZ4 and brotli return a not implemented error naming the codec.
- That decoder is tested against seven blocks from Arrow's Snappy codec, which is the same `RawCompress` entry point the Parquet writers call, and the bytes are committed rather than generated because a test that needs a Python environment is a test that does not run. Hand built blocks prove the decoder agrees with my reading of the format description, which is not the same as proving it agrees with the compressors it will meet, because both are wrong in the same direction wherever I misread something. This is `spec/16-testing.md`'s other writers idea arriving for the codec before it arrives for the container.
- `cargo xtask compress` asks whether the decoder is the bottleneck rather than how fast it is, so the unit is megabytes a second of output and the thing to read it against is the cold read rate from `io` on the same machine. It found two things. The overlapping copy stepped by the offset, so a run of 64 identical bytes was 64 one byte moves, and doubling took the runs row from 1195 MiB/s to 3482. And a fixed sixteen byte write for short elements was half right: worth 7 to 13 percent on literals on every payload, and 19 percent slower on the run heavy row when applied to copies, because the non overlapping copy is already one `memmove`. Four builds differing only in which paths took it settled that, and the numbers are in the doc comment next to the code.
- On `server3` the table reads 3591 MiB/s for runs, 4963 for repeated URLs, 720 for near constant integers and 18923 for incompressible bytes, against roughly 1400 MiB/s of cold read. The one row under the line is the integers, and it is not the decoder: Snappy gets 1.5 to one on raw little endian integers because the low byte of every value differs, so the block is nearly all short literals. Delta encoding gets an order of magnitude on the same column by knowing what it is looking at, which is the argument for `rudb-encoding` above this crate, and the poor ratio is asserted by a test rather than left as a comment.
- An expression is prepared once per pipeline instead of walked once per chunk. The tree walk did four things per chunk that depend on nothing about the chunk: it recursed, it resolved every column reference by a linear search through the schema, it cloned a `LogicalType` per node, and it copied the whole column a column reference names. `Prepared` is a post order array so evaluating it is a loop, references are resolved to positions when the pipeline is built, and a column reference is not a step at all, so the column is never copied. `Prepared` is immutable and asserted `Send` and `Sync`, and the per instance working space is a separate `Scratch`, which is the split the scheduler needs at layer eight.
- The first measured version of that was slower than the tree walk, 0.91 times on a chain of eight additions in BIGINT, and the reason is the whole point of measuring it. It held every intermediate to the end of the array, which is nine live buffers at eight kilobytes where the tree walk has two that stay in L1. Liveness is computed when the expression is prepared and a slot is emptied as soon as its last reader has run. The tree walk stays, because it is what the prepared form is checked against expression by expression and row by row.
- A filter threads its selection through its conjuncts instead of running each one over every row and combining the flags. On four conjuncts that each keep a fifth of the rows, three quarters of the old work was over rows an earlier conjunct had already dropped, which is exactly TPC-H Q6. The comparison kernel already took an index closure per side, so `compare::refine` is the same loops with the output position mapped, generic over the mapping rather than holding it in a field, because a mapping the compiler cannot see through is an indirect call in a loop that is otherwise three instructions.
- Every ClickBench query now goes through the front end in a test. The claim is that the SQL is the SQL, so `testdata/clickbench.sql` is the official DDL and all forty three queries character for character, and the test asserts the exact set that fails rather than a subset, so closing a gap without deleting its line fails as loudly as opening a new one. It started at thirty four of forty three.
- Two of those gaps closed here. `NOT NULL`, which the official DDL declares on ninety of the hundred and five columns and which was stopping the table from existing at all, with DuckDB's constraint error text and a per column check that reads a flat vector through its validity mask and asks a dictionary or a constant value by value, since either can hold the null in the body it points at. And comparing a string against the type on the other side, which is what seven queries need for `EventDate >= '2013-07-01'`: promotion is the right question for a `UNION` and the wrong question for a comparison, where `x = '1'` asks whether x is one. Forty of forty three plan now, and the three left are `EXTRACT` at q19, `REGEXP_REPLACE` at q29 and `DATE_TRUNC` at q43.

## 0.2.3

What the kernel table of 0.2.2 was for. It found two missing loops, both of them worth a factor of forty rather than a percentage, and this is the release where the fallback column is empty. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The comparison kernel has a dictionary against flat loop and the flat against dictionary that mirrors it. This pair had no loop, and on `server3` it cost 82.95 nanoseconds a row on BIGINT against 1.2 for the dictionary against constant pair beside it, on the same data and the same operator. VARCHAR went 155.81 to 5.79 and the arena case where the prefix decides went 197.88 to 5.93. It is not a rare shape: a dictionary against a flat column is what a filtered column compared against an unfiltered one is, which is every conjunct after the first.
- The arithmetic kernels have all four dictionary pairings, dictionary against flat, flat against dictionary, dictionary against constant and constant against dictionary. BIGINT against a constant went 91.80 to 1.60 and against a flat column 94.25 to 1.74, DOUBLE 88.11 to 1.91, UHUGEINT 91.29 to 2.46.
- The argument that kept arithmetic from having them is worth writing down because it was true and was still the wrong call. The macro's doc comment said a dictionary belongs in a different loop rather than a different index mapping, because arithmetic over a dictionary wants to compute once per distinct value and hand back a dictionary over the answers. That is still true and it is still the better loop. What it was being used for was an argument for having no loop at all, at forty times the cost of the mapping that was already written and already tested on the three pairings that had it.
- The better loop has a question in it that the mapping does not, and that is the reason to write it carefully rather than quickly. A dictionary's values array can hold an entry no code refers to. Computing over the values evaluates that entry, and if it overflows then a vector with no overflowing row in it raises an error. The per row mapping only ever touches an entry some row points at, so it cannot invent an error the row at a time path would not have produced. The property test's dictionary now leaves its upper half unreferenced so that stays checked rather than remembered.
- All of this was found by a measurement rather than by reading the code, which is the part to keep. Both pairs were correct the whole time and no test in either repository could have noticed, because the answer was right and only the time was wrong. `rudb-bench kernels` now checks 618 cells against a committed record on two machines, and the run reports `0 of them on the row at a time path`.
- That record also says which machine a kernel measurement belongs on. Mean interquartile range over the 618 cells is 1.1 percent on `gamingpc-wsl` and 20.7 percent on `server3`, worst cell 6.9 percent against 562.6, and no cell on `gamingpc-wsl` is over 50 percent where 57 on `server3` are. A threshold widened by a spread of 562 percent is a threshold of eleven times, which is not a gate.

## 0.2.2

The measurement M2b closes on, and the reason 0.2.1 is not on crates.io. The storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- `cargo xtask kernels` is the kernel table: five of them, over every physical layout, five form pairs and three null rates, plus string comparison at prefix decides and prefix ties, the select against compact surface swept over selectivity by chunk shape, and the vector size sweep from 256 to 4096. The five kernel files were each landed with a number quoted in a pull request, and a number quoted in a pull request is a number that exists once. This one runs again on a machine of record, which is what makes a regression something the next change trips over rather than something somebody notices two milestones later. It lives here rather than in `rudb-bench` because it needs in process access to `rudb-kernels` and `rudb-vector` against the working tree, and a harness in another repository would be measuring the last published crate. There is a `--json` form so `rudb-bench` can drive it as a subprocess the way it drives every other engine.
- The sampling rules came out of the front end benchmark into `xtask/src/timing.rs` before any of that was written, so the two tables are produced by one apparatus rather than by two that used to agree. Rule two from `spec/15-rudb-bench.md` is the median of nine samples with the interquartile range printed next to it, never a minimum, and it is now in one place.
- The 2b target of fixed width flat against flat comparison under one nanosecond a row is met at 32 bits and narrower with no nulls, on `server3`: Bool 0.25, Int8 0.27, Int16 0.26, Int32 0.41, UInt8 0.26, UInt16 0.30, UInt32 0.42, Float32 0.70. It is not met at 64 and 128 bits, where Int64 is 1.13, UInt64 1.22, Int128 1.52, UInt128 1.82 and Float64 3.52.
- Having nulls is the cliff rather than how many. Int8 goes 0.27 at no nulls, 1.64 at one percent and 1.44 at fifty, and every narrow layout does the same thing. So the boundary worth optimizing against is `Validity::Mask` against `Validity::AllValid`, and a mask of all ones is the case to catch, not a low null rate.
- 129 of 618 cells fell through to the row at a time path and the table stars every one of them, which turns two missing loops from a counter nobody prints into a column. Comparison has no loop for a dictionary against a flat vector, 60 to 120 nanoseconds a row for fixed width against 1.0 to 1.9 for the same dictionary against a constant. The function call path has no dictionary loop at all, so both dictionary columns of the arithmetic table are the oracle's numbers at 73 to 105.
- The compaction crossover says noise when the gap between the two pass columns is smaller than the spread of the two timings it came out of. That is not a cosmetic choice: a depth computed from a gap that size swings between seven and never between two runs of the same binary, and a table that prints one of those is worse than a table that admits it did not measure it. Where it is firm, one bigint column says 7 to 8 later passes and four say 6 to 9, and the shape with a varchar column says noise or never at every selectivity.
- The vector size sweep does not support the reason `VECTOR_SIZE` is 1024. Comparison goes 1.84 nanoseconds a row at 256, 1.44 at 512, 1.15 at 1024, 1.15 at 2048 and 1.13 at 4096. The curve flattens after 1024 and never turns back up, so the L1d residency argument is not what this machine says, and the sweep is there to find out whether it holds on a machine with a smaller one.
- Two measurement bugs were found by reading the first runs rather than by a test, which is the part worth keeping. The constant operand was read out of the run that already had nulls applied, so at fifty percent nulls it was usually NULL, the comparison took its all invalid fast path, and the cell was a measurement of that fast path filed under a kernel it never called. And the fourth form pair was the wrong one: `dict/flat` was in the table as though it were specialized, and the five pairs with loops are flat against flat, flat against constant, constant against flat, dictionary against constant and constant against dictionary.
- 0.2.1 is not on crates.io and will not be. The publish step runs with `--locked`, the committed `Cargo.lock` was still at 0.2.0, and cargo refused to update it, so the job failed after the tag was pushed, the release was created and all five archives were built. The tag stays where it is rather than being moved, because a tag that is already out is worse to move than a gap in the crates.io history is to explain. `cargo xtask version` now moves the lock file too, and the gate asks git whether the lock differs from what is committed, which is the check that catches the real failure: the file on disk was never stale for long, because any cargo command rewrites it, and what was stale was the commit.

## 0.2.1

Layer one of the engine plan, continued. The data plane's last three interface decisions and the list the kernels generate their loops from. Every box on M2b is ticked and the storage format version is unchanged, because nothing here writes a file.

- The physical layouts are one list, in `rudb-vector`, and every kernel generates its per layout loops from it. Each kernel already wrote its body once and let a local macro repeat it, so the loops were never the problem. The list was: the set of layouts each macro is expanded over was written out at ten call sites across four files, and a list written ten times is a list that is wrong in one of them. That failure is not a compile error, it is one layout missing from one kernel's match, falling through to the row at a time path, and what anybody notices is that one column type is thirty times slower than the others. `for_each_layout` hands a named group to a caller's macro, each entry being the variant, the element type and the value that fills a slot whose row is null.
- The groups have rules rather than membership lists. `ordered` is the layouts whose SQL order is the derived order of the element type, which is why neither the floats nor the strings are in it. `exact` is the integers whose every value fits in an `i128`. `narrow` is the integers under 128 bits, which is both the group whose whole vector totals inside an `i128` and the group a run of `i128` narrows into, and those two rules having the same answer is why there is one group rather than two.
- The list is checked rather than trusted. `Data::len` is generated from the `all` group and its match has no wildcard arm, so a variant added to `Data` without being added to the list does not compile. Every other group is pinned to `all` by a test, each one plus the layouts it deliberately leaves out being exactly the group above it, so a new fixed width layout that nobody adds to `ordered` fails a test with the name of the layout in the message. Deriving the groups from one list instead of checking them against it would need a macro that can compare two identifiers, which `macro_rules` cannot do, and the alternative is a procedural macro crate for nine lists.
- The payload of a flat vector is a `Buffer<T>` rather than a `Vec<T>`, which is the seam the buffer manager arrives through. At layer three a scan reads a page and the vector should point into it rather than copy out of it, and that copy is the largest one in the system because it is every byte of every column every query reads.
